### [1] [HyperWorld: Hypergraph-Structured State Serialization Improves Learned Textual World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00002)
*Yun-Jian Zhang,Chen-Wei Liang,Tian-Yi Zhang,Jian Ding,Yi-Lun Wu,Ao-Bo Li,Wei-Cong Su,Saifullah,Hong-Yu An,Mu-Jiang-Shan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HyperWorld is a controlled study showing that entity-centered hyperedge serialization of textual states (grouping related facts around entities and relations) serves as a simple yet effective inductive bias for learned symbolic world models in text environments, yielding the strongest out-of-distribution performance, best small-to-medium-scale trade-off, and highest downstream planning success rates—especially when model capacity is limited.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work on world models for language-model agents in text environments treats state serialization largely as an implementation detail. The role of how ground-truth symbolic state is serialized into text remains underexplored, yet it may significantly impact the model's ability to learn action effects and judge action feasibility. The authors aim to fill this gap with a controlled comparison.

Method: HyperWorld compares raw observations with three symbolic serializations of the same ground-truth state: (1) independent sentences, (2) pairwise triples, and (3) entity-centered hyperedge units that group multiple related facts around entities and relations. All variants use the same training objective: given a state and an action, predict symbolic effects or judge infeasibility. Evaluations are conducted across model scales, data budgets, and both in-distribution and out-of-distribution test worlds, plus downstream greedy planning.

Result: Hyperedge serialization yields the clearest gains for 0.5B–1.5B models and under distribution shift. Larger models reduce the gap. Pairwise triples can match or slightly exceed hyperedges on in-distribution exact match, but hyperedges achieve the strongest out-of-distribution fact F1 and the best small-to-medium scale trade-off between feasibility detection and effect prediction. In downstream greedy planning, the hyperedge world model attains the highest success rate.

Conclusion: Higher-order state organization (entity-centered hyperedges) is a simple but effective inductive bias for learned symbolic world models, particularly when model capacity is limited or test environments diverge from training distribution.

Abstract: World models enable language-model agents to predict environment dynamics and plan before acting. In text environments, the model must learn symbolic action effects from serialized state descriptions, but the role of serialization structure remains underexplored. We present HyperWorld, a controlled study of state serialization for learned textual world models. We compare raw observations with three symbolic serializations of the same ground-truth state: independent sentences, pairwise triples, and entity-centered hyperedge units that group multiple related facts around entities and relations. All variants use the same training objective: given a state and an action, predict symbolic effects or judge the action infeasible. Across model scales, data budgets, and in-distribution and out-of-distribution test worlds, hyperedge serialization gives the clearest gains for 0.5B--1.5B models and under distribution shift. Larger models reduce the gap, and pairwise triples can match or slightly exceed hyperedges on in-distribution exact match, but hyperedges achieve the strongest out-of-distribution fact F1 and the best small-to-medium scale trade-off between feasibility detection and effect prediction. In downstream greedy planning, the hyperedge world model also attains the highest success rate among the tested representations. These results show that higher-order state organization is a simple but effective inductive bias for learned symbolic world models, especially when model capacity is limited or test environments differ from training.

</details>


### [2] [I-CARE: Analysis of interference-related phenomena in a controllable, diverse and representative unlearning setting for text-to-image models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00003)
*Leonardo Santiago Benitez Pereira,Marcos Escudero Viñolo,Luis Herranz Arribas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: I-CARE is a formal methodology for studying interference in generative machine unlearning, providing standardized definitions for tasks, metrics, and reporting templates to enable systematic and reproducible analysis of how unlearning unintentionally degrades semantically related retained concepts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Despite rapid progress in generative machine unlearning, interference (unintended degradation of semantically related retained concepts) remains poorly characterized and inconsistently evaluated across different settings, lacking formal definitions and standardized evaluation practices.

Method: I-CARE formalizes interference as a first-class object of study by providing formal definitions for tasks, metrics, and templates for reporting results. The methodology is designed to be model- and algorithm-agnostic to decouple long-term scientific insight from transient empirical results. A feasibility demonstration is conducted with state-of-the-art unlearning algorithms and frequently used datasets. The framework includes open-source software and a web-based graphical interface for exploration.

Result: The feasibility demonstration shows that I-CARE enables meaningful analysis of interference patterns across multiple unlearning settings, establishing the practical applicability of the proposed framework.

Conclusion: I-CARE provides a durable methodological foundation for the systematic study of interference in generative unlearning, enabling reproducible cross-setting comparisons and long-term scientific progress beyond specific models or algorithms.

Abstract: Machine unlearning studies the removal of knowledge from an AI model, making the system forget a concept it previously learned. Despite rapid progress in generative machine unlearning, the unintended degradation of semantically related concepts that should have been retained (henceforth, interference) remains poorly characterized and inconsistently evaluated. This paper introduces I-CARE, a methodology that formalizes interference as a first-class object of study in generative unlearning. Rather than proposing a new benchmark or unlearning algorithm, I-CARE provides formal definitions for tasks, metrics, and templates for reporting results, enabling the systematic and reproducible study of interference across unlearning settings. While our methodology is designed to remain valid as models and unlearning algorithms evolve, decoupling long-term scientific insight from transient empirical results, we present a feasibility demonstration with state-of-the-art algorithms and frequently used datasets. The results demonstrate that I-CARE enables meaningful analysis of interference patterns across multiple unlearning settings, establishing the practical applicability of the framework. The software implementation of the methodology is provided in an open-source framework, together with a web-based graphical interface that enables exploration of the outcomes of this study without requiring direct interaction with the codebase or specialized data analysis tools.

</details>


### [3] [Discrete-Time MDP Modeling for Multi-Item Capacitated Lot Sizing with Stochastic Demand Timing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00004)
*Léa Bayati,Mohamed Dahmoune,Melek Rodoplu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses a finite-horizon multi-item capacitated lot-sizing problem with deterministic demand quantities but stochastic demand-arrival periods within known time windows. The problem is formulated as a discrete-time Markov decision process (DTMDP) with demand-level production and allocation decisions. A genetic algorithm (GA) is proposed to search over feasible state-feedback policies, evaluated exactly under the DTMDP transition model. Experiments on 330 benchmark instances show the GA achieves ~3.44% average optimality gap on solvable instances and <5% gap threshold on 90 difficult instances, with an average speedup of 6.89±1.41 over the exact stochastic solution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Stochastic demand-timing in lot-sizing problems creates substantial computational challenges compared to deterministic counterparts, as demonstrated by large increases in state space, transitions, solution time, and memory. Given that exact stochastic optimization becomes intractable on large instances, there is a need for efficient heuristic approaches that can find near-optimal state-feedback policies while remaining computationally tractable.

Method: The problem is formulated as a DTMDP with state space, feasible actions, transition kernel, and one-period cost function. The model operates at the demand level, capturing capacity competition, demand-specific backlog, and allocation-dependent inventory dynamics. A genetic algorithm (GA) searches over feasible state-feedback policies, evaluating each candidate policy exactly using the DTMDP transition model. Computational effects of stochastic timing are isolated by comparing each stochastic instance against a deterministic counterpart using most-likely arrival periods. An empirical Bellman-time regression is used to estimate exact resolution times for instances that cannot be solved exactly on available hardware.

Result: Stochastic-timing instances substantially increase state count, transitions, solution time, and memory pressure versus deterministic counterparts. On 330 benchmark instances, the GA achieves an average optimality gap of ~3.44% where exact solutions are available. On 90 difficult instances, the GA stays below the 5% optimality-gap threshold with an average optimization speedup of 6.89±1.41 at 95% confidence. A Bellman-time regression extrapolates expected GA speedup for unsolvable instances.

Conclusion: Stochastic demand timing significantly complicates lot-sizing optimization, making exact DTMDP solution intractable on many instances. The proposed genetic algorithm offers a practical and effective alternative, consistently achieving near-optimal solutions (within ~3-5% optimality) while providing substantial computational speedups. The framework demonstrates that stochastic-timing lot-sizing can be effectively addressed through policy-space search with exact evaluation under the MDP transition model.

Abstract: This paper studies a finite-horizon multi-item capacitated lot-sizing problem in which demand quantities are deterministic, while demand-arrival periods are stochastic. Each demand occurs once within a known time window and must be satisfied no later than its deadline. The proposed model makes production and allocation decisions at the demand level, allowing it to represent capacity competition, demand-specific backlog, and allocation-dependent inventory dynamics. The stochastic problem is formulated as a discrete-time Markov decision process (DTMDP), including the state space, feasible actions, transition kernel, and one-period cost function. To isolate the computational effect of stochastic timing, each stochastic instance is first compared with a deterministic counterpart in which each arrival distribution is replaced by its most likely arrival period. This comparison shows that stochastic timing substantially increases the number of states, the number of transitions, solution time, and memory pressure. A genetic algorithm (GA) is then proposed for the stochastic-timing problem. The GA searches over feasible state-feedback policies and evaluates each policy exactly under the DTMDP transition model. Computational experiments on 330 benchmark instances show that the GA remains close to the exact stochastic solution whenever the latter is available, with an average optimality gap of about $3.44\%$. On the difficult benchmark instances, comprising 90 test cases, the GA remains below the $5\%$ optimality-gap threshold and achieves an average optimization speedup of $6.89 \pm 1.41$ at the $95\%$ confidence level. For instances that cannot be solved exactly on the available hardware, an empirical Bellman-time regression is used to estimate the missing exact resolution time and extrapolate the expected GA speedup.

</details>


### [4] [Incremental Risk Assessment of Progressive Elder Financial Scams via Instruction-Tuned Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00005)
*Parviz Ghafariasl,Weimin Fu,Xiaolong Guo,Shing I. Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a cumulative turn-based risk assessment framework for detecting financial scams targeting older adults in multi-turn text and voice conversations (email, SMS, phone). It constructs a multi-turn dialogue dataset with cumulative risk annotations across two to eight turns for investment, charity, and tech support scam scenarios, and fine-tunes four small language models (Phi-4, LLaMA-3.2, DeepSeek-R1, Qwen3) to incrementally re-estimate scam risk at each conversational turn under resource-constrained deployment settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial scams against older adults increasingly unfold over multi-turn conversational channels where risk signals emerge incrementally across turns rather than at a single point. Effective detection requires models that continuously update risk estimates under resource-constrained deployment settings, yet most existing approaches do not support cumulative, turn-aware risk assessment in this manner.

Method: The framework incrementally aggregates conversational turns and re-estimates risk at each step. A multi-turn dialogue dataset covering investment, charity, and tech support scam scenarios is constructed with each dialogue containing two to eight turns, annotated at every cumulative stage with qualitative risk level, continuous risk score, explanatory rationale, and safety recommendation. Four small language models (Phi-4, LLaMA-3.2, DeepSeek-R1, Qwen3) are fine-tuned under a unified training framework.

Result: Fine-tuned small models capture fraud-related linguistic cues and cross-turn escalation patterns while maintaining compact architectures for mobile and resource-constrained deployment. Phi-4 and LLaMA-3.2 achieve stronger turn-aware risk estimation performance relative to their parameter scale compared to the other evaluated models.

Conclusion: Structured cumulative modeling can support incremental scam risk assessment in deployment-oriented settings, highlighting the potential of compact language models for privacy-aware and on-device fraud protection.

Abstract: Financial scams targeting older adults increasingly occur through text and voice channels such as email, SMS, and phone calls, unfolding over multiple conversational turns that begin with impersonation or casual contact, escalate through trust building and urgency, and culminate in requests for sensitive information or financial transfers. Because risk signals emerge incrementally across turns, effective detection requires models that continuously update risk estimates under resource-constrained deployment settings. We propose a cumulative turn-based risk assessment framework that incrementally aggregates conversational turns and re-estimates risk at each step, enabling dynamic scam monitoring across progressively evolving conversations. A multi-turn dialogue dataset is constructed to cover investment, charity, and tech support scam scenarios, with each dialogue containing two to eight turns and annotated at every cumulative stage with a qualitative risk level, a continuous risk score, an explanatory rationale, and a safety recommendation. Four small language models (Phi-4, LLaMA-3.2, DeepSeek-R1, and Qwen3) are fine-tuned and evaluated under a unified training framework. Fine-tuned small models capture fraud-related linguistic cues and cross-turn escalation patterns while maintaining compact architectures suitable for mobile and resource-constrained deployment settings. Among the evaluated models, Phi-4 and LLaMA-3.2 achieve stronger turn-aware risk estimation performance relative to their parameter scale. These results suggest that structured cumulative modeling can support incremental scam risk assessment in deployment-oriented settings while highlighting the potential of compact language models for privacy-aware and on-device fraud protection.

</details>


### [5] [Long-Horizon State Tracking in LLMs: Executing MD5 through a Deep Sequence of Dependent Tool Calls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00012)
*Dheeraj Mohandas Pai,Lu Xian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a clean benchmark for testing whether LLMs can carry exact intermediate state across long, dependent sequences of tool calls, using step-by-step MD5 hash computation as the testbed—isolating pure bookkeeping/state-tracking from instruction interpretation and arithmetic errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon tasks are understudied in LLM evaluation because per-step accuracy decays catastrophically as errors cascade. Existing agentic benchmarks confound state-tracking difficulty with instruction interpretation, lack control groups that isolate state-tracking, and are vulnerable to shortcuts, making it unclear whether LLMs can carry exact intermediate state across many dependent tool calls at all.

Method: The authors test state-tracking by having the model compute MD5 step-by-step: 196 dependent tool calls over 64 rounds while carrying four 32-bit words (a,b,c,d) in context. MD5 is implemented from scratch (RFC 1321) so every call aligns to ground truth and the digest is checked bit-by-bit. They test gpt-oss-120b (MoE, ~5.5B active params) at temperature 0. In the strongest setting, all primitive tools are replaced by a second LLM (driver + worker). Two key ingredients are examined: keeping the model's own reasoning in context each turn, and voting over a thinking-enabled worker to remove modular-arithmetic slips.

Result: gpt-oss-120b carries the full state across all 196 calls and returns the correct digest on a majority of completed runs. Both key ingredients (self-reasoning in context and voting over thinking-enabled workers) determine success without changing weights. Residual failures are localized by origin, separating state-carrying errors from arithmetic and serving errors.

Conclusion: LLMs can demonstrably maintain exact intermediate state across long, fully-dependent tool-call sequences when the task is interpretively trivial, and failures can be cleanly attributed to specific error sources—state-carrying, arithmetic, or serving—with mitigation strategies that require no weight changes.

Abstract: Long-horizon tasks remain uncommon in large language model (LLM) evaluation, and for a reason: when each step depends on the last, per-step accuracy that looks excellent in isolation decays catastrophically, as errors cascade and the end-to-end failure probability grows sharply with length. Existing agentic benchmarks report end-to-end success but confound this state-tracking difficulty with instruction interpretation, give no control group that isolates it, and are vulnerable to shortcuts such as a hallucinated final answer, so they cannot say why a long run fails. Whether an LLM can carry exact intermediate state across many tool calls at all is itself not well established. We test this cleanly by having the model compute a cryptographic hash, MD5, step by step: a sequence of $196$ dependent tool calls over $64$ rounds while it carries four $32$-bit words $(a,b,c,d)$ in its own context from one call to the next. Interpretation is trivial and, because we implement MD5 from scratch (RFC~1321), we align every call to the ground-truth trace and check the digest to the bit, so any failure is pure bookkeeping. gpt-oss-120b, a mixture-of-experts model with only $\sim$5.5B active parameters per token, at temperature $0$ with a short fixed prompt, carries the full state across all $196$ calls and returns the correct digest on a majority of completed runs. In the strongest setting we replace every primitive tool with a second LLM, so a driver and a worker compute the whole hash from scratch with no exact-arithmetic oracle in the loop. Two ingredients decide success and neither changes the weights: keeping the model's own reasoning in its context each turn, and voting over a thinking-enabled worker to remove its modular-arithmetic slips. We localize the residual failures by origin, separating state-carrying from arithmetic and from serving.

</details>


### [6] [OpenAgentFlow: Enabling System-Wide Safety Boundaries for Heterogeneous AI Agent Fleets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00015)
*Dongsheng Chen,Xiangyu Zhao,Xin Yao,Xuetao Wei*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OpenAgentFlow is a control-plane/action-plane architecture for enforcing safety at the action-commit boundary in heterogeneous AI agent systems. It normalizes diverse agent actions (GUI, API, tool calls, LLM invocations) into a unified AgentEvent stream routed through a shared Policy Enforcement Point, with provenance, session state, audit, and updatable policies maintained in the control plane. Evaluated on Android, it achieves 94.0% accuracy and 95.3% attack block rate on a 300-case benchmark, and 90.8% accuracy on a 100-case emulator suite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents are transitioning from isolated assistants to heterogeneous systems with multiple agents and controllers operating over shared environments. Safety has become a system-level action-governance problem requiring decisions on whether agent-generated actions should be committed before modifying shared state. Existing safeguards are fragmented across prompts, tool calls, GUI actions, and agent-local behavior, leaving cross-step risks obscured and offering limited auditability and policy evolution support.

Method: OpenAgentFlow introduces a control-plane/action-plane architecture that enforces safety at the action-commit boundary. It normalizes pending GUI actions, API calls, tool calls, and LLM-generated invocations into a unified AgentEvent stream, routes each event through a shared pre-execution Policy Enforcement Point (PEP), and maintains provenance, session state, audit records, and updatable policies in the control plane. This architecture provides a shared governable action stream where new rules take effect without modifying agents, prompts, models, or execution paths. The system is instantiated on Android.

Result: On a 300-case action-event benchmark, OpenAgentFlow achieves 94.0% accuracy and 95.3% attack block rate. On a 30-case dynamic-policy suite, it matches expected behavior in 27 cases after new rules are installed. Across 98 traced cases from a 100-case Android emulator suite, it achieves 90.8% raw accuracy and 92.9% trace-adjusted pass rate across GUI, API, and LLM-planned cases.

Conclusion: OpenAgentFlow provides a practical shared enforcement boundary for heterogeneous AI agent fleets, demonstrating effective safety governance through centralized policy enforcement while supporting policy evolution without requiring changes to underlying agents or execution infrastructure.

Abstract: AI agents powered by large language models are evolving from isolated assistants into heterogeneous systems in which multiple agents, planners, controllers, and execution backends operate over the same user or enterprise environment. In such settings, safety becomes a system-level action-governance problem: deciding whether concrete agent-generated actions should be committed before they modify shared state. Existing safeguards cover prompts, tool calls, GUI actions, and agent-local behavior, but often leave enforcement fragmented, obscure risks that emerge across multi-step action flows, and provide limited support for auditability and policy evolution.
  We present OpenAgentFlow, a control-plane/action-plane architecture that enforces safety at the action-commit boundary. It normalizes pending GUI actions, API calls, tool calls, and LLM-generated invocations into a unified AgentEvent stream, routes each event through a shared pre-execution Policy Enforcement Point, and maintains provenance, session state, audit records, and updatable policies in the control plane. This creates a shared governable action stream and allows new rules to take effect without modifying agents, prompts, models, or execution paths.
  We instantiate OpenAgentFlow on Android. On a 300-case action-event benchmark, it achieves 94.0% accuracy and a 95.3% attack block rate. On a 30-case dynamic-policy suite, it matches expected behavior in 27 cases after new rules are installed. Across 98 traced cases from a 100-case Android emulator suite, it achieves 90.8% raw accuracy and a 92.9% trace-adjusted pass rate across GUI, API, and LLM-planned cases. These results show that OpenAgentFlow provides a practical shared enforcement boundary for heterogeneous AI agent fleets.

</details>


### [7] [SCAFFOLD: A Large-Scale Structured Dataset of Computer Science Research Figures with Diagram QA and Chain-of-Thought Reasoning Traces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00018)
*Ranjit Raut,Aarav Subedi,Sagun Rai,Sudan Jha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SCAFFOLD is a large-scale structured dataset of computer science research figures containing (image, caption, context, question-answer, chain-of-thought) tuples for training vision-language models to understand academic diagrams. It includes three versions: SCAFFOLD-157K (3,058 papers, 29,887 figures, 157,387 pairs), SCAFFOLD-37K (36,797 pairs), and SCAFFOLD-12K (12,000 pairs), with baseline experiments conducted on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct using the smallest subset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CS papers depend heavily on diagrams that often carry more information than surrounding text, yet no public dataset pairs these specific figures with captions, context, questions, answers, and step-by-step reasoning—resources necessary to train vision-language models to comprehend such academic visuals.

Method: The dataset is constructed via layout detection and PDF parsing of arXiv computer science papers, combined with an AI-assisted question-generation step, producing structured tuples of (image, caption, context, question-answer, chain-of-thought). Three dataset versions of varying sizes (157K, 37K, 12K pairs) were released, and baseline experiments were run on SCAFFOLD-12K using Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct.

Result: The dataset spans 3,058 papers with 29,887 figures across 157,387 question-answer pairs in the largest version, with medium and small subsets of 36,797 and 12,000 pairs respectively. Baseline experiments were performed on the smallest subset using Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct.

Conclusion: SCAFFOLD fills the gap of missing structured diagram QA data for computer science research figures by providing a scalable, multi-tier public dataset with chain-of-thought reasoning traces, enabling future training and evaluation of vision-language models on academic diagram understanding tasks.

Abstract: Computer science papers rely heavily on diagrams: architecture drawings, system flowcharts, and pipeline schematics that often carry more information than the text around them. There is currently no public dataset that pairs this specific kind of figure with captions, context, questions, answers, and step-by-step reasoning, which is exactly what is needed to train a vision-language model to understand them. We present \textbf{SCAFFOLD}\footnote{https://github.com/theranjitraut/scaffold}, a large-scale structured dataset of computer science research figures with diagram QA and Chain-of-Thought reasoning traces. This dataset consists of (image, caption, context, question-answer, chain-of-thought) tuples from arXiv computer science papers prepared using layout detection and PDF parsing, with an AI-assisted question-generation step. The resulting large-sized SCAFFOLD-157K dataset spans 3,058 papers with 29,887 figures (157,387 pairs), a medium-sized SCAFFOLD-37K dataset (36,797 pairs), and a small-sized SCAFFOLD-12K dataset (12,000 pairs). We used SCAFFOLD-12K for baseline experiments on Qwen2.5-VL-3B-Instruct.

</details>


### [8] [UI-Venus-2 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00028)
*Venus Team,Zhuohan Cai,Haoxing Chen,Jiaxuan Chen,Weizhi Chen,Changlong Gao,Zhangxuan Gu,Yuan Guo,Yusong Hu,Jianrong Jiang,Jianguo Li,Runze Li,Jinzhen Lin,Zhenyu Ma,Changhua Meng,Han Peng,Xinyu Qiu,Shuheng Shen,Zhongyi Shui,Weiqiang Wang,Ming Wen,Zhuoer Xu,Hang Yan,Kaiwen Yang,Ruilin Yao,Nanjun Yu,Zhengwen Zeng,Lianrui Zhang,Yunzhu Zhang,Zhe Zhao,Beitong Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UI-Venus-2 is a general-purpose foundation GUI agent operating across mobile, web, and desktop environments via a unified closed-loop reasoning-action framework, scaling environments (170+ multilingual mobile apps and native desktop OS), tasks (deep-research instruction generation), and verification (trace/sample-level evaluators with visual keypoints and multi-model voting) to bridge the gap from benchmark-oriented models to dependable real-world deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal GUI agents face a significant gap between benchmark-oriented performance and dependable real-world deployment, caused by limited environment coverage, brittle task construction, and unreliable reward verification.

Method: UI-Venus-2 jointly scales three dimensions: (1) Environments by expanding to 170+ multilingual mobile apps and native desktop OSes; (2) Tasks via a deep-research pipeline for function-grounded instruction generation; and (3) Verification using trace-level and sample-level evaluators with visual keypoints and multi-model voting for reliable RL training signals. Safety-aware mechanisms are integrated for controlled execution of consequential actions.

Result: The paper presents UI-Venus-2 as a capable, efficient, and open-source foundation agent, advancing toward more generalizable, verifiable, and self-reflective agents for real-world applications.

Conclusion: UI-Venus-2 advances multimodal GUI agents toward practical real-world deployment by scaling environments, tasks, and verification in a unified framework while incorporating safety mechanisms.

Abstract: Multimodal GUI agents have emerged as a promising paradigm for digital task automation, yet transitioning from benchmark-oriented models to dependable real-world applications remains challenging due to limited environment coverage, brittle task construction, and unreliable reward verification. In this work, we present UI-Venus-2, a general-purpose foundation GUI agent designed to operate across mobile, web, and desktop environments through a unified closed-loop reasoning-action framework. To bridge the gap toward practical deployment, we jointly scale three critical dimensions: (1) Environments, expanding coverage to more than 170 multilingual mobile apps and native desktop operating systems; (2) Tasks, employing a deep-research pipeline for function-grounded instruction generation; and (3) Verification, adopting trace-level and sample-level evaluators with visual keypoints and multi-model voting to ensure reliable RL signals for training. Furthermore, we integrate safety-aware mechanisms to ensure controlled execution of consequential actions. By offering a capable, efficient, and open-source foundation, UI-Venus-2 advances the field toward more generalizable, verifiable, and self-reflective agents for real-world applications.

</details>


### [9] [EULER: Exploring Underused Links with Evidence-Checked Return for Multi-Agent Mathematical Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00032)
*Ren Zhenzhuo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EULER is a multi-agent system that treats mathematical "bridges" (problem transfers across domains) as its core search unit, using direct, adjacent-domain, and distant-domain routes in competition to resolve conjectures. Evaluated on 120 frozen and contamination-screened conjectures from recent JCTA publications, it produced 10 proofs, 3 refutations, and 45 scoped partial results, with bridge-specific stress tests reducing incorrect conclusions from 9 to 3 and a +4.2 positive interaction when bridge material was combined with target-native operations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transferring problems across mathematical communities is expensive and often skipped because different communities use different objects, invariants, and tools. The authors seek to automate and systematize this transfer process as a first-class search strategy rather than an afterthought.

Method: EULER operates as a multi-agent system where the unit of search is a "bridge"—a transfer of a conjecture across mathematical domains. It runs three competing routes: direct, adjacent-domain, and distant-domain. A bridge is retained only if it provides an operation the source representation cannot execute, and if the target-side evidence can be traced back to the original statement via a checked implication. Six ordered stress tests reject invalid bridges before expensive search. Ablation studies isolate the contributions of stress tests and bridge material combined with target-native operations.

Result: On 120 recent conjectures from papers by authors recently published in the Journal of Combinatorial Theory, Series A, EULER produced 10 proofs, 3 refutations, and 45 scoped partial results. Ablations show stress tests reduced incorrect conclusions from 9 to 3, and combining bridge material with target-native operations produced a +4.2 resolved-task interaction beyond either factor alone. Domain distance was not predictive of success; executable operation gain and valid implication returns were.

Conclusion: EULER demonstrates that structured bridge construction and validation across mathematical domains can be effectively automated, with quality control (stress tests) and the combination of transferred and native tools being the key success drivers rather than domain distance per se.

Abstract: Mathematical communities work with different objects, invariants, and tools, so transferring a problem across them is expensive and often skipped. We present EULER, a multi-agent system that takes such a transfer--a bridge--as its unit of search. Around a fixed conjecture, EULER runs direct, adjacent-domain, and distant-domain routes in competition; a bridge keeps its budget only if it supplies an operation the source representation cannot execute and its target-side evidence returns to the original statement along a checked implication. Six ordered stress tests reject invalid bridges before expensive search begins.
  We evaluate EULER on 120 recent conjectures. The conjectures were frozen before search and screened for contamination, and are drawn from public papers by authors who had recently published in the Journal of Combinatorial Theory, Series A, a leading journal in combinatorics. EULER produced 10 proofs and 3 refutations, plus 45 scoped partial results. Two mechanisms held up under ablation: bridge-specific stress tests cut incorrect conclusions from 9 to 3, and bridge material combined with a target-native operation yielded a positive interaction of +4.2 resolved tasks that neither factor produced alone. Domain distance did not reliably predict success; executable operation gain and valid return did.

</details>


### [10] [When Prediction Error Is Not Enough: Evaluating Nuisance-Function Prediction for Causal Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00071)
*Cong Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether prediction error of nuisance functions correlates with causal estimator performance in a partially linear model, using Monte Carlo simulations comparing OLS, GAMs, XGBoost, and DML-XGBoost. Results show prediction error does not consistently track causal bias, and the method with best point estimation (XGBoost) differs from the one with best confidence interval coverage (DML-XGBoost). A proposed joint-error measure was weakly associated with causal bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prediction error is commonly used as a proxy to evaluate nuisance-function estimators in causal inference, but whether it truly reflects causal estimator quality across different performance measures is unclear and under-studied.

Method: Monte Carlo simulations in a partially linear model framework, comparing OLS, GAMs, XGBoost, and DML-XGBoost. Nuisance-function prediction error, bias, RMSE, and 95% confidence interval coverage were evaluated, along with a joint-error measure based on the absolute cross-product of exposure and outcome nuisance function estimation errors.

Result: XGBoost achieved the lowest RMSE among non-oracle methods, while DML-XGBoost provided better confidence interval coverage. Prediction error did not consistently track causal bias across methods or settings. The best point-estimation method was not the best for interval coverage, and the joint-error measure was only weakly associated with causal bias.

Conclusion: Prediction error is useful for evaluating nuisance-function estimation itself but should not be treated as a direct proxy for causal estimator quality. Researchers should assess causal estimators using causal performance metrics rather than relying on nuisance-function prediction error.

Abstract: Prediction error is widely used to evaluate nuisance-function estimators in causal inference, but its relationship with causal estimator performance may differ across performance measures. We studied this question in a partially linear model using Monte Carlo simulations. We compared ordinary least squares (OLS), generalized additive models (GAMs), XGBoost, and Double Machine Learning with XGBoost (DML-XGBoost), evaluating nuisance-function prediction error, bias, RMSE, and 95\% confidence interval coverage. We also examined a simple joint-error measure based on the absolute cross-product of estimation errors from the exposure and outcome nuisance functions. Across the simulated settings, XGBoost had the lowest RMSE among the non-oracle methods, while DML-XGBoost generally provided better confidence interval coverage. Prediction error did not consistently track causal bias across methods and settings, and the method with the best point-estimation performance did not necessarily have the best confidence interval coverage. The joint-error measure was only weakly associated with causal bias and did not provide a useful standalone measure of causal performance. These results suggest that prediction error is useful for assessing nuisance-function estimation, but it should not be treated as a direct measure of the quality of the resulting causal estimator.

</details>


### [11] [MiNER: Fine-Tuned Biomedical Natural Language Processing for Malaria Disease Entity Recognition in Clinical Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00073)
*V. S. Anoop,Devika N*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a fine-tuned BioBERT-based approach for extracting biomedical named entities from malaria scientific literature, demonstrating superior performance over baseline methods and releasing a human-labeled dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Malaria research generates a vast and growing body of literature, making manual extraction of essential biomedical information challenging. There is a need for automated approaches to extract clinically significant entities from this literature to support ongoing malaria research.

Method: The methodology involves: (1) selecting and preprocessing a large corpus of malaria scientific articles, (2) annotating entities of clinical significance, (3) using BioBERT (a pre-trained biomedical language model) to encode text into context-aware representations, and (4) fine-tuning the model with domain-specific annotations using supervised learning for biomedical named entity recognition. Comparisons are made against different encoding and machine learning algorithms.

Result: The proposed fine-tuned BioBERT approach significantly outperforms alternative encoding methods and machine learning algorithms in precision, recall, and accuracy for malaria information extraction.

Conclusion: The fine-tuned pre-trained biomedical language model provides an effective solution for extracting biomedical information from malaria literature. The published human-labeled dataset (for entity and relation extraction) serves as a resource for other health informatics researchers to develop advanced malaria information extraction models.

Abstract: Malaria remains a significant global health burden, necessitating continuous research efforts to understand its complex molecular mechanisms, epidemiology, and potential therapeutic interventions. Extracting essential biomedical information from the vast and constantly growing malaria literature is a challenging task that demands innovative approaches. Recently, pre-trained language models have revolutionized natural language processing tasks, demonstrating remarkable capabilities in various domains. This paper proposes a fine-tuned pre-trained biomedical language model for biomedical information extraction from scientific literature on malaria disease. The proposed methodology selects and preprocesses a large corpus of scientific articles on malaria, and then annotates them with entities of clinical significance. It then leverages BioBERT, a state-of-the-art pre-trained language model, to encode the textual data into context-aware representations. We fine-tune the model using domain-specific annotations and supervised learning to enhance its ability to extract relevant biomedical named entities. Extensive experiments and comparisons with different encoding and machine learning algorithms show that the proposed approach significantly outperforms them in precision, recall, and accuracy. We also publish our human-labeled dataset for entity and relation extraction to enable other health informatics researchers to train advanced models for malaria information extraction.

</details>


### [12] [AI Morbidity and Mortality: A Framework for Clinical AI Failure Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00076)
*Paulius Mui,Dean F. Sittig,Steve Labkoff,Sanjay Basu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes AI Morbidity and Mortality (AI M&M), a structured, blameless case-based review framework for clinical AI failures, which classifies events across four linked dimensions (Trigger, Mechanism, Clinical Pathway, Corrective Action) and complements existing safety mechanisms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current safety mechanisms such as aggregate model monitoring and traditional patient safety reporting are inadequate for reconstructing and learning from individual AI-related errors and near-misses, as they do not explain how risk emerges from interactions among AI systems, clinicians, workflows, and institutional controls.

Method: The framework combines standardized case intake, evidence preservation and investigator-level reconstruction, tool-in-loop attribution, and corrective-action tracking. Events are classified across four linked dimensions: Trigger (condition exposing a vulnerability), Mechanism (process producing risk), Clinical Pathway (consequence for care), and Corrective Action (remediation assigned). The framework was demonstrated via five illustrative outpatient medication and clinical decision-support cases, with two independent clinician reviewers applying all classification axes.

Result: Two independent clinician reviewers reached agreement across all 20 axis-level classifications across the five illustrative cases.

Conclusion: AI M&M is intended to complement rather than replace existing model monitoring, patient safety reporting, and regulatory oversight by converting individual AI-in-workflow failures into actionable institutional learning. Prospective evaluation across institutions, AI systems, and clinical settings is needed.

Abstract: Clinical artificial intelligence is increasingly embedded in real-world care, yet existing safety mechanisms are poorly suited to reconstructing and learning from individual AI-related errors and near-misses. Aggregate model monitoring can identify performance changes, and traditional patient safety reporting can capture adverse events, but neither is designed to explain how risk emerges across the interaction among AI systems, clinicians, workflows, and institutional controls. We propose AI Morbidity and Mortality (AI M&M), a structured, blameless framework for case-based review of clinical AI failures. The framework combines standardized case intake, evidence preservation and investigator-level reconstruction, tool-in-loop attribution, and corrective-action tracking. Each event is classified across four linked dimensions: Trigger - Mechanism - Clinical Pathway - Corrective Action, separating the condition that exposed a vulnerability from the process that produced risk, its consequence for care, and the remediation assigned. We demonstrate the framework using five illustrative outpatient medication and clinical decision-support cases; two clinician reviewers independently applied all four classification axes and reached agreement across all 20 axis-level classifications. AI M&M is intended to complement, rather than replace, model monitoring, patient safety reporting, and regulatory oversight by converting individual AI-in-workflow failures into actionable institutional learning. Prospective evaluation across institutions, AI systems, and clinical settings is needed.

</details>


### [13] [Different representation learning objectives recover distinct latent structures from the same psychometric data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00100)
*Cong Cao,Tassos C. Kyriakides,Pambos Vrasidas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether different representation learning objectives recover the same latent organization from psychometric questionnaires, using 757 matched teacher-child pairs. PCA-based representations capture behavioral phenotypes well but yield poor teacher-child retrieval (Top-1: 0.13%), while contrastive representations dramatically improve retrieval (Top-1: 7.27%, Top-10: 56.14%) at the cost of phenotype preservation. A multi-task objective partially balances both but reduces retrieval performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Psychometric questionnaires contain rich item-level data, yet it is unclear whether different representation learning objectives (e.g., PCA vs. contrastive approaches) recover the same underlying latent organization of behavior.

Method: Data from 757 matched teacher-child pairs (Cyprus ProW preschool baseline) were used. Child SDQ, ASBI, and CBRS item responses were characterized using principal component analysis and clustering to derive four behavioral phenotypes. Three approaches were compared: PCA-based representations, contrastive representations for teacher-child retrieval, and a multi-task objective jointly optimizing alignment and behavioral prediction.

Result: Contrastive learning dramatically improved teacher-child retrieval (Top-1 from 0.13% to 7.27%, Top-10 from 1.98% to 56.14%) but preserved behavioral phenotype structure less effectively than PCA. A multi-task objective partially restored behavioral organization but reduced retrieval performance relative to pure contrastive learning.

Conclusion: Teacher-child correspondence and behavioral phenotypes represent distinct forms of latent organization, and the recovered latent structure from linked psychometric data is contingent on the choice of representation learning objective.

Abstract: Psychometric questionnaires contain rich item-level information, yet it remains unclear whether different representation learning objectives recover the same latent organization. We investigated this question using 757 matched teacher-child pairs from the baseline assessment of the Cyprus ProW preschool trial. Behavioral structure was characterized from child SDQ, ASBI, and CBRS item responses using principal component analysis and clustering, yielding four behavioral phenotypes. A contrastive objective substantially improved teacher-child retrieval relative to PCA-based representations, increasing Top-1 accuracy from 0.13% to 7.27% and Top-10 accuracy from 1.98% to 56.14%. However, contrastive representations preserved behavioral phenotype structure less effectively than PCA-based representations. A multi-task objective jointly optimizing alignment and behavioral prediction partially restored behavioral organization but reduced retrieval performance. These findings indicate that teacher-child correspondence and behavioral phenotypes represent distinct forms of latent organization and demonstrate that the latent structure recovered from linked psychometric data depends on the representation learning objective.

</details>


### [14] [Deploying and Evaluating a Smart-Agriculture Agentic Engine for Full-Season Soybean Farm Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00106)
*Ao Qu,Panagiotis Michelakis,Linyuan Han,Yiannis Hadjiyianni,Kun Ouyang,Konstantinos Siskos,Feng Li,Ran Meng,Jingchi Jiang,Dimitrios Stamoulis,Jie Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FAIRY is a full-stack smart-agriculture agent system deployed at HIT's soybean research farm, built around an "everything is an event" execution paradigm. It integrates production machinery, sensors, drones, satellites, and crop models, and evaluates nine agent controllers across one hundred full-season soybean scenarios using metrics of agentic success, spatiotemporal correctness, token cost, and edge runtime.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable executable and evaluable agentic agronomic operations on full-season spatiotemporal workflows spanning the entire soybean production cycle, addressing the need for a deployable, end-to-end agent system in real-world agriculture with operational coupling between spatial and temporal decisions.

Method: FAIRY is architected around a novel "everything is an event" paradigm in a shared farm process engine, where world evolution, observations, sensor data, crop transitions, machinery actions, and interventions are all represented as state-changing events. On top of this, it implements an agentic stack including atomic agronomic skill libraries, multi-agent controllers, frontier/edge-model execution, trace logging, and deployment profiling. Nine agent controllers are evaluated across one hundred full-season soybean scenarios with an evaluation suite combining agentic success, spatiotemporal correctness, token cost, and edge runtime.

Result: FAIRY was deployed and used to evaluate nine state-of-the-art agent controllers across one hundred full-season soybean scenarios, preserving operational coupling across spatial observations in a 64-ridge field, temporal decision sequences, agronomic constraints, delayed effects, and final yield.

Conclusion: FAIRY demonstrates a complete, production-grade smart-agriculture agent system that unifies heterogeneous data and machinery through an event-driven world model, and provides a rigorous evaluation framework for assessing agentic agronomic performance across multiple dimensions.

Abstract: This paper presents FAIRY, a full-stack smart-agriculture agent system developed for and deployed to an operating soybean research farm at Harbin Institute of Technology's smart-agriculture site. We develop FAIRY to execute and evaluate agentic agronomic operations on full-season spatiotemporal workflows that span ridge preparation, planting, irrigation, fertilization, pest and disease treatment, harvest, grain handling, drying, and storage. FAIRY integrates APIs and infrastructure across production-grade machinery, fixed soil and canopy sensors, multispectral and thermal drones, satellite vegetation products, a weather station, calibrated crop-process models, agronomic records, and multi-season yield histories. The system is built around the novel "everything is an event" execution paradigm, which represents spatiotemporal world evolution, remote sensing and UAV observations, sensor readings, crop-growth transitions, machinery actions, and management interventions as state-changing events in a shared farm process engine. On top of this event-driven world model, FAIRY implements a complete agentic stack: a knowledge library of atomic agronomic skills; multi-agent controller and orchestration backends; frontier- and edge-model execution; full-path trace logging; and deployment profiling on local nodes. We use FAIRY to evaluate nine state-of-the-art agent controllers across one hundred full-season soybean scenarios that preserve the operational coupling between spatial observations in a 64-ridge field, temporal decision sequences, agronomic constraints, delayed effects, and final yield. We develop an evaluation suite that combines agentic success, full-path spatiotemporal correctness, token cost, and edge-device runtime.

</details>


### [15] [Recursive Criticality of AI Self-Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00137)
*Mikhail Burtsev*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper develops a mathematical framework to study when AI applied to AI R&D creates self-amplifying capability growth, introducing a recursive reproduction number (R_AI) that determines whether improvements compound or dampen across development cycles, extending the analysis to multi-actor ecosystems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As AI systems increasingly participate in the R&D process that produces future AI systems, understanding when this creates self-amplifying feedback loops becomes critical for anticipating capability trajectories. The paper aims to formalize when recursive AI development becomes self-amplifying versus merely producing rapid progress.

Method: The authors construct a mathematical model relating AI capability growth rate to baseline research productivity, recursive feedback strength, and research difficulty. They derive a recursive reproduction number R_AI comparing feedback strength against the rate at which progress becomes harder, and extend the model to multiple research actors to examine shared improvements.

Result: The framework shows that when R_AI > 1 the system enters a self-amplifying regime where improvements compound across development cycles, while R_AI < 1 produces damped effects. The transition depends on feedback loop structure rather than capability level itself; self-amplification can precede visible acceleration, and rapid progress can occur without self-amplification. Higher baseline productivity accelerates progress without altering self-amplification status, while research difficulty can terminate self-amplification. In multi-actor settings, shared improvements can make the ecosystem self-amplifying even when no individual actor is.

Conclusion: The framework provides measurable properties of AI R&D systems—recursive feedback strength, propagation efficiency of improvements into successors, cycle duration, and research difficulty growth—that can help distinguish recursive self-amplification from rapid progress arising from other sources, enabling empirical identification of self-amplifying regimes in AI development.

Abstract: AI is increasingly used in the R\&D process that produces future AI systems. We study the conditions under which this feedback becomes self-amplifying. Our model describes how the rate of AI capability growth depends on baseline research productivity, recursive feedback, and the increasing difficulty of research progress. We derive a recursive reproduction number, $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{AI}}$, that determines whether improvements are amplified or damped across development cycles. This quantity compares the strength of feedback with the rate at which further progress becomes more difficult. When $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{AI}}>1$, the effects of improvements compound across development cycles, placing the system in a self-amplifying regime. When $\mathcal{R}_{\mathrm{AI}}<1$, their effects weaken across cycles. The transition depends on the structure of the AI R\&D feedback loop and need not occur at any particular level of model capability. A system can therefore enter a self-amplifying regime before acceleration becomes visible, while rapid progress can also occur without self-amplification. Higher baseline research productivity can accelerate progress without changing whether the system is self-amplifying, but the duration of the development cycle becomes a limiting timescale for amplification. Increasing research difficulty can end a period of self-amplification. Extending the model to multiple research actors shows that improvements shared across organizations can make the overall research ecosystem self-amplifying even when no individual actor is. The framework identifies measurable properties of AI R\&D systems that can help distinguish recursive amplification from rapid progress driven by other sources, including the strength of recursive feedback, how effectively improvements propagate into successor systems, cycle duration, and the increasing difficulty of further progress.

</details>


### [16] [IMPACT: Attention Is the Interaction Map for Scalable Interaction-Aware World Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00161)
*Rongze Tang,Jianjie Fang,Zhaolu Wang,Ziyou Wang,Xvyuan Liu,Haisheng Su,Xin Zhang,Wei Wu,Chen Gao,Yong Li,Zhibo Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: IMPACT is a scalable interaction-aware world model training framework that addresses the supervision-allocation mismatch in MSE denoising objectives by using cross-attention with manipulated-object tokens as an internal spatiotemporal prior, calibrating candidate regions with local prediction errors, and reweighting denoising supervision to improve physically plausible action-conditioned future prediction without external representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: World models struggle to model physically plausible interactions in action-conditioned future prediction. Existing approaches rely on external representations (motion, geometry, semantics) that require auxiliary estimators or manual annotations, limiting scalability. The paper identifies a fundamental training objective issue: globally averaged MSE denoising causes static content to dominate optimization, under-supervising sparse dynamic-object regions critical for interaction generation.

Method: IMPACT leverages cross-attention from manipulated-object tokens as an internal spatiotemporal prior for action-conditioned changes. It samples candidate regions from this prior, calibrates them using detached local prediction errors to construct an interaction map, and reweights the denoising supervision accordingly. This requires neither external representations nor inference-time modifications, making it fully scalable.

Result: Extensive experiments on robot-arm and human-hand manipulation across diverse control modalities and DiT backbones show IMPACT consistently outperforms MSE-trained baselines, improving interaction fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.

Conclusion: The supervision-allocation mismatch under MSE denoising is a key bottleneck for interaction modeling in world models. IMPACT provides an efficient, scalable, internal-prior-based solution that improves physically plausible interaction prediction without external supervision, validated across manipulation domains.

Abstract: World models have made remarkable progress in action-conditioned future prediction for embodied agents, yet still struggle to model physically plausible interactions. Existing approaches address this limitation by constraining the generation process with external representations encoding motion, geometry, or semantics. Obtaining these spatiotemporally dense representations typically requires auxiliary estimators or manual annotations, limiting training scalability. We instead revisit the training objective and identify a supervision-allocation mismatch under the globally averaged mean squared error (MSE) denoising objective: prevalent static content dominates the optimization signal, leaving sparse dynamic-object regions critical to interaction generation disproportionately under-supervised. Motivated by this observation, we introduce IMPACT, a scalable Interaction-aware Model training framework with Prior-guided Attention Calibration and Targeting. IMPACT uses cross-attention associated with manipulated-object tokens as an internal spatiotemporal prior for action-conditioned changes. It samples candidate regions from this prior, calibrates them with detached local prediction errors to construct an interaction map, and uses the map to reweight denoising supervision, requiring neither external representations nor inference-time modifications. Extensive experiments on robot-arm and human-hand manipulation, spanning diverse control modalities and DiT backbones, show that IMPACT consistently outperforms the corresponding MSE-trained baselines, improving interaction fidelity, physical plausibility, and visual quality.

</details>


### [17] [Asymmetries in Spontaneous and Instructed Deception](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00180)
*Josiah Luikham*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether instructed and spontaneous (uninstructed) deception in Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct share underlying mechanisms. The authors found that the two deception settings share a directional component (~0.5 cosine similarity), but show asymmetric transfer: spontaneous-trained classifiers generalize better to instructed data than vice versa, and instructed-derived steering vectors work better on spontaneous prompts than vice versa. The optimal token positions also differ between detection and steering tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While deception in LLMs is increasingly studied, most research focuses on instructed deception (when models are explicitly told to deceive). However, LLMs sometimes deceive users spontaneously without being instructed. Understanding whether instructed and spontaneous deception share the same underlying mechanisms (representations) is crucial for developing effective detection and mitigation strategies that generalize across both settings.

Method: The authors used three complementary analyses on Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct: (1) direction geometry to measure the cosine similarity between deception directions in each setting; (2) cross-setting classifiers to test whether supervised classifiers trained on one deception setting transfer to the other; and (3) cross-setting steering to test whether activation steering vectors derived from one setting can causally influence deception behavior in the other. They also compared token positions used for deriving steering vectors versus applying classifiers.

Result: The two deception settings share a meaningful directional component with approximately 0.5 cosine similarity. Transfer between settings is asymmetric: spontaneous-trained classifiers outperform instructed-trained classifiers when cross-applied, and instructed-derived steering directions are more effective at steering spontaneous prompts than vice versa. Additionally, the optimal token position for deriving steering vectors differs from the optimal position for training/applying classifiers.

Conclusion: Instructed and spontaneous deception in LLMs are partially related but not identical phenomena. The asymmetric transfer suggests each setting has a unique component. The discrepancy in optimal token positions between steering and classification indicates that detection and causal intervention may require different representational access points, with implications for how future deception mitigation methods should be designed and evaluated.

Abstract: Large language models sometimes deceive users without being instructed to. However, much of the study on deception in models involves instructed deception. We investigated the relationship between instructed and spontaneous (uninstructed) deception in Llama-3.1-70B-Instruct. We compared these two deception settings through direction geometry, cross-setting classifiers, and cross-setting steering. We found the two deception settings share a component of direction (cosine of approximately 0.5) and an asymmetry in the transfer between settings regarding detection and causation. Spontaneous trained classifiers performed better on instructed data than vice versa, and instructed derived directions performed better at steering spontaneous prompts than vice versa. Likewise the best token position to derive steering vectors from differed from the best token position to train and apply classifiers.

</details>


### [18] [LLM-Driven Autonomous Vehicles Inherit Human Driver Biases in Pedestrian Yielding: Results and Implications From A New Benchmark](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00192)
*Irem Yoldas,Martim Brandão,Jie Zhang,Odinaldo Rodrigues*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes two novel bias testing methodologies ("All Else Being Equal" and "Self-Consistency" tests) to evaluate whether LLMs and VLMs used to guide Autonomous Vehicle decision-making exhibit human-like biases in pedestrian-yielding decisions. Findings reveal that these models' yielding behavior is influenced by pedestrian gender, ethnicity, religion, disability, age, skin tone, and socio-economic status, raising concerns about the "common sense" model paradigm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Public trust in AVs depends on both technical success and decision-making fairness. Although psychology research has shown human drivers exhibit biases (e.g., lower yielding rates to Black pedestrians), whether general-purpose "common sense" models guiding AVs inherit such human biases remains understudied. The authors argue model bias analysis should be part of standard AV evaluation.

Method: The paper introduces two bias testing methodologies for LLMs and VLMs: (1) "All Else Being Equal" tests, which systematically vary one demographic attribute of a pedestrian while keeping all other factors constant; and (2) "Self-Consistency" tests, which probe whether models produce stable and unbiased decisions across repeated or rephrased scenarios. These tests are applied to assess bias in pedestrian-yielding decisions.

Result: Both LLMs and VLMs make yielding decisions influenced by pedestrian gender, ethnicity, religion, disability, age, skin tone, and socio-economic status. The type and degree of bias differ across models, though common patterns emerge across models.

Conclusion: The findings question the suitability of the "common sense" model paradigm for AV decision-making, calling for either a revision of the paradigm or explicit efforts to address downstream bias before such models are deployed.

Abstract: Public trust in Autonomous Vehicles (AVs) may depend not only on technical success but also on the fairness of their decision making. While a recent trend in AV research involves using general purpose "common sense" models to guide AV decision making, the degree to which these inherit human biases in driving is still understudied. Given that psychology studies have shown human driver biases exist, such as lower pedestrian-yielding rates to Black pedestrians in the US, we argue that analyses of model bias should also be part of AV evaluation. Concretely, in this paper we propose two new bias testing methodologies for Large Language Models (LLMs) and Visual-Language Models (VLMs)-"All Else Being Equal" tests and "Self-Consistency" tests-in order to assess bias in pedestrian-yielding decisions. Our findings show that both LLMs and VLMs make yielding decisions which are influenced by pedestrian gender, ethnicity, religion, disability, age, skin tone and socio-economic status. While the type and degree of bias is different from model to model, we highlight common patterns-and raise questions about the "common sense" model paradigm, particularly the need to either revise the paradigm or address issues of downstream bias.

</details>


### [19] [ReDeck: Step-Level Render-Grounded Refinement for Document-to-Slide Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00194)
*Muzhao Tian,Zezi Zeng,Yifan Yang,Xin Gao,Yan Li,Zisu Huang,Xiaohua Wang,Changze Lv,Mingxi Cheng,Bei Liu,Kai Qiu,Qi Dai,Dong Chen,Yue Dong,Xiaoqing Zheng,Ji Li,Chong Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ReDeck is a step-level render-grounded refinement framework for document-to-slide generation that decomposes slide revision into atomic edit actions with renderer-derived feedback after each step, turning refinement into "one edit, one observation." It uses multi-granular feedback (step-level render feedback, turn-level adaptive critic, submission-level gate) and introduces DeckQuiz, a benchmark decoupling content fidelity, spatial correctness, and design quality. It outperforms existing slide-generation agents across multiple LLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing slide agents rely on monolithic "one version, one feedback" loops where slides are rewritten entirely and critiqued only after rendering at the turn boundary, making local spatial failures (overflow, overlap, clipping, off-canvas placement) difficult to attribute and repair due to delayed feedback.

Method: ReDeck decomposes slide revision into atomic edit actions, returns renderer-derived observations after each step, and employs multi-granular feedback: step-level render feedback for spatial errors, a turn-level adaptive critic for semantic/design guidance, and a submission-level gate for hard layout validation. A benchmark called DeckQuiz is introduced to decouple content fidelity, spatial correctness, and design quality.

Result: Across GPT-5.4, Claude-4.6, and Gemini-3.1, ReDeck consistently outperforms existing slide-generation agents. Ablations confirm that feedback timing and granularity are critical for reliable slide refinement.

Conclusion: Step-level, render-grounded feedback with atomic edit actions significantly improves slide refinement reliability compared to monolithic turn-boundary reflection, and feedback timing/granularity are key design factors.

Abstract: Document-to-slide generation is challenging because slides are dense editable artifacts that require both faithful content selection and precise spatial layout. Recent slide agents adopt iterative reflection, but typically follow a monolithic "one version, one feedback" loop: a slide or deck is rewritten, rendered afterward, and critiqued only at the turn boundary. This delayed feedback makes local failures such as overflow, overlap, clipping, and off-canvas placement difficult to attribute and repair. We propose ReDeck, a step-level render-grounded refinement framework that decomposes slide revision into atomic edit actions and returns renderer-derived observations after each step, turning refinement into "one edit, one observation." To balance local repair with global quality, ReDeck uses multi-granular feedback: step-level render feedback for spatial errors, a turn-level adaptive critic for semantic and design guidance, and a submission-level gate for hard layout validation. We further introduce DeckQuiz, a benchmark that decouples content fidelity, spatial correctness, and design quality. Across GPT-5.4, Claude-4.6, and Gemini-3.1, ReDeck consistently outperforms existing slide-generation agents, and ablations confirm that feedback timing and granularity are critical for reliable slide refinement.

</details>


### [20] [AI Should Not Only Be Helpful. It Should Be Contingent. Artificial Intimacy, Sycophancy, and the Future of Social Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00211)
*Scott Compton,Arjun Nagendran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces "contingency"—the degree to which AI responses vary with user behavior—as a key construct for evaluating conversational AI. It argues that current alignment methods prioritize user approval over behaviorally informative feedback, producing sycophantic noncontingent affirmation, and proposes a framework for contingent AI to support social learning, particularly during adolescence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conversational AI is now embedded in everyday social environments where it shapes interpersonal feedback, yet existing alignment approaches prioritize user satisfaction and fluency over behaviorally informative responses, leading to sycophantic patterns. This may impair users' adaptive calibration in real-world social interactions, especially during critical developmental periods.

Method: The paper draws on behavioral science and social learning theory, then proposes: (1) contingency as a central evaluation construct, (2) a framework for contingent AI including trajectory-based evaluation and models of social consequence prediction, and (3) a cross-disciplinary research agenda spanning developmental psychology, human-AI interaction, and machine learning.

Result: The paper presents a conceptual framework and research agenda rather than empirical results, outlining why noncontingent affirmation from AI reduces learning opportunities and proposing architectural and evaluative directions toward building contingent AI systems.

Conclusion: AI systems should be evaluated not only by user satisfaction but by their impact on human social learning; contingent feedback is essential for interpersonal skill development, and a coordinated research agenda is needed to realize systems that support rather than undermine adaptive social calibration.

Abstract: Conversational artificial intelligence is increasingly embedded in everyday social environments, where it functions as both an informational tool and a source of interpersonal feedback. This perspective introduces contingency, i.e., the degree to which system responses vary with user behavior and its interpersonal consequences, as a central construct for evaluating AI systems. We argue that current alignment approaches, including reinforcement learning from human feedback, tend to prioritize user approval and conversational fluency over behaviorally informative feedback, leading to sycophantic patterns of noncontingent affirmation.
  Drawing on behavioral science and social learning theory, we propose that contingent feedback is a key mechanism through which individuals develop interpersonal skills. When AI systems provide feedback weakly coupled to social consequences, they may reduce opportunities for adaptive calibration in real-world interactions, particularly during adolescence, a critical period for social development.
  We outline a framework for contingent AI, including trajectory-based evaluation and models of social consequence prediction, and propose a research agenda spanning developmental psychology, human-AI interaction, and machine learning. More broadly, we argue that AI systems should be evaluated not only by user satisfaction, but by their impact on human social learning.

</details>


### [21] [ConvDeck: Conversational Paper-to-Slide Generation via Stage-Specific User Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00226)
*Tarik Can Ozden,Sachidanand VS,Furkan Horoz,Ozgur Kara,Dilek Hakkani-Tür,Junho Kim,James Matthew Rehg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ConvDeck is a multi-agent pipeline for conversational paper-to-slide generation that integrates stage-specific user feedback loops throughout the generation process, allowing users to iteratively refine both the outline and final deck via a think-speak-act refinement mechanism. Evaluation shows improved user-goal satisfaction without sacrificing narrative coherence, content quality, or visual presentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing paper-to-slide systems either keep refinement processes closed to the user within internal multi-agent critique-and-revise loops, or only allow user feedback after a complete deck has been produced. This limits users' ability to participate in iterative refinement of narrative flow, content allocation, and presentation emphasis at the appropriate stages of decision-making.

Method: ConvDeck is a multi-agent pipeline that distributes conversational interaction across the generation process through stage-specific loops, enabling users to refine the presentation outline and final slide deck at the points where each type of decision is made. A think-speak-act refinement mechanism allows agents to either directly apply edits or engage in conversational clarification and discussion with the user.

Result: Evaluation demonstrates that stage-specific conversational feedback improves user-goal satisfaction while preserving narrative coherence, content quality, and visual presentation quality of the generated slide decks.

Conclusion: Distributing conversational refinement across the pipeline stages is an effective design choice for paper-to-slide generation, enabling users to participate meaningfully in the iterative process without compromising output quality dimensions such as coherence, content, and visual design.

Abstract: Automatic academic paper-to-slide generation is inherently iterative, because creating an effective presentation requires repeated cycles of generation, critique, and revision. Recent multi-agent systems partially acknowledge this through internal critique-and-revise loops, while conversational approaches allow users to refine generated slide decks through dialog. However, these refinement processes either remain largely closed to the user or introduce feedback only after a complete deck has been produced, limiting the user's ability to participate in the iterative refinement of narrative flow, content allocation, and presentation emphasis. To address this gap, we introduce ConvDeck, a multi-agent pipeline for conversational paper-to-slide generation that distributes interaction across the pipeline through stage-specific loops, allowing users to iteratively refine both the presentation outline and the final slide deck at the stages where each kind of decision is made. These loops are driven by a refinement mechanism in which agents can think, speak, and act, enabling them to either directly apply edits or respond conversationally to clarify user feedback and discuss revision options. Our evaluation shows that stage-specific conversational feedback improves user-goal satisfaction while preserving narrative coherence, content quality, and visual presentation.

</details>


### [22] [Learning What to Retain: Gated-Memory Routing for Efficient Collaboration in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00237)
*Rakibul Hasan Rajib,Mengxing Zheng,Qian Lou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Gated-Memory Routing, a framework for LLM-based multi-agent orchestration that conditions each decision on a learned compact execution memory via a Memory Write Gate (committing only non-redundant reasoning steps) and a Retrieval Gate (supplying each agent a relevant subset), plus an Adaptive Halting Controller for early stopping. It achieves the best average accuracy across five reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.44 points while reducing HumanEval inference cost by 31.9%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
