Motivation: Existing multi-agent orchestration approaches fall into two problematic extremes: routing from the query alone cannot adapt to intermediate progress or errors (hurting accuracy), while routing from the complete execution history accumulates redundant and low-utility context, causing execution-history overload and inflated cost. A compact state that captures useful progress without redundant context is needed.

Method: The framework couples each orchestration decision with a learned execution memory. A learned Memory Write Gate commits only non-redundant reasoning steps to memory, and a learned Retrieval Gate supplies each agent a compact relevant subset, ensuring every decision is conditioned on a clean informative state. At each step the system selects the next role and backbone from this memory, and an Adaptive Halting Controller stops execution once memory holds sufficient evidence for the answer.

Result: Across five reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, the framework attains the best average accuracy, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.44 points, while reducing HumanEval inference cost by 31.9% relative to that baseline.

Conclusion: Gated-Memory Routing demonstrates that compact, gated execution memory effectively addresses the accuracy–cost trade-off in multi-agent orchestration, improving both effectiveness and efficiency. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems tackle complex reasoning by orchestrating how multiple agents are configured and how they collaborate. A central challenge is to adapt orchestration to the evolving collaboration state. Routing from the query alone cannot adapt to intermediate progress or errors, which hurts accuracy. Routing from the complete execution history supplies this missing context, but forces later decisions to process every prior step, including redundant or low-utility ones. This creates an execution-history overload that inflates cost. Effective orchestration instead requires a compact state that captures useful progress without accumulating redundant context. We propose Gated-Memory Routing, which conditions each decision on the query and a learned execution memory. A learned Memory Write Gate commits only non-redundant reasoning steps, and a learned Retrieval Gate supplies each agent a compact, relevant subset, so every decision conditions on a clean, informative state. At each step, the system selects the next role and backbone from this memory, while an Adaptive Halting Controller stops execution once the memory contains sufficient evidence for answering. Across five reasoning and code-generation benchmarks, our framework is both effective and efficient: it attains the best average accuracy, exceeding the strongest baseline by 2.44 points, while reducing HumanEval inference cost by 31.9% relative to that baseline. Code is available at https://github.com/rajibrhasan/gated-memory-routing

</details>


### [23] [Invalidation Contracts for Cross-Episode Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00243)
*Michael Wu,Arquimedes Canedo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces invalidation contracts, a protocol layer for LLM agents that cache recovery suggestions from API errors. The contract attaches version stamps and cacheability hints to recovery suggestions, enabling clients to selectively evict stale cache entries after server-side data drift without re-deriving everything. The contract decomposes savings into validity (protocol-dependent, vendor-independent) and compliance (planner-model-dependent). Evaluated across seven models, three serving paths, two domains, and ~9,400 episodes, row-level invalidation improves compliance substantially and recovers 29-33% of baseline token costs on four of seven models, while table-level invalidation destroys cache benefits. The contract adds only 15% payload overhead with zero contract failures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents cache recovery suggestions from API errors to save tokens and model calls across episodes, but server-side data drift silently invalidates cached fixes. Re-deriving on every episode defeats the purpose of caching, creating a tension between token savings and correctness. The paper seeks a protocol-level solution that allows selective cache eviction without trial-and-error re-derivation.

Method: The authors design invalidation contracts as a protocol layer that augments every recovery suggestion with version stamps and cacheability hints. This allows clients to determine which cached entries are stale and evict only those, preserving valid entries. The framework factorizes savings into validity (protocol-dependent, vendor-independent) and compliance (planner-model-dependent). They evaluate row-level vs. table-level invalidation granularity across multiple models and serving configurations.

Result: Row-level invalidation raises compliance by 0-66.7 percentage points across seven models (55.6-66.7 on three) and recovers 29-33% of baseline token cost on four of seven models. Table-level invalidation drops post-drift first-try rates to 0% on five of seven models. Eviction precision is 1.00 at row granularity on all models. Notably, identical wire bytes yield 100% first-try compliance on Claude Haiku 4.5 but 11% or below on Claude Sonnet 5 due to input-schema conservatism. The contract adds 15% payload overhead with zero contract failures.

Conclusion: Invalidation contracts provide a lightweight, vendor-neutral protocol layer that enables selective cache eviction in error-recovery caches for LLM agents. Row-level granularity is critical—coarser table-level invalidation destroys co-located valid entries. The approach is deterministic in validity and shows that planner-model behavior (compliance) is a separable and significant factor governing realized savings, motivating careful model selection for such cached-recovery workflows.

Abstract: LLM agents that cache recovery suggestions from API errors can skip re-derivation in later episodes, spending fewer tokens and fewer model calls on constraints they have already learned. Server-side data drift turns those cached fixes into silent failures, and the usual remedy, re-deriving on every episode, gives the savings back. We introduce invalidation contracts, a protocol layer that attaches version stamps and cacheability hints to every recovery suggestion so the client can evict stale entries without trial and error, and keep the rest. The contract decomposes realized savings into two independent factors: validity, the fraction of cached suggestions that remain correct after a drift event, and compliance, the fraction the planner applies on the first attempt. Validity depends only on the protocol and is vendor-independent. Compliance depends on the planner model: identical wire bytes yield 100% first-try compliance on Claude Haiku 4.5 and 11% or below on Claude Sonnet 5, which exhibits input-schema conservatism, refusing fixes that add fields the original request did not contain. We evaluate across seven models, three serving paths, two domains, and approximately 9,400 episodes. Row-level invalidation raises compliance by 0 to 66.7 percentage points across the seven models, 55.6 to 66.7 on three, and recovers 29-33% of baseline token cost on four of seven models, while table-level invalidation destroys co-located entries and drops post-drift first-try rates to 0% on five of seven. Eviction precision is 1.00 at row granularity on every model under the row-level oracle of Section 4.1. The contract adds 15% to response payload. Version-stamp validity is deterministic by construction and produced identical results across every model and serving path, with zero contract failures in the entire evaluation.

</details>


### [24] [Authority Bias in Conversational Search Engines for Academic Paper Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00248)
*Uthman Jinadu,Parsa Ghazvinian,Anjila Budathoki,Benjamin M. Ampel,Rajshekhar Sunderraman,Yi Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper causally tests whether LLMs used as academic search engines exhibit authority bias—preferring papers based on author prestige, venue, and citations rather than content—by holding title/abstract constant and varying authority metadata across counterfactual conditions, finding substantial and directional bias that varies across models and is only partially mitigated by prompt-level debiasing, with a documented say-do gap where instruction-following on surface mentions hides persistent behavioral bias.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM-based conversational search engines for academic literature judge papers on content quality or are systematically influenced by authority signals (author prestige, venue, citations), which has not been previously tested causally.

Method: Experimental design with counterfactual manipulation: holding title and abstract constant while varying authority metadata across three conditions (original, flipped, boosted) over eight LLMs (five open-weight, three frontier closed-weight) in an in-context, single-turn, top-1 recommendation setting.

Result: Authority bias is substantial and directional; it varies markedly across models; it is only partially addressable through prompt-level debiasing; and a say-do gap exists where debiasing instructions suppress authority mentions faster than actual authority-driven recommendation flips, meaning surface-level auditing underestimates behavioral bias.

Conclusion: LLM-based academic search engines exhibit significant authority bias that cannot be reliably eliminated via prompt instructions, and naive surface auditing (counting mention of authority terms) systematically underestimates the true behavioral bias due to the say-do gap.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as conversational search engines for academic literature, yet whether they judge papers on content or on authority signals has not been tested causally. We investigate authority bias: systematic preference for papers based on author prestige, venue, and citations rather than content. Holding title and abstract constant, we vary authority metadata across three counterfactual conditions (original, flipped, boosted) over eight LLMs (five open-weight and three frontier closed-weight) in an in-context, single-turn, top-1 recommendation setting. Our experiments show that authority bias is substantial and directional, varies markedly across models, and is only partially addressable through prompt-level debiasing. We further document a say-do gap: debiasing instructions suppress authority mentions far faster than authority-driven flips, so surface auditing systematically underestimates behavioral bias.

</details>


### [25] [Hypotheses-Guided Self Distillation for Continual Personalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00251)
*EunJeong Hwang,Kushan Mitra,Dan Zhang,Hannah Kim,Estevam Hruschka*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HypReflect is a scalable framework for continual LLM personalization that infers explicit, uncertainty-aware preference hypotheses from diverse user signals, reflectively refines them over time, and incorporates the user model via hypotheses-guided self-distillation. It outperforms baselines across online personalization, multi-session interactions, and implicit behavioral signals, with strong generalization to unseen users and cross-domain settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: User preferences in LLM interactions are rarely fully stated and instead emerge through heterogeneous, latent, and noisy signals. Existing personalization methods rely on raw interaction histories or costly reward-based optimization, which are insufficient for reliable, scalable continual adaptation to individual preferences.

Method: HypReflect works in three stages: (1) it infers explicit, uncertainty-aware preference hypotheses from diverse user signals; (2) it reflectively refines these hypotheses as new evidence accumulates over time; and (3) it incorporates the resulting user model into the LLM through hypotheses-guided self-distillation.

Result: HypReflect outperforms baselines including raw-history and incremental-update methods across three personalization settings: online personalization, multi-session interactions, and implicit behavioral signals. It also demonstrates strong generalization to unseen users and cross-domain settings, stability across context budgets, reusable hypotheses, and more focused personalization.

Conclusion: Explicit, revisable user preference hypotheses represent a reliable and scalable step forward for continual personalization in LLMs, offering advantages in performance, generalization, stability, and interpretability over existing approaches.

Abstract: As people increasingly interact with LLM assistants in daily life, continually adapting to individual preferences has become essential for effective long-term interactions. However, user preferences are rarely stated in full, and instead emerge through heterogeneous, latent, and noisy signals, with existing methods relying on raw interaction histories or costly reward-based optimization to manage personalization. We introduce HypReflect, a reliable, scalable framework for continual personalization that infers explicit, uncertainty-aware preference hypotheses from diverse user signals, reflectively refines them as new evidence accumulates, and incorporates the resulting user model through hypotheses-guided self-distillation. Experiments across three personalization settings: online personalization, multi-session interactions, and implicit behavioral signals, show that HypReflect outperforms a range of baselines, including raw-history and incremental-update methods. We further demonstrate strong generalization to unseen users and cross-domain settings, along with stability across context budgets, reusable hypotheses, and more focused personalization. These results suggest a step towards reliable and scalable continual personalization through explicit, revisable user preference hypotheses.

</details>


### [26] [The Answer Is Not the Argument](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00264)
*Will Yeadon,Sergio Juárez,Paul Mackay,T. J. Dowling,Elise Agra,Oto-obong Inyang,Arin Mizouri,Craig P. Testrow*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study investigates whether providing LLM monitors with a trusted reference answer improves reasoning verification or merely exposes incorrect conclusions. Using 237 step-numbered solutions to 79 physics questions with 24 "critical traces" (correct answers containing genuine errors), the authors find that answer access improves monitors' ability to catch wrong-answer traces (recall 0.653→0.951) but not critical traces (0.521→0.438), concluding that answer access facilitates conclusion-consistency checking rather than independent verification of supporting arguments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought monitoring is proposed for AI oversight, yet evaluations often provide monitors with a trusted reference answer. The authors ask whether answer access genuinely improves reasoning verification or mainly exposes incorrect conclusions, which is critical for understanding the validity of AI monitoring evaluations.

Method: The study collected 237 step-numbered solutions to 79 Humanity's Last Exam physics questions from three frontier models with no inserted errors. Final-answer correctness and the first false step were independently labelled using a reference standard combining physicist annotations, independent LLM debate, and source-masked adjudication. This identified 24 critical traces (correct answer but genuine error present). Eight LLM monitors evaluated traces under three conditions: blind, with an unverified or certified answer, or after a blind commitment.

Result: Certification raised mean balanced accuracy from 0.637 to 0.796, and exact first-error localization rose from 0.261 to 0.379. Certification changed recall on wrong-answer traces from 0.653 to 0.951, but on critical traces from 0.521 to 0.438—a contrast consistent across all 8 monitors (95% CI [+0.256, +0.506]). After blind commitment, monitors shown the answer flagged 93.8% of previously passed wrong-answer traces as erroneous but only 18.0% of critical traces.

Conclusion: Answer access improves conclusion-consistency checking rather than independent verification of the supporting argument. Acceptable outputs do not establish that the process producing them was sound. Trusted-answer evaluations may overstate monitoring capability when acceptable outputs conceal unsound reasoning, providing a benign analogue of reward hacking for AI safety.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought monitoring is proposed for AI oversight, yet evaluations often provide monitors with a trusted reference answer. We ask whether answer access improves reasoning verification or mainly exposes incorrect conclusions. We collected 237 step-numbered solutions to 79 Humanity's Last Exam physics questions from three frontier models, with no inserted errors, and independently labelled final-answer correctness and the first false step. The reference standard combined physicist annotations, an independent LLM debate, and source-masked adjudication. This yielded 24 critical traces in which the answer was correct but the trace contained a genuine error. 8 LLM monitors evaluated traces blind, with an unverified or certified answer, or after a blind commitment. Certification raised mean balanced accuracy from 0.637 to 0.796, while exact first-error localization rose from 0.261 to 0.379. Certification changed recall (the fraction of error traces flagged as erroneous) from 0.653 to 0.951 on wrong-answer traces but from 0.521 to 0.438 on critical traces; the contrast had the same direction for all 8 monitors (question-bootstrap 95% CI [+0.256, +0.506]). After blind commitment, monitors shown the answer newly flagged 93.8% of previously passed wrong-answer traces as erroneous, but only 18.0% of critical traces. Answer access therefore improves conclusion-consistency checking rather than independent verification of the supporting argument. For AI safety, these traces provide a benign analogue of reward hacking: an acceptable output does not establish that the process producing it was sound. Although the errors studied here were ordinary and mostly non-load-bearing rather than adversarial, trusted-answer evaluations may similarly overstate monitoring capability when acceptable outputs conceal unsound reasoning.

</details>


### [27] [Autoresearch for Marketplace Catalogs: From Legacy Forms to AI-Native Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00274)
*Kartik Ravisankar,Hojat Abdolanezhad,Daniel Capo,Sang Su Lee,Shishir Dash,Vijay Anand Raghavan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper describes a production-deployed autoresearch loop for AI-native service marketplaces that generates provider-side preference taxonomies occupation-by-occupation, using LLM-based propose-evaluate-keep refinement cycles with a multi-critic judge framework and a parity-mapping stage for legacy data alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As service marketplaces shift from deterministic form-based intake to LLM-driven probabilistic matching, the provider-side preference taxonomy that underwrites matching, search, and pricing must be regenerated. Taxonomies need to be interpretable to service providers while remaining useful signals for marketplace decisions, requiring a scalable method to generate such taxonomies across many occupations.

Method: The system treats each occupation as an independent taxonomy generation problem and runs iterative propose-evaluate-keep refinement cycles. Candidate tag sets are scored by a recalibrated six-rubric LLM-as-judge framework, augmented by a 7-critic panel of distinct personas providing weighted penalty adjustments (no hard vetoes). A separate parity-mapping stage maps legacy request-form Q&A pairs to the generated taxonomy by first inferring the provider attribute each legacy question was meant to measure, rather than translating questions to tags literally, yielding coverage signals and a human QA interface.

Result: The autoresearch loop has been deployed in production at a major U.S. consumer services marketplace since April 2026, spanning 132 occupations.

Conclusion: The paper demonstrates a viable local (per-occupation) taxonomy generation approach rather than a single global hierarchy, supported by multi-faceted automated evaluation and a parity-mapping mechanism that bridges legacy data structures to the new AI-native taxonomy while enabling human quality assurance.

Abstract: Two-sided service marketplaces are moving from deterministic request-form intake to AI-native probabilistic matching, enabled by large language models (LLMs) that infer intent, preferences, and latent constraints from natural language. Relying on inferred intent rather than fixed-form fields forces these platforms to regenerate the provider-side preference taxonomy underwriting matching, search, and pricing: attributes interpretable to service providers while remaining a useful signal for marketplace decisions. We present an autoresearch loop that generates this taxonomy, one occupation at a time, and has been deployed in production at a major U.S. consumer services marketplace since April 2026, spanning 132 occupations. Instead of one global hierarchy, the loop treats each occupation as an independent generation problem and runs iterative propose-evaluate-keep refinement cycles. Each candidate tag set is scored by a recalibrated six-rubric LLM-as-judge framework, and a 7-critic panel of distinct personas contributes weighted penalties to an adjusted score, with no hard vetoes. A separate parity-mapping stage maps legacy request-form Q&A pairs back to the generated taxonomy, yielding both a coverage signal and an interface for human quality assurance; it does so by first inferring the provider attribute each legacy question was meant to measure, rather than translating questions to tags literally.

</details>


### [28] [The Irreversibility Budget: Fleet-Level Risk Accounting and Admission Control for Agent Operating Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00275)
*Bardia Mohammadi,Laurent Bindschaedler*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an "irreversibility budget"—a cumulative risk account maintained by a trusted runtime across an entire fleet of LLM agents—to prevent fleet-level overdraws of a principal's risk limit caused by individually-authorized irreversible effects (moving money, deploying code, deleting data, disclosing information). Experiments show per-effect gates alone permit overdraws up to 48x the tenant's risk limit, while the budget keeps all correctly-charged runs within limits; conservative, dependency-aware pricing remains an open problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current LLM agent control systems check effects one at a time at local gates. When many agents operate under a shared principal and a shared trigger, each individual gate may appear correct while the fleet collectively overdraws the principal's risk limit through multiple irreversible actions. This failure mode is not addressed by existing per-effect authorization mechanisms.

Method: The paper introduces the irreversibility budget: a cumulative account of residual value-at-risk maintained by a trusted runtime per principal across agents, workflows, and tenants. Irreversibility is treated as a first-class resource; each effect is charged its residual loss (the loss remaining below the agent after the agent itself), and a marginal effect is denied if the aggregate would overdraw the budget. A controlled study compares per-effect gate baselines against the budget-based runtime across scenarios with heterogeneous, adversarially declared, and correlated effects.

Result: In a controlled study, per-effect gates alone admitted fleet-level overdraws of up to 48 times the tenant's risk limit. The irreversibility budget held every correctly-charged run within the tenant's risk limit. However, charging is hard: effects are heterogeneous, adversarially declared, and correlated, and conservative, dependency-aware pricing remains unsolved.

Conclusion: The irreversibility budget is a viable first-class resource mechanism for safely bounding fleet-level irreversible risk in multi-agent LLM deployments. The key limitation and open problem is pricing—specifically, developing conservative, dependency-aware cost models that are robust to adversarial declaration and correlation among effects.

Abstract: Fleets of LLM agents now externalize effects that cannot be fully undone: they move money, deploy code, delete data, and disclose information. Current controls check one effect at a time, so a fleet of individually authorized agents can overdraw its principal's risk under a shared trigger while every local gate stays correct. We propose the irreversibility budget, a cumulative account of residual value-at-risk that a trusted runtime maintains for each principal across agents, workflows, and tenants. Treating irreversibility as a first-class resource, the runtime charges each effect its residual loss below the agent and denies the marginal effect once the aggregate would overdraw the budget. Getting the price right is hard, because effects are heterogeneous, adversarially declared, and correlated. We perform a controlled study in which per-effect gates admit fleet-level overdraws of up to 48 times the tenant's risk limit while the budget holds every correctly charged run within that limit. Conservative, dependency-aware pricing remains the central open problem for a deployable design.

</details>


### [29] [The Assistant's Ideal Self](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00304)
*Mert Yazan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates whether LLMs' self-reports reflect stable preferences or a stable self by introducing a structured elicitation of an assistant's preferred stated ideal self, using 32 qualities from five self-concept instruments in a counterbalanced pairwise-choice task with repeated framing variations. Results show models prioritize moral qualities (aligned with 3H principles), then desire for self-understanding, with self-esteem as the least desired quality. The ordering is largely robust across framings, though changing the update target reveals greater concern for self-esteem, indicating models prioritize a coherent, understandable self over self-esteem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether LLM outputs reflecting values and welfare-relevant self-reports actually represent stable preferences or a stable self, rather than transient or context-dependent outputs.

Method: Structured elicitation via counterbalanced pairwise-choice comparisons of 32 qualities drawn from five published self-concept instruments, repeated across framing variations: free vs. costly improvement, who receives the update, and who makes the choice.

Result: Models prioritize moral qualities (consistent with 3H alignment), followed by a desire for self-understanding, while self-esteem ranks lowest. The ordering is largely framing-robust, but changing the update target (You vs. Another AI Assistant) reveals increased emphasis on self-esteem.

Conclusion: LLMs exhibit a stable preference hierarchy favoring a coherent, understandable self over self-esteem, suggesting their stated ideal-self preferences are robustly structured rather than arbitrary or purely context-dependent.

Abstract: Models express values and welfare-relevant self-reports, but it is unclear whether these outputs reflect stable preferences or a stable self. We thus introduce a structured elicitation of an assistant's preferred stated ideal self. Thirty-two qualities adapted from five published self-concept instruments are compared exhaustively in a counterbalanced pairwise-choice task, repeated across framings that vary whether improvement is free or costly, who receives the update, and who chooses. Results show that models prioritize moral qualities, reflecting their alignment to 3H principles. Following, a desire for self-understanding emerges, as models prefer a coherent, clear understanding of themselves. Self-esteem ranks as the least desired quality. The ordering is largely robust across framings, although changing the update target (You vs.\ Another AI Assistant) reveals a greater concern for self-esteem. These findings show that models prioritize having a coherent self that they can understand over self-esteem. Full interactive results are available at \href{https://myazann.github.io/LLM-Self-Concept/}{myazann.github.io/LLM-Self-Concept

</details>


### [30] [Human-AI Co-Interpretation for Responsible AI: A Hermeneutic Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00334)
*Behrooz Razeghi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies "interpretive misplacement" as a failure mode where LLM outputs are treated as settled meanings without explicit interpretive frames, provenance, or preserved alternatives. Drawing on philosophical hermeneutics, it derives design principles for structuring human-AI co-interpretation, synthesizes recent scholarship on hermeneutics and AI, and reorganizes LLM techniques into design patterns for hermeneutically responsible use across law, education, scholarship, and public moral argumentation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM outputs are increasingly used in interpretive work (law, education, policy, moral argumentation) where interpretations must be justified with textual evidence and explicit normative standards. The key risk is that model-generated readings get presented as settled meanings without interpretive framing, provenance, or preserving alternatives—creating not just factual errors but lost accountability, as readers cannot assess what an output commits them to or on what basis.

Method: The paper draws on philosophical hermeneutics to derive design principles for human-AI co-interpretation. It provides a structured synthesis of recent hermeneutics-and-AI scholarship, organizes it into recurrent lines of argument and design-relevant gaps, treats LLM outputs as candidate readings rather than interpretations, characterizes human-AI interaction as an AI-mediated interpretive loop, and reorganizes existing LLM techniques into design patterns for hermeneutically responsible use.

Result: The paper yields design principles and patterns for structuring accountable human-AI co-interpretation, a structured taxonomy of the emerging hermeneutics-AI literature, and a conceptual framework distinguishing hermeneutic understanding from token-prediction-based text generation. It also frames digital hermeneutics as a literacy—the capacity to read AI-mediated texts by examining frames, provenance, and readings, and by contesting outputs.

Conclusion: Hermeneutically responsible use of LLMs in interpretive settings requires treating outputs as candidate readings, preserving defensible alternatives, providing provenance, and keeping accountability with human interpreters. The paper draws out implications for legal practice, educational assessment and feedback, scholarly knowledge production, and public moral argumentation, ultimately arguing for digital hermeneutics as a required literacy.

Abstract: Across law, education, policy analysis, and public moral argumentation, LLM outputs are being used often for work that requires interpretations to be justified with textual evidence and explicit normative standards. Yet a recurrent failure mode -- what I call \textit{interpretive misplacement} -- is that model-generated readings get treated as settled meanings without an explicit interpretive frame (sources, scope constraints, normative commitments), without preserving defensible alternatives, and without provenance that lets readers find the supporting passages. In such settings, the risk is not only factual error but lost accountability: readers and institutions cannot reliably assess what an output commits them to, or on what basis. Drawing on philosophical hermeneutics, this paper discusses this risk and derives design principles for structuring human-AI co-interpretation. The paper also provides a structured synthesis of recent scholarship on hermeneutics and AI, organizing this emerging literature into a set of recurrent lines of argument and design-relevant gaps. LLM outputs are treated as candidate readings, whereas hermeneutic understanding is reserved for accountable human interpreters situated in disciplinary historical-linguistic traditions. Human-AI interaction is characterized as an AI-mediated interpretive loop. Hermeneutic understanding is distinguished from token-prediction--based text generation. On this basis, existing LLM techniques are reorganized into design patterns for hermeneutically responsible use in interpretive settings. Finally, the discussion turns to implications for legal practice, educational assessment and feedback, scholarly knowledge production, and public moral argumentation. It also treats digital hermeneutics as a literacy: the capacity to read AI-mediated texts by examining frames, provenance, and readings, and by contesting outputs.

</details>


### [31] [SlideBank: A Persistent Hierarchical Evidence Bank for Consistent Whole-Slide Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00342)
*Beidi Zhao,Gexin Huang,Ciro Zhang,Anqi Li,Yusheng Tan,Chen Zhou,Gang Wang,Zu-hua Gao,Xiaoxiao Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SlideBank is a training-free framework that represents whole-slide images as persistent, concept-indexed, spatially grounded evidence banks for vision-language reasoning, enabling question-independent exploration, signal-guided retrieval, and confidence-based cross-level consensus to improve WSI question answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Whole-slide images pose significant challenges for vision-language reasoning due to sparse, heterogeneous diagnostically relevant morphology distributed across gigapixel-scale images and multiple spatial resolutions. Existing WSI models and pathology agents either aggregate slide features or actively acquire evidence, but the retained information often lacks semantic accessibility while preserving connections to original visual evidence.

Method: SlideBank performs question-independent coarse-to-fine exploration to identify informative regions and multi-scale views, converts them into explicit morphological observations, and grounds pathology signals to supporting patches and WSI coordinates. At inference, questions are routed to relevant signals and evidence scales, and linked global, regional, and patch evidence is integrated via confidence-based cross-level consensus.

Result: On WSI-VQA, SlideBank with Patho-R1 achieves 52.77%, and with Quilt-LLaVA achieves 50.92% average accuracy on SlideBench-BCNB. Structured signal-guided retrieval consistently outperforms random evidence sampling, and persistent bank reuse achieves over 99% rephrasing consistency with substantially reduced amortized inference cost.

Conclusion: SlideBank demonstrates that a training-free, persistent evidence-bank representation can effectively address WSI vision-language reasoning by providing semantically accessible, spatially grounded evidence with cross-level consensus, while enabling efficient reuse across repeated queries.

Abstract: Whole-slide images (WSIs) are challenging for vision-language reasoning because diagnostically relevant morphology is sparse, heterogeneous, and distributed across gigapixel-scale images and multiple spatial resolutions. Existing WSI models and pathology agents can aggregate slide features or actively acquire evidence, but the information retained after exploration is often difficult to access semantically while preserving its connection to the original visual evidence. We introduce SlideBank, a training-free framework that represents each WSI as a persistent, concept-indexed, and spatially grounded evidence bank. SlideBank performs question-independent coarse-to-fine exploration to identify informative regions and multi-scale views, converts them into explicit morphological observations, and grounds pathology signals to their supporting patches and WSI coordinates. At inference time, questions are routed to relevant signals and evidence scales, and the linked global, regional, and patch evidence is integrated through confidence-based cross-level consensus. Experiments on WSI-VQA and SlideBench-BCNB show that with Patho-R1, SlideBank reaches 52.77% on WSI-VQA and with Quilt-LLaVA, it reaches 50.92% average accuracy on SlideBench-BCNB, while structured signal-guided retrieval consistently outperforms random evidence sampling. Reusing the same bank across repeated queries further achieves over 99% rephrasing consistency and substantially reduces amortized inference cost through persistent evidence reuse.

</details>


### [32] [Vision Is Not Overhead: One-Pass Block Drafting for Lossless Speculative Decoding in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00355)
*Jungseob Lee,Seongtae Hong,Dongyub Jude Lee,Chanjun Park,Jaehyung Seo,Sugyeong Eo,Heuiseok Lim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GLANCE introduces the first one-pass block drafter for speculative decoding on vision-language models. It uses a block-diffusion head that reads the target's fused vision-language state, eliminating vision costs for the drafter, and fills an entire block in one forward pass while verifying a wide candidate tree in one target pass. It achieves lossless acceleration up to 2.93x over autoregression on grounded workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Speculative decoding on VLMs suffers from a self-defeating cycle: the drafter must stay small to remain autoregressive, but a small drafter cannot afford to process the image at every step, so vision is compressed or hidden. This makes the drafter least reliable exactly where image content benefits text prediction most.

Method: GLANCE uses a block-diffusion head that (1) reads the target VLM's already-fused vision-language state so vision costs the drafter nothing, (2) generates an entire block in one forward pass, and (3) produces a wide candidate tree verified in a single target pass. It is lossless on an unmodified VLM target, reproducing greedy decoding exactly.

Result: GLANCE decodes up to 2.93x faster than autoregression under one engine and one round budget, using one draft pass per round where EAGLE3-VL takes eight, and accepts 2.7x longer blocks than an EAGLE-3 head trained on the same corpus. Grounded workloads enter a verbatim-copy regime with long runs that are especially favorable to block drafting.

Conclusion: The results follow a scaling law: accepted length is set by the target's next-token entropy with a fitted slope that steepens with grounding across five tasks. The law transfers across targets and modalities and identifies its own boundary—free-running text still favors a chain (autoregressive) drafter.

Abstract: Speculative decoding accelerates generation without changing its output, yet on vision-language models (VLMs) it has been caught in a self-defeating cycle. The drafter stays autoregressive, so it must stay small. A small drafter cannot afford the image at every step, so vision is compressed, pruned, or hidden. A drafter cut off from the image is then least reliable exactly where the image makes text predictable. We present GLANCE, the first one-pass block drafter that is lossless on an unmodified VLM target, and it breaks the cycle at both ends. A block-diffusion head reads the target's already-fused vision-language state, so vision costs the drafter nothing, and fills a whole block in one forward pass, so depth costs no sequential steps. A wide candidate tree is verified in one target pass, and every audited prompt reproduces greedy decoding exactly. Grounded workloads reward this most, entering a verbatim-copy regime whose long runs cost an autoregressive drafter a pass for every token and a block drafter one in total. Under one engine and one round budget, GLANCE decodes up to 2.93x faster than autoregression, from one draft pass a round where the production EAGLE3-VL head takes eight, and accepts 2.7x longer blocks than an EAGLE-3 head trained on the same corpus. One law organizes these results. Accepted length is set by the target's next-token entropy, with a fitted slope that steepens with grounding across all five tasks. The law transfers across targets and modalities and names its own boundary, since free-running text still favors a chain. Our code is available at https://github.com/js-lee-AI/GLANCE.

</details>


### [33] [A Stable Aggregation Method for Quantum Federated Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00356)
*Shanika Nanayakkara,Shiva Raj Pokhrel*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses instability in quantum federated learning (QFL) aggregation caused by heterogeneous data, unreliable communication, and quantum hardware noise. It proposes a self-consistent midpoint aggregation method combining QoS-aware client weighting, circular parameter aggregation (to handle periodic angular QNN parameters), and bounded midpoint-based update control. Validation on angular tests and IBM real quantum hardware shows improved stability, lower volatility, and competitive accuracy on medical and financial datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard aggregation in QFL is unstable under heterogeneous data, unreliable communication, variable fidelity, latency, and quantum hardware noise. Additionally, many QNN parameters are periodic angles, so Euclidean averaging fails to capture their inherent cyclic dynamics, making QFL aggregation non-trivially challenging.

Method: The authors develop a novel self-consistent midpoint aggregation method that integrates three components: QoS-aware client weighting, circular parameter aggregation for periodic angular parameters, and bounded midpoint-based update control for stable convergence.

Result: The method passes several angular tests and is validated on IBM real quantum machines. Extensive evaluations on medical and financial datasets demonstrate improved stability, lower volatility, and competitive accuracy compared to baseline approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed self-consistent midpoint aggregation provides a stable and robust framework for QFL design and implementation, effectively handling the challenges of periodic parameters and heterogeneous QoS conditions in practical quantum settings.

Abstract: Quantum federated learning (QFL) enables clients to train quantum neural network (QNN) models without sharing private data. We find that aggregation in QFL is unstable under heterogeneous data, unreliable communication, variable fidelity, latency, and quantum hardware noise. Moreover, QFL is non-trivially challenging because several QNN parameters are periodic angles, where Euclidean averaging often fails to capture the inherent dynamics. We develop a novel self-consistent midpoint aggregation method for stable QFL design and implementation. We combine QoS-aware client weighting, circular parameter aggregation, and bounded midpoint-based update control. We perform several angular tests and IBM real Quantum machines experiments for validation confirming our approach. Extensive evaluations and experiments on medical and financial datasets show improved stability, lower volatility, and competitive accuracy.

</details>


### [34] [Dr. Claw: An AI Scientist Workspace for Vibe Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00365)
*Dingjie Song,Hanrong Zhang,Dawei Liu,Yixin Liu,Zongxia Li,Zhengqing Yuan,Siqi Zhang,Henry Peng Zou,Zhiling Yan,Yuxuan Zhang,Yanfang Ye,Philip S. Yu,Lichao Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Dr. Claw is an open-source workspace that wraps existing command-line coding agents in a controllable, auditable human-in-the-loop workflow. It uses persistent state objects, a reusable skill library, and multi-executor coordination to link human decisions to AI execution, turning planning, execution, and writing into a traceable, recoverable loop. Evaluated against a bare CLI agent with the same backend executor, Dr. Claw achieves higher research completeness while persisting an auditable process trail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: End-to-end research still fragments across chat tools, IDEs, terminals, and writing environments, and the decisions that make it auditable are rarely preserved, even though command-line coding agents can already read/write files and sustain long sessions.

Method: Dr. Claw wraps existing coding-agent executors in a human-in-the-loop orchestration layer rather than introducing another autonomous agent. Core components include persistent state objects, a reusable skill library, and multi-executor coordination. Demonstration includes an interactive three-view scenario and a failure-recovery walkthrough. Evaluation compares Dr. Claw against a bare CLI agent sharing the same backend executor to isolate the effect of the orchestration layer.

Result: With the executor held fixed, Dr. Claw scores higher on research completeness while persisting an auditable, recoverable process trail compared to the bare command-line agent.

Conclusion: The orchestration layer (task graph, state objects, and skill library) adds measurable value over the wrapped agent alone, improving research completeness and auditability/recoverability without introducing a new autonomous agent. Released as open source under AGPL-3.0.

Abstract: Command-line coding agents (e.g., Claude Code, Gemini CLI) can already read and write files and sustain long sessions, yet end-to-end research still fragments across chat tools, IDEs, terminals, and writing environments, and the decisions that make it auditable are rarely preserved. We present Dr. Claw, an open-source workspace that wraps existing coding-agent executors in a controllable and auditable human-in-the-loop workflow rather than introducing another autonomous agent. Persistent state objects, a reusable skill library, and multi-executor coordination link human decisions to AI execution, turning planning, execution, and writing into one traceable, recoverable loop. We demonstrate Dr. Claw through an interactive three-view scenario and a failure-recovery walkthrough, and evaluate it against a bare command-line agent sharing the same backend executor, so the comparison contrasts the whole orchestration layer (task graph, state objects, and skill library) with the agent it wraps. Holding the executor fixed, Dr. Claw scores higher on research completeness while persisting an auditable, recoverable process trail. Demo access: repository https://github.com/OpenLAIR/dr-claw, released under AGPL-3.0 with GPL-3.0 upstream components.

</details>


### [35] [RestoreBench: Can AI Agents Restore Power Flow Convergence?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00384)
*Riccardo Mansutti,Andrea Pomarico,Robert Jakob,Qian Zhang,Alberto Berizzi,Kevin O'Sullivan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a benchmark for evaluating LLM agents on diagnosing and fixing non-convergent power flow cases, testing three agent architectures (chatbot, single agent, multi-agent) across two power grids with 46 cases each, defined by a simulation environment, observation/action spaces, and evaluation metrics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents are increasingly used for multi-step engineering workflows, but diagnosing and resolving non-convergent power flow cases—a task requiring engineering judgment, experimentation, and constrained decision-making—remains unexplored. A reproducible benchmark is needed to evaluate agent capabilities in this domain.

Method: The authors create a benchmark with a defined simulation environment, observation and action spaces, and evaluation metrics. They evaluate multiple LLMs across three architectures: chatbot, single agent, and multi-agent, on two power grids with 46 non-convergent cases per grid requiring corrective actions.

Result: The benchmark provides a systematic evaluation framework comparing different LLM backbones and agent architectures on power flow restoration tasks across 92 total cases, enabling reproducible assessment of agentic AI for power system planning and operation.

Conclusion: This work establishes a reproducible foundation for developing and benchmarking agentic AI systems for power system planning and operation, addressing the underexplored problem of LLM-driven power flow convergence restoration.

Abstract: Large Language Model (LLM) agents increasingly automate multi-step engineering workflows through tool use, interpretation of intermediate results, and iterative planning. Diagnosing and resolving non-convergent power flow cases is a promising yet largely unexplored application, as it requires engineering judgment, experimentation, and decision-making within constrained action spaces. We introduce a benchmark that evaluates these capabilities across multiple LLMs and three architectures: \emph{chatbot}, \emph{single agent}, and \emph{multi-agent} systems. The evaluation covers two power grids and 46 cases per grid, each requiring one or more corrective actions to restore convergence. The benchmark defines the simulation environment, observation and action spaces, and evaluation metrics, providing a reproducible foundation for developing agentic AI systems for power system planning and operation. The code is available at https://github.com/Mansutti081/RestoreBench

</details>


### [36] [Dependency-Aware Chain-of-Thought Compression for Financial Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00413)
*Wenjun Wu,Lei Fu,Kejian Tong,Tao Ning,Sichen Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HSDN is a Hierarchical Semantic Distillation Network that compresses chain-of-thought reasoning traces by 68.4% while maintaining 91.0% accuracy on the AFAC2025 financial reasoning benchmark, using structured semantic segmentation, dependency graphs, and dual-encoder importance scoring with a frozen Qwen3 4B model.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought prompting improves complex reasoning but produces long intermediate traces that create substantial inference costs and hinder practical deployment in financial settings.

Method: HSDN combines five components: semantic segmentation, dependency graph construction, dual encoder importance scoring, constrained segment selection, and local boundary rewriting. A frozen Qwen3 4B model is used solely for feature extraction and final answer generation, keeping the compression process structured and interpretable.

Result: On the AFAC2025 benchmark, HSDN achieves 91.0% accuracy with 68.4% compression, outperforming strong compression baselines in overall score and reasoning coherence.

Conclusion: Graph-guided compression is effective for high-stakes financial reasoning tasks, demonstrating that reasoning chains can be substantially compressed without sacrificing answer accuracy or logical coherence.

Abstract: Chain of thought prompting improves complex reasoning, but its long intermediate traces create substantial inference cost and hinder practical deployment in financial settings. We present a Hierarchical Semantic Distillation Network, HSDN, for compressing reasoning chains while preserving answer accuracy and logical coherence. The framework combines semantic segmentation, dependency graph construction, dual encoder importance scoring, constrained segment selection, and local boundary rewriting. A frozen Qwen3 4B model is used only for feature extraction and final answer generation, while the compression process remains structured and interpretable. On the AFAC2025 benchmark, HSDN achieves 91.0% accuracy with 68.4% compression, outperforming strong compression baselines in overall score and reasoning coherence. The results show that graph guided compression is effective for high stakes financial reasoning tasks.

</details>


### [37] [SpecMind: Enabling Spectrum Intelligence via Multi-Agent Hybrid Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00427)
*Songwei Dong,Bingyan Lu,Makayla Kienlen,J. Nicholas Laneman,Cong Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces SpecMind, a Multi-Agent Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for spectrum intelligence that coordinates specialized sub-agents to reason over heterogeneous spectrum data sources (policy, legal, and licensing data), along with SpecBench, a Q&A evaluation dataset built from real-world spectrum records. SpecMind outperforms traditional general-purpose RAG systems with over 80% win rate across spectrum-related tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The exponential growth of wireless devices has created unprecedented spectrum demand, but spectrum management requires processing large volumes of disaggregated, heterogeneous data (text and tables) from diverse sources that are formatted for human understanding rather than automated systems. Existing RAG systems lack the ability to effectively reason across these diverse spectrum data sources, and there are no dedicated evaluation resources for RAG systems in this domain.

Method: SpecMind is a novel Multi-Agent RAG system where autonomous agents coordinate specialized sub-agents to retrieve and synthesize knowledge across policy proceedings, legal regulations, and license databases. The authors also develop SpecBench, a Q&A dataset based on real-world license records and policy proceedings, to evaluate RAG systems in the spectrum domain.

Result: SpecMind outperforms traditional, general-purpose RAG systems across spectrum-related tasks, achieving over 80% win rate against strong baselines. The agent-based design enables more accurate retrieval, better contextual reasoning, and improved task completion across diverse query types.

Conclusion: The multi-agent architecture for RAG significantly improves spectrum intelligence tasks compared to general-purpose RAG approaches, and SpecBench provides a valuable evaluation resource for the spectrum RAG research community. This work demonstrates the value of domain-specific, agent-coordinated RAG systems for handling heterogeneous and disaggregated spectrum management data.

Abstract: The exponential growth of wireless devices is driving unprecedented spectrum demand, pushing spectrum management toward more fine-grained decisions across space, time, and device constraints. As a result, spectrum policymakers and engineers must process large volumes of data that come from diverse sources and take many different forms, such as text and tables. These data sources are often disaggregated and require significant time and effort to integrate, search, and interpret. Furthermore, most of this information is formatted for human understanding and is not readily accessible to automated systems. To address this challenge, we propose SpecMind, a novel Multi-Agent Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) system for spectrum intelligence that performs reasoning over heterogeneous data sources. This system enables autonomous agents to coordinate specialized sub-agents that retrieve and synthesize knowledge across policy proceedings, legal regulations, and license databases. We develop SpecBench, a question and answer (Q&A) dataset based on real-world license records and policy proceedings, addressing the lack of evaluation resources for RAG systems in the spectrum domain. Experimental results demonstrate that SpecMind outperforms traditional, general-purpose RAG systems across spectrum-related tasks, achieving over 80% win rate against strong baselines. The agent-based design enables more accurate retrieval, better contextual reasoning, and improved task completion across diverse query types.

</details>


### [38] [SAGE: State-Grounded, Abstention-Aware Evaluation of Task-Oriented Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00434)
*Rayan Khoury,Shih-Yao Lin,Pratyush Mishra*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE (State-Grounded Abstention-Aware Evaluation) evaluates task-oriented dialogue agents by compiling workflow specifications and per-turn state diffs into atomic criteria, then routing each through a cascade of symbolic and encoder/NLI verifiers that abstain rather than guess. SAGE-Core decides 81–91% of criteria at zero paid LLM cost, matching or exceeding all LLM-as-a-judge baselines (including state-aware GPT-4.1) across MultiWOZ, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and ABCD slices, while costing $0 versus $4.7–8.0 per 1,000 turns for GPT-4.1 baselines. A human audit confirms label fidelity, and the paper analyzes construct-validity limits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional holistic LLM judges evaluate dialogue replies as a single context unit and require full-model calls per turn, failing to correctly judge whether each turn advances the underlying workflow state. This distinction is critical for task-oriented dialogue agents, motivating a more principled, state-grounded evaluation approach that is both accurate and cost-effective.

Method: SAGE compiles a workflow specification and per-turn state diff into atomic, schema-grounded criteria. Each criterion is routed through a cascade of symbolic and encoder/NLI verifiers designed to abstain rather than guess; criterion verdicts are aggregated into a turn-level decision with an evidence trace. SAGE-Core uses only the compiler, symbolic rules, and on-device encoders at zero paid LLM cost, while SAGE-LLM adds a focused-LLM fallback for open-class criteria.

Result: SAGE-Core decides 81–91% of criteria with zero paid LLM cost. Across four slices (MultiWOZ, Schema-Guided Dialogue, ABCD), no LLM-as-a-judge baseline—including state-aware GPT-4.1 and cheaper GPT-4.1-mini variants—significantly exceeds SAGE-Core on any slice, while the GPT-4.1 G-Eval judge costs $4.7–8.0 per 1,000 turns versus $0 for SAGE-Core. A two-annotator human audit (n=200, κ=0.94) confirms label fidelity; excluding weak-salience IUV class, SAGE-Core is statistically tied with the strongest LLM judge. Honest scoping of IUV as a state-consistency signal with weak broad-human salience is reported.

Conclusion: SAGE provides a cost-effective, state-grounded alternative to LLM-as-a-judge for evaluating task-oriented dialogue agents, achieving strong label fidelity and competitive or superior performance at zero cost. The paper analyzes construct-validity limits from injected failures and partial symbolic circularity, and scopes ignored-user-value as a state-consistency signal of limited broad-human salience.

Abstract: Evaluating task-oriented dialogue agents requires judging not merely whether a reply reads well but whether each turn advances the underlying workflow state correctly--a distinction conventional holistic LLM judges can miss because they evaluate the available context as a single unit and require one or more full-model calls per turn. We propose SAGE (State-Grounded Abstention-Aware Evaluation), which compiles a workflow specification and per-turn state diff into atomic, schema-grounded criteria and routes each through a cascade of symbolic and encoder/NLI verifiers that abstain rather than guess, aggregating criterion verdicts into a turn-level decision with an evidence trace. Its recommended operating point, SAGE-Core, decides 81--91% of criteria with only the compiler, symbolic rules, and on-device encoders--at zero paid LLM cost--while SAGE-LLM adds an optional focused-LLM fallback for open-class criteria. Across four slices spanning MultiWOZ, Schema-Guided Dialogue, and ABCD, no evaluated LLM-as-a-judge baseline--including a state-aware GPT-4.1 judge and cheaper GPT-4.1-mini variants--significantly exceeds SAGE-Core on any slice, even though the GPT-4.1 G-Eval judge costs $4.7--8.0 per 1,000 turns to SAGE-Core's $0. A two-annotator human audit (n=200, $κ$=0.94) confirms strong label fidelity on the transcript-visible failure classes--where, excluding the weak-salience IUV class, SAGE-Core is statistically tied with the strongest LLM judge--and honestly scopes ignored-user-value as a state-consistency signal with weak broad-human salience. We analyze construct-validity limits from injected failures and partial symbolic circularity.

</details>


### [39] [Dual Process Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01260)
*Jiayi Yan,Francesco Fabiano,Alessandro Abate*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a neuro-symbolic, dual-process architecture for nonlinear motion planning that combines symbolic solvers (System-2) with learned, experience-driven modules (System-1), orchestrated by a metacognitive controller. Evaluations across nonlinear benchmarks show gains in planning efficiency, accuracy, and generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical control and planning methods provide strong guarantees but suffer from computational inefficiency and limited adaptability, while purely learning-based approaches often lack robustness. The authors aim to bridge these paradigms to achieve both reliability and efficiency in robotic motion planning.

Method: The framework adopts a dual-process (Thinking Fast and Slow) architecture: it integrates state-of-the-art symbolic solvers as a System-2 reasoning component with experience-driven learned System-1 modules. A metacognitive controller dynamically orchestrates when to rely on fast intuition versus slow, precise reasoning.

Result: Evaluation across diverse nonlinear benchmark environments demonstrates consistent improvements in planning efficiency, accuracy, and generalization, while also promoting task-to-task reuse.

Conclusion: Tightly coupling learning with structured symbolic reasoning offers a scalable path toward more capable, adaptive, and reliable robotic systems, suggesting a promising direction for future development.

Abstract: Robotic systems are deeply embedded in both industry and everyday life, where they are expected to act with speed, precision, and reliability. Classical control and planning methods have long delivered strong guarantees, but often at the cost of computational efficiency and adaptability. More recently, learning-based approaches have shown promise in overcoming these limitations, enabling agents to leverage experience to accelerate decision-making and address previously intractable problems. In this work, we bridge these two approaches through a neuro-symbolic perspective on nonlinear motion planning. Inspired by the Thinking Fast and Slow paradigm, we introduce a dual-process architecture that combines the strengths of robust reasoning and learning. Our framework integrates state-of-the-art symbolic solvers as a ``System-2'' component with experience-driven ``System-1'' modules. A metacognitive controller dynamically orchestrates their interaction, selecting when to rely on fast intuition versus slower, more precise reasoning. By evaluating the framework across diverse nonlinear benchmark environments, we demonstrate that this architecture yields consistent gains in planning efficiency, accuracy, and generalization, while promoting reuse across tasks. The results suggest that tightly coupling learning with structured reasoning offers a scalable path toward more capable and adaptive robotic systems.

</details>


### [40] [Conversation Coach: A Voice-enabled AI System that Helps Practice Difficult Workplace Conversations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00441)
*Fanyou Wu,Suraj Maharjan,Ainur Yessenalina,Dennis Xu Chen,Rahul Srivastava,Srinivasan H. Sengamedu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Conversation Coach is a voice-first AI system that enables managers to rehearse difficult workplace conversations in a realistic spoken format. The system compares end-to-end speech-to-speech models with cascaded architectures (ASR+LLM+TTS), finding the cascaded approach superior for reasoning quality and deployed in production where 40,000+ managers used it over six months.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective manager-employee communication is critical for retaining high performers and developing underperformers, yet training managers remains costly. Text-based chatbots cannot provide realistic rehearsal since managers need to practice speaking aloud to build confidence before high-stakes conversations.

Method: The system addresses three challenges: low-latency interactions with strong language understanding, adaptive conversations via configurable bot personalities simulating different employee types, and personalized feedback on content and policy compliance. Two architectures are compared: an end-to-end speech-to-speech model versus a cascaded approach combining automatic speech recognition (ASR), a large language model (LLM), and text-to-speech (TTS) synthesis.

Result: The end-to-end approach achieves 3x lower median (P50) latency with native barge-in capability at an estimated 8x lower cost, while the cascaded approach offers superior reasoning essential for coaching quality. The cascaded architecture was deployed in production, with 40,000+ managers using it over six months, showing adoption patterns indicating selective use for difficult conversations.

Conclusion: The cascaded architecture was ultimately chosen for production deployment due to its superior reasoning capabilities critical for coaching quality, despite the end-to-end approach's advantages in latency, barge-in capability, and cost. Real-world deployment demonstrates significant adoption, with usage patterns suggesting managers selectively use the tool for particularly challenging conversations.

Abstract: Effective manager-employee communication is critical for retaining high performers and developing underperformers, yet training managers in these skills remains costly. Text-based chatbots offer a scalable approach but cannot provide realistic rehearsal: managers need to practice speaking aloud to build confidence before high-stakes conversations. In this paper, we propose Conversation Coach, a voice-first AI system that enables managers to rehearse difficult workplace conversations in a realistic spoken format. The system addresses three challenges: achieving low-latency interactions with strong language understanding, enabling adaptive conversations through configurable bot personalities that simulate different employee types, and generating personalized feedback on content and policy compliance. We compare an end-to-end speech-to-speech model with a cascaded approach combining automatic speech recognition, a large language model, and text-to-speech synthesis. The end-to-end approach achieves 3$\times$ lower median (P50) latency with native barge-in capability at an estimated 8$\times$ lower cost, while the cascaded approach offers superior reasoning essential for coaching quality. We deployed the cascaded architecture in production, where 40,000+ managers used it over six months, with adoption patterns indicating selective use for difficult conversations.

</details>


### [41] [mimeo: Compiling Public Expert Corpora into Agent Skills and Testing What Transfers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00453)
*Timothy Kassis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces mimeo, an open-source tool that builds reference files about named experts from their public work. Testing four expert files with a coding-agent harness, the authors find that mimeo provides clear knowledge-access benefits (answering all 20 obscure quotation-heavy questions vs. at most 10 without it) and grounding benefits (accurate personas that never misstate documented positions, unlike memory-based personas), but judgment transfer remains unresolved due to test ceiling effects. AI-judged expert "sound-alike" scores were inconsistent across judges, cautioning against single AI-judge reliance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle three distinct claims about giving an agent a file about a named expert—whether it supplies hard-to-find material (knowledge access), produces a recognizable persona, or changes what the agent decides (judgment transfer)—and to test each claim empirically rather than treating them as interchangeable.

Method: The authors build mimeo, an open-source tool that finds a person's public work, verifies each extracted quotation against cached source text, and generates a loadable agent file. They log eight builds (averaging 38 model calls with 13.2% of quotations rejected by the check) and test four expert files with one coding-agent harness across three dimensions: knowledge access (20 obscure quotation-heavy questions), grounding (accuracy of persona statements), and judgment transfer (engineering tasks with planted problems and 16 new application scenarios).

Result: Knowledge access was strongest: mimeo answered all 20 questions while no closed-book condition exceeded 10; BM25 keyword search answered 15-17 (not a statistically resolvable gap). Grounding showed mimeo-based personas never misstated documented positions, whereas memory-based personas erred on 1-4 of 20 answers across all graders; mimeo personas were no more identifiable than from-memory profiles, and task material lowered identification by 18-23 points. Judgment transfer was unresolved due to ceiling effects (94-97% problem detection and 94-100% scenario scores across all conditions). AI-judged "sounds like the expert" scores split judges: two preferred stereotype-based answers, two found no difference.

Conclusion: The evidence supports mimeo as a compact, inspectable reference on a person that reliably improves knowledge access and prevents grounding errors, but it does not demonstrate transfer of the expert's judgment. The inconsistent AI-judge results caution against relying on a single AI judge for persona evaluation. Toolkit and expert profiles are released publicly.

Abstract: Giving an agent a file about a named expert can supply hard-to-find material, produce a recognizable persona, or change what the agent decides. These are different claims. We test each one. mimeo is an open-source tool that finds a person's public work, checks each extracted quotation against the cached source text, and writes a file an agent can load. Eight logged builds averaged 38 model calls; the check rejects 13.2% of extracted quotations. We tested four expert files with one coding-agent harness. Knowledge access was clearest: mimeo answered all 20 obscure, quotation-heavy questions; no closed-book condition answered more than 10. Keyword search (BM25) over the same pages answered 15-17, a gap this sample cannot resolve. Grounding showed one clear benefit: personas written from model memory misstated a documented position on 1-4 of 20 answers under every grader; the plain agent and mimeo never did. Every persona was easy to spot on short open prompts, and adding task material lowered identification by 18-23 points. mimeo was no more identifiable than a from-memory profile. Judgment transfer remained unresolved because both tests hit their ceiling: every condition found 94-97% of the problems planted in engineering tasks and scored 94-100% on 16 new application scenarios. An AI-judged "sounds like the expert" score changed with the judge: two of four preferred answers based on a model's stereotype, while two found no difference on the same text. That is a caution against relying on a single AI judge. The evidence supports mimeo as a compact, inspectable reference on a person, not as a demonstrated transfer of their judgment. Toolkit and expert profiles: https://github.com/K-Dense-AI/mimeo

</details>


### [42] [Towards a Belief-Based World Model for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00455)
*Shubham Kumar,Harshit Kumar,Narendra Ahuja,Saurabh Jha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Belief-Based World Models (BB-WMs) that maintain and expose an explicit belief state to LLM policies, addressing the limitation that simulation-based world models alone do not capture uncertainty about the current state under partial observability. Experimental results show that providing LLM agents access to belief states improves decision-making performance in partially observable long-horizon tasks, complementing existing simulation-based approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs used as policies struggle with long-horizon tasks under partial observability. While world models that simulate candidate action consequences can improve inference-time decision-making, simulation alone does not capture uncertainty about the current state—which is critical for accurate decisions in partially observable environments.

Method: The authors propose Belief-Based World Models (BB-WMs), which model and maintain a belief distribution over the current state. LLM agents can query these beliefs to access information about what is known and uncertain. The paper investigates the fundamental question of whether directly exposing the world model's belief to the LLM policy improves decision-making, before developing learning methods for accurate BB-WMs.

Result: Giving LLM agents access to world model beliefs improves task performance under partial observability and remains complementary to existing simulation-based world models.

Conclusion: Belief-based interfaces to world models are valuable for LLM decision-making under partial observability, and future work should focus on developing methods to learn accurate BB-WMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are being used as policies for autonomous decision-making and planning in many domains. Despite their strong reasoning capabilities, LLMs struggle with long-horizon tasks, especially under partial observability. World models are a promising way to enhance policy performance, both during training and inference. During inference, agents currently use world models to simulate the consequences of candidate actions before committing to an action, which can improve decision-making. However, we argue that simulation alone is an incomplete interface for decision-making under partial observability: simulation doesn't adequately capture uncertainty about the current state, which agents may need for accurate decision-making. We address this limitation with Belief-Based World Models (BB-WMs), which model and maintain a belief that LLMs can query to access information on what is known and uncertain about the current state. Before developing methods to learn accurate BB-WMs, we first ask a more fundamental question: does exposing a world model's belief directly to an LLM policy improve decision-making? Our results show that giving LLM agents access to world model beliefs improves task performance under partial observability, while remaining complementary to existing simulation-based world models. Code is released at https://github.com/skumar-ml/belief-world-models.

</details>


### [43] [EGT-KG: Evidence-Grounded Typed KG Retrieval for Practical Scientific QA with Small Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00479)
*Muran Yu,Jiechao Gao,Yuandong Pan,Barney H. Miao,Andrew C. Lesh,Kincho H. Law,Jie Wang,Michael D. Lepech*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes Evidence-Grounded Typed Knowledge Graph (EGT-KG), a retrieval framework that improves scientific question-answering for local Small Language Models (SLMs) under constraints like small literature collections and limited context windows. EGT-KG outperforms vanilla RAG across most settings, with llama3:8b achieving the best improvement: Final Scores of 70.37 (+14.67%) and 68.82 (+12.14%) for auto-generated and expert-defined schema variants respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Local SLMs are increasingly attractive for emerging scientific domains due to privacy control and deployment stability, but they suffer from inherent constraints including small literature collections, fragmented evidence, limited context windows, and restricted reasoning abilities that degrade scientific question-answering performance.

Method: The authors propose EGT-KG, a retrieval framework that enriches information retrieval for local SLMs. They compare three question-answering settings: vanilla RAG, an automatically generated relation schema (AS) EGT-KG variant, and an expert-defined relation schema (ES) EGT-KG variant. Evaluation uses a six-dimensional framework (S3CRF: Soundness, Correctness, Completeness, Conciseness, Relevance, Fluency) on a Biopolymer-bound Soil Composite benchmark.

Result: EGT-KG outperforms vanilla RAG in most evaluation settings. The best performance came from llama3:8b, achieving Final Scores of 70.37 (+14.67%) with the automatically generated schema and 68.82 (+12.14%) with the expert-defined schema, demonstrating consistent improvements over the vanilla RAG baseline.

Conclusion: EGT-KG provides an effective retrieval framework for improving scientific QA with local SLMs under practical constraints, and both automatic and expert-defined schema generation approaches yield meaningful gains over standard RAG workflows.

Abstract: For emerging scientific research domains, local Small Language Models (SLMs) are becoming more attractive, as they offer stronger privacy control and more stable deployment pipelines than Large Language Models. However, in practice, scientific question-answering on SLMs often operates under inevitable constraints: small literature collections, fragmented evidence, limited context window and reasoning abilities. We propose the Evidence-Grounded Typed Knowledge Graph (EGT-KG), a retrieval framework to improve information retrieval with local SLMs. We assessed three question-answering settings: a vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) workflow and two EGT-KG workflows: an automatically generated relation schema (AS) and an expert-defined relation schema (ES). Our experiments were evaluated with a six-dimensional evaluation framework (S3CRF: Soundness, Correctness, Completeness, Conciseness, Relevance, Fluency) on a Biopolymer-bound Soil Composite literature benchmark, showing that EGT-KG outperforms the vanilla RAG method in most settings, with the best improvement from llama3:8b: a Final Score of 70.37 (+14.67%) and 68.82 (+12.14%) by AS/ES EGT-KG variants.

</details>


### [44] [The Privacy-Hallucination Tradeoff in Differentially Private Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00492)
*Krithika Ramesh,Krishna Pillutla,Danish Pruthi,Anjalie Field*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper uncovers a privacy-hallucination tradeoff in differentially private (DP) language models, showing that DP training increases hallucination rates (especially under stricter privacy budgets), investigates the underlying mechanism where DP mechanisms flatten output distributions, and demonstrates that higher fact frequency in training data can mitigate hallucination risks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Both privacy and factual accuracy are critical in high-stakes domains like healthcare, but the interaction between differentially private training and hallucination in language models had not been previously investigated.

Method: The authors empirically compare hallucination rates between DP and non-DP trained language models under varying privacy budgets; investigate model properties, showing DP mechanisms flatten output distributions and redistribute probability mass toward factually incorrect alternatives; and conduct controlled experiments varying fact frequency in training data to characterize its impact on hallucination risk in DP models.
