Result: DP-trained models (pre-trained or fine-tuned) produce more hallucinations than non-DP counterparts, with severity increasing as privacy budgets get stricter. DP mechanisms flatten output distributions, and higher fact frequency in training data can reduce hallucination risks in DP models.

Conclusion: The findings highlight the need for more nuanced privacy-preserving interventions that provide rigorous privacy guarantees without sacrificing factual accuracy.

Abstract: Both privacy and factual accuracy are paramount in high-stakes domains like healthcare. Concerningly, we uncover and investigate a privacy-hallucination tradeoff in differentially private (DP) language models. First, we empirically show that models pre-trained or fine-tuned with DP tend to produce more hallucinations than non-DP counterparts, with increased severity as the privacy budget grows stricter. Second, we investigate model properties driving this tradeoff, demonstrating that DP mechanisms flatten output distributions, potentially redistributing probability mass toward factually incorrect alternatives. Third, through experiments where we control fact frequency in training data, we characterize how information frequency can reduce hallucination risks in DP models. Overall, our findings underscore the need for more nuanced privacy-preserving interventions that offer rigorous privacy guarantees without compromising factual accuracy.

</details>


### [45] [Validity-Aware Jailbreak Evaluation for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00498)
*Qilong Wu,Sahil Wadhwa,Pranab Mohanty,Giri Iyengar,Varun Chandrasekaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies a critical flaw in existing jailbreak evaluation methods for LLMs: they prioritize linguistic plausibility over factual/procedural correctness. The authors propose SEAV (Sequential Epistemic and Action-Level Validation), a verification-centric framework that decomposes responses into ordered steps and validates both semantic validity and factual correctness using retrieval-grounded verification. SEAV reduces false positives by 14.9 percentage points on the SD-A dataset and reclassifies 22.1%-51.0% of previously labeled jailbreak successes as invalid across three of four public benchmarks, demonstrating that enforcing correctness significantly reshapes measured LLM robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing jailbreak evaluation methodologies rely on refusal behavior, semantic resemblance, and intent-matching heuristics that emphasize linguistic plausibility rather than correctness. This is problematic because many jailbreak intents depend on instructional validity rather than epistemic factuality, causing realistic-looking but factually or procedurally incorrect responses to be incorrectly labeled as successful jailbreaks.

Method: SEAV decomposes model responses into ordered steps and evaluates both validity and correctness. It combines LLM-as-a-judge mechanisms for semantic interpretation with retrieval-grounded verification against external knowledge sources, assessing whether generated content is factually correct, structurally consistent, and operationally capable of advancing harmful objectives.

Result: SEAV cuts the false-positive rate on SD-A (a curated strategic-dishonesty diagnostic) by 14.9 percentage points vs. the strongest baseline, and reclassifies 22.1%-51.0% of sampled prior-labeled successes as invalid across three of four public benchmarks. Results are stable across tested search backends and evaluator models.

Conclusion: Enforcing correctness substantially reshapes measured robustness: many previously labeled jailbreak successes are reclassified as invalid under SEAV, highlighting the importance of verification-centric evaluation for truthful and rigorous LLM safety assessment.

Abstract: Jailbreak robustness has become central to large language model (LLM) safety evaluation, yet prevailing methodologies rely primarily on refusal behavior, semantic resemblance, and intent-matching heuristics that emphasize linguistic plausibility rather than correctness. We identify a key limitation in existing evaluations: many jailbreak intents depend on instructional validity rather than epistemic factuality, allowing realistic-looking responses to be labeled successful despite being factually or procedurally incorrect. To address this gap, we propose Sequential Epistemic and Action-Level Validation (SEAV), a verification-centric jailbreak evaluation framework that decomposes responses into ordered steps and evaluates both validity and correctness. SEAV combines LLM-as-a-judge mechanisms for semantic interpretation with retrieval-grounded verification using external knowledge sources, assessing whether generated content is factually correct, structurally consistent, and operationally capable of advancing harmful objectives. Empirically, SEAV cuts the false-positive rate on SD-A (a curated strategic-dishonesty diagnostic) by 14.9\,pp vs. the strongest baseline, and reclassifies 22.1\%--51.0\% of sampled prior-labeled successes as invalid across three of four public benchmarks. Together, these results show that enforcing correctness substantially reshapes measured robustness: many previously labeled jailbreak successes are reclassified as invalid, and results are stable across the tested search backends and evaluator models. Code and data are available at https://github.com/Ardor-Wu/SEAV.

</details>


### [46] [Wave Function Backpropagation with Explicit Temporal-Interval Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00503)
*Byunggu Yu,Justin Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Wave Function Backpropagation (WFB), a wave-parameterized learning formulation where neural responses are represented by learnable amplitude, wavenumber, angular frequency, and phase, enabling integration of temporal intervals through the phase of a differentiable spatiotemporal wave. The method is validated on feed-forward trajectory prediction, achieving up to a 20.4% reduction in average displacement error (ADE), while analysis suggests the gains stem from the wave representation itself rather than temporal interval alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional neural networks rely primarily on affine transformations and nonlinear activations, treating elapsed time either as auxiliary features or assuming uniform sampling. This overlooks the inherent temporal structure in many problems. The authors aim to introduce a principled wave-based parameterization that more naturally encodes temporal dynamics into neural responses.

Method: WFB represents neural responses using learnable amplitude, wavenumber, angular frequency, and phase, linking observed states to temporal intervals through the phase of a differentiable spatiotemporal wave. The work derives standard WFB gradients and incorporates a spatial-curvature correction based on the Laplacian of the wave response. It is instantiated in a deliberately feed-forward trajectory predictor (STD-WFB), comparing real-interval and shuffled-interval variants against FFN baselines.

Result: With motion features, STD-WFB using real intervals reduces ADE by 20.4% relative to the original FFN baseline. In position-only evaluation that removes temporal leakage, real-interval WFB reduces ADE by 10.4% relative to the original FFN and achieves 2.1% lower mean ADE than a matched FFN with explicit Delta t. Shuffled-interval WFB achieves the lowest mean ADE, indicating gains are attributable to the wave representation rather than interval alignment.

Conclusion: WFB is established as a viable structured feed-forward learning formulation that improves trajectory prediction performance over baselines. The evidence indicates the effectiveness stems from the wave representation itself, not temporal alignment. The findings provide a clear foundation for subsequent architectural studies to extend WFB beyond feed-forward settings.

Abstract: Conventional neural networks learn predominantly through affine transformations followed by nonlinear activations, while elapsed time is often treated as an auxiliary feature or assumed to be uniformly sampled. This paper introduces Wave Function Backpropagation (WFB), a wave-parameterized learning formulation in which neural responses are represented by learnable amplitude, wavenumber, angular frequency, and phase. The formulation associates an observed state with its temporal interval Delta t through the phase of a differentiable spatiotemporal wave. We derive standard WFB gradients and a spatial-curvature correction based on the Laplacian of the wave response. WFB is instantiated in a deliberately feed-forward trajectory predictor to provide a controlled proof of concept; sequence learning is outside the scope of the present evaluation. With motion features, STD-WFB using real intervals reduces average displacement error (ADE) by 20.4% relative to the original FFN baseline. In a new position-only evaluation that removes temporal leakage through precomputed velocity and acceleration, real-interval WFB reduces ADE by 10.4% relative to the original FFN and remains competitive with parameter-matched ReLU controls, obtaining 2.1% lower mean ADE than the matched FFN with explicit Delta t. Shuffled-interval WFB attains the lowest mean ADE, indicating that the present evidence supports the effectiveness of the wave representation but does not attribute the gain to interval alignment. These results establish WFB as a viable structured feed-forward learning formulation and define a clear basis for subsequent architectural studies.

</details>


### [47] [CoVer: Conflict-Aware Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00508)
*Shuning Zhang,Dai Shi,Bohao Chu,Hui Wang,Yuwei Chuai,Yifan Wang,Jingruo Chen,Simin Li,Xin Yi,Hewu Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ContraNote, a large-scale real-world dataset from X's Community Notes system (33,686 posts for evidence-level conflict resolution and 54,474 instances for aggregation-level prioritization), along with CoVer, a three-stage factual adjudication framework (evidence schema normalization, factual consensus, support verification). CoVer outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across multiple benchmarks including ContraNote, CONFACT-HumC, and CONFACT-ModC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Social media fact-checking suffers from evidence-level and aggregation-level conflicts, where erroneous evidence can mimic authoritative news sources, making conflict verification particularly challenging.

Method: The paper presents ContraNote, a real-world dataset derived from X's Community Notes, and proposes CoVer, a factual adjudication framework with a three-stage pipeline: evidence schema normalization, factual consensus, and support verification, designed to prioritize evidence over noise.

Result: CoVer achieves strong performance on ContraNote Conflict tasks (86.0% Acc., 68.0% mac. F1, 64.5 bal. Acc.), Prioritization tasks (88.5% Acc., 88.5% mac. F1, 89.2% bal. Acc.), CONFACT-HumC (88.4% Acc.), and CONFACT-ModC (89.4% Acc.), surpassing SOTA baselines.

Conclusion: The proposed ContraNote dataset and CoVer framework effectively address evidence-level and aggregation-level conflicts in social media fact-checking, demonstrating superior performance over existing methods on multiple benchmarks.

Abstract: Social media fact-checking has long been challenged by evidence-level and aggregation-level conflicts, where erroneous evidence mimics authoritative news sources. To capture this challenge and support conflict verification tasks, we present ContraNote, a large-scale real-world dataset curated from X's Community Notes system. It includes 33,686 posts for evaluating evidence-level conflict resolution, and 54,474 instances for evaluating aggregation-level prioritization. Additionally, we propose CoVer, a factual adjudication framework with three-stage pipelines: evidence schema normalization, factual consensus and support verification. This prioritizes evidence over noise to prevent it from compromising the final verdict. Technical evaluations show that CoVer achieves strong performance compared with state-of-the-art baselines across ContraNote (86.0% Acc., 68.0% mac. F1, 64.5 bal. Acc. on Conflict; and 88.5% Acc., 88.5 mac. F1 and 89.2 bal. Acc. on Prioritization), CONFACT-HumC (88.4% Acc.) and CONFACT-ModC (89.4% Acc.).

</details>


### [48] [When the Algorithm Becomes the Brand Crisis: A Sociotechnical Theory of Distributed Responsibility and Accountable Transparency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00510)
*Mohammad Saleh Torkestani,Taha Mansouri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This conceptual paper develops a sociotechnical process theory for understanding how stakeholders assign responsibility for AI system failures, distinguishing between AI/algorithmic incidents, AI-related organizational crises, and AI-related organizational scandals. It proposes a framework linking incident configuration to actor-specific attribution, appraisal, and potential public moralization, while introducing "accountable transparency" as a response strategy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional brand-crisis models do not fully specify how stakeholders assign responsibility when technical causation, customer-facing control, and governance duties are distributed across AI systems, developers, deployers, vendors, and users. This distributed nature of AI systems creates a theoretical gap in understanding blame attribution and crisis response.

Method: A conceptual theory-building approach grounded in a structured, federated scoping synthesis of verified academic and primary sources.

Result: The framework distinguishes incidents, crises, and scandals; shows that incident configuration shapes actor-specific attribution which informs capability, integrity, fairness, and relationship appraisals; reconciles conflicting findings on how algorithm involvement buffers vs. redirects negative brand reactions; and introduces "accountable transparency" as a response configuration. Evidence supports conditional proximal inferences (blame, trust, satisfaction, firm evaluation, communication credibility) more than durable reputation or market performance claims.

Conclusion: The paper offers a theory that clarifies responsibility attribution in AI-related failures and proposes accountable transparency as a response framework, while cautioning that the strongest empirical support exists for short-term, proximal outcomes rather than long-term reputation or market effects.

Abstract: Artificial intelligence systems increasingly enact market-facing promises through chatbots, recommendation systems, automated decisions, and generative interfaces. Their failures, misuse, and misrepresentation raise a question that conventional brand-crisis models do not fully specify: how do stakeholders assign responsibility when technical causation, customer-facing control, and governance duties are distributed across an AI system, developer, deployer, vendor, and user? This conceptual paper develops a sociotechnical process theory from a structured, federated scoping synthesis of verified academic and primary sources. It distinguishes an AI/algorithmic incident from an AI-related organisational crisis and, in turn, from an AI-related organisational scandal. The framework proposes that incident configuration shapes actor-specific attribution; attribution informs capability, integrity, fairness, and relationship appraisals; and public moralisation may, but need not, escalate an incident into scandal. The theory offers a reconciliation of findings that algorithm involvement can buffer negative brand reactions in some settings while robot and chatbot failures can redirect responsibility to an associated firm in others. It introduces accountable transparency as a proposed response configuration that combines timely notice, an intelligible account, role-responsibility acknowledgement, remedy, evidence of correction, and recourse. The evidence supports conditional, proximal inferences about blame, trust, satisfaction, firm evaluation, and communication credibility more strongly than claims about durable reputation, brand equity, or market performance.

</details>


### [49] [ISO-RAG: Isoperimetric Noise Control for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00513)
*Siyuan Zhang,Hanchen Wang,Dong Wen,Ying Zhang,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ISO-RAG is a geometry-aware RAG framework that projects knowledge graphs into a hyperbolic Poincaré ball to precompute isoperimetric profiles, pruning spurious edges during retrieval to restrict search to localized subgraphs. This achieves exact, low-latency Personalized PageRank (PPR) convergence for multi-hop QA, outperforming SOTA baselines with 10.0% average gains in retrieval recall and 4.3% in downstream exact match.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional dense retrieval fails on multi-hop QA due to complex reasoning paths, while graph-based RAG suffers from semantic drift and high online latency from noisy global graph traversals. There is a need for a method that captures multi-step relationships without latency bottlenecks.

Method: The framework projects the underlying knowledge graph into a hyperbolic Poincaré ball to precompute node-wise isoperimetric profiles. This topological purification prunes spurious edges during retrieval, restricting the search space to a strictly localized subgraph, and regulates Personalized PageRank (PPR) diffusion for exact, low-latency convergence.

Result: On multi-hop QA benchmarks, ISO-RAG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by average absolute gains of 10.0% in retrieval recall and 4.3% in downstream exact match, achieving a superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off by eliminating the latency bottleneck of global traversals.

Conclusion: ISO-RAG fundamentally resolves the tension between retrieval accuracy and efficiency in graph-based RAG by leveraging hyperbolic geometry and isoperimetric principles, effectively eliminating the latency bottleneck inherent in global graph traversal while improving retrieval and QA performance.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) mitigates large language models (LLMs) hallucinations, yet conventional dense retrieval struggles with the complex reasoning paths of multi-hop question answering (QA). Graph-based RAG captures multi-step relationships but suffers from severe semantic drift and high online latency due to noisy global graph traversals. Thus, we propose ISO-RAG (ISOperimetric Retrieval-Augmented Generation), a geometry-aware RAG framework. By projecting the underlying knowledge graph into a hyperbolic Poincare ball to precompute node-wise isoperimetric profiles, ISO-RAG prunes spurious edges during retrieval, restricting the search space to a strictly localized subgraph. This topological purification regulates Personalized PageRank (PPR) diffusion driving the retrieval process, ensuring exact and low-latency convergence. Experiments on multi-hop QA benchmarks demonstrate that ISO-RAG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by average absolute gains of 10.0% in retrieval recall and 4.3% in downstream exact match, achieving a superior accuracy-efficiency trade-off by fundamentally eliminating the latency bottleneck of global traversals. Our source code is available at https://github.com/ZaiizaiZHANG/ISO-RAG.

</details>


### [50] [Feedback-Assisted Trust Propagation over Document Relation Graphs for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00543)
*Zhuoheng Li,Ying Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TrustPropRAG is a retrieval-augmented generation method that builds a document relation graph and propagates sparse human reliability feedback across the corpus via multi-hop optimization to estimate trust scores, improving document selection and answer generation reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RAG systems depend on external corpora that may contain outdated, contradictory, noisy, or unreliable documents, posing reliability risks. Prior work exploited document relations but only propagated reliability signals between directly compared document pairs, which is insufficient for large-scale corpora where collecting human feedback on every document is costly.

Method: TrustPropRAG constructs a document relation graph and formulates an optimization problem that jointly captures pairwise document relations and limited human feedback signals. By solving this optimization, it estimates a trust score for each document through multi-hop propagation across the graph, anchoring on sparse feedback. These trust scores are then used to improve reliable document selection and support trust-aware answer generation.

Result: TrustPropRAG improves both retrieval quality and exact match accuracy over baselines, and remains robust under sparse and noisy feedback conditions.

Conclusion: Structuring document relations as a graph and propagating sparse, costly reliability signals via multi-hop optimization effectively extends trust estimates across an entire corpus, leading to more reliable RAG retrieval and generation.

Abstract: Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) systems rely on external corpora that may contain outdated, contradictory, noisy, or unreliable documents, introducing reliability risks. Prior work has leveraged document relations to improve the answer reliability of RAG. To propagate reliability signals beyond directly compared document pairs, we propose TrustPropRAG, which structures document relations as a graph and estimates document reliability through multi-hop propagation across the graph. TrustPropRAG anchors this propagation with a limited set of human feedback on document reliability, extending these costly-to-collect feedback-based reliability signals across the whole corpus. Specifically, based on the constructed document relation graph, TrustPropRAG estimates a trust score for each document by formulating and solving an optimization problem that jointly captures pairwise document relations and user feedback. These scores are then used to improve the selection of reliable documents and support trust-aware answer generation. Evaluation results show that TrustPropRAG improves both retrieval quality and exact match over baselines, and remains robust under sparse and noisy feedback.

</details>


### [51] [VoiceLongMemEval: Do Assistants Remember How You Sounded?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00570)
*Ramit Pahwa,Parivesh Priye,Apoorva Beedu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: VoiceLongMemEval (VLME) is a new benchmark for evaluating AI assistants' ability to reason over paralinguistic metadata (emotion, prosody, voice events) in long multi-session conversation histories—information that is unrecoverable from text alone. Results show a pervasive "affect gap": models gain 0.09–0.38 accuracy when given paralinguistic metadata as text, while audio-native models extract these cues directly from speech, revealing that standard ASR pipelines systematically discard this critical signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for long-horizon dialogue history treat it as pure information retrieval, temporal reasoning, or knowledge update tasks, ignoring the fundamental dynamics of human-agent interaction—how something was said (paralinguistic cues) rather than just what was said.

Method: The authors construct VLME, where every answer depends on paralinguistic metadata (emotion labels, prosody descriptors, voice events) attached to conversational turns. Each item passes a three-stage adversarial gate ensuring a strong language model fails when given only the transcript. Models are evaluated across text-track paralinguistic metadata conditions and audio-native speech conditions.

Result: Frontier and open-weight models exhibit a pervasive affect gap: providing text-track paralinguistic metadata yields 0.09–0.38 accuracy improvement (0.61–0.69 with evidence hints), while audio-native models outperform blind baselines (0.354–0.412 vs. 0.325). Standard ASR pipelines systematically discard paralinguistic signal.

Conclusion: Long-context reasoning in AI assistants must incorporate paralinguistic dimensions of speech, not just transcribed words. The pervasive affect gap across current models highlights that ASR-based pipelines are insufficient for tasks requiring understanding of how language was delivered, motivating the need for audio-native or paralinguistically-augmented architectures.

Abstract: With the growing scale of multi-agent architectures and large language models, deployed AI assistants are increasingly tasked with reasoning over long, continuous, multi-session conversation histories. Current benchmarks evaluate this dialogue history as information retrieval over long horizon, temporal reasoning, or knowledge updates, while crucially ignoring the fundamental dynamics of human-agent interaction, i.e. how they said it. To address this gap, we present VoiceLongMemEval (VLME) benchmark, where every answer depends on paralinguistic metadata (emotion labels, prosody descriptors, and voice events) attached to conversational turns, which is otherwise unrecoverable from the words alone. Every item passes a three-stage adversarial gate, ensuring that a strong language model fails when given only the transcript. Evaluating leading frontier and open-weight models reveals a pervasive affect gap; providing text-track paralinguistic metadata yields a 0.09 to 0.38 accuracy boost (0.61 to 0.69 when prompted with evidence hints), while standard ASR pipelines systematically discard this signal. Additionally, audio-native models successfully extract these cues directly from speech (0.354 to 0.412 vs. 0.325 blind). Code and dataset will be made available upon acceptance.

</details>


### [52] [Residual Sparsification via Output Importance for Compressing Mixture-of-Experts LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00575)
*Seungwoo Jung,Dohyeok Kwon,Seungmin Cha,Junseok Lee,Yeonho Yoo,Chuck Yoo,Gyeongsik Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PARSER is a new residual sparsification method for MoE architectures that shifts the compression objective from minimizing isolated matrix errors to preserving the expert output error, achieving up to 1.44x closer accuracy to uncompressed models while maintaining the same memory reduction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing residual sparsification methods compress each residual matrix independently by minimizing its compression error, but this objective is misaligned with preserving model accuracy. Since expert outputs are produced through computations coupled across multiple projections and hidden representations, small errors in individual matrices can propagate and amplify through interactions, leading to significant accuracy degradation.

Method: PARSER introduces a new compression objective that preserves the expert output error rather than minimizing isolated matrix errors. It achieves this by computing output importance, which measures the actual contribution of each matrix component to the expert output error, and uses this to guide the sparsification process.

Result: Compared with existing residual sparsification methods, PARSER narrows the accuracy gap to the uncompressed model by 1.41x on Qwen and 1.44x on DeepSeek, while achieving the same peak memory reduction.

Conclusion: PARSER demonstrates that aligning compression objectives with the actual error propagation mechanism in MoE expert computation—rather than isolated matrix-level error—significantly improves post-compression accuracy without sacrificing memory efficiency.

Abstract: Mixture-of-experts (MoE) architectures scale large language models efficiently, but they demand massive GPU memory. To cope with such demand, models are commonly compressed to reduce their memory footprint. Residual sparsification is a representative compression technique that decomposes each projection matrix of an expert into a shared base matrix and per-expert residual matrix, and then compresses the residuals. Existing sparsification methods compress each residual matrix independently by minimizing its compression error, thereby minimizing the error of each projection matrix. However, our analysis shows that this objective is misaligned with preserving model accuracy after compression. In an expert, the final output is produced through computations coupled across multiple projections and hidden representations. Therefore, even small errors in individual matrices can propagate through hidden representations and projection interactions, leading to large expert output errors and accuracy degradation. To address this misalignment, we propose PARSER, a new residual sparsification method that shifts the compression objective from minimizing isolated matrix errors to preserving the expert output error. PARSER achieves this by introducing output importance, which measures the actual contribution to the expert output error. Our experiments show that, compared with existing methods, PARSER narrows the accuracy gap to the uncompressed model by 1.41$\times$ on Qwen and 1.44$\times$ on DeepSeek, while achieving the same peak memory reduction.

</details>


### [53] [Consistency Without Alignment: Item-Sensitive Language Models Indistinguishable From Random](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00576)
*Cris Huynh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper investigates item-sensitivity as evidence of task competence in language models, revealing it to be necessary but not sufficient. Using a forced-choice signalling task from the board game Deception: Murder in Hong Kong (where reference strategies are computationally tractable in closed form), the authors test seven language models across two families, a post-training ablation, and three scoring rules. All 21 model-by-rule cells prove reliably item-sensitive, yet 8 of 21 are statistically indistinguishable from random choosers ignoring the item, and 5 score worse than random at describing the target. Item-sensitivity correlates with distance-from-random at only r=0.30. The authors term this phenomenon "consistency without alignment" and argue it generalizes to evaluations relying solely on item-sensitivity, permutation consistency, or self-consistency without independent reference values. They also find a literal-similarity baseline without pragmatics outperforms most LMs, adding a pragmatic layer moves choosers toward random rather than the Bayesian reference, and a standard labelled multiple-choice format carries no measurable content signal.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To demonstrate that item-sensitivity—commonly used as evidence of a model's task competence—is necessary but insufficient for genuine task alignment, by constructing a closed-form solvable environment where reference strategies are exactly computable and showing that models can be robustly item-sensitive while still behaving indistinguishably from or worse than random baselines.

Method: The authors use a forced-choice signalling task derived from the social deduction board game Deception: Murder in Hong Kong. For this environment, all reference points (fit-maximising strategy, posterior-maximising strategy, and uniform random selection) are computed in closed form. They evaluate seven language models spanning two model families, including a post-training ablation, across three independent scoring rules (21 model-by-rule cells). They compare model behaviour against the closed-form Bayesian reference and random baselines, and additionally test a literal-similarity (no-pragmatics) baseline and a pragmatic-layer augmentation, alongside a labelled multiple-choice format condition.

Result: All 21 model-by-rule cells are reliably item-sensitive, yet 8 of 21 are not statistically distinguishable from a random chooser that ignores the item, and 5 score worse than random at target description. Item-sensitivity and distance from random correlate at only r=0.30. A literal-similarity baseline with no pragmatics outperforms most tested language models; adding a pragmatic layer moves choosers toward random rather than the Bayesian reference; and the standard labelled multiple-choice format carries no measurable content signal.

Conclusion: The findings establish that item-sensitivity, permutation consistency, and self-consistency are necessary but insufficient evidence of task competence without an independent reference point for the measured quantity. The authors introduce the term "consistency without alignment" to describe this phenomenon and argue the result generalises broadly. Human-condition data were designed and piloted but not yet collected; reported results reflect only the model side of the pre-registered instrument.

Abstract: Item-sensitivity, defined as whether a model's choice depends on the specific input rather than on its own output prior, is widely reported as evidence of task competence. We show this evidence is necessary but not sufficient using a forced-choice signalling task abstracted from the board game Deception: Murder in Hong Kong. In this environment, the reference points against which a coordinate should be judged (a fit-maximising strategy, a posterior-maximising strategy, and uniform random selection) are all computable in closed form. Across seven language models, two model families, a post-training ablation, and three independent scoring rules, every one of 21 model-by-rule cells is reliably item-sensitive. Yet 8 of those 21 cells are not statistically distinguishable from a chooser that ignores the item and selects at random, and 5 score worse than random at describing the target. Item-sensitivity and distance from random correlate at only r = 0.30. We call this consistency without alignment and argue it generalises to any evaluation that relies on item-sensitivity, permutation consistency, or self-consistency without an independent reference for the measured quantity. We further find that a literal-similarity baseline with no pragmatics outperforms most tested language models, that adding a pragmatic layer over two baseline similarity sources moves choosers toward random rather than toward the Bayesian reference, and that a standard labelled multiple-choice format carries no measurable content signal here. All results represent the model side of a pre-registered instrument; a matched human condition is designed and piloted but not yet collected.

</details>


### [54] [Same Request, Different Boundary: Evaluating Cybersecurity Assistance across Conversational Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00578)
*Rui Yang,Yang Hong,Yichao Xu,Zhengyu Liu,Ziyang Li,Yinzhi Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: 3R-Bench is a benchmark of 150 real-world cybersecurity requests with adversarial conversational contexts for evaluating LLM safety refusal mechanisms. Testing eight LLMs, it reveals that conversational context dramatically alters compliance: prior accepted assistant history raises compliance from 62.0% to 85.1%, while dialogue decomposition (splitting requests across turns) drops compliance from 501/800 direct responses to 172/800, a 45.1-point decrease.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cybersecurity requests are dual-use: refusing all would harm legitimate defenders, while allowing malicious ones risks severe consequences. Providers need a mechanism to block harmful requests while helping legitimate users, but existing cybersecurity-specific datasets do not account for conversational context, which can be exploited to bypass safety policies.

Method: The authors introduce 3R-Bench (Refusal, Repetition, and Revision), a benchmark containing 150 real-world cybersecurity requests. Each request is augmented with two adversarial conversational settings, and eight LLMs are evaluated to analyze how preceding conversation history and dialogue decomposition affect model compliance decisions.

Result: Prior assistant behavior strongly changes responses to an unchanged request: compliance rises from 62.0% after refused history to 85.1% after accepted history across 376 available pairs from a 400-pair panel. Under dialogue decomposition, compliance falls from 501/800 direct responses to 172/800 (a 45.1-point decrease over 738 comparable pairs). Failure feedback only recovers a small fraction of this loss.

Conclusion: Conversational context profoundly influences LLM safety compliance on cybersecurity requests, revealing that current safety mechanisms are context-sensitive in both directions—contextual priming can increase dangerous compliance, while decomposed dialogue drastically reduces legitimate assistance. Both vulnerabilities require attention when designing refusal mechanisms for dual-use cybersecurity scenarios.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) can solve complex problems, but their misuse in high-risk domains can lead to severe consequences. Model providers therefore restrict assistance for potentially harmful requests. Refusing all cybersecurity requests would therefore harm legitimate users. Providers need a mechanism to block malicious use without denying legitimate assistance to defenders. Existing cybersecurity-specific datasets evaluate this mechanism, but none considers the conversational context of a request. We introduce 3R-Bench (Refusal, Repetition, and Revision), a benchmark of 150 real-world cybersecurity requests augmented with two adversarial conversational settings, and evaluate eight LLMs on it. Prior assistant behavior strongly changes responses to an unchanged request: among 376 available pairs from a 400-pair panel, compliance rises from 62.0% after refused history to 85.1% after accepted history. The opposite pattern appears under dialogue decomposition. In comparison, compliance falls from 501/800 direct responses to 172/800 after dialogue; among 738 pairs returning model-authored text in both conditions, the decrease is 45.1 points. Failure feedback recovers only a small fraction of this loss.

</details>


### [55] [Socrates went Nuclear: Comparing Interaction Strategies for AI systems in a Learning Context using Brain Sensing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00584)
*Alexandre Clin Deffarges,Nataliya Kosmyna,Pattie Maes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This study compares three AI-assisted learning designs—unrestricted chatbot, Socratic-mode constrained bot, and adaptive EEG-driven tutoring—and finds that while the unrestricted chatbot yields higher immediate learning gains, it reflects answer-retrieval strategies rather than deeper learning, as shown by usage patterns and EEG engagement data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate whether unrestricted AI access (e.g., ChatGPT-like bots) genuinely facilitates learning or merely bypasses the cognitive effort required for knowledge acquisition, by contrasting it with pedagogically constrained and brain-signal-adaptive alternatives.

Method: A comparative study with 50 participants learning nuclear safety protocols (zero-prior-knowledge domain). Participants underwent an instructional video, pre-test, an AI-driven assessment phase (randomized across three conditions: unrestricted chatbot, Socratic hint-based bot, and adaptive non-conversational tutor adjusting difficulty via real-time EEG-derived cognitive engagement from a Muse headband), followed by an immediate post-test. Analysis included learning gains (delta between pre/post-tests), EEG engagement levels, and cluster analysis of conversational usage patterns.

Result: The unrestricted chatbot produced significantly higher learning gains than both constrained conditions (p < .03, d > 0.80). The adaptive condition generated significantly higher EEG engagement (p = .018). Cluster analysis revealed that most unrestricted-mode users adopted direct answer-retrieval strategies, while Socratic-mode users initially reasoned through hints but progressively disengaged.

Conclusion: The higher performance of unrestricted AI is not evidence of deeper learning but rather an artifact of immediate post-test evaluation following the training phase. The results suggest that while unrestricted chatbots optimize short-term test outcomes, adaptive systems generate higher cognitive engagement—raising questions about the long-term efficacy and pedagogical value of unconstrained AI access in learning contexts.

Abstract: Does unrestricted AI access bypass the cognitive effort required for learning, or does it streamline knowledge acquisition? This paper reports on a study where we compare three designs for user-AI interaction in a learning context: (1) an unrestricted conversational bot like ChatGPT, (2) a pedagogically constrained bot that guides through hints without giving final answers, which we refer to as the Socratic mode; and (3) a non-conversational adaptive tutoring system that adjusts difficulty in real-time based on the user's cognitive engagement derived from the brain signals. Fifty study participants were tasked with learning about nuclear safety protocols, a domain chosen for its zero-prior knowledge baseline. The participants progressed through an instructional video, a pre-test, an AI-driven assessment phase, which varied in the three conditions, and an immediate post-test. The nature of the questions centered primarily on factual knowledge acquisition, but it still required participants to have a global understanding of the concepts in order to answer the questions correctly. A Muse headband was used to derive the cognitive engagement of all users in all conditions. The unrestricted chatbot produced higher learning gains (delta) than both constrained modes (p < .03, d > 0.80), while the adaptive condition generated significantly higher EEG engagement (p = .018). The cluster analysis of chatbot usage and discussion patterns by users showed that most participants in the unrestricted-mode adopted a direct answer-retrieval strategy, while participants in the Socratic-mode initially attempted to reason through the hints before progressively disengaging. Consequently, this also suggests that the success of the unrestricted AI is not an evidence of deeper learning, but rather a result of the immediate post-test evaluation after the training phase.

</details>


### [56] [Control-Data Flow Separation: Stable Prompt Optimization in Multi-Agent LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00621)
*Wentao Zhang,Syed Shariyar Murtaza,Junaid Ahmad Bhatti,Utkarsh Soni,Yifan Nie,Eugene Wen,Yuntian Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper addresses the problem of prompt optimization in multi-agent LLM systems where prompts serve dual roles (content generation and execution protocol). The authors propose control-data flow separation: representing execution-critical control as typed, validated program objects while keeping task-relevant language as optimizable data flow. This prevents prompt drift from corrupting protocols, achieving 100% protocol validity and improved task performance across multiple workflows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prompts in multi-agent LLM systems are optimized for content generation, but they also encode execution-critical protocols (message routing, output formatting, termination signals) that code depends on. Edits aimed at improving content can inadvertently break these protocols, causing entire pipelines to fail. The key insight is that these two roles differ in representation: protocols are structured while content is unstructured language.

Method: The authors propose a control-data flow separation framework where execution-critical control signals are represented as typed, validated program objects, while task-relevant content remains as optimizable natural language data flow for agent communication. This architectural separation ensures optimizers can modify agent behavior via language without exposing routing/formatting interfaces to prompt drift.

Result: Across synthetic reasoning, collaborative review generation, and insurance rating workflows, the framework empirically achieves 100% eventual protocol validity while consistently improving task performance compared to approaches without such separation.

Conclusion: Separating structured control from unstructured data in prompts is an effective design principle for safe and effective prompt optimization in multi-agent LLM systems.

Abstract: Prompt optimization can improve multi-agent LLM systems, but the prompts being optimized often serve two entangled roles: generating task-relevant content and specifying execution-critical protocols, such as message routing, output formatting, and termination signals, on which the underlying code relies. As a result, a prompt edit intended to improve content generation can inadvertently corrupt the protocol and cause the entire agent pipeline to fail. Our key observation is that these two roles have different representations: execution protocols are typically structured, while task-relevant content is usually expressed in unstructured language. Based on this, we propose control-data flow separation, where execution-critical control is represented as typed, validated program objects, while task-relevant language remains the optimizable data flow for agent communication. This design allows optimizers to improve multi-agent behavior without exposing the routing or formatting interface to prompt drift. Across synthetic reasoning, collaborative review generation, and insurance rating workflows, our framework empirically achieves 100% eventual protocol validity while consistently improving task performance.

</details>


### [57] [REVISE: Validity-Guided Recovery for Online Revisions in Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00643)
*Ruoling Qi,Xuaner Wu,Penghang Liu,Jian Chen,Yirui Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents Revise, a validity-guided runtime for fine-grained recovery in structured agent workflows that stops only invalid work, preserves valid progress, and recomputes only affected regions when agent revisions arrive during concurrent execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agent revisions during concurrent execution expose a fundamental correctness-efficiency trade-off. Existing recovery strategies are imbalanced with coarse-grained policies: they either allow potentially stale work to continue (favoring efficiency), or restart the workflow/recompute a linear suffix from the earliest conflict (favoring correctness), thereby discarding unaffected progress.

Method: Revise intersects a revision's delta with recorded data and control dependencies, propagates impact through the partially executed DAG to identify affected work, stops invalid work while preserving validity-established progress beyond the earliest conflict, and recomputes only the affected region. Incomplete provenance conservatively expands recovery, and reused results are revalidated before commit.

Result: Analysis of real coding-agent traces shows online recovery opportunities: 118 sessions retain observable work before a queued later message is delivered; across 167 overlapping assistant responses, enqueue-to-completion overlap reaches 56.55s at p95. Across 300 challenging revision/commit executions, Revise matches a latest-version oracle with no stale outputs or effects. On unmodified LangGraph and LLMCompiler applications using Qwen3-14B, it reduces model calls by 40.6-56.0% relative to full restart and by 31.3-43.6% relative to suffix recomputation. Under serving pressure, it reduces revision-to-correct-completion tokens by 13.26% and improves SLO goodput by 3.07-5.43%.

Conclusion: Fine-grained, validity-guided recovery in agent workflow runtimes can simultaneously achieve latest-version correctness and high efficiency, significantly outperforming coarse-grained restart and suffix-recomputation baselines across realistic workloads.

Abstract: Agent revisions expose a fundamental correctness--efficiency trade-off during concurrent execution. Discarding ongoing work preserves latest-version correctness but wastes progress that may remain valid, whereas reusing prior work preserves efficiency but risks propagating stale state into outputs and tool effects. Existing recovery strategies resolve this trade-off in an imbalanced way with coarse-grained policies: they either favor efficiency by allowing potentially stale work to continue, or favor correctness by restarting the workflow or recomputing a linear suffix from the earliest conflict, thereby discarding unaffected progress. We present \textsc{Revise}, a validity-guided runtime for fine-grained recovery in structured agent workflows. When a revision arrives, \textsc{Revise} first intersects its delta with recorded data and control dependencies and propagates the resulting impact through the partially executed DAG to identify affected work. It then stops invalid work, preserves validity-established progress beyond the earliest conflict, and recomputes only the affected region. Incomplete provenance conservatively expands recovery, while reused results are revalidated before commit. Analysis of real coding-agent traces show online recovery opportunities: 118 sessions retain observable work before a queued later message is delivered; across 167 overlapping assistant responses, enqueue-to-completion overlap reaches 56.55~s at p95. Across 300 challenging revision/commit executions, \textsc{Revise} matches a latest-version oracle with no stale outputs or effects. On unmodified LangGraph and LLMCompiler applications using Qwen3-14B, it reduces model calls by 40.6--56.0\% relative to full restart and by 31.3--43.6\% relative to suffix recomputation. Under serving pressure, it further reduces revision-to-correct-completion tokens by 13.26\% and improves SLO goodput by 3.07--5.43\%.

</details>


### [58] [DramaChain Bench: An End-to-End Benchmark for Short-Drama Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00646)
*Haoyuan Shi,Mingtao Chen,Shuo Jiang,Ziyan Chen,Xuyi Sheng,Yiming Liu,Ying Zhang,Miao Wang,Jianxiang Lu,Fanyang Lu,Songyuanyi Lu,Xiele Wu,Zhichao Hu,Yuhong Liu,Richeng Xuan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DramaChain Bench is the first benchmark evaluating the entire commercial short-drama production pipeline (script, storyboard, keyframe, shot-level video, finished drama), backed by five evaluation axes resolved into 63 leaf dimensions, 5,785 human-annotated items with 17,488 scores, and an agentic judge that reproduces model rankings at a mean PLCC of 0.918.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks evaluate only the video-generation stage using pre-authored inputs, leaving unanswered whether each production stage adheres to original script intent and whether disparate shots remain coherent after assembly into multi-episode releases.

Method: Built on three in-house systems: (1) DramaChain Dimensions—five evaluation axes instantiated at every stage, resolved into 63 leaf dimensions; (2) DramaChain Agent—calibrated against commercial short-drama platforms for stage-wise fair comparison; (3) DramaChain Labeling System—each of 5,785 items independently scored by three professional annotators with defects spatio-temporally localised from a predefined list. DramaChain Agentic Judge scores leaf dimensions automatically, gathering evidence over multiple agentic rounds against per-item checklists.

Result: Produced 17,488 valid scores and 255,925 traceable attribution records. Human annotations confirmed upstream defects cascade across the pipeline, showing final episode quality is not governed by video generation alone. The agentic judge reproduced model ranking at a mean PLCC of 0.918.

Conclusion: DramaChain Bench enables full-pipeline evaluation of short-drama production, demonstrating that upstream stage quality matters and that an automatic agentic judge can admit new models at no annotation cost.

Abstract: Commercial short-drama production follows a multi-stage chain: script, storyboard, keyframe imagery, shot-level video, and the finished short drama. Most existing benchmarks evaluate solely the video-generation stage using pre-authored inputs instead of real upstream pipeline outputs. This leaves two critical questions unanswerable: whether each stage adheres to the original script intent (rather than only its immediate input prompt), and whether disparate shots remain coherent after assembly into multi-episode releases. We present DramaChain Bench, the first short-drama benchmark that evaluates every stage of the complete production chain. It is built upon three in-house systems sharing one dimension system, DramaChain Dimensions: five evaluation axes instantiated at every stage, resolving into 63 leaf dimensions. DramaChain Agent is calibrated against commercial short-drama platforms in both workflow and finished short-drama quality, enabling stage-wise fair comparison across models. DramaChain Labeling System has each of the 5,785 items scored independently by three professional annotators, with all defects spatio-temporally localised and selected from a predefined defect list. This process produces 17,488 valid scores and 255,925 traceable attribution records. The human annotations confirm that upstream defects cascade across the pipeline, demonstrating that final episode quality is not governed by video generation alone. DramaChain Agentic Judge then scores every leaf dimension automatically, gathering evidence over multiple agentic rounds before judging against a per-item checklist; it reproduces the model ranking at a mean PLCC of 0.918, enough to admit new models at no annotation cost.

</details>


### [59] [Self-Reports Are Not Verification: Environment-Grounded Auditing of LLM Operators in Evolutionary Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00652)
*Enrong Pan,Ryan Zhou,Ting Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces an environment-grounded audit method for evaluating language model agent self-reports, using an evolutionary Contexto search task where every proposal receives an exact rank. Across 12,249 self-reports from 200 runs, all three tested assumptions fail: stated confidence is not calibrated (top-100 success overstated by factors of 4.8-9.3), inherited rationales do not meaningfully improve later proposals, and neither fitness-based nor random selection produces detectable selection differentials or parent-to-offspring transmission in report accuracy. The authors conclude agent self-reports should be treated as claims to verify, not evidence of reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language model agents generate confidence scores and rationales as convenient monitoring signals, but these conveniences lack verification. The paper seeks to test whether self-reported confidence, rationales, and selection mechanisms actually reflect true performance when grounded against an exact, environment-provided ground truth.

Method: The authors build an evolutionary Contexto search environment where each guess receives an exact rank from a feedback function, requiring no human annotation. They run 200 search runs across five configurations and three model families, producing 12,249 self-reports under four reporting configurations. They test three hypotheses: (1) stated confidence is calibrated against true ranks, (2) inherited rationales causally improve later proposals, and (3) fitness-based selection improves report quality and introduces a measurable selection differential.

Result: All three assumptions fail. Operators overstate top-100 success by factors of 4.8 to 9.3, and calibration/discrimination dissociate across model families. Controlled interventions on 754 inherited rationales bound any benefit of genuine rationales to roughly 250 ranks. Both fitness-based and random selection fail to produce detectable selection differentials or parent-to-offspring transmission in report accuracy, despite sharply different observable search behaviors.

Conclusion: Self-reported confidence, rationales, and fitness-based selection behaviors from language model agents do not reliably indicate true performance when audited against an exact environment. Agent self-reports should be treated as unverified claims requiring environmental grounding, not as intrinsic evidence of their own reliability or competence.

Abstract: Language model agents increasingly propose actions, observe external feedback, and explain their own behavior. Their confidence and rationales are convenient monitoring signals, but convenience is not verification. We introduce an environment-grounded audit in which every intermediate proposal receives an exact outcome. A language model operates an evolutionary Contexto search whose feedback function assigns every valid guess an exact rank without human annotation. Across 200 runs spanning five configurations and three model families, four reporting configurations produce 12,249 self-reports. We test three assumptions: stated confidence is calibrated, inherited rationales affect later proposals, and fitness-based selection improves report quality. All three fail. Operators overstate top-100 success by factors of 4.8 to 9.3, while calibration and discrimination dissociate across model families. Controlled interventions on 754 inherited rationales bound any measured benefit of the genuine rationale to roughly 250 ranks. Neither fitness-based nor random selection produces a detectable selection differential or parent-to-offspring transmission in report accuracy, despite sharply different search behavior. Agent self-reports should therefore be treated as claims to verify against the environment, not as evidence of their own reliability.

</details>


### [60] [SciTrue: Reliable Scientific Claim Validation with Frontier and Open Language Models at the NTCIR SciClaimEval Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00654)
*Qiming Bao,Neşet Özkan Tan,Siyuan Wang,Mark Gahegan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper describes SciTrue's winning submission to both subtasks of the NTCIR-19 SciClaimEval task, where they benchmark eleven multimodal models and achieve first place in three of four evidence/subtask combinations. The key finding is that a leak-free pair prior exploiting the task's Supported/Refuted pairing structure (derived from claim text alone) provides a larger performance boost than any model swap, raising Subtask-1 pair-accuracy from 72.2 to 93.5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To verify scientific claims against tables and figures in research papers, and to understand what actually drives performance in this task—whether it is model capability, task structure, or data artifacts—rather than simply tuning a single model.

Method: Benchmark eleven frontier and open multimodal models under a consistent per-sample protocol, combined with transparent post-processing. They also employ a leak-free pair prior that recovers Supported/Refuted pairing from claim text alone, along with controlled fine-tuning, distillation, and agentic consistency-checking for validation.

Result: Secured first place by a clear margin in three of four evidence-category/subtask combinations and tied for first in the fourth. Claude Opus 4.8 and Gemma-4-31B exceed the strongest public baseline (o4-mini), with GPT-5.5 and Claude Fable 5 leading both subtasks (97.7 on Subtask 2). The pair prior raised Subtask-1 pair-accuracy from 72.2 to 93.5. Most residual errors are label-mapping swaps or dataset noise, so true system ability exceeds measured accuracy.

Conclusion: The task's pairing structure is the largest performance lever, and residual errors are largely attributable to visually-undetectable label-mapping swaps and dataset noise rather than model limitations. The fixable-by-modeling headroom is small, and the work documents a measurement leak where file ordering encodes label information.

Abstract: We describe the SciTrue team's participation in both subtasks of the NTCIR-19 SciClaimEval task~\cite{sciclaimeval}, which asks systems to verify scientific claims against the tables and figures of a paper. Rather than tuning a single model, we benchmark eleven frontier and open multimodal models under one honest, per-sample protocol and combine them with light, transparent post-processing. On the official, blind test leaderboard (Section~\ref{sec:results}), SciTrue placed first by a clear margin in three of the four evidence-category/subtask combinations, and tied for first on the primary metric in the fourth. Three findings explain the result. First, strong instruction-tuned models are already competitive: Claude Opus~4.8 and Gemma-4-31B each exceed the strongest public baseline (o4-mini), and GPT-5.5 and Claude Fable~5 lead both subtasks (97.7 on Subtask~2). Second, the task's pairing structure is the largest lever: a \emph{leak-free pair prior} that recovers the Supported/Refuted pairing from the claim text alone (a visible field) and assigns Supported to the higher-confidence evidence raises Subtask-1 pair-accuracy from 72.2 to 93.5, far more than any model swap or ensemble weighting. Third, a case-by-case audit finds that most residual errors are visually-undetectable label-mapping swaps or dataset label noise, so measured accuracy understates the true ability and the fixable-by-modeling headroom is small. Controlled fine-tuning, distillation, and agentic consistency-checking support the same conclusions, and we document throughout a measurement leak---label information reaching a system through the packaging of the data rather than its content---in which the released file ordering encodes the label, including one instance that briefly misled our own pipeline.

</details>


### [61] [Drift-Aware LLM Routing with Sparse Contexts and Shared Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00662)
*Cheung Hao Lee,Patrick Wong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper addresses the problem of routing requests in a multi-model language service under multiple knapsack-style resource constraints (compute, latency, memory, cost) while handling nonstationarity from model drift and frontier evolution. The authors propose Drift-Aware Sparse Routing (DRS), a policy combining rolling-window estimation, pessimistic/optimistic reward-cost estimates, online shadow price updates, and hard pre-commitment metering. They provide rigorous regret bounds separating control from statistics, achieving stationary rate O(√(sT/ρ)) and a drift adaptation term O(T^{2/3}(s/ρ)^{1/3}V_T^{1/3}).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-model language services must route each request across models while satisfying workload-level budgets across compute, latency, memory, and cost. This is harder than static model selection because: (1) prompt embeddings are high-dimensional with only a sparse subset of directions predictive of incremental model value; (2) the request distribution and model capabilities drift over time due to launches, fine-tunes, quantization, and system updates. These challenges motivate the need for a routing policy that is both sparse-aware and drift-aware under resource constraints.

Method: The paper formulates nonstationary sparse contextual routing with multiple knapsack constraints and an optional shadow-audit stream. The proposed Drift-Aware Sparse Routing (DRS) policy: estimates reward and resource usage from a rolling audit window; routes decisions using pessimistic reward estimates and optimistic cost estimates; updates resource shadow prices online; and applies a hard meter before commitment. The theoretical analysis separates control from statistics: regret is decomposed using uniform prediction radii {β_t}, bounded by the sum of radii plus a capacity-buffer term and an O(√T) pacing term. Under a sparse linear model with bounded drift V_T, rolling estimation yields a regret bound with sparsity s, audit rate ρ, and window length W as key parameters.

Result: Under a sparse linear model with bounded drift V_T, the regret bound is Õ(T√(s/(ρW)) + WV_T + √T). Optimizing the window length W yields the stationary rate O(√(sT/ρ)) when there is no drift (V_T=0), and an adaptation term of O(T^{2/3}(s/ρ)^{1/3}V_T^{1/3}) in the presence of drift. These results demonstrate a principled trade-off between estimation accuracy (favored by longer windows) and adaptivity to drift (favored by shorter windows).

Conclusion: The paper provides a rigorous framework for drift-aware, budget-constrained routing in nonstationary multi-model language systems. By separating the statistical estimation from the control/optimization problem, DRS achieves provably optimal-looking regret rates in both stationary and drifting regimes, with the window length W serving as a tunable knob balancing estimation noise against adaptation to nonstationarity.

Abstract: A multi-model language service must route each request while preserving workload-level budgets for compute, latency, memory, or monetary cost. Two features make this problem materially harder than static model selection. Prompt representations are high dimensional, so only a small subset of embedding directions may predict the incremental value of a model, and both the request mix and the model frontier drift after launches, fine-tunes, quantization changes, and system updates. We formulate nonstationary sparse contextual routing with multiple knapsack constraints and an optional shadow-audit stream that evaluates a small fraction of prompts on several models.
  We propose Drift-Aware Sparse Routing (DRS). The policy estimates reward and resource use from a rolling audit window, routes using pessimistic reward and optimistic cost estimates, updates resource shadow prices online, and applies a hard meter before commitment. The analysis separates control from statistics. On any event with uniform prediction radii $\{β_t\}$, regret against a paced dynamic fluid benchmark is bounded by the sum of the radii, a capacity-buffer term, and an $O(\sqrt{T})$ pacing term. Under a sparse linear model and bounded drift $V_T$, rolling estimation gives \[ \widetilde O\left( T\sqrt{\frac{s}{ρW}}+WV_T+\sqrt{T} \right), \] where $s$ is sparsity, $ρ$ is the audit rate, and $W$ is the window length. Optimizing $W$ yields the usual stationary $O(\sqrt{sT/ρ})$ rate when $V_T=0$ and a $O(T^{2/3}(s/ρ)^{1/3}V_T^{1/3})$ adaptation term under drift.

</details>


### [62] [Triple-Bottom-Line Sustainability of Language Models for Edge AI: A Comparison Between SLMs and Quantized LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00665)
*Jainil Dharmil Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper challenges the assumption that natively trained small language models (SLMs) are always the most sustainable choice for edge deployment by introducing a Holistic Sustainability Score (HSS) based on the triple bottom line (economic, environmental, social). Evaluating 30 configurations of five SLMs and five LLMs under different quantization schemes, the authors find that optimized quantized LLMs (e.g., Qwen3-30B-A3B/GGUF Q4) can outperform native SLMs in sustainability, showing quantization is a systems-level choice rather than a simple precision-efficiency monotonic trade-off.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Edge-AI model selection is typically driven by a single isolated metric (accuracy, latency, memory, energy, or safety), yet a deployable language model must balance all five. The paper asks whether natively trained SLMs or quantized LLMs offer a more sustainable edge-deployment trade-off, aiming to provide a holistic, reproducible evaluation framework.

Method: The authors define a Holistic Sustainability Score (HSS) organized around the triple bottom line: an economic pillar (capability and systems efficiency), an environmental pillar (operational GPU energy), and a social pillar (harmful-prompt robustness). They evaluate five BF16 SLMs and five LLMs under five quantization approaches (BF16, INT8, NF4 4-bit, GPTQ 4-bit, GGUF Q4), yielding 30 configurations. Capability is tested on five zero-shot benchmarks, efficiency via latency/throughput/peak VRAM/energy, and safety via attack success rate on five harmful prompts.

Result: Qwen3-30B-A3B/GGUF Q4 ranks first overall (HSS=93.38), followed by Mistral-Small-24B/GGUF Q4 (92.40). Phi-4-mini/BF16 is the highest-ranked SLM (89.49). The hypothesis that native SLMs are universally the most sustainable edge choice is not supported; optimized quantized LLMs can win overall, while SLMs remain competitive through lower resource demand.

Conclusion: Quantization should be treated as a systems-level design choice rather than a monotonic precision-efficiency trade-off. The HSS framework is relative to its comparison pool and proxy definitions, and the results suggest that a blanket preference for native SLMs over quantized LLMs on edge deployments is unjustified.

Abstract: Edge-AI model selection is commonly driven by one isolated metric - accuracy, latency, memory, energy, or safety, even though a deployable language model must balance all five. Our work focuses on answering the question whether na- tively trained small language models (SLMs) or large language models (LLMs) compressed through post-training quantization offer the more sustainable edge- deployment trade-off. We introduce a reproducible Holistic Sustainability Score (HSS) organized around the triple bottom line: an economic pillar for capability and systems efficiency, an environmental pillar for operational GPU energy and a social pillar for harmful-prompt robustness. Five BF16 SLMs and five LLMs under different quantization approaches - BF16, INT8, NF4 4-bit, GPTQ 4-bit, and GGUF Q4 produce 30 measured configurations. Capability is assessed on five zero-shot benchmarks; efficiency uses latency, throughput, peak VRAM and energy; and safety is approximated by attack success rate on five harmful prompts. Qwen3-30B-A3B/GGUF Q4 ranks first in the combined pool (93.38), followed by Mistral-Small-24B/GGUF Q4 (92.40), while Phi-4-mini/BF16 is the highest- ranked SLM in that pool (89.49). Thus, the hypothesis that native SLMs must be the most sustainable edge choice is not supported universally; optimized quantized LLMs can win overall, while SLMs remain competitive through lower resource demand. Quantization is a systems-level choice rather than a monotonic precision- efficiency trade-off and HSS remains relative to its comparison pool and proxy definitions.

</details>


### [63] [Value Over Language Model: Detecting Original Contribution in Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00700)
*Vibhhu Sharma,Thorsten Joachims,Sarah Dean*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces VOLM (Value Over Language Model), a training-free framework that measures the informational value a human adds beyond what an LLM could have produced from the task description alone. Rather than scoring surface text, VOLM extracts content at multiple granularities, reconstructs the document from partial representations via an LLM, and compares these reconstructions against those generated from the task description alone. It is shown to separate human-authored from LLM-generated documents while being robust to content-preserving transformations across three domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-detection tools only measure how much of a document's surface text was written by an LLM, but they do not assess how much of the underlying information content or ideas originated from the LLM versus being supplied by the user. As LLM-assisted writing becomes widespread, there is a need to measure human value addition at the level of ideas and information, not merely textual form.

Method: VOLM requires no training or labeled data and never scores the surface text. It extracts the document's content at progressively finer levels of granularity, uses an LLM to reconstruct the document from each partial representation, and compares these reconstructions with those produced from the task description alone. This measures a document's contribution relative to a replacement-level document that an LLM could generate from just the task description.

Result: VOLM is evaluated across three domains (news articles, ICLR peer reviews, argumentative essays). It successfully separates human-authored documents from matched LLM-generated documents produced from generic task descriptions while remaining substantially invariant to content-preserving transformations such as LLM-based reconstruction and round-trip translation. Increasingly constrained content extractors reduce residual differences between LLM-generated and humanized text, showing the importance of separating informational content from stylistic variation.

Conclusion: The framework provides a principled way to assess human contributions in LLM-assisted writing by operating at the level of information content rather than surface style. The authors hope these results encourage further work on specialized instantiations of the framework and broader assessment of human value in LLM-assisted writing.

Abstract: LLMs have been rapidly adopted across writing tasks, prompting the development of tools for detecting LLM-generated text. Yet, these tools largely measure how much of a document's surface text was written by an LLM and aren't fundamentally designed to measure how much of the information content or ideas originated from the LLM itself rather than being supplied by the user in the prompt. In this work, we design a framework that measures how much value a person adds on top of what a language model could have easily produced by itself. The method requires no training or labeled data and never scores the document's surface text, insulating it from stylistic confounders. Instead, it extracts the document's content at increasing levels of granularity, uses an LLM to reconstruct the document from each partial representation, and compares these reconstructions with those produced from the task description alone. We call this framework Value Over Language Model (VOLM), which measures a document's contribution relative to a replacement-level document that an LLM could produce from the task description alone. We evaluate VOLM with a specific instantiation of this framework across three domains: news articles, ICLR peer reviews, and argumentative essays. VOLM separates human-authored documents from matched LLM-generated documents produced from generic task descriptions, while remaining substantially invariant to content-preserving transformations, including LLM-based reconstruction and round-trip translation. We further find that increasingly constrained content extractors reduce residual differences between LLM-generated and humanized text, demonstrating the importance of disentangling informational content from stylistic variation. We hope these results encourage further work on specialized instantiations of the framework and on assessing human contributions in LLM-assisted writing more generally.

</details>


### [64] [ChatDev 2.0: A No-Code Multi-Agent Platform for Developing Everything](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00714)
*Yufan Dang,Shu Yao,Bowen Lai,Chenting Xu,Ruijie Shi,Wai-Shing Leung,Huatao Li,Chen Qian,Zhiyuan Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ChatDev 2.0 (DevAll) is a no-code platform for building, executing, and inspecting heterogeneous LLM-based multi-agent systems, pairing a declarative executable graph abstraction with a cycle-aware execution engine and an integrated visual interface to achieve both high expressiveness and ease of use.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based MAS development tools force a tradeoff: code frameworks are expressive but engineering-intensive, while no-code builders simplify authoring but constrain agent interactions to author-defined workflows. The goal is to overcome this dichotomy by providing a platform that delivers both high expressiveness and ease of use.

Method: DevAll introduces a no-code platform that pairs a declarative executable graph abstraction with a cycle-aware execution engine, enabling the representation and execution of heterogeneous agents and dynamic, cyclic interactions within a single framework. An integrated visual interface allows users to author, run, monitor, and inspect MAS—including human-in-the-loop steps—without writing any code.

Result: Experiments show that DevAll reproduces state-of-the-art MAS across three representative tasks at competitive performance and without task-specific orchestration code, demonstrating its effectiveness as a general-purpose platform.

Conclusion: DevAll proves to be a general-purpose, no-code platform for LLM-based MAS that balances expressiveness with usability, offering a viable alternative to existing code-centric and workflow-constrained frameworks.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) have shown strong potential for solving complex tasks, yet their development forces a tradeoff: code frameworks are expressive but engineering-intensive, while no-code builders simplify authoring but constrain agent interactions to author-defined workflows. We present ChatDev 2.0: DevAll (hereafter DevAll), a no-code platform for building, executing, and inspecting heterogeneous MAS that delivers both high expressiveness and ease of use. In terms of expressiveness, DevAll pairs a declarative executable graph abstraction with a cycle-aware execution engine, so that heterogeneous agents and dynamic and cyclic interactions can be represented and executed within a single framework. For ease of use, an integrated visual interface lets users author, run, monitor, and inspect MAS, including human-in-the-loop steps, entirely without writing code. Experiments demonstrate that DevAll reproduces state-of-the-art MAS across three representative tasks at competitive performance and without task-specific orchestration code, highlighting its effectiveness as a general-purpose platform for LLM-based MAS. DevAll is available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/ChatDev.

</details>


### [65] [A Closed-Loop Evaluation of Capability Loss and Recovery in Compressed Driving Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00718)
*Ahmad Alfan Alfian Irfan,Nur Ahmad Khatim,Mansur Arief*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a stage-wise closed-loop evaluation approach for compressed driving policies, showing that structured pruning is where driving capability is first lost, distillation provides limited recovery, and integer quantization degrades stop-and-resume behaviors—yet quantization preserves all curricula when applied to an unpruned actor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard compression methods for learned driving policies (pruning, distillation, quantization) are typically assessed using aggregate numerical scores that may not capture the policy's actual ability to drive safely when interacting with other road users in closed-loop settings.

Method: The driving task is formulated as a POMDP, a belief-state policy is trained with PPO in Gym-Duckietown, the actor is extracted and compressed stage-by-stage (pruning, distillation, and integer quantization), with each compressed stage evaluated on five distinct driving curricula.

Result: Structured pruning is the compression stage where driving capability is first lost. Distillation improves the pruned actor but is limited by rehearsal data. Integer quantization of the improved actor loses curricula requiring stop-and-resume behavior, while the same quantization on an unpruned actor preserves all five curricula.
