Conclusion: The study provides an empirical analysis addressing how to accept compressed driving policies for deployment, emphasizing the need for closed-loop, curriculum-based evaluation to achieve safe and statistically reliable automated driving.

Abstract: Many automobile and mobility companies deploy learned driving policies on embedded computers with limited memory and power. Pruning, knowledge distillation, and quantization are the standard methods to reduce the size and the inference cost of these policies. However, these methods are commonly assessed by aggregate numerical scores, and such scores may not reflect the ability of the policy to drive safely when interacting with other road users. In this study, we propose a stage-wise closed-loop evaluation approach to follow a driving policy through a compression pipeline. We formulate the driving task as a partially observable Markov decision process (POMDP) and train a belief-state policy with proximal policy optimization (PPO) in Gym-Duckietown. We then extract the actor, compress it one stage at a time, and evaluate it on five driving curricula. We show that structured pruning is the stage at which the driving capability is first lost. Meanwhile, distillation improves the pruned actor, but the improvement is limited by its rehearsal data. Integer quantization of the improved actor loses some of the curricula that require the vehicle to stop and then resume. Interestingly, the same procedure on the unpruned actor preserves all five curricula. Our study thus provides an empirical analysis aiming to answer the currently active discussions on how to accept a compressed driving policy, so as to achieve a safe and statistically reliable deployment of automated driving functions.

</details>


### [66] [SOVER: Formal Certification of Optimization Reformulations via LLM-Assisted SMT Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00728)
*Swapnil Bhattacharyya,Mayank Baranwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SOVER is an LLM-assisted SMT framework for certifying equivalence of mathematical optimization reformulations, using Z3 for mixed-integer linear checks and dReal for continuous nonlinear checks, achieving 99.33% classification accuracy on a new NLEquiv-150 benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Validating LLM-generated translations/reformulations of optimization problems through empirical solver runs alone is unreliable due to local minima, timeouts, numerical artifacts, and subtle semantic divergences between formulations. A formal certification approach is needed.

Method: SOVER separates semantic mapping from formal certification: it uses LLMs to extract semantic mappings between formulations, then employs Z3 to check domain cross-feasibility and global objective-order preservation for mixed-integer linear programs, and dReal for tolerance-aware feasibility/range and ε-argmin checks on continuous nonlinear formulations. A new benchmark NLEquiv-150 is introduced with 100 equivalent and 50 hard non-equivalent reformulation pairs.

Result: With LLM-extracted mappings, SOVER correctly classifies 149/150 pairs (99.33%), including all 50 hard negative cases, with the single error attributed to incomplete mapping extraction.

Conclusion: SOVER demonstrates that formal SMT-based certification, combined with LLM semantic mapping extraction, provides a highly reliable approach for validating optimization reformulations, far surpassing empirical solver-based validation alone.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable promise in translating and reformulating complex mathematical optimization problems across modeling languages. However, validating such transformations through empirical solver executions alone is unreliable, as solver outcomes may be affected by local minima, structural timeouts, numerical artifacts, and subtle semantic divergence between formulations. We introduce SOVER, an LLM-assisted SMT framework that separates semantic mapping from formal certification: Z3 checks domain cross-feasibility and global objective-order preservation for mixed-integer linear formulations, while dReal provides tolerance-aware feasibility/range and $ε$-argmin checks for continuous nonlinear formulations. We also introduce NLEquiv-150, a public benchmark of 100 equivalent and 50 deliberately hard non-equivalent nonlinear reformulation pairs. With LLM-extracted mappings, SOVER classifies 149/150 pairs (99.33%) correctly, including all 50 hard negatives; the sole error is an incomplete mapping extraction.

</details>


### [67] [Agentic Empirical Asset Pricing: Methodological Foundations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00731)
*Yingjian Pan,Xiaowei Ding,Kay Giesecke*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Agentic Empirical Asset Pricing (AEAP), a paradigm where LLM agents autonomously conduct the scientific discovery process for asset pricing. It provides a reference architecture for factor discovery, a rigorous evaluation standard, and an out-of-sample backtesting method for the discovery system itself. Using SEADS against five baselines on US equity panels, results show no single metric ranks systems consistently across all dimensions, while a rolling re-execution approach evaluates the reliability of the discovery process rather than static outputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation practices for LLM-based asset pricing systems backtest only the outputs (factors or trades) rather than the autonomous discovery systems themselves. A rigorous framework is needed to evaluate the reliability of the entire discovery process, not just its static outputs.

Method: The authors define AEAP and its core building blocks, contribute a reference architecture for factor discovery, propose a rigorous evaluation standard for discovered factors, and develop a method for out-of-sample backtesting the discovery system. They evaluate SEADS against five re-implemented baselines on two US equity panels using this multi-axis standard, and perform a rolling re-execution to assess process reliability.

Result: No single metric ranks the systems consistently across evaluation axes, motivating the need for multi-dimensional evaluation. The rolling re-execution reveals complementary insights about the reliability of the discovery process. Negative findings and limitations are reported, surfacing additional evaluation pitfalls for future AEAP systems.

Conclusion: Evaluation of AEAP systems must extend beyond output backtesting to assess the autonomous discovery process itself on multiple axes simultaneously, as no single metric adequately ranks systems. This work lays groundwork for more robust assessment standards in agentic asset pricing research.

Abstract: Recent advances in LLM agents enable a new paradigm for asset pricing, which we call Agentic Empirical Asset Pricing (AEAP): systems that autonomously conduct the scientific discovery process itself. We define AEAP and identify its core building blocks. Existing evaluation practices backtest only the outputs (factors or trades), not the autonomous discovery system that produced them. We focus on factor discovery, contributing a reference architecture, a rigorous evaluation standard for discovered factors, and a method for out-of-sample backtesting the discovery system. As a concrete instance of that architecture, we evaluate SEADS against five re-implemented baselines on two US equity panels using this standard: no single metric ranks the systems consistently, motivating evaluation on multiple axes at once. A separate rolling re-execution then asks the complementary question of whether the discovery process itself, not one static output, is reliable. We also report negative findings and limitations that surface further evaluation pitfalls for future AEAP systems.

</details>


### [68] [Escaping Redundant Reasoning: Structure-Aware Search for Inference-Time LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00738)
*Lu Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BASIN is a training-free, structure-aware selection method that mitigates "reasoning basin collapse"—the tendency of inference-time search in LLMs to over-concentrate on similar trajectories—by grouping reasoning states into basins and penalizing repeated visits to the same strategy. It improves over Tree of Thoughts by up to +22pp on Game of 24 and +6.7pp on MuSR, with a quality-aware variant (QA-BASIN) preserving high-quality basins for better robustness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference-time search with LLMs often collapses onto a small set of structurally or semantically similar trajectories, leaving alternative reasoning paths underexplored. This failure mode, termed "reasoning basin collapse," limits the effectiveness of fixed-compute search and motivates the need for diversity-aware selection.

Method: BASIN groups reasoning states into basins and penalizes repeated visits to the same strategy, reallocating search compute across genuinely distinct reasoning paths without any training. A quality-aware variant, QA-BASIN, selectively preserves high-quality basins to avoid over-exploration from unconditional diversification. The paper also formalizes the "redundancy gap" Δ, a metric measuring how differently search concentrates for correct versus incorrect predictions.

Result: Under matched inference budgets, BASIN improves over Tree of Thoughts (ToT) by up to +22 percentage points on Game of 24 and +6.7 points on MuSR. Standard ToT often operates near Δ≈0, while BASIN consistently shifts Δ positive, providing interpretable evidence for why basin-aware selection helps.

Conclusion: Structure-aware selection is a simple, training-free, and general approach to improving inference-time reasoning in LLMs. BASIN and QA-BASIN demonstrate that explicitly diversifying across reasoning basins yields substantial gains under fixed compute, while the redundancy gap provides a diagnostic lens for understanding when such diversification is beneficial.

Abstract: Inference-time search with large language models (LLMs) often concentrates on a small set of structurally or semantically similar trajectories, leaving alternatives underexplored---a failure mode we call \textit{reasoning basin collapse}. We introduce BASIN, a training-free, structure-aware selection method that groups reasoning states into basins and penalizes repeated visits to the same strategy, thereby reallocating search across genuinely distinct reasoning paths under a fixed compute budget. Under matched inference budgets, BASIN improves over Tree of Thoughts (ToT) by up to $+22$pp on Game of 24 and $+6.7$pp on MuSR. A quality-aware variant, QA-BASIN, further improves robustness by preserving high-quality basins when unconditional diversification over-explores. To explain when basin-aware selection helps, we introduce the redundancy gap $Δ$, which measures how differently search concentrates for correct versus incorrect predictions: standard ToT often operates near $Δ\approx 0$, while BASIN consistently shifts $Δ$ positive. More broadly, BASIN suggests structure-aware selection as a simple and general approach to improving inference-time reasoning. Code can be found at https://github.com/GitHubLuCheng/basin.

</details>


### [69] [ContextPipe: Database-Inspired Context Assembly for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00749)
*Peng Xu,Zuyu Zhang,Yuze Sun,Feng Tian,Long Wang,Chen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ContextPipe, a framework that treats context assembly in long-horizon LLM agents as a relational database query execution problem, implementing a five-phase pipeline (Plan, Bind, Optimize, Execute, Feedback) with structured source catalogs, a cache-aware optimizer, and EXPLAIN ANALYZE tracing. Compared to append-only context construction on SWE-bench Pro Qutebrowser, it reduces tokens by 31%, LLM calls by 23%, and response time by 9%, at the cost of a lower KV cache hit ratio.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Context assembly logic in production LLM agents is ad hoc and fragmented across prompt builders, compaction routines, cache-break workarounds, and provider-specific shims. The authors argue this unstructured approach leads to inefficiencies and a lack of auditability under hard context-window budgets and byte-sensitive prompt caches, motivating a principled, disciplined framework.

Method: ContextPipe adopts a database-query-execution lens, noting structural isomorphism between context assembly and relational query processing (hard budget constraints, tiered caches, statistics-driven optimization). It implements a five-phase pipeline: Plan, Bind, Optimize, Execute, Feedback, backed by a structured data-source catalog, a deterministic cache-aware optimizer, and an EXPLAIN ANALYZE trace for observability.

Result: Preliminary evaluation on the SWE-bench Pro Qutebrowser subset shows ContextPipe reduces total token volume by 31%, LLM calls by 23%, and response time by 9% compared to append-only context construction, though at the cost of a lower KV cache-hit ratio.

Conclusion: ContextPipe establishes that applying relational database query-processing discipline to context assembly yields measurable efficiency gains while making context auditable, replayable, and failure-isolated. The trade-off in KV cache-hit ratio is acknowledged as a cost of this approach.

Abstract: Long-horizon large language model (LLM) agents require context assembly: the runtime must decide what to include in each prompt, in what order, and when to compact history under a hard context-window budget and a byte-sensitive prompt cache. In production agentic systems, this logic is scattered across prompt builders, ad hoc compaction routines, cache-break workarounds, and per-provider shims. We argue that context assembly is structurally isomorphic to query execution in a relational database: both execute under a hard budget, exploit a tiered cache, and leverage statistics. We adopt this discipline in ContextPipe: a five-phase pipeline (Plan Bind Optimize Execute Feedback) backed by a structured data-source catalog, a deterministic cache-aware optimizer, and an EXPLAIN ANALYZE trace. We show that context in ContextPipe is auditable, replayable, and failure-isolated. A preliminary evaluation using the SWE-bench Pro Qutebrowser subset shows that, compared with the append-only context construction policy, ContextPipe reduces total token volume by 31%, LLM calls by 23%, and response time by 9%, at the cost of a lower KV cache-hit ratio.

</details>


### [70] [S^3martCirc: Self-supervised Smart Circuit Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00755)
*Wendy Zheng,Yinhan He,Liang Wu,Jundong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: S^3martCirc is a unified framework for mechanistic interpretability that simultaneously discovers circuits and interprets their functional roles in Large Language Models, replacing the traditional two-stage sequential paradigm with a joint discovery approach.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current mechanistic interpretability approaches for LLMs follow a sequential two-stage paradigm—first identifying important circuit components, then interpreting their functional roles—which overlooks the fundamental codependency between a component's importance and its function. Additionally, existing functional interpretation relies on subjective judgment rather than quantifiable metrics, and roles are often tied to specific nodes limiting generalization.

Method: S^3martCirc abstracts node behavior into two general computational roles that generalize across tasks and defines a quantitative metric for assigning them. This enables importance and functional role to be discovered jointly rather than in sequence, unifying circuit discovery and functional interpretation under a self-supervised framework.

Result: Extensive experiments demonstrate that S^3martCirc outperforms existing methods in circuit discovery.

Conclusion: By unifying circuit discovery and functional interpretation into a single framework with quantifiable, generalizable computational roles, S^3martCirc addresses key limitations of sequential MI approaches and achieves superior circuit discovery performance.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable performance across diverse tasks, from text summarization to question answering. Despite these capabilities, their black-box nature obscures internal decision-making processes. Mechanistic interpretability (MI) aims to address this by reverse-engineering neural networks into human-understandable algorithms. Current MI approaches for LLMs typically follow a two-stage paradigm: first identifying important components (circuit discovery), where components are typically individual nodes such as an attention head or feedforward neuron, and second determining the role they play in a certain task (functional interpretation). However, this sequential approach overlooks a fundamental insight: a component's importance and its functional role are inherently codependent. Unifying these stages presents two key challenges: (1) functional roles are often tied to specific nodes or components, limiting generalization, and (2) their identification relies on subjective interpretation rather than quantifiable metrics. To address these challenges, we propose S^3martCirc (Self-supervised Smart Circuit Discovery), a unified framework that simultaneously discovers circuits and interprets functionality. S^3martCirc abstracts node behavior into two general computational roles that generalize across tasks and defines a quantitative metric for assigning them, enabling importance and functional role to be discovered jointly rather than in sequence. Extensive experiments show that our framework outperforms existing methods in circuit discovery.

</details>


### [71] [Automated Tree Knowledge Graph Construction using Ontology Expansion and Retrieval from Vietnamese History Textbooks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00763)
*Ket Doan Nguyen,Minh N. H. Nguyen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes an end-to-end pipeline for hierarchical KG construction from low-resource language documents and systematic evaluation of graph traversal strategies for KG-based RAG. A three-phase hybrid relation extraction with Union-Find deduplication, approximate cross-batch search, and dual-LLM validation builds a two-tier tree KG from Vietnamese high school History textbooks (750 nodes, 4,341 edges). Three graph traversal strategies (Top-Down, Horizontal, Bottom-Up) are evaluated on 1,210 synthetic Vietnamese queries, showing Top-Down with structure outperforms vector baseline by 4.7 NDCG@10 points, though structural information can degrade retrieval when queries lack structural context.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing KG-based RAG methods lack automatic KG construction via ontology expansion for low-resource languages (e.g., Vietnamese) and lack systematic evaluation of hierarchical knowledge retrieval strategies, motivating an end-to-end pipeline addressing both gaps.

Method: The method comprises: (1) a three-phase hybrid relation extraction pipeline including intra-batch deduplication via Union-Find, approximate cross-batch search, LLM extraction with a centroid filter for prompt reduction, and a five-step dual-LLM validator to control ontology bloat; (2) a two-tier architecture separating unmergeable structural nodes (preserving document hierarchy) from mergeable content nodes; and (3) evaluation of three graph traversal strategies—Top-Down, Horizontal, and Bottom-Up—on a synthetically generated benchmark of 1,210 Vietnamese queries from 109 subgraphs across five query directions.

Result: The constructed tree KG from Vietnamese high school History textbooks (~400 pages) contains 750 nodes and 4,341 semantic edges, with ontology growth tightly controlled from 40 to 41 types. The Top-Down traversal with structure beats the vector baseline by 4.7 percentage points in NDCG@10; however, structural traversal degrades performance on queries that do not require structural context.

Conclusion: Tree-structural information in hierarchical KG-based RAG provides value beyond flat cosine similarity for retrieval, but the benefit is conditional—structural context helps when queries require it and can hurt when they do not. The proposed pipeline enables automatic KG construction and benchmarking for low-resource language settings.

Abstract: Hierarchical Knowledge graph (KG)-based retrieval augmented generation (RAG) has emerged as a powerful approach for supporting large language models with structured knowledge. However, there are primary challenges: (i) the lack of methods for automatic KG construction using ontology expansion for low-resource languages such as Vietnamese, (ii) the absence of systematic evaluation for knowledge retrieval strategies leveraging the hierarchical structures. In this paper, we propose an end-to-end pipeline for KG construction and retrieval strategies evaluation. In the KG construction, we employ a three-phase hybrid relation extraction pipeline: intra-batch deduplication via Union-Find, approximate cross-batch search, and LLM extraction with a centroid filter that reduces prompts combined with a five-step dual-LLM validator to prevent bloated ontology. A two-tier architecture consists of unmergeable structural nodes to preserve the document structure and mergeable content nodes. The retrieval evaluation consists of three graph traversal strategies: Top-Down, Horizontal, and Bottom-Up, which are evaluated on a synthetically generated benchmark of 1,210 Vietnamese queries from 109 subgraphs, categorized by five query directions. In this paper, we construct the tree knowledge graph from Vietnamese high school History textbooks (nearly 400 pages) to produce 750 nodes and 4,341 semantic edges with controlled ontology growth from 40 to 41 types. Among experimental graph traversal strategies, the Top-Down strategy with structure surpasses the vector baseline by 4.7 percentage points in NDCG@10. As a result, tree-structural information provides valuable information beyond flat cosine similarity but degrades performance when the query does not require structural context.

</details>


### [72] [DiagEvo: Diagnosis-Guided Self-Evolution via Hierarchical Error Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00768)
*Xincheng Wei,Yifan Ding,Yoshua Li,Dongsheng Ma,Rongxiang Weng,Xunliang Cai,Wenjian Ding,Yao Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DiagEvo is a self-play framework for language-model self-evolution that derives curriculum direction from the solver's own failure history rather than external task resources. A diagnostician extracts recurring error causes into a hierarchical error-cause memory, tracking skills as Active or Mastered, while a challenger balances targeted generation with exploration. It consistently outperforms baselines across nine benchmarks with three different solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Self-play self-evolution can plateau without guidance. Existing unguided methods (difficulty, learnability, diversity) keep questions challenging but don't specify which unresolved reasoning weaknesses to target. Guided methods rely on external task resources outside the self-play loop. The authors show direction can be derived from the solver's own failure history instead.

Method: DiagEvo introduces a diagnostician that extracts recurring error causes from the solver's failure history and stores them in a hierarchical error-cause memory. Related causes are grouped under skill nodes, each tracked as Active or Mastered based on self-consistency on targeted questions. A challenger uses these states and recurrence counts to balance cause-targeted question generation with free exploration. Double-confidence filtering retains intermediate-difficulty questions only when the most common solver answer has a clear vote lead.

Result: With a default 4B diagnostician, DiagEvo outperforms all baselines in mean accuracy across nine benchmarks for all three solvers (Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, OctoThinker-8B). On Qwen3-8B it achieves 72.3% mean accuracy on five math benchmarks (4.5 points above R-Zero) and 57.4% across all nine benchmarks (1.1 points above DARC). Ablations confirm both the hierarchical error-cause memory and double-confidence filtering contribute to gains.

Conclusion: The needed curriculum direction for self-play can be derived entirely from the solver's own failure history without external task resources. The hierarchical error-cause memory and double-confidence filtering are both key components driving the observed improvements.

Abstract: Self-play is an effective paradigm for language-model self-evolution, but without guidance, solver performance can plateau or decline across rounds. Unguided methods steer question generation with signals such as difficulty, learnability, or diversity. These signals keep questions challenging and varied but do not specify which unresolved reasoning weaknesses later rounds should target. Guided methods obtain direction from external task resources, including human examples, document corpora, or specified difficulty targets, and therefore rely on task information supplied outside the self-play loop. We show that the needed direction can instead be derived from the solver's own failure history. We introduce DiagEvo, whose diagnostician extracts recurring error causes from this history and stores them in a hierarchical error-cause memory. The memory groups related causes under skill nodes and tracks each as Active or Mastered according to self-consistency on targeted questions. The challenger uses these states and recurrence counts to balance cause-targeted generation with free exploration. Double-confidence filtering retains intermediate-difficulty questions only when the most common solver answer has a clear vote lead. DiagEvo derives its curriculum from information produced during self-play, without external task resources. With the default 4B diagnostician, DiagEvo outperforms every baseline in mean accuracy across all nine benchmarks for each of the three solvers: Qwen3-4B, Qwen3-8B, and OctoThinker-8B. On Qwen3-8B, it reaches 72.3% mean accuracy across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, 4.5 percentage points above R-Zero. Its mean accuracy across all nine benchmarks is 57.4%, 1.1 percentage points above DARC. Ablations show that the hierarchical error-cause memory and double-confidence filtering both contribute to these gains.

</details>


### [73] [When Features Become Instances: Inverted Contrastive Learning for Unsupervised Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00782)
*Utsab Ghosh,Roshni Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICLFS is a novel unsupervised feature selection method that inverts the data matrix to treat features as instances, applies contrastive learning with masked positive views and a shuffled negative view, and uses projector-space embedding magnitude (norm) as a saliency signal for feature ranking, refined by a Laplacian-Gated Ranking Correction. It achieves the best clustering accuracy on 10 of 12 benchmark datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical UFS methods rely on indirect structural criteria (similarity, locality, sparsity, cluster geometry, reconstruction) because feature utility is hard to define without class labels. The authors seek a stronger alternative by viewing UFS through the lens of representation consistency.

Method: ICLFS transposes the data matrix so each feature is represented by a sample-profile vector; builds multiple masked positive views and a shuffled negative view; trains projector-space representations under an InfoNCE objective; uses embedding magnitude (norm) in projector space as saliency for ranking features; applies Laplacian-Gated Ranking Correction to suppress locally redundant features while preserving salient ones.

Result: On 12 benchmark datasets under the standard clustering-based UFS evaluation protocol, ICLFS achieves the best clustering accuracy on 10 datasets against both classical and neural baselines, and remains competitive on the remaining two.

Conclusion: Feature-wise contrastive representation consistency is a strong and effective alternative to neighborhood-, cluster-, and reconstruction-based UFS formulations.

Abstract: Unsupervised feature selection seeks a compact subset of informative features without access to class labels, making feature utility difficult to define. Existing UFS methods therefore rely on indirect structural criteria, such as similarity preservation, locality, sparsity, cluster geometry, or reconstruction quality. In this paper, we instead study UFS through representation consistency and propose Inverted Contrastive Learning for Unsupervised Feature Selection (ICLFS), a feature-wise contrastive framework that reformulates UFS as a representation learning problem over features rather than samples. ICLFS first inverts the data matrix so that each feature is represented by its sample-profile vector, then constructs multiple masked positive views together with a shuffled negative view, and learns projector-space representations that remain consistent across these structured perturbations under an InfoNCE-based objective. Motivated by recent findings that cosine-based and InfoNCE-based training affect embedding norms, we use projector-space embedding magnitude as the saliency signal for ranking features. The resulting norm-based ranking is subsequently refined through Laplacian-Gated Ranking Correction, which suppresses locally redundant candidates while preserving salient ones. Extensive experiments on 12 benchmark datasets show that ICLFS achieves the best clustering accuracy on 10 datasets against both classical and neural baselines under the standard clustering-based UFS evaluation protocol, while remaining competitive on the other two. These results show that feature-wise contrastive representation consistency provides a strong and effective alternative to neighborhood, cluster, and reconstruction-based UFS formulations.

</details>


### [74] [StudyBench: Can Self-Evolution Squeeze Textbooks for Olympiad Capability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00787)
*Yinghao Chen,Zixi Chen,Bingxiang He,Ziqing Qiao,Huan-ang Gao,Yinuo Xu,Yuxin Zuo,Zeyuan Liu,Yuhao Zhan,Chaojun Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: StudyBench is a controlled physics benchmark that directly measures how efficiently self-evolution methods convert training material into problem-solving capability. Benchmarking reveals that improvements on application-level problems rarely transfer to harder olympiad-level problems, a "Guidance Gap" shows methods lag far behind in-context guidance, and all methods hit a "Compute Plateau" before exhausting compute. The residual gap is thus a method problem, not a data or compute problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors argue that an ideal self-evolution method should mirror human learning—mastering a discipline from only a handful of well-written textbooks—but there is no direct measurement for how efficiently such methods convert raw training material into transferable problem-solving capability.

Method: The authors introduce StudyBench, a controlled physics benchmark with two test sets: an Application Set (difficult textbook problems measuring absorption ability) and a Transfer Set (olympiad-level problems measuring transfer ability). They benchmark representative self-evolution methods across three base models and conduct a guidance ablation to compare method performance against in-context guidance.

Result: Benchmarking shows that improvements on the Application Set rarely translate to the harder Transfer Set. The guidance ablation reveals a "Guidance Gap"—even the strongest method closes only a small fraction of what the same material unlocks when provided as in-context guidance. Additionally, every method hits a "Compute Plateau," saturating well before exhausting its compute budget.

Conclusion: The remaining performance gap is a method problem rather than a data or compute problem. StudyBench provides a clean, controlled benchmark that makes self-evolution progress a measurable, quantifiable target for future research.

Abstract: Humans need to study only a handful of well-written textbooks to master a discipline and attempt its hardest problems. We argue that an ideal self-evolution method should share the same property, that is autonomously learning from raw training material for transferable problem-solving capability. However, we still lack a direct measurement for it. We introduce StudyBench, a controlled physics benchmark that directly measures how efficiently a self-evolution method converts training material into capability. We organise the test set into an Application Set, consisting of difficult textbook problems and evaluating absorption ability, and a Transfer Set, consisting of olympiad-level problems and evaluating transfer ability. Benchmarking representative self-evolution methods across three base models, we find that improvements on the Application Set rarely translate to the harder Transfer Set. A guidance ablation exposes a Guidance Gap: even the strongest method closes only a small fraction of what the same material unlocks when supplied as in-context guidance. Besides, every method hits a Compute Plateau, saturating well before exhausting its compute budget. The remaining gap is therefore a method problem rather than a data or compute problem. By offering a clean and controlled benchmark, StudyBench turns self-evolution progress from an open-ended pursuit into a measurable target for future research. Our code is released at https://github.com/thunlp/StudyBench.

</details>


### [75] [Towards a Reliable and Practical Eval Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00805)
*Emma Thuong Nguyen,Abhishek Ghose*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Presents an end-to-end evaluation pipeline for LLM-based software systems that combines eval checklist creation with learned aggregation to improve agreement across LLM judges and accuracy against human judgments, while also providing self-consistency, explanations, and prediction uncertainty.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Effective evals are needed as quality gates in LLM software development, but existing work addresses only isolated aspects of eval reliability rather than the full set of practical requirements.

Method: An end-to-end eval pipeline that (1) creates eval checklists and (2) uses learned aggregation for checklist responses, designed to improve inter-judge agreement and alignment with human judgments.

Result: Empirical demonstrations show the framework improves agreement across LLM judges and accuracy against human judgments, while additionally providing self-consistency, explanations, and prediction uncertainty.

Conclusion: A unified eval pipeline addressing multiple reliability dimensions simultaneously offers a more practical and effective approach to evaluating LLM-based software systems than fragmented existing methods.

Abstract: LLM-based software systems increasingly require effective "evals" as quality gates in the development lifecycle. However, existing work typically addresses individual aspects of eval reliability rather than the full set of practical requirements. We present an end-to-end eval pipeline that combines eval checklist creation, with learned aggregation for checklist responses, to improve agreement across LLM judges and accuracy against human judgments. The framework additionally pro- vides self-consistency, explanations, and prediction uncertainty, and we empirically demonstrate its effectiveness.

</details>


### [76] [One Policy, Any Budget: Internalizing Budget-Aware Search via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00813)
*Xiaowei Sun,Jin Li,Yili Hong,Yikun Fu,Yanghua Xiao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnySearch is a framework enabling LLM-based search agents to adapt to varying budget constraints at deployment through two-phase training: first with budget state injection and structured reasoning prompts under linearly decaying budgets, then scaffold-free training under adaptively sampled constraints. A composite reward couples accuracy with budget efficiency using an adaptive weighting mechanism.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL-based LLM search agents are trained under fixed budgets and cannot adapt when budget constraints vary at deployment time.

Method: AnySearch uses a two-phase training approach: (1) Phase 1 trains with explicit budget state injection and structured reasoning prompts guiding efficient allocation under linearly decaying budgets; (2) Phase 2 removes the scaffold and trains with adaptively sampled budget constraints to match inference conditions. Both phases optimize a composite reward combining answer accuracy with budget efficiency through absolute and relative signals, where an adaptive weight amplifies efficiency signals for high-accuracy queries and attenuates them for low-accuracy ones.

Result: Experiments on seven general and multi-hop QA benchmarks show AnySearch outperforms baselines across all budget scales, generalizes to unseen constraints beyond training range, and achieves superior tool productivity without excessive token overhead.

Conclusion: Budget-aware search policies can be trained to generalize across varying constraints at deployment through curriculum RL with scaffold training and adaptive reward weighting, eliminating the need for retraining per budget.

Abstract: While reinforcement learning has enabled LLM-based search agents to invoke external tools, existing methods train under fixed budgets and cannot adapt when constraints vary at deployment. We propose AnySearch, a framework that enables a single policy to perform budget-aware search under any budget constraint through a training scaffold and curriculum reinforcement learning. In the first phase, we train the agent with explicit budget state injection and structured reasoning prompts that guide efficient allocation under linearly decaying budgets. In the second phase, the scaffold is removed and the agent learns to operate autonomously under adaptively sampled budget constraints, matching inference conditions. Both phases are optimized with a composite reward that couples answer accuracy with budget efficiency through absolute and relative signals, where an adaptive weight amplifies the efficiency signal for high-accuracy queries and attenuates it for low-accuracy ones. Extensive experiments on seven general and multi-hop QA benchmarks show that our method outperforms baselines across all budget scales, generalizes to unseen constraints beyond the training range, and achieves superior tool productivity without excessive token overhead. Our code is available at https://github.com/xwsun01/AnySearch.

</details>


### [77] [AnalysisBank: An Expert Analysis Pattern Library for Financial Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00818)
*Yajing Yang,Yunshan Ma,Kelvin J. L. Koa,Min-Yen Kan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AnalysisBank is a framework for analytical-level financial report generation that distills expert reports into a reusable library of "Analyses" (each pairing a data signal, an analytical move, and expert span), then composes reports by retrieving and applying matched moves at inference time. It increases novel, data-grounded insights by 1.7-3.7x over structural-level baselines across LLM backbones and financial benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Financial report generation should be composed from data-derived analytical insights rather than high-level topics or structural sections, as current structural-level approaches fail to produce novel, data-grounded content.

Method: AnalysisBank distills expert reports into a reusable library of Analyses, each pairing a data signal, an analytical move, and the expert span it was derived from. At inference, it matches input signals to library entries and applies retrieved moves to compose the final report.

Result: Analysis of Analyses from 550 expert reports reveals a heavy-tailed distribution of 47-52 signal types across 13 move types. On two financial benchmarks with four LLM backbones, AnalysisBank increases the proportion of novel, data-grounded insights by 1.7-3.7x over structural-level baselines. Transfer to scientific writing confirms the distinction generalizes beyond finance.

Conclusion: Analytical-level report generation (composing from data-signal-to-move mappings) outperforms structural-level composition for financial reporting, and the approach generalizes to other domains such as scientific writing.

Abstract: We argue that financial report generation should operate at the analytical rather than structural level, composing content from data-derived insights rather than high-level topics or sections. To this end, we propose AnalysisBank, which distills expert reports into a reusable library of Analyses, each pairing a data signal, an analytical move, and the expert span it was derived from. At inference time, AnalysisBank matches input signals to library entries and applies the retrieved moves to compose the report. A study of Analyses distilled from 550 expert reports reveals a heavy-tailed distribution of 47-52 signal types spanning 13 move types. On two financial benchmarks across four LLM backbones, AnalysisBank increases the proportion of novel, data-grounded insights by 1.7-3.7x over structural-level baselines. Transfer to scientific writing suggests that the distinction generalizes beyond finance. Code and the distilled Analysis library are available at https://github.com/yajingyang/AnalysisBank.

</details>


### [78] [Polished but Unresolved: Identifying Late-Stage Pressure States in Long-Horizon Tool-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00823)
*Haoyang Chen,Yi Liu,Jianzhi Shao,Xiaozhou Xu,Zhe Sun,Wei Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies a "late-stage pressure state" in long-horizon tool-use agents—a bias toward prematurely submitting a polished-looking final answer while key constraints remain unresolved. The authors train a linear probe to detect this state from hidden states, use activation interventions to manipulate it, and propose Probe-Sensed Pressure Relief (PSPR), a plugin that applies lightweight pressure relief under moderate pressure and structured organization under high pressure risk, improving existing agent methods on multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon tool-use agents often face a failure mode where they prematurely submit final answers that appear complete and polished while key constraints are still unresolved. This "pressure state" is underexplored, and understanding whether it is identifiable and controllable can lead to better agent reliability.

Method: The authors (1) train a linear probe on hidden states to show the pressure state is identifiable; (2) apply activation interventions along the pressure direction to verify causality between pressure and early submission behavior; (3) perform controlled context manipulations to study mitigating factors (constraint clarity and action mapping); (4) based on findings, propose PSPR—a plugin that applies a lightweight pressure-relief direction under moderate pressure and switches to structured organization under high-pressure risk.

Result: The linear probe can identify the pressure state from hidden states; activation interventions along the pressure direction alter both the pressure score and whether the agent continues tool use or submits early. Constraint clarity and action mapping mitigate pressure. PSPR consistently strengthens existing agent methods across multiple long-horizon benchmarks.

Conclusion: Pressure states in tool-use agents are detectable and manipulable, and strategically relieving pressure via a lightweight plugin (PSPR) improves agent performance on long-horizon tasks, offering a practical mechanism-based enhancement for existing agents.

Abstract: Long-horizon tool-use agents need not only to search and plan, but also to decide when to finalize. We study late-stage pressure states, in which an agent is biased toward submitting a final answer that appears complete and polished while key constraints remain unresolved. We first train a linear probe to show that this pressure state is identifiable from the agent's hidden states. Then, we use activation interventions along this pressure direction and find that shifting the hidden states changes both the pressure score and whether the agent continues tool use or submits early. Through controlled context manipulations, we further see that the pressure is mitigated by constraint clarity and action mapping. Based on these findings, we propose Probe-Sensed Pressure Relief (PSPR), a plugin that applies lightweight pressure relief direction under moderate pressure and moves to structured organization under high pressure risk. Experiments on multiple long-horizon benchmarks show that our method consistently strengthens existing agent methods.

</details>


### [79] [FLaG: Frequency-Domain Latent-attention Gated Pooling for Token Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00831)
*Kewei Li,Rongying Zhang,Xueli Wang,Xiwen Gong,Zhongjian Wang,Qiuchen Zhao,Lan Huang,Ruochi Zhang,Fengfeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FLaG is a plug-in token aggregation module that operates in the Fourier domain instead of the original token space, re-expressing encoder outputs via rFFT spectral components, summarizing spectral tokens with latent queries, applying a sample-conditioned channel gate, and reconstructing modulated representations for pooling. Across AMP prediction, ResNet18 image classification, and RoBERTa-based language tasks, it achieves state-of-the-art or competitive results (best macro Spearman, RMSE, Recall@50 on four AMP settings; best top-1 on CIFAR-10; best mean on 5/7 language metrics). Mechanistic analyses reveal low-frequency prediction dominance across encoder layers, rising high-frequency sensitivity in the final layer, peptide-specific positional responses, and sample/species-specific spectral allocation in latent cross-attention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Token aggregation methods (e.g., mean/max/attention pooling) typically operate only in the original token space, which may discard informative frequency-domain structure in token-level representations. There is a need for a transferable aggregation bias that works across diverse backbones (protein, vision, text) and downstream tasks.

Method: FLaG converts encoder token representations to the Fourier domain by taking the nonredundant real FFT (rFFT) spectrum, concatenating real and imaginary components as spectral tokens, summarizing these with learnable latent queries via cross-attention, generating a sample-conditioned residual channel gate that modulates spectral channels, and then reconstructing the modulated token representations for the final pooling/aggregation. It is a plug-in module compatible with arbitrary encoders.

Result: FLaG achieves the best macro-averaged Spearman correlation, RMSE, and Recall@50 across four AMP backbone-species settings, and the highest top-1 accuracy on CIFAR-10. On language tasks, it achieves the best mean results on five of seven metrics (mean pooling remains strongest on STSBenchmark). Mechanistic analyses show low-frequency prediction sensitivity across most encoder layers, increased relative high-frequency sensitivity in the final layer, peptide-specific positional responses, and a gate that broadly amplifies spectral channels while latent cross-attention exhibits sample- and species-specific spectral allocation.

Conclusion: FLaG provides a transferable frequency-domain aggregation bias that generalizes across protein, visual, and textual representations, although its benefit depends on the backbone and downstream task. It offers a novel frequency-domain perspective for token pooling and is released with code and data.

Abstract: Token aggregation converts token-level representations into fixed-dimensional sample representations, but most pooling methods operate only in the original token space. We introduce Frequency-Domain Latent-attention Gated Pooling (FLaG), a plug-in aggregation module that re-expresses encoder outputs in the Fourier domain before final pooling. FLaG represents the nonredundant rFFT spectrum through concatenated real and imaginary components, summarizes spectral tokens with learnable latent queries, derives a sample-conditioned channel gate, and reconstructs modulated token representations for downstream aggregation. We evaluate the same architecture across ESM2-based antimicrobial peptide (AMP) activity prediction, ResNet18 image classification on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100, and three RoBERTa-based language tasks. FLaG achieves the best macro-averaged Spearman correlation coefficient, RMSE, and Recall@50 across four AMP backbone-species settings and the highest top-1 accuracy on CIFAR 10. It also achieves the best mean results on five of seven language metrics, although mean pooling remains strongest on STSBenchmark. AMP-side mechanistic analyses reveal low-frequency prediction sensitivity across most encoder layers, with increased relative high-frequency sensitivity in the final layer, and pronounced peptide-specific positional responses. The residual gate broadly amplifies spectral channels while preserving the low-frequency-dominated energy profile, whereas latent cross-attention exhibits sample- and species-specific spectral allocation. Overall, FLaG provides a transferable frequency-domain aggregation bias across protein, visual, and textual representations, with benefits that depend on the backbone and downstream task. Supplementary materials, source code, and data are available at https://www.healthinformaticslab.org/supp/ and https://github.com/Kewei2023/AMPCliff/tree/FLaG.

</details>


### [80] [Towards Generalizable Visually Grounded Exploration of Household Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00845)
*Linhao Zheng,Zeming Liu,Wangke Chen,Li Zeng,Wanxiang Che,Heyan Huang,Yuhang Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces VGEBench, a benchmark for evaluating Generalizable Visually Grounded Exploration in VLMs, using a Logic-Driven State Machine framework to simulate multi-turn interaction loops for novel device operation without manuals.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current embodied exploration paradigms rely heavily on imitation learning from human-annotated trajectories, limiting generalization. Existing benchmarks fail to evaluate the dynamic Hypothesis-Interaction-Refinement process needed for functional device operation, as they depend on explicit documents and annotated trajectories.

Method: The authors construct a Logic-Driven State Machine framework that simulates multi-turn interaction loops, compelling agents to achieve goals through active visual perception and feedback-driven correction, testing their ability to ground abstract world knowledge into fine-grained visual affordances.

Result: Experimental results show that existing VLMs face significant challenges in translating semantic knowledge into physical execution and maintaining long-horizon state tracking.

Conclusion: VGEBench bridges the gap in evaluating generalizable visually grounded exploration, revealing critical limitations of current VLMs for general autonomous embodied agents.

Abstract: Recent advancements in Vision-Language Models (VLMs) have demonstrated impressive capabilities in static visual recognition and high-level semantic reasoning. However, current embodied exploration paradigms still heavily rely on imitation learning from human-annotated trajectories, which severely limits agents' generalization ability. The key bottleneck of realizing general autonomous embodied agents lies in Generalizable Visually Grounded Exploration: the ability to operate novel devices without manuals or specific training by actively grounding abstract world knowledge into fine-grained visual affordances. Yet, existing benchmarks fail to evaluate this capability: they generally rely on explicit documents and annotated trajectories, neglecting the dynamic Hypothesis-Interaction-Refinement process essential for functional device operation. To bridge this gap, we introduce VGEBench, a comprehensive benchmark designed to evaluate the generalizable visually grounded exploration capabilities of VLMs. Unlike static datasets, we construct a Logic-Driven State Machine framework. This framework simulates multi-turn interaction loops, compelling agents to achieve goals by active visual perception and feedback-driven correction. Experimental results demonstrate that existing VLMs face significant challenges in translating semantic knowledge into physical execution and maintaining long-horizon state tracking.

</details>


### [81] [Verifiable Disaster Storylines and Causal Knowledge Graphs: A Citation-Grounded Pipeline from Heterogeneous Humanitarian Sources](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00858)
*Ivan Decostanzi,Michele Ronco,Sergio Consoli,Christina Corbane,Lorenzo Bertolini,Indaco Biazzo,Daria Mihaila,Manuel Garcia-Herranz,Felix Schwebel,Yelena Mejova,Kyriaki Kalimeri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents a pipeline that integrates structured disaster data (EM-DAT) with unstructured sources (ReliefWeb, EMM) using Retrieval-Augmented Generation to produce source-grounded disaster storylines and causal knowledge graphs, evaluated by domain experts and non-experts across three crisis use cases.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanitarian response requires rapid synthesis of heterogeneous, high-volume information, which routinely exceeds human analytical capacity in the critical early hours of a crisis.

Method: The pipeline combines structured EM-DAT disaster records with unstructured documents from ReliefWeb and EMM via Retrieval-Augmented Generation to extract structured storylines covering 17 fields (including severity, drivers, child-sensitive indicators) and construct causal knowledge graphs with citation-grounded explanatory narratives for full traceability.

Result: Human evaluation with 9 domain experts and 9 non-experts across three crisis use cases confirms high retrieval precision, strong faithfulness of causal relations, and clear expert preference for citation-grounded components over ungrounded alternatives.

Conclusion: The pipeline is designed to scale to the full EM-DAT catalogue, with the goal of publicly releasing a narrative-enriched version of the database to support situational awareness for responders and analysts.

Abstract: Effective humanitarian response depends on the rapid synthesis of heterogeneous, high-volume information sources - a task that routinely exceeds human analytical capacity in the critical early hours of a crisis. We present a pipeline that combines structured disaster records from EM-DAT with unstructured documents from ReliefWeb and the European Media Monitor (EMM) to produce source-grounded disaster storylines and causal knowledge graphs supporting situational awareness for responders and analysts. Using Retrieval-Augmented Generation, the pipeline extracts structured storylines - tabular event profiles covering 17 fields, from severity and key drivers to child-sensitive impact indicators - and constructs causal knowledge graphs where each node and edge is enriched with citation-grounded explanatory narratives, enabling full traceability back to primary sources. We evaluate the system on three diverse crisis use cases through a human evaluation involving 9 domain expert and 9 non-expert evaluators. Results confirm high retrieval precision, strong faithfulness of extracted causal relations, and a clear expert preference for citation-grounded components over ungrounded alternatives. The pipeline is designed to scale to the full EM-DAT catalogue, with the goal of publicly releasing a narrative-enriched version of the database.

</details>


### [82] [Reinforcement Learning Enhanced LLM Agents for Complex Vehicle Routing Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00859)
*Yi Chen,Zikang Yu,Jiahai Wang,Jinbiao Chen,Jianpeng Zhou,Zizhen Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RLEA is a multi-agent framework that automates the modeling of complex Vehicle Routing Problems by combining LLM-based agents with a lightweight neural Planner trained via Soft Q-learning, an evolutionary memory module, and retrieval-augmented generation. It achieves a 16.67% higher success rate than prior state-of-the-art across 48 VRP variants while reducing runtime errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modeling complex VRP variants for optimization solvers requires substantial domain expertise, which limits the accessibility of advanced optimization technologies to non-experts.

Method: RLEA employs a multi-agent framework with a lightweight neural Planner trained using Soft Q-learning to orchestrate LLM-based agents. It incorporates an evolutionary memory module and retrieval-augmented generation to leverage accumulated experience and external solver knowledge during program generation and refinement.

Result: Evaluated on 48 distinct VRP variants across various solvers, RLEA outperforms the previous state-of-the-art with a 16.67% higher success rate and significantly fewer runtime errors.

Conclusion: Integrating reinforcement learning with LLM-based reasoning proves highly effective for automated optimization modeling, enabling broader access to advanced optimization solvers.

Abstract: Vehicle Routing Problems (VRPs) are fundamental combinatorial optimization problems with widespread applications in various scenarios. The advanced optimization solvers can effectively solve such problems. However, modeling complex VRP variants for solvers often requires substantial domain expertise, which limits the accessibility of advanced optimization technologies. In this paper, we propose Reinforcement Learning Enhanced LLMAgents(RLEA), a multi-agent framework designed to automate the modeling of complex VRPs. RLEA introduces a lightweight neural Planner trained with Soft Q-learning to efficiently orchestrate the actions of LLM-based agents. In addition, we equip the system with an evolutionary memory module and retrieval-augmented generation, enabling the agent to leverage both accumulated experience and external solver knowledge during program generation and refinement for solving VRPs. We evaluated 48 distinct VRP variants across various solvers. The experimental results demonstrate that RLEA outperforms the previous state-of-the-ar method, achieving a 16.67% higher success rate while significantly reducing runtime errors. These results validate that integrating reinforcement learning with LLM-based reasoning is highly effective for automated optimization modeling. The appendix is available at: https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.19134435.

</details>


### [83] [Beyond the Clock: Measuring the Value of Adaptive Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00874)
*Ayushi Chadha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies meta-level control in hierarchical agentic systems, asking when a higher-level controller should preserve versus revise a strategy commitment governing lower-level computation. Using a hierarchical latent reasoner, the authors find that learned revision timing produces diverse policies across seeds but none outperform the best forced timing policy. Deep intervention and counterfactual analyses reveal a strong fixed schedule captures most of the measurable value from timing, and that score-level evidence can be misleading when predictability is dominated by decision position rather than within-position discrimination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agentic systems become compound, decisions about meta-level control (when to preserve vs. revise a lower-level strategy) grow more important. The paper aims to systematically separate whether a controller's state-dependent behavior actually translates into task-performance value, beyond merely being able to vary its actions with internal state.

Method: The authors study a hierarchical latent reasoner where a manager decides to retain or replace a commitment governing lower-level computation. They train across multiple seeds and compare learned revision-timing policies against forced timing policies on frozen checkpoints. They conduct intervention studies (examining order-sensitivity and timing consequences), exhaustive enumeration of schedules, and counterfactual PERSIST/REPLAN diagnostic analyses.

Result: Learned revision timing yields qualitatively different policies across seeds—ranging from near-deterministic early clocks to more state-conditioned schedule distributions—yet none outperform the best forced timing policy. Intervention studies show timing is consequential and order-sensitive, while exhaustive enumeration indicates a strong fixed schedule captures most measurable value from timing at this decision budget. Counterfactual diagnostics show score-level evidence can mislead when predictability is driven by decision position rather than within-position discrimination.

Conclusion: Learned meta-level control should be evaluated along three separate axes: (1) whether its score depends on state, (2) whether that dependence changes realized behavior, and (3) whether those changes capture outcome value beyond a strong non-adaptive policy. This framework helps separate state dependence from actual decision value in agentic system control.

Abstract: As agentic systems become compound systems, increasingly important decisions move above task execution itself: when should a higher-level controller preserve the strategy guiding another process, and when should it revise it? We study this meta-level control problem in a hierarchical latent reasoner whose manager can retain or replace a commitment governing lower-level computation. Across three precommitted training seeds, learned revision timing produces qualitatively different policies, ranging from an almost deterministic early clock to substantially more state conditioned schedule distributions, yet none outperforms the best forced timing policy evaluated on the same frozen checkpoint. This separates state dependence from decision value: a controller can vary its actions with internal state without turning that variation into a reproducible task-performance benefit. A deeper intervention study on the original checkpoint shows that timing itself is consequential and order-sensitive, while exhaustive enumeration reveals that a strong fixed schedule captures most of the measurable value available from timing at this decision budget. Counterfactual PERSIST/REPLAN diagnostics further show why score-level evidence can be misleading when predictability is dominated by decision position rather than within-position discrimination. Together, these results argue that learned meta-level control should be evaluated along three separate axes: whether its score depends on state, whether that dependence changes realized behavior, and whether those changes capture outcome value beyond a strong non-adaptive policy.

</details>


### [84] [FractalNet-Based Heterogeneous Federated Learning for Orbital Edge Intelligence in Satellite Mega-Constellations: A Wildfire Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00875)
*Sai Puppala,Koushik Sinha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a heterogeneous federated learning framework based on the FractalNet architecture for satellite mega-constellations, addressing the mismatch between terrestrial federated learning paradigms and the unique constraints of orbital edge intelligence (SWAP-C heterogeneity, radiation tolerance, link availability, and propagation delay). It introduces a contact-window-constrained, depth-heterogeneous federated optimization method with a distributed path scheduler assigning model depth per satellite constraints, tiered periodic update pooling to reduce overhead, and a three-tier agentic control plane. A wildfire detection case study demonstrates natural semantic hierarchy across orbital shells (LEO pixel-scale, MEO regional, GEO/HEO risk-scale), validated through simulations on convergence, communication efficiency, energy, pooling savings, robustness, and latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current learning architectures for satellite mega-constellations are inherited from terrestrial federated learning and ground-centric operations, which are ill-suited to satellites with orders-of-magnitude differences in SWAP-C, radiation tolerance, link availability, and propagation delay. There is a need for orbital-native learning frameworks that account for the heterogeneous computational capabilities and intermittent connectivity inherent to space-based systems.

Method: The method includes: (1) a FractalNet-based heterogeneous federated learning architecture supporting depth heterogeneity across satellites; (2) formalization of contact-window-constrained, depth-heterogeneous federated optimization; (3) a distributed path scheduler that assigns model depth as a function of SWAP-C constraints, predicted inter-satellite contacts, and training statistics; (4) tiered periodic update pooling (rather than per-contact aggregation) to reduce message overhead and energy; and (5) a three-tier agentic control plane for in-space scheduling, anomaly escalation, and policy-governed autonomy.

Result: Simulations on mega-constellation scenarios validate the approach across convergence behavior, communication efficiency, energy adaptation, scheduled-pooling savings, robustness, and latency. The wildfire detection case study demonstrates emergent semantic specialization: LEO learns pixel-scale thermal anomalies, MEO learns regional fire-front dynamics, and GEO/HEO learns larger-scale risk propagation.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a viable heterogeneous federated learning paradigm for orbital edge intelligence, capitalizing on the natural multi-resolution sensing hierarchy of different orbital shells, and demonstrates measurable gains in communication and energy efficiency while maintaining robust convergence under space-specific constraints.

Abstract: Satellite mega-constellations are emerging as large-scale sensing, communication, and computation fabrics, yet their learning architectures remain largely inherited from terrestrial federated learning and ground-centric mission operations--- ill-suited to satellites that differ by orders of magnitude in Size, Weight, Power, and Cost (SWAP-C), radiation tolerance, link availability, and propagation delay. We propose a heterogeneous federated learning method based on the FractalNet architecture for orbital edge intelligence. We formalize contact-window-constrained, depth-heterogeneous federated optimization and introduce a distributed path scheduler that assigns model depth as a function of SWAP-C constraints, predicted inter-satellite contacts, and training statistics. To reduce message overhead and energy consumption, each tier pools updates periodically rather than at every contact opportunity, and a three-tier agentic control plane governs in-space scheduling, anomaly escalation, and policy-governed autonomy. As a case study, we apply the framework to wildfire detection, where each orbital shell naturally learns a different semantic level of situational awareness: pixel-scale thermal anomalies at low Earth orbit (LEO), regional fire-front dynamics at medium Earth orbit (MEO), and larger-scale risk propagation at geostationary or high Earth orbit (GEO/HEO). Experiments on simulated mega-constellations validate the approach across convergence, communication efficiency, energy adaptation, scheduled-pooling savings, robustness, and latency.

</details>


### [85] [Towards reliable multimodal disaster severity assessment through preference optimization and explainable vision-language reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00879)
*Yuanjun Zhang,Fuzel Ahamed Shaik,Suvojit Acharjee,Fahad Khalid,Mourad Oussalah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-stage training framework (SFT + DPO) for multimodal disaster damage assessment, built on a unified HITL annotation pipeline that derives two complementary datasets (ReasoningSet for SFT and PreferenceSet for DPO). The approach improves classification accuracy from 73.64% to 78.29%, boosts Macro-F1 by 29%, enhances explanation quality by ~25%, and demonstrates robustness across InternVL-3-8B and LLaVA-1.5-7B models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multimodal disaster damage assessment models face two key limitations: scarce annotated data and insufficient evaluation of reasoning/explanation quality. Reliable disaster response requires models that not only predict accurately but also provide transparent, auditable explanations aligned with human judgment.

Method: A two-stage training framework combining Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) within a unified data construction pipeline. A single Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) annotation workflow generates two complementary datasets: ReasoningSet (validated rationales for SFT) and PreferenceSet (paired rationales for DPO-based alignment). Evaluations use automatic metrics, model-based scoring, and human ranking.

Result: SFT improves accuracy from 73.64% to 78.29% and boosts Macro-F1 by 29% over baseline; explanation quality improves by ~25%. DPO alignment further enhances interpretability. Cross-model validation on InternVL-3-8B and LLaVA-1.5-7B confirms robustness and generalizability. The framework improves detection of underrepresented mild damage, reduces high-risk misclassifications, and better aligns model reasoning with human judgment.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides a reproducible pathway for developing reliable multimodal disaster damage assessment systems that deliver auditable, actionable insights for emergency management, while strengthening the connection between model reasoning and human decision-making.

Abstract: Reliable disaster damage assessment requires models that provide both accurate predictions and transparent explanations. However, existing multimodal approaches are limited by scarce annotated data and insufficient evaluation of reasoning quality. This study proposes a two-stage training framework that integrates Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) within a unified data construction pipeline. From a single Human-in-the-Loop (HITL) annotation workflow, two complementary datasets are derived, namely ReasoningSet, which contains validated rationales for SFT, and PreferenceSet, which comprises paired rationales for DPO-based alignment. The framework evaluates both classification performance and explanation quality using automatic metrics, model-based scoring, and human ranking. Experimental results show that SFT improves accuracy from 73.64% to 78.29% and increases Macro-F1 by 29% compared to the baseline, while explanation quality improves by approximately 25%. Subsequent DPO alignment further enhances interpretability on the PreferenceSet. Cross-model validation on InternVL-3-8B and LLaVA-1.5-7B demonstrates the robustness and generalizability of the approach. The proposed framework improves detection of underrepresented mild damage cases, reduces high-risk misclassifications, and strengthens alignment between model reasoning and human judgment. Overall, it provides a reproducible pathway to develop reliable multimodal systems that deliver auditable, actionable disaster insights for emergency management.

</details>


### [86] [Denoising Diffusion Generative Models Secretly Calculate Attentions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00885)
*Farzan Haddadi,Leila Monfared,Ebrahim Rezaii,Mohammadreza Malek-Mohammadi,Pejman Zakalvand,Narges Mokhtari*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that denoising diffusion models inherently employ an attention mechanism structurally similar to that of transformers, suggesting attention is a universal principle in machine learning. It further shows equivalences between auto-encoders and attention-based models, enabling interchange of designs. As a demonstration, the authors reformulate diffusion to derive a simplified attention-based image generation algorithm that achieves comparable performance with significantly less training time and computational resources.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While diffusion models dominate image generation and transformers dominate natural language processing, the two are typically viewed as distinct paradigms. This work explores whether underlying architectural similarities exist, motivated by the desire to unify these approaches and reduce the heavy computational cost and lengthy training inherent to diffusion-based image generation.

Method: The authors theoretically show that the diffusion process naturally induces an attention-like operation analogous to that in transformers. They establish formal equivalences between diffusion models and attention mechanisms, and between auto-encoders and attention-based models. Leveraging these equivalences, they reformulate the diffusion framework into a simplified attention-based algorithm for image generation.

Result: The proposed attention-based implementation achieves performance comparable to standard diffusion models while requiring significantly less training effort and fewer computational resources.

Conclusion: Attention emerges as a universal learning principle shared across diffusion models, transformers, and auto-encoders. These equivalences allow architectural components to be interchanged based on practical constraints, paving the way for more efficient generative models built on attention without the computational burden of full diffusion training.

Abstract: Denoising diffusion models are the dominant architecture for image generation, whereas most natural language generation and modeling are primarily handled by well-known transformer architectures employing attention mechanism. Here, we show that diffusion models also inherently use an attention mechanism very similar to that of transformers. Therefore, attention emerges as a universal machine learning principle, based on a general training objective. We also show similarities in basic functional principle of auto-encoders and attention-based models. These equivalences allows us to interchange these designs based on practical requirements. As an example, we can reformulate the diffusion framework to reduce the lengthy training process and computation-intensive image generation. Using this approach, a simplified algorithm is proposed for image generation which is based on attention mechanism. Results show that the attention-based implementation achieves comparable performance with significantly less effort and computational resources.

</details>


### [87] [CacheBridge: Efficient Cross-Model KV Cache Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00891)
*Xingyu Qu,Siyuan Lu,Zhiyu Chen,Sheng Wang,Tao Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CacheBridge is a closed-form cross-model KV cache transfer method that co-designs architecture-indexed mapper support, attention-aligned calibration, and bounded mapper construction to improve cross-LLM context sharing, achieving substantial storage reduction, speedup, and accuracy gains over Full-Head Mapping.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-Head Mapping, while avoiding KV replay, maps each target KV head from all source heads in selected layers, making transfer quality sensitive to architectural differences and causing mapper storage and application cost to grow with layer support.

Method: CacheBridge co-designs three components while retaining a closed-form affine interface: (1) architecture-indexed mapper support that restricts each target head to a matched source head, (2) attention-aligned calibration that weights reconstruction errors by causal attention sensitivity, and (3) bounded mapper construction using a fused GPU kernel to compute weighted sufficient statistics without materializing full observation tensors.

Result: Across three transfer directions, CacheBridge recovers both Ministral 3 transfer directions where Full-Head Mapping loses substantial accuracy while preserving 99.83% mean target retention on Qwen3. On Qwen3 14B→32B, it reduces mapper storage by 8x, accelerates application by up to 3.0x, matches Full-Head Mapping with one tenth of the calibration data, and reduces 500-sequence construction from 92.63 to 8.63 seconds (10.7x).

Conclusion: CacheBridge demonstrates that cross-model KV transfer can be made more efficient and accurate through careful co-design of architecture awareness, attention-weighted calibration, and bounded computation, all while preserving the deployment-friendly closed-form affine interface.

Abstract: Sharing context between LLMs in a multi-model system requires the receiving model to prefill the shared prefix because KV caches are model-specific. Recent closed-form cross-model KV transfer, hereafter Full-Head Mapping, avoids this replay by fitting a training-free affine mapper from source to target caches. However, its full-head design maps each target KV head from every source KV head in the selected layers, making transfer quality sensitive to architectural differences and causing mapper storage and application cost to grow with layer support. To this end, we introduce CacheBridge, which co-designs architecture-indexed mapper support, attention-aligned calibration, and bounded mapper construction while retaining a closed-form affine interface for online deployment. CacheBridge restricts each target head to a matched source head, weights reconstruction errors by causal attention sensitivity, and uses a fused GPU kernel to construct weighted sufficient statistics without materializing full observation tensors. Across three transfer directions, CacheBridge recovers the two Ministral 3 transfer directions where Full-Head Mapping loses substantial accuracy while preserving 99.83\% mean target retention on Qwen3. On Qwen3 $14\mathrm{B}\to32\mathrm{B}$, it reduces mapper storage by $8\times$, accelerates application by up to $3.0\times$, matches \fullhead with one tenth of the calibration data, and reduces 500-sequence construction from 92.63 to 8.63 seconds ($10.7\times$).

</details>


### [88] [CARE: Contrastive Anchor-based Rubric Evolution for Large Language Model Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00892)
*Siyuan Li,Xinxin Song,Chen Ruinian,Jingjing Fan,Tingxiong Xiao,Yangen Hu,Ke Zeng,Jinli Suo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CARE (Contrastive Anchor-based Rubric Evolution) is a reinforcement learning method that dynamically evolves rubrics for post-training LLMs on open-ended tasks by contrasting the highest-scoring rollout against an anchor response from a frontier model at each training step, using two complementary branches (Adaptive and Chase) to repair reward misspecification and sharpen rubrics respectively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rubric-based RL decomposes open-ended instructions into flexible rubrics, better suited than RLVR for open-ended tasks. However, static rubrics get hacked as the policy evolves, and existing dynamic approaches suffer from undirected rubric extraction, unreliable hack detection, and unbounded rubric proliferation.
