Method: CARE grounds each rubric evolution step in a high-quality anchor response generated by a frontier model conditioned on the prompt and current rubrics. At each training step, the highest-scoring rollout is contrasted against the anchor, driving two mechanisms: an Adaptive branch that reactively repairs reward misspecification, and a Chase branch that proactively converts frontier-level quality gaps into sharper rubrics. Together they maintain discriminative accuracy in the high-reward region.

Result: On WildChecklist-9K with Qwen2.5-7B-Base and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, CARE achieves state-of-the-art performance on Arena-Hard-2.0, InfoBench, and FollowBench. It is the only method whose win rate against GPT-4.1 anchor responses shows sustained improvement across 300 training steps. Additional results on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B show generalization across model families.

Conclusion: CARE effectively addresses the limitations of existing dynamic rubric approaches by providing grounded, adaptive rubric evolution that maintains discriminative accuracy in the high-reward region where over-optimization originates, achieving SOTA results and generalizing across model families.

Abstract: Rubric-based reinforcement learning decomposes open-ended instructions into prompt-specific, flexible rubrics, making it better suited than reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards for post-training LLMs on open-ended tasks. However, static rubrics are inevitably hacked as the policy evolves, and existing dynamic approaches introduce new problems: undirected rubric extraction, unreliable hack detection, and unbounded rubric proliferation. We propose $\textbf{CARE}$ ($\textbf{C}$ontrastive $\textbf{A}$nchor-based $\textbf{R}$ubric $\textbf{E}$volution), which grounds every rubric evolution step in a high-quality anchor response generated by a frontier model conditioned on the prompt and its rubrics. At each training step, CARE contrasts the highest-scoring rollout against the anchor, enabling two complementary mechanisms: an Adaptive branch that reactively repairs reward misspecification; and a Chase branch that proactively converts frontier-level quality gaps into sharper rubrics. Together, the two branches $\textbf{maintain discriminative accuracy in the high-reward region}$---the precise region where reward over-optimization mostly originates. Experiments on WildChecklist-9K with Qwen2.5-7B-Base and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that CARE achieves state-of-the-art performance on Arena-Hard-2.0, InfoBench, and FollowBench, and is the $\textbf{only}$ method whose win rate against GPT-4.1 anchor responses shows sustained improvement throughout 300 training steps; additional results on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen3-8B further indicate that CARE generalizes across model families.

</details>


### [89] [In-Context Neurofeedback: Can LLMs Control Their Internal Representations through Privileged Access?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00904)
*Koshiro Aoki,Ryota Takatsuki,Gouki Minegishi,Yusuke Haruki,Daisuke Kawahara*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates whether LLMs can genuinely control their own internal representations. It finds that a previous neurofeedback study claiming LLMs can control internal states used non-privileged control targets (inferable from prompts), allowing superficial mechanisms to drive the effect. When the authors redesign the paradigm so control targets are truly privileged (not inferable from prompts), LLMs fail to reliably control these internal representations, suggesting earlier claims were overstated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To rigorously test whether LLMs possess genuine metacognitive control over their internal representations, particularly following a prior study that claimed neurofeedback-based control but used targets a third party could infer from prompts alone.

Method: The authors redesign the neurofeedback paradigm so that the control target satisfies the privileged access requirement—meaning the target is not inferable from the prompt by an external observer—mimicking the stricter conditions of human neurofeedback experiments in cognitive neuroscience, and then test whether LLMs can control such privileged internal representations.

Result: Under the privileged-access setting, the models do not demonstrate reliable control over their internal representations, failing to replicate the success of the prior study under the stricter condition.

Conclusion: Prior claims of LLM internal representation control cannot exclude reliance on superficial mechanisms. Rigorous assessments of LLM metacognition require evaluation methods that demand privileged access to internal states.

Abstract: Whether large language models (LLMs) can control their own internal representations matters for both machine metacognition and AI safety. A recent study applied neurofeedback to LLMs and claimed that they can control their internal representations. However, the reported control may rely on superficial mechanisms rather than genuine internal access because the control targets in that study are not privileged, meaning that a third party can infer them from the prompt. We redesign the neurofeedback paradigm for LLMs so that the control target satisfies the privileged access requirement, which is closer to neurofeedback experiments in human cognitive neuroscience. Under this stricter setting, the models do not demonstrate reliable control over privileged internal representations, suggesting that previously reported control cannot exclude the possibility that it relies on superficial mechanisms. Our results indicate that rigorous assessments of metacognition in LLMs require evaluation methods that demand privileged access.

</details>


### [90] [RPCBench: A Benchmark for Proactive Premise Critique in LLM-based Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00918)
*Zhongru Chen,Yuan Wu,Yi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RPCBench is a new benchmark for evaluating Recommender-Premise Critique: whether LLMs can detect, diagnose, and handle faulty premises in natural-language recommendation requests. It contains evidence-grounded instances across five domains and ten premise-failure types, with a fine-grained evaluation framework covering proactive detection, error localization, post-detection handling, and evidence faithfulness. Evaluation of 11 LLMs reveals proactive detection as the main bottleneck, worst performance on underspecified-premise errors, and that critique quality peaks at intermediate reasoning length.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are increasingly used as interactive recommender assistants, yet existing recommender benchmarks focus on ranking/generation/preference satisfaction, and existing error-detection benchmarks are not grounded in recommendation-specific user and candidate evidence. A gap exists in evaluating whether models can recognize flawed recommendation requests, not just produce plausible recommendations.

Method: The authors introduce RPCBench, a benchmark with evidence-grounded test instances spanning five recommendation domains and ten premise-failure types. Each instance provides a visible recommendation context and a corrupted user query. A fine-grained evaluation framework is designed to measure proactive detection, error localization, post-detection handling strategy, and evidence faithfulness. The benchmark is used to systematically evaluate 11 LLMs.

Result: Proactive detection is the main bottleneck in Recommender-Premise Critique. Models perform worst on underspecified-premise errors. Target-critical information density matters more than redundant evidence, and longer reasoning does not monotonically improve critique quality: performance peaks at intermediate reasoning length, with overly long reasoning incurring an overthinking penalty.

Conclusion: RPCBench reveals significant limitations in current LLMs as interactive recommender assistants, particularly in proactive detection of flawed premises. The findings suggest that model training should focus on proactive premise verification, especially for underspecified requests, and that reasoning length should be optimally calibrated to avoid overthinking.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used as interactive recommender assistants. Their evaluation should therefore go beyond plausible item recommendation and test whether they can recognize flawed recommendation requests. Existing recommender benchmarks mainly assess ranking, generation, or preference satisfaction, while existing error-detection benchmarks are usually not grounded in recommendation-specific user and candidate evidence. To address this gap, we introduce RPCBench, a benchmark for evaluating Recommender-Premise Critique: the ability to detect, diagnose, and properly handle faulty premises in natural-language recommendation requests. RPCBench contains evidence-grounded test instances from five recommendation domains and covers ten types of premise failures. Each instance provides a visible recommendation context and a corrupted user query. We further design a fine-grained evaluation framework that measures proactive detection, error localization, post-detection handling strategy, and evidence faithfulness. Through a systematic evaluation of 11 LLMs, we find that proactive detection is the main bottleneck in Recommender-Premise Critique, and models perform worst on underspecified-premise errors. We also observe that target-critical information density matters more than redundant evidence, and that longer reasoning does not monotonically improve critique quality: performance peaks at intermediate reasoning length, while overly long reasoning is accompanied by an overthinking penalty. The code is available at https://github.com/ZhongruChen/RPCBench.

</details>


### [91] [VIBE-Bench: Evaluating Personalized Large Language Models When Profiles Don't Mean Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00921)
*Yiwen Jiang,Yang Deng,Stephanie Fong,Zimu Wang,Yaling Shen,Wei Feng,Hongxi Yang,Xiangyu Zhao,Zhongxing Xu,Deval Mehta,Xuelian Cheng,Zongyuan Ge*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper identifies profile-preference conceptual misalignment (PRCM) as an underexplored failure regime in personalized LLMs, where observable profile cues and query-specific preferences reside in different concept spaces, making semantic retrieval insufficient. The authors introduce VIBE-Bench, a psychology-grounded benchmark with 3,504 personas and 12,239 dialogues, demonstrating that current PLLMs rely on shallow semantic correlations and fail at cross-concept preference reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing personalization benchmarks assume preference reasoning can be solved via retrieving semantically related history. The authors argue this overlooks a practically important scenario where user profile cues and query-specific preferences are conceptually misaligned, requiring reasoning beyond surface semantic overlap—a regime existing methods and benchmarks fail to capture.

Method: The authors (1) formalize profile-preference conceptual misalignment (PRCM) as a distinct challenge; (2) construct VIBE-Bench, a benchmark with two psychology-grounded tasks comprising 3,504 personas and 12,239 dialogues with a manually verified gold test set; and (3) evaluate several existing personalization methods to assess their cross-concept preference reasoning capabilities.

Result: Experiments reveal that current PLLMs and personalization methods predominantly rely on shallow semantic correlations and fail to acquire robust cross-concept mappings, confirming PRCM as a distinct and persistent failure regime in personalized LLMs.

Conclusion: The findings establish PRCM as a distinct failure regime in PLLMs and position VIBE-Bench as a focused testbed for advancing preference reasoning beyond semantic matching, motivating future work on robust cross-concept personalization.

Abstract: Personalized Large Language Models (PLLMs) aim to tailor responses to individual users, where a central challenge is preference reasoning: inferring query-relevant preferences from user-related history. Existing benchmarks, however, largely assume that such preference can be retrieved from semantically related history. We study an underexplored but practically important regime, profile-preference conceptual misalignment (PRCM), where observable profile cues and query-specific preferences lie in different concept spaces, making semantic retrieval inconsistent for personalization. We introduce VIBE-Bench, a benchmark with two psychology-grounded tasks, 3,504 personas and 12,239 dialogues, including a manually verified gold test set, and requires cross-concept preference reasoning beyond surface semantic overlap. Experiments with several personalization methods show that current PLLMs largely rely on shallow semantic correlations and fail to acquire robust cross-concept mappings. These findings establish PRCM as a distinct failure regime in PLLMs and position VIBE-Bench as a focused testbed for advancing preference reasoning beyond semantic matching.

</details>


### [92] [Few-Shot Out of Domain Intent Detection with Covariance Corrected Mahalanobis Distance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00961)
*Jayasimha Talur,Oleg Smirnov,Paul Missault*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper analyzes why the Mahalanobis distance-based OOD intent detection method fails in few-shot settings and proposes a covariance-corrected Mahalanobis distance to improve performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OOD intent detection is critical for conversational agents, and while Mahalanobis distance works well generally, it underperforms in the practically important few-shot setting where limited training data is available.

Method: The authors analyze the reasons behind the poor few-shot performance of the Mahalanobis distance approach and propose a covariance-corrected Mahalanobis distance for detecting out-of-domain intents.

Result: The proposed covariance-corrected Mahalanobis distance improves OOD intent detection performance in few-shot settings where the original method failed to outperform baselines.

Conclusion: Covariance correction addresses the limitation of Mahalanobis distance in few-shot OOD intent detection, making it effective in low-resource scenarios.

Abstract: Conversational agents like chatbots and voice assistants are trained to understand and respond to user intents. On encountering an utterance with an intent different from the ones they have been trained on, these agents are expected to classify the intent as `unknown' or `out of domain'. This problem is known as out of domain (OOD) intent detection. Podolskiy et al. (2021), showed that Mahalanobis distance can be used effectively for identifying OOD intents, outperforming competing approaches. However, their method fails to outperform the baselines in the practically important few-shot setting. In this paper we analyze the reason for low performance and propose a covariance corrected Mahalanobis distance for detecting out-of-domain intents.

</details>


### [93] [CoBRA: Learning Tool-Use Boundaries via Counterfactual Margins](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00967)
*Wenhao Zou,Xianglong Liu,Wendong Bi,Hanjie Wang,Simin Zhao,Gong Zhi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CoBRA is a counterfactual boundary-learning framework for tool-augmented language models that estimates the instance-level marginal reward benefit of using external tools, partitions data into internal-favored, external-favored, and ambiguous cases, and trains the model to make optimal tool-call decisions via Boundary-Aware Cold-Start SFT followed by MARS-RL with counterfactual marginal advantages.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing tool-calling trigger methods rely on absolute signals (difficulty, confidence, task reward) and lack an explicit estimate of the instance-level marginal benefit of tool use, leading to unnecessary calls (latency, cost, noise, error propagation) or missed calls (hurting knowledge-intensive queries).

Method: CoBRA (1) constructs internal and external experts from the same base model, (2) collects paired trajectories to estimate the reward margin between tool-free and tool-augmented answering, (3) partitions data into internal-favored, external-favored, and ambiguous cases based on this margin, (4) uses clear-margin samples for Boundary-Aware Cold-Start SFT, and (5) optimizes boundary decisions with MARS-RL using reference-split rollouts and counterfactual marginal advantages.

Result: Experiments with retrieval as the main tool on Qwen3-4B show that CoBRA improves tool-use efficiency and boundary-sensitive answer accuracy while maintaining strong performance on tool-dependent out-of-distribution questions.

Conclusion: CoBRA demonstrates that explicitly modeling the counterfactual marginal benefit of tool use enables more efficient and accurate tool-calling decisions without sacrificing robustness on tool-dependent out-of-distribution queries.

Abstract: As large language models increasingly act through external tools, deciding when to call a tool has become a central problem alongside deciding how to use it. Unnecessary tool calls introduce latency, cost, retrieval noise, and error propagation, while missed calls hurt knowledge-intensive queries or questions requiring up-to-date evidence. Existing methods typically trigger tools from absolute query or generation signals, such as difficulty, confidence, or final task reward, and therefore lack an explicit estimate of the instance-level marginal benefit of tool use. We propose CoBRA, a counterfactual boundary-learning framework for tool-augmented language models. CoBRA first constructs internal and external experts from the same base model, collects paired trajectories, and estimates the reward margin between answering with and without tools. This margin partitions data into internal-favored, external-favored, and ambiguous cases. CoBRA then uses clear-margin samples for Boundary-Aware Cold-Start SFT, followed by MARS-RL with reference-split rollouts and counterfactual marginal advantages to optimize boundary decisions. Experiments with retrieval as the main tool on Qwen3-4B show that CoBRA improves tool-use efficiency and boundary-sensitive answer accuracy while maintaining strong performance on tool-dependent out-of-distribution questions.

</details>


### [94] [Figures as Programs: Recursive Generation of Editable Scientific Figures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01006)
*Yepeng Liu,Dasen Dai,Chengzhi Liu,Yiren Song,Hai Ci,Yu Zhang,Qi Zhang,Mike Zheng Shou,Xin Eric Wang,Yuheng Bu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FigTree is a multi-agent system that automatically transforms scientific papers into editable vector figures by formulating figure generation as recursive SVG program construction, using figure decomposition into local regions and a render-critic refinement loop for accurate visual defect repair.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating scientific methodology figures is labor-intensive and requires multiple refinement rounds. Existing image-generation models produce visually appealing raster figures but struggle with single-step human-satisfactory generation, and precise edits to raster figures are difficult for both humans and models.

Method: FigTree formulates scientific figure generation as recursive SVG program construction within a multi-agent system. It grounds figure content in the source paper, decomposes the figure into a hierarchy of local regions, generates each region as a short SVG program, assembles fragments, and uses a render-critic refinement loop that jointly inspects the rendered figure and underlying program to trace visual defects to specific statements for precise repair.

Result: Extensive evaluations show FigTree produces high-quality scientific figures while enabling more effective editing compared to existing raster-based methods.

Conclusion: Representing figures as structured SVG programs with a grounded multi-agent decomposition and render-critic refinement enables both high-quality generation and precise editability, addressing key limitations of raster-based figure generation approaches.

Abstract: Scientific methodology figures are essential for communicating complex methods clearly, yet creating them remains labor-intensive and typically requires multiple rounds of refinement. Recent image-generation models can synthesize visually appealing raster figures, but producing a human-satisfactory result in a single generation step remains difficult. Moreover, precise edits to raster figures are challenging for both humans and models. We formulate scientific figure generation as recursive SVG program construction and propose \textsc{FigTree}, a \textit{multi-agent} system that automatically transforms a scientific paper into a structured vector figure. \textsc{FigTree} grounds figure content in the source paper, decomposes a figure into a hierarchy of local regions, generates each region as a short SVG program, and assembles the resulting fragments. A render-critic refinement loop jointly inspects the rendered figure and its underlying program, enabling visual defects to be traced to specific statements and accurately repaired. We conduct extensive evaluations of \textsc{FigTree} on figure quality and editability, showing that \textsc{FigTree} produces high-quality figures, while also enabling more effective editing than existing raster-based methods.

</details>


### [95] [Spawn Freely, Act Sparingly: Progressive Risk Vesting for Recursive LLM-Agent Trees](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01035)
*Molly Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Progressive Risk Vesting (PRV), a mechanism for governing recursive LLM agents by holding a trajectory-level risk budget in escrow and debiting it as branches are activated for irreversible actions. The authors prove an anytime harm bound for adaptively generated trees, analyze a branching model where trajectory harm transitions as the authority reproduction number R_A crosses one, and derive risk/compute shadow prices from a finite-type occupancy model. Key findings include distinct scaling laws of harm versus local risk p below, at, and above criticality, and a threshold rule for branch activation when marginal value per unit risk decreases. A design rule emerges: search broadly in sandboxed settings but grant recursive authority sparingly with explicit risk charges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recursive LLM agents can spawn specialist branches that may request tools to send data or deploy code. There is an open question of when a branch should receive authority to take irreversible actions. The paper aims to formalize safety controls that distinguish harmless sandboxed exploration from capability activation that crosses irreversible-action boundaries, and to provide provable harm guarantees for adaptive agent trees.

Method: The paper formalizes two mechanisms: sandbox spawning (external controls prevent specified harm) versus capability activation (branch crosses an irreversible-action boundary). It proposes Progressive Risk Vesting (PRV) where a trajectory-level risk budget is held in escrow and debited as branches activate. The authors prove an anytime harm bound for adaptively generated trees under conditional certificate validity given full pre-activation history. They analyze a stylized branching model centered on the authority reproduction number R_A and a finite-type occupancy model yielding risk and compute shadow prices, which produce threshold rules for nested fanout modes.

Result: The paper proves that delayed vesting preserves every policy available under irrevocable spawn charging when gates, branch charges, and compute constraints are fixed. In the branching model, trajectory harm changes discontinuously in character as R_A crosses one: proportional to p below criticality, proportional to sqrt(p) at criticality, and retaining a positive floor above it as local risk p approaches zero. Shadow prices from the occupancy model lead to threshold rules for nested fanout modes with decreasing marginal value per unit risk. Branching calculations and split-sample experiments illustrate the results, with synthetic studies explicitly not estimating safety in deployed agents.

Conclusion: The analysis suggests a practical design rule: search broadly within sandboxed environments but grant recursive authority sparingly, always with an explicit risk charge. The phase-transition behavior at R_A = 1 highlights that criticality in branching authority can qualitatively change harm scaling, and PRV provides a principled accounting mechanism for managing escalation risk in adaptive agent trees.

Abstract: Recursive LLM agents can broaden their search by spawning specialists. Some branches later request tools that send data or deploy code. When should a branch receive authority to act? We distinguish sandbox spawning, in which external controls prevent the specified harm, from capability activation, in which a selected branch crosses an irreversible-action boundary. Progressive Risk Vesting (PRV) holds a trajectory-level risk budget in escrow and debits it as branches are activated. We prove an anytime harm bound for adaptively generated trees. Branch outcomes may be dependent, but each local certificate needs to remain valid conditional on the full pre-activation history, including the information used to select the request. When activation gates, branch charges, and compute constraints are held fixed, delayed vesting preserves every policy available under irrevocable spawn charging. Marginal risk estimates can still fail after branch selection. In a stylized branching model, trajectory harm changes as the authority reproduction number $\mathcal{R}_A$ crosses one. As local risk $p$ approaches zero, trajectory harm is proportional to $p$ below criticality, proportional to $\sqrt{p}$ at criticality, and retains a positive floor above it. A finite-type occupancy model yields risk and compute shadow prices. For nested fanout modes with decreasing marginal value per unit risk, these prices produce a threshold rule. Branching calculations and a split-sample experiment illustrate the results. These synthetic studies do not estimate safety in deployed agents. The analysis suggests a design rule: search broadly in the sandbox and grant recursive authority sparingly, with an explicit risk charge.

</details>


### [96] [Data-Driven Persona-Conditioned Agents for A/B Test Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01038)
*Ziyad Benomar,Weronika Łajewska,Leonardo Perelli,Saab Mansour*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a simulation framework that uses LLM-powered agents conditioned on data-driven personas (built from anonymized real user behavioral data) to predict A/B test outcomes before running real experiments. The framework frames A/B test simulation as a structured question task and systematically studies question design, persona data sources, behavioral depth vs. population diversity trade-offs, and subsampling strategies. On a benchmark of 40 A/B tests, the best configuration achieves 0.75-0.90 directional accuracy depending on the metric type.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional A/B testing requires real user traffic, significant engineering effort, and weeks of measurement per experiment. Prior simulation approaches relied on synthetic or rule-based personas that lack fidelity to real user populations. There is a need for a fast, low-cost pre-screening method to predict experiment outcomes before committing real resources.

Method: The authors construct LLM-powered agent personas from anonymized real user behavioral data including activity patterns, engagement signals, and inferred demographics. They frame A/B test simulation as a structured question-answering task and systematically investigate: (i) question design formats, (ii) effects of persona data source and domain alignment, (iii) the trade-off between per-persona behavioral depth and population diversity, and (iv) efficient population subsampling strategies. The framework is evaluated on a benchmark of 40 A/B tests spanning two metric types.

Result: The best configuration achieves 0.75-0.90 directional accuracy depending on the test metric, demonstrating that data-driven personas provide a viable path toward fast, low-cost experiment pre-screening.

Conclusion: Data-driven personas grounded in real behavioral signals enable more faithful population modeling and can effectively predict A/B test outcomes in simulation, offering a practical alternative or complement to fully live experiments.

Abstract: A/B testing is the gold standard for evaluating product changes, but each experiment requires real user traffic, engineering effort, and weeks of measurement. We propose a simulation framework that predicts A/B test outcomes using LLM-powered agents conditioned on data-driven personas grounded in real user behavioral signals. Unlike prior work that relies on synthetic or rule-based personas, our agents are constructed from anonymized behavioral data-activity patterns, engagement signals, and inferred demographics-enabling more faithful population modeling. We frame A/B test simulation as a structured question task and systematically study (i) question design formats, (ii) the impact of persona data source and domain alignment, (iii) the trade-off between per-persona behavioral depth and population diversity, and (iv) efficient population subsampling. On a benchmark of 40 A/B tests spanning two metric types, our best configuration achieves 0.75-0.90 directional accuracy depending on the test metric, demonstrating that data-driven personas are a viable path toward fast, low-cost experiment pre-screening.

</details>


### [97] [AgentFactory: Towards Automated Agentic System Design and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01045)
*Enci Zhang,Haofeng Wang,Yuesheng Zhu,Xiaole Cui,Guibo Luo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentFactory is a framework that jointly optimizes both foundation models and workflow structures in agentic systems while balancing multiple objectives (performance, cost, and efficiency). It uses advanced LLMs as optimizers with a three-stage optimization pipeline, achieving an average 9.1% improvement over baselines across eight benchmarks in five domains, with particularly strong gains in domain-specific tasks like medicine and finance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current approaches to designing agentic systems rely heavily on manual effort, which limits adaptability and scalability. Existing automated optimization methods overlook the crucial role of model capabilities and focus only on single performance metrics, failing to address real-world deployment constraints such as cost and efficiency.

Method: AgentFactory uses a three-stage optimization pipeline where advanced LLMs act as optimizers to navigate the search space of possible configurations. The framework jointly optimizes both foundation models (including fine-tuning) and workflow structures while considering multiple objectives (performance, cost, efficiency) through an iterative optimization process that adapts to task-specific requirements.

Result: Evaluated across eight benchmarks spanning five domains (general reasoning, coding, mathematics, medicine, finance), AgentFactory consistently outperforms both manually designed methods and existing automated approaches, achieving an average improvement of 9.1%, with 19.6% improvement on MedQA and 18.7% on FinEval.

Conclusion: AgentFactory establishes itself as a promising approach for developing more capable and efficient agentic systems through automated joint optimization of models and workflows under multi-objective constraints.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities as powerful components in agentic systems, enabling sophisticated reasoning and complex task execution. However, current approaches to manually designing and optimizing agentic systems heavily rely on manual effort, limiting their adaptability and scalability. Recent work has explored the automated optimization of workflow designs. However, these approaches often overlook the crucial role of model capabilities and focus on single performance metrics, failing to address real-world deployment constraints. In this paper, we present AgentFactory, a framework that jointly optimizes both foundation models and workflow structures in agentic systems while considering multiple objectives including performance, cost, and efficiency. AgentFactory leverages advanced LLMs as optimizers to navigate the vast search space of possible configurations, employing a three-stage optimization pipeline to automatically discover effective combinations of fine-tuned models and optimized workflows. Through an iterative optimization process, our framework systematically explores and evaluates different agentic system designs, adapting to task-specific requirements while maintaining operational efficiency. We evaluate AgentFactory across eight benchmarks spanning five domains, including general reasoning, coding, mathematics, medicine, and finance. Our experiments demonstrate that AgentFactory consistently outperforms both manually designed methods and existing automated approaches, achieving an average improvement of 9.1% across all benchmarks, with particularly significant gains in domain-specific tasks (19.6% on MedQA and 18.7% on FinEval). These results establish AgentFactory as a promising approach for developing more capable and efficient agentic systems through automated optimization.

</details>


### [98] [QILP-0: Constructing Observational Declarative Twins of Quantum Circuits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01049)
*Marina de la Cruz Echeandía,César Luis Alonso,Tony Ribeiro,Alfonso Ortega de la Puente*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QXymb is a general framework for building observational declarative twins of quantum circuits, with QILP-0 as its first complete order-0 specialization that constructs finite multi-valued propositional logic programs from observed circuit behavior. The pipeline separates logical certification from numerical/discretization uncertainty, and achieves exact, complete, conflict-free reconstruction (strict accuracy = 1) across exhaustive Bars & Stripes (16–100 qubits) and Low-Depth MNIST (all 14,708 digit-0/1 instances) benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a principled, general framework for obtaining declarative (logic-based) twins of quantum circuits — i.e., finite logical programs that exactly reproduce observed circuit behavior within a declared observational scope — while separating logical exactness from numerical, backend, provider, and discretization uncertainty.

Method: QXymb/QILP-0 pipeline: (1) traverses a declared family of quantum observables incrementally under a reproducible structural grading and observational reference horizon; (2) quantifies progress via reference-relative coverage against a fixed target-independent reference; (3) organizes observable responses via target-independent geometry while deterministically mapping latent structure back to original observable columns; (4) converts selected observable profiles into a finite relation via admissible target-independent discretization; (5) uses the target only afterwards to audit twin-admissibility and induce the declarative logic program; (6) certifies exact observational declarative twin status when the theory completely and correctly reconstructs the resulting finite discrete relation.

Result: In Bars & Stripes (16–100 qubits), both product and grid-CZ embeddings were exhaustively analyzed. In Low-Depth MNIST, all 14,708 digit-0/1 instances were analyzed before and after a trained variational quantum transformation. In every reported relation, the induced QILP-0 theory achieved complete, conflict-free reconstruction with strict accuracy exactly equal to one.

Conclusion: QILP-0 delivers certified exact observational declarative twins of quantum circuits in both discrete and continuous (discretized) observational branches, achieving perfect reconstruction on all evaluated settings up to 100 qubits and across full MNIST subset, evidencing that logical exactness can be fully separated from peripheral numerical/backend/discretization uncertainties retained as audit metadata.

Abstract: This paper introduces QXymb, a general framework for constructing observational declarative twins of quantum circuits, and develops QILP-0, its first complete order-0 specialization. QILP-0 constructs a finite multi-valued propositional logic program from observed circuit behaviour within a declared observational scope.
  The pipeline traverses a declared family of quantum observables incrementally according to a reproducible structural grading and a declared observational reference horizon. Progress is quantified through reference-relative coverage against a fixed target-independent reference. Observable responses are organized through target-independent geometry, while retained latent structure is mapped deterministically back to original observable columns before symbolic processing, preserving observational semantics and provenance.
  Selected observable profiles are converted into a finite relation through admissible target-independent discretization. The target is used only afterwards to audit twin-admissibility and induce the declarative theory. A theory is certified as an exact observational declarative twin when it completely and correctly reconstructs the resulting finite task-conditioned discrete relation. Logical exactness is therefore separated from numerical, backend, provider, and discretization uncertainty, which is retained as audit metadata.
  Validation uses two complementary QML settings. Exhaustive Bars & Stripes experiments compare product and grid-CZ embeddings from 16 to 100 qubits and exercise the native-discrete branch. Low-Depth MNIST analyses all 14,708 digit-0/1 instances before and after a trained variational quantum transformation and exercises continuous discretization. In every reported relation, the induced QILP-0 theory achieves complete, conflict-free reconstruction with strict accuracy equal to one.

</details>


### [99] [WorldBench: Culturally Grounded Benchmark for Multilingual Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01056)
*Leonardo Ranaldi,Sherrie Shen,Jushi Kai,Alexandra Birch*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WorldBench is a comprehensive, multilingual benchmark of 1,600 persona-grounded everyday workflow tasks across seven languages and eight cultures, where LLM agents act via structured actions in a sandbox. It introduces Constrained Task Success (CTS) combining instructions and testbeds to score task completion and environment preservation. Frontier models achieve only 49.2% CTS, revealing gaps between correctness and state preservation, particularly in long-horizon and multilingual scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks for LLM-powered multi-step agents rarely test state preservation, cross-lingual performance, and realistic grounded scenarios, leaving a gap in evaluating agents on genuine, persona-grounded everyday workflows.

Method: WorldBench comprises 1,600 tasks across seven languages and eight cultures, filtered and refined via human annotator feedback with cultural/linguistic expertise. Agents act in a sandbox via structured actions. The evaluation introduces Constrained Task Success (CTS), which combines natural language instructions and testbeds to score task completion, minimal modification, and complementary metrics through deterministic and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations.

Result: Frontier models reach only 49.2% CTS. All models exhibit large gaps between correctness and environment preservation, demonstrating brittleness in multilingual agentic scenarios, especially for long-horizon tasks and under state-preservation constraints.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents remain brittle in multilingual, agentic settings; the gap between task correctness and environment preservation indicates a need for improving agent robustness and state-preserving behavior in realistic cross-cultural workflows.

Abstract: Despite the growing use of LLM-powered agents to solve multi-step tasks in complex environments, existing benchmarks rarely test state preservation, performance across languages, and application to realistic, grounded scenarios. To address these concerns, we present WorldBench: a comprehensive, multilingual benchmark of genuine, persona-grounded everyday workflows, where agents can act in a sandbox via structured actions. WorldBench comprises 1,600 tasks across seven languages and eight cultures, filtered and refined through feedback from human annotators with language- and culture-specific expertise. For evaluation, we extend metrics from previous works and introduce Constrained Task Success (CTS), which combines natural language instructions and testbeds to score task completion, minimal modification, and other complementary metrics through deterministic and LLM-as-a-Judge evaluations. Our experiments show that frontier models reach only 49.2% CTS, with all models demonstrating large gaps between correctness and environment preservation. We thereby show that current agents remain brittle in multilingual, agentic scenarios, especially for long-horizon tasks and under state-preservation constraints

</details>


### [100] [User Representation via Cross Multi-source Behavior Pre-training for Mobile Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01057)
*Chengqi Yang,Yiran Qiao,Feng Liu,Xingyu Lou,Zijun Zhou,Xiaoyun Mo,Changwang Zhang,Jiayuan Xu,Jun Wang,Xiang Ao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CM-PTM is a cross multi-source behavior pre-training model for mobile game user representation learning that uses hierarchical cascaded mask-then-predict proxy tasks on device-level behavioral logs to jointly model cross-source dependencies and fine-grained app-action dynamics, achieving significant gains on downstream recommendation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing user representation pre-training methods focus on single-app or app-level behaviors, overlooking the cross-source and multi-granular nature of user activities at the device level, where intent emerges from complex interactions among heterogeneous behavior sources and hierarchical action structures.

Method: CM-PTM employs hierarchical cascaded mask-then-predict proxy tasks: first inferring the source of the next behavior, then progressively refining predictions at the app-action level. This unified pre-training paradigm jointly captures cross-source dependencies and fine-grained behavioral dynamics.

Result: Extensive experiments on large-scale real-world mobile datasets show that CM-PTM effectively captures users' endogenous interests and consistently delivers significant performance gains on downstream mobile game recommendation tasks.

Conclusion: Device-level, cross-source, multi-granular pre-training is a viable and effective paradigm for mobile user representation learning, outperforming conventional app-centric approaches on downstream personalization tasks.

Abstract: User representation pre-training has become a fundamental paradigm for alleviating data sparsity in downstream personalization tasks. However, existing studies predominantly focus on single-app or app-level behaviors, overlooking the inherently cross-source and multi-granular nature of user activities on mobile devices. At the device level, user intent emerges from complex interactions among heterogeneous behavior sources and hierarchical action structures, posing challenges that cannot be addressed by conventional app-centric modeling. To tackle this issue, we propose CM-PTM, a novel Cross Multi-source Behavior Pre-Training Model tailored for mobile game user representation learning on device-level behavioral logs. CM-PTM employs hierarchical cascaded mask-then-predict proxy tasks that first infer the source of the next behavior and then progressively refine predictions at the app-action level. This design enables unified modeling of cross-source dependencies and fine-grained behavioral dynamics within a single pre-training paradigm. Extensive experiments on large-scale real-world mobile datasets demonstrate that CM-PTM effectively captures users' endogenous interests and consistently delivers significant performance gains on downstream mobile game recommendation tasks.

</details>


### [101] [ARISE-RL: Agentic Rubric-Grounded Iterative Self-Evolution with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01058)
*Fanrui Zhang,Ruixue Ding,Qiang Zhang,Xi Chen,Boli Chen,Shihang Wang,Qiuchen Wang,Hongmin Zhan,Jinxin Bian,Li xingchao,Peijin Zheng,Hao cheng,Pengjun Xie,Kaipeng Zhang,Jiawei Liu,Zheng-Jun Zha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ARISE-RL is a full-cycle self-evolution framework for open-ended agent training that couples a task/rubric Generator and a reasoning Solver through rubric-mediated co-evolution. It addresses unstable rewards in long-horizon agentic tasks by grounding rubric criteria in real tool observations, learning from fine-grained rubric satisfaction signals, and using Reward-Gated Self-Evolution Distillation (RG-SED) to selectively distill memory-augmented policy variants back into itself only when empirical reward improves. The authors also introduce ECR-Bench, an expert-calibrated rubric benchmark covering single-tool deep research and multi-tool travel planning, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Open-ended agent training via RL suffers from (1) lack of verifiable gold answers and scalable rubrics, and (2) brittle/unstable rewards in long-horizon tasks that yield weak rollout contrast, obscuring fine-grained optimization signals for group-based policy learning.

Method: ARISE-RL couples a task/rubric Generator with a reasoning Solver through rubric-mediated co-evolution. The Generator produces valid intermediate-difficulty tasks grounded in real tool observations aligned with the Solver's capability boundary. The Solver learns from fine-grained rubric satisfaction signals via multi-step reasoning and tool use. A Reward-Gated Self-Evolution Distillation (RG-SED) mechanism selectively distills a memory-augmented variant of the same policy back into itself only when empirical reward improvement is confirmed, preventing distribution mismatch and blind imitation of noisy guidance.

Result: Extensive experiments show ARISE-RL achieves robust and stable overall state-of-the-art performance across all evaluated benchmarks, including the newly introduced ECR-Bench covering single-tool deep research and multi-tool travel planning.

Conclusion: ARISE-RL effectively addresses reward instability and scalability limitations in open-ended RL agent training through rubric-mediated co-evolution and reward-gated self-distillation, establishing a robust full-cycle self-evolution paradigm and a rigorous expert-calibrated evaluation benchmark.

Abstract: Training open-ended agents via reinforcement learning (RL) is hindered by the lack of verifiable gold answers and scalable rubrics. Moreover, even near the model's capability boundary, long-horizon open-ended agentic tasks often yield brittle and unstable rewards, resulting in weak or noisy rollout contrast that obscures fine-grained optimization signals for group-based policy learning. To address these challenges, we propose ARISE-RL, a novel full-cycle self-evolution framework that couples a task/rubric Generator and a reasoning Solver through rubric-mediated co-evolution. The Generator grounds tool-related rubric criteria in real tool observations and is rewarded for producing valid, intermediate-difficulty tasks aligned with the Solver's evolving capability boundary. The Solver, in turn, learns from fine-grained rubric satisfaction signals through multi-step reasoning and tool use. We further introduce Reward-Gated Self-Evolution Distillation (RG-SED), which selectively distills a memory-augmented variant of the same policy back into itself only when the memory yields empirical reward improvement, thereby reducing distribution mismatch and avoiding blind imitation of noisy guidance. Finally, to support rigorous evaluation, we present ECR-Bench, an expert-calibrated rubric benchmark suite covering single-tool deep research and multi-tool travel planning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that ARISE-RL consistently achieves robust and stable overall state-of-the-art performance across all evaluated benchmarks.

</details>


### [102] [Space Generative AI with Solar Energy Harvesting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01062)
*Jierui Zhang,Jianhao Huang,Zhanwei Wang,Kaibin Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a framework for solar-powered satellite-based generative AI, where a satellite executes a diffusion-based image-generation model and downlinks results within strict time windows. It identifies fundamental computation-communication (C2) trade-offs under shared harvested-energy budgets and develops a joint resource-optimization framework. A two-step approach first characterizes maximum downlink throughput under continuous solar energy harvesting (establishing a separation principle), then derives a closed-form, low-complexity step-selection policy in the dominant constant-power regime. Experiments under realistic orbital dynamics show significant end-to-end performance gains over static baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Satellites can extend generative AI to remote areas lacking terrestrial infrastructure, but their deployment is constrained by limited, time-varying solar energy harvesting. There is a need to balance computation (generation steps) and communication (downlink) under shared energy budgets to maximize end-to-end generative performance.

Method: The authors exploit deterministic orbital motion to predict solar-EH dynamics and propose a two-step approach: (1) characterize maximum downlink throughput for a fixed generation depth under continuous solar EH, establishing a separation principle decoupling waiting-time selection from transmit-power control; (2) formulate a joint C2 utility-maximization problem and derive a closed-form, low-complexity step-selection policy in the dominant constant-power regime.

Result: Extensive experiments under realistic orbital dynamics demonstrate that the proposed policy dynamically balances generation quality and transmission reliability, yielding significant E2E performance gains over static computation-centric and communication-centric baselines across diverse solar-EH states.

Conclusion: The framework and derived policies enable effective energy-aware scheduling of space-based generative AI services, demonstrating that leveraging predictable solar-energy dynamics from orbital motion allows tractable joint optimization of computation and communication resources for improved end-to-end generative performance.

Abstract: Satellites are emerging as promising platforms to extend generative \emph{artificial intelligence} (AI) services to remote areas lacking terrestrial infrastructure. However, deploying space generative AI is fundamentally constrained by the limited, time-varying onboard energy supplied by solar \emph{energy harvesting} (EH). This paper presents a framework for solar-powered space generative AI in which a satellite receives a user prompt, executes a diffusion-based image-generation model, and downlinks the compressed result within a strict time window. We identify the fundamental \emph{computation--communication} (C$^2$) trade-offs governed by the shared harvested-energy budgets. Specifically, increasing the number of generation steps improves intrinsic image quality but depletes energy and time available for downlink transmission, whereas prioritizing communication guarantees reliable delivery but sacrifices semantic quality. To balance these trade-offs and maximize \emph{end-to-end} (E2E) generative performance, we exploit the predictable solar-EH dynamics induced by deterministic orbital motion and develop a joint C$^2$ resource-optimization framework using a tractable two-step approach. First, we characterize the maximum downlink throughput for a fixed generation depth under continuous solar EH. This establishes a separation principle that decouples waiting-time selection from optimal transmit-power control. Next, we formulate a joint C$^2$ utility-maximization problem and derive a closed-form, low-complexity step-selection policy in the dominant constant-power regime. Extensive experiments under realistic orbital dynamics demonstrate that the proposed policy dynamically balances generation quality and transmission reliability. This yields significant E2E performance gains over static computation- and communication-centric baselines across diverse solar-EH states.

</details>


### [103] [Latent Recurrent Thoughts: Recurrent Refinement of Proposed Latents for Reasoning with Frozen LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01117)
*Zhaoliang Chen,Jie Fu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LRT (Latent Recurrent Thoughts) performs reasoning in a model's continuous representation space using a tiny recurrent reasoner that refines latent states over many steps, while a frozen LLM decodes the answer. This decouples computational depth from model size and eliminates the need for explicit reasoning traces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chain-of-thought reasoning in discrete token space has inherent limitations: errors propagate across committed steps, and eliciting good traces requires imitation data of reasoning traces. Reasoning in continuous representation space (vectors rather than words) avoids these constraints, but raises the question of how latent states should be computed.

Method: LRT keeps a frozen LLM for sequence modeling/decoding while a task-dedicated proposer supplies base latent thoughts. A tiny recurrent reasoner refines these latents through bounded residual corrections over many steps, enabling iterative processing whose depth is decoupled from model size. The frozen LLM then decodes the final answer from the refined latents.

Result: On symbolic reasoning tasks (Countdown-4, Sudoku) with answer supervision but no reasoning traces, and natural-language reasoning tasks (HumanEval, MBPP, StrategyQA), LRT substantially outperforms prior frozen-decoder continuous-space reasoning methods under identical decoder, prompt, data, and training budget. It also outperforms non-thinking-mode chain-of-thought prompting on the same backbone at a small fraction of inference compute.

Conclusion: Iterative latent-state reasoning guided by a frozen LLM decoder offers a more efficient and effective alternative to discrete token-space chain-of-thought, avoiding error propagation and the need for reasoning trace supervision while decoupling reasoning depth from model capacity.

Abstract: Chain-of-thought reasoning unfolds in discrete token space: each step is committed as text, errors propagate, and eliciting good traces presupposes traces to imitate. Reasoning instead in a model's continuous representation space - where intermediate states are vectors rather than words - sidesteps these constraints, but leaves open how those latent states should be computed. We approach this along two axes. First, we keep a large language model (LLM) frozen and use it for what it is already good at - modeling and decoding sequences - while a small auxiliary network supplies continuous latent thoughts as input. Second, we produce those latents by recurrence: a tiny recurrent reasoner refines them over many steps, decoupling the depth of computation from the size of the model, so that the latents are a product of iterative processing rather than a single forward pass. We instantiate this as Latent Recurrent Thoughts (LRT): a task-dedicated proposer supplies base latents, a recurrent reasoner refines them through bounded residual corrections, and the frozen LLM decodes the answer. On symbolic reasoning with answer supervision but no reasoning traces (Countdown-4, Sudoku) and on natural-language reasoning (HumanEval, MBPP, StrategyQA), LRT substantially outperforms prior frozen-decoder continuous-space reasoning methods under an identical decoder, prompt, data, and training budget, and outperforms non-thinking-mode chain-of-thought prompting on the same backbone at a small fraction of its inference compute.

</details>


### [104] [Jailbreaking Text-to-Image Models Through Cracks: Navigating Heterogeneous Safety Filters via Multi-Agent Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01168)
*Kaiyan Wen,Shijie Zhang,Lu Yu,Guangdong Bai*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces the Detection Surface, a geometric framework characterizing decision boundaries of heterogeneous T2I safety filters, and proposes CRACK, a multi-agent debate framework for adaptive jailbreak search against composite defenses, achieving up to 99.63% attack success rate with fewer queries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing jailbreak studies against T2I models either optimize against individual filters or query the complete pipeline with aggregate feedback, making it difficult to identify the active constraint and adapt to conflicts across heterogeneous, multi-layer safety stacks.

Method: The authors introduce a unified geometric framework called Detection Surface to characterize decision boundaries of heterogeneous safety filters and their joint effects. Based on this analysis, they propose CRACK, a multi-agent debate framework with three agents (Attack Agent, Defense Agent, Judge Agent) that decompose jailbreak search into exploration, diagnosis, and arbitration, using layer-specific diagnostic feedback and reward-guided refinement for adaptive multimodal reinforcement learning.

Result: CRACK achieves Attack Success Rates (ASR) of up to 99.63% under composite defenses across multiple T2I models, datasets, and safety configurations, while requiring fewer queries than existing methods and maintaining semantic fidelity.

Conclusion: The Detection Surface framework reveals that successful evasion is governed by a sparse, non-convex region shaped by cross-layer conflicts, and CRACK demonstrates that adaptive debate-based search can effectively navigate these constraints, outperforming existing jailbreak methods under composited safety defenses.

Abstract: Text-to-image (T2I) models remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks that elicit Not-Safe-For-Work (NSFW) content, despite increasingly being guarded by heterogeneous, multi-layer safety stacks combining text filters, image classifiers, and cross-modal detectors. Existing jailbreak studies either optimize against individual filters or query the complete pipeline with aggregate feedback, making it difficult to identify the active constraint and adapt to conflicts across safety layers.In this paper, we introduce the \emph{Detection Surface}, a unified geometric framework that characterizes the decision boundaries induced by heterogeneous T2I safety filters and their joint effect on the jailbreak search space. This formulation reveals that successful evasion is governed by a sparse and non-convex region shaped by cross-layer conflicts, where mutations that bypass one filter may increase exposure to another. Motivated by this analysis, we propose \emph{CRACK}, a multi-agent debate framework for adaptive jailbreak search that decomposes jailbreak search into exploration, diagnosis, and arbitration. CRACK coordinates an Attack Agent, a Defense Agent, and a Judge Agent to iteratively generate prompt mutations, obtain layer-specific diagnostic feedback, and optimize mutation strategies through reward-guided refinement. Through repeated rounds of debate, CRACK adapts its search direction to the evolving cross-layer constraints while preserving the original harmful intent. Extensive experiments across multiple T2I models, datasets, and safety configurations show that CRACK achieves Attack Success Rates (ASR) of up to 99.63\% under composite defenses, while requiring fewer queries than existing methods and maintaining semantic fidelity.

</details>


### [105] [FinLifeBench: Exhaustive Life-Event History and Financial-State Reconstruction from Longitudinal Banking Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01198)
*Hangyeul Lee,Juyoung Oh,Jaeyong Ko,Sunmin Kim,Jaeik Park,Hyunkyu Kim,Jungmin Son,Pilsung Kang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces FinLifeBench, a benchmark with 6,000 Korean banking sessions from 20 synthetic trajectories, evaluating two cumulative-dialogue tasks: reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session, and reconstructing a complete 34-path financial state at checkpoints. Across 11 LLMs, event-anchor recall drops from 0.591 (15 sessions) to 0.445 (300), with errors dominated by omitted events; financial-state reconstruction struggles with outdated info (best GCA@15 = 0.470). The two tasks show only weak association, indicating models recover evidence but fail to maintain complete, temporally valid longitudinal records.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks focus on QA, bounded episodes, or targeted recall rather than exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction across cumulative banking interactions. There is a need to evaluate whether assistants can maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records as life changes emerge incidentally over time.

Method: FinLifeBench evaluates two tasks on the same cumulative dialogue: (1) reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session, and (2) reconstructing a complete 34-path financial state at consecutive checkpoints. It uses 6,000 eight-turn Korean banking sessions from 20 independent synthetic trajectories with deterministic exhaustive gold labels for 24 event types and 34 state paths, plus consensus QA. Eleven LLMs are evaluated under full-context conditions.

Result: Across eleven LLMs, event-anchor recall falls from 0.591 at 15 sessions to 0.445 at 300 sessions. Errors are primarily from omitted events rather than poor anchor localization. Financial-state reconstruction frequently treats superseded or potentially outdated information as current, with best GCA@15 reaching 0.470. Performance on the two reconstruction tasks is only weakly associated.

Conclusion: Models can localize evidence for recovered events yet still fail to maintain complete and temporally valid longitudinal records, revealing a gap between event recovery and sustained longitudinal state maintenance in LLM-based banking assistants.

Abstract: Repeated banking interactions require assistants to maintain complete, current, and traceable customer records as life changes emerge incidentally in routine requests. Existing benchmarks emphasize question answering, bounded episodes, or targeted recall rather than exhaustive longitudinal reconstruction. We introduce FinLifeBench, which evaluates two tasks over the same cumulative dialogue: reconstructing every life-event instance with its first-establishing session and reconstructing a complete 34-path financial state at consecutive checkpoints. The benchmark contains 6,000 eight-turn Korean banking sessions from 20 independent synthetic trajectories, with deterministic, exhaustive gold for 24 event types and 34 state paths and consensus quality assurance. Across eleven LLMs under a full-context condition, event-anchor recall falls from 0.591 at 15 sessions to 0.445 at 300. Errors are driven primarily by omitted events rather than poor anchor localization, while financial-state reconstruction frequently treats superseded or potentially outdated information as current; the best GCA@15 reaches 0.470. Performance on the two reconstruction tasks is only weakly associated. These results show that models can localize evidence for recovered events while still failing to maintain complete and temporally valid longitudinal records.

</details>


### [106] [H2Table: Hierarchical Hypergraph-Enhanced Large Language Models for Complex Table Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01216)
*Jia Ling,Yangfan Wang,Chen Tang,Haoming Tan,Yang Yang,Yi Guan,Jingchi Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: H2Table is a novel framework that represents complex tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs, uses a tailored hypergraph encoder for message passing between headers and cells, and employs learnable query vectors to bridge structural embeddings into LLMs, achieving significant improvements on complex table QA tasks (e.g., +22.88% over SOTA on HiTab with depth-4 nested tables).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern LLMs struggle with table reasoning because current approaches linearize tables into sequences, which overlooks the intrinsic two-dimensional and hierarchical structure of tables. This loss of structural information limits performance especially on complex tables with nested headers.

Method: H2Table represents complex tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs. It designs a specialized hypergraph encoder that enables message passing between hyperedges (headers) and nodes (cells) to capture semantic entailment relationships. A set of learnable query vectors serves as a lightweight bridge to extract representative structural embeddings from the encoder and feed them into the LLM.

Result: The approach effectively handles complex table QA tasks with hierarchical nested headers. On the HiTab dataset, H2Table achieves an average improvement of 22.88% over state-of-the-art baselines on highly complex tables with a nesting depth of four.

Conclusion: Modeling tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs with a dedicated hypergraph encoder significantly improves table reasoning over complex structured tables, outperforming linearization-based approaches, especially at deeper nesting levels.

Abstract: Tables are ubiquitous across diverse domains, yet reasoning over them remains a significant challenge for modern large language models (LLMs). Current approaches typically linearize tables into sequences, inherently overlooking their intrinsic two-dimensional and hierarchical structure. To address this, we propose H2Table (Hierarchical Hypergraph-Enhanced Table Reasoning), a novel framework that represents complex tables as hierarchical nested hypergraphs. To process this representation, we design a tailored hypergraph encoder to facilitate message passing between hyperedges (headers) and nodes (cells), thereby perceiving the semantic entailment relationships between them within complex tables. Furthermore, we introduce a set of learnable query vectors acting as a lightweight bridge to extract representative structural embeddings from the encoder into the LLM. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach effectively handles complex table question answering tasks with hierarchical nested headers. Notably, on the HiTab dataset, H2Table achieves an average improvement of 22.88% over state-of-the-art baselines on highly complex tables with a nesting depth of four. Our code is available at: https://github.com/lila120/h2table.

</details>


### [107] [Prompt-Robust Language Models: Which Training Strategies Work?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01217)
*Frederic Sadrieh,Michal Štefánik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper systematically reproduces and compares strategies for addressing large language models' sensitivity to prompt formulation, including robustness fine-tuning methods (CoIN, PPCL) and data construction strategies. Results show that while robustness fine-tuning improves over standard fine-tuning and in-context learning, the best-to-worst prompt gap remains 40-57% of performance. Surprisingly, simpler data construction (one template per batch) often outperforms the recent robustness-enhancing methods. Diagnostics reveal auxiliary objectives only move their penalized quantity without generalizing, and per-template gradient conflicts on 57-64% of parameters explain why mixing formulations in batches hampers optimization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models remain highly sensitive to prompt formulation despite strong overall performance. Prior work has proposed solutions via refined data construction or dedicated robustness objectives, but these have not been systematically compared under controlled conditions to measure their true effectiveness in addressing prompt sensitivity.

Method: The authors reproduce and compare existing robustness strategies under controlled conditions, testing standard fine-tuning, in-context learning, robustness fine-tuning methods (CoIN for contrastive alignment, PPCL for consistency regularization), and data construction strategies (training on one template per batch). They also perform diagnostics analyzing per-template gradient signs and whether auxiliary objectives generalize beyond their penalized quantities.

Result: Robustness fine-tuning improves over standard fine-tuning and in-context learning, but the best-to-worst prompt gap remains 40-57% of performance. CoIN and PPCL often fail to outperform the simplest data construction strategy of one template per batch. Diagnostics show auxiliary objectives move their penalized quantities without generalizing, and per-template gradients have conflicting signs on 57-64% of parameters, meaning mixed-template batches force the optimizer to reconcile competing updates.

Conclusion: Current robustness-enhancing methods are insufficient for eliminating prompt sensitivity in LLMs. Simple data construction strategies can be more effective than sophisticated auxiliary objectives. The findings suggest that prompt-agnostic learning is hindered by gradient conflicts when formulations are mixed, and future approaches should address this underlying optimization challenge rather than relying on auxiliary objectives that do not generalize.

Abstract: Despite their strong performance, large language models remain highly sensitive to prompt formulation. Prior work addresses this through refined data construction or through dedicated robustness objectives. We reproduce and compare these strategies under controlled conditions, and measure how effective they are in addressing models' prompt sensitivity. We find the current robustness fine-tuning methods improve over standard fine-tuning and in-context learning, but the best-to-worst prompt gap remains as high as 40-57% of performance. Moreover, the recent robustness-enhancing methods we test - CoIN for contrastive alignment and PPCL for consistency regularization - often fail to outperform the simplest data construction strategy: training on one template per batch. Our diagnostics explain these results. The auxiliary objectives move the quantity they penalize, but do not generalize beyond it. Additionally, data construction strategies differ due to the conflicting signs of per-template gradients on 57-64% of parameters. Thus, batches that mix formulations force the optimizer to reconcile competing updates instead of finding a shared, prompt-agnostic one.

</details>


### [108] [Measuring the Behavioral Fidelity of Long-Horizon Human Activity Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01257)
*Yi Fei Cheng,Fan Yang,Iremsu Bas,Koichiro Niinuma,Narishige Abe,David Lindlbauer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces a framework for evaluating behavioral fidelity in long-horizon activity simulations and finds, through a 43-hour office activity case study, that statistical priors best match real activity distributions but over-fragment routines and suppress within-person variability, motivating holistic multi-metric evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based human simulators are increasingly used in policy, evaluation, and training contexts, yet their behavioral fidelity in long-horizon real-world activity remains largely unexplored compared to survey responses and dialogue. Faithful reproduction of real behavioral patterns is critical for trustworthy simulation.

Method: The authors collect a 43-hour multi-camera dataset of in-the-wild office activity and evaluate behavioral fidelity across temporal granularities (e.g., event-level, sequence-level) and levels of analysis. They compare trace-derived conditioning mechanisms for LLM simulation: persona descriptors, few-shot exemplars, and statistical transition and time-of-day priors.

Result: Behavioral fidelity is not uniform across metrics. Statistical priors yield activity and sequence distributions closest to real behavior, but over-fragment routines and suppress within-person variability compared to other conditioning mechanisms.

Conclusion: A holistic evaluation framework spanning multiple metrics, temporal granularities, and levels of analysis is necessary to properly assess behavioral fidelity in long-horizon activity simulations, as single-conditioning approaches trade off different aspects of realism.

Abstract: As LLM-based human simulators are increasingly used for policy, evaluation, and training, they must faithfully reproduce real behavioral patterns. While prior work has examined behavioral fidelity in survey responses and dialogue, longer-horizon real-world activity remains largely unexplored. We introduce a framework for evaluating behavioral fidelity in long-horizon activity simulations across temporal granularities and levels of analysis. As a case study, we collect a 43-hour multi-camera dataset of in-the-wild office activity and compare trace-derived conditioning mechanisms: persona descriptors, few-shot exemplars, and statistical transition and time-of-day priors. We find that behavioral fidelity is not uniform across metrics: statistical priors bring activity and sequence distributions closest to real behavior, yet over-fragment routines and suppress within-person variability. These findings motivate a more holistic evaluation that spans multiple metrics, temporal granularities, and levels of analysis.

</details>


### [109] [Making Prospective Memory SLM-Shaped: Typed Intention Stores for Small-Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01272)
*Jinqing Zhao,Chengcan Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Prospective Intention Store (PIS), a training-free, agentic scaffold that puts lifecycle logic in code and scoped language work on the model, enabling small models to far outperform prior state-of-the-art scaffolds on the PM-Bench prospective memory benchmark.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier LLMs struggle at prospective memory tasks—the best published scaffold only reaches 65.1% Set-F1. The authors argue the bottleneck is schema-constrained state tracking rather than open-ended reasoning, implying small models could handle it if the action space were properly typed.

Method: PIS externalizes lifecycle logic into code (the "intention store") while limiting language-model responsibilities to scoped actions within a typed action space. It is agentic but training-free, requiring neither selector fine-tuning nor trajectory distillation.

Result: DeepSeek-Chat with PIS achieves 82.9% Set-F1 on PM-Bench. On Gemma-E2B, PIS reaches 66.2% vs. 4.2% without a store and 6.6% with seven retrospective memories. PIS achieves 70.1% Set-F1 where retrospective memory methods max out at 54.4%, setting a new state of the art and enabling small models to surpass the published large-model scaffold.

Conclusion: Structural state tracking via an external typed store can substitute for massive model scale in prospective memory, and decomposing the problem into code-managed lifecycle logic plus scoped language work yields substantially better performance than end-to-end (or retrospective-memory-based) approaches.

Abstract: Prospective memory means carrying out a deferred intention at the right future cue while other work continues. Benchmarks now isolate it as an agent skill, yet frontier LLMs still struggle: the best published PM-Bench scaffold reaches only 65.1% Set-F1. We argue that this loop is schema-constrained state tracking rather than open-ended reasoning, and that small models can execute it when the action space is typed. We propose the Prospective Intention Store (PIS) that puts lifecycle logic in code and scoped language work on the model. The scaffold is agentic and training-free: no selector fine-tuning and no trajectory distillation. On PM-Bench, DeepSeek-Chat with PIS reaches 82.9% Set-F1. On Gemma-E2B, Set-F1 is only 4.2% without a store and at most 6.6% under seven retrospective memories, while PIS reaches 66.2%. PIS further reaches 70.1% Set-F1, where retrospective memory methods stay at most 54.4%. PIS sets a new state of the art on this benchmark and enables small models to surpass the published large-model scaffold.

</details>


### [110] [Analog-DB: An Agent-First Analog Integrated Circuit Database, From Blocks to Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01286)
*Danial Noori Zadeh,Mohamed B. Elamien*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents analog-db, an open-source, versioned database for sharing analog integrated circuit designs via a shareable design representation. It uses a domain-specific language to capture designs as process-neutral topologies, reusable testbenches, and machine-readable datasheets. The system enables circuit composability, retargetability, and AI-agent reuse. Across a 23-circuit regulator corpus, all bindings passed their specification bands; 17 of 23 failed imported sizings were repaired via gm/ID sizing loops; and a supervised case study showed a coding agent fixed real defects in an instrumentation amplifier. The database contains 68 circuits across 16 classes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sharing analog integrated circuit designs is difficult because foundry non-disclosure agreements restrict process details and testbenches behind published results are rarely released, limiting reproducibility and reuse.

Method: The approach uses a domain-specific language to represent each design as a process-neutral topology with reusable testbenches and a machine-readable datasheet under one schema. A parameterization scheme exposes functional sub-blocks and device sizes as named parameters with matching constraints, enabling composability and retargetability. A schema-governed contract and queryable catalog allow AI design agents to discover and reuse designs directly.

Result: All 23 circuit-kit bindings on three open kits met their specification bands (typical corner, matched devices, no layout), 10 of 23 met a common class band. Seventeen of 23 imported sizings that failed testbenches closed under gm/ID sizing loops within one to three iterations. A coding agent fixed four hand-entry defects and a missing common-mode feedback loop in a chopper instrumentation amplifier on an open 130nm kit. The database holds 68 circuits across sixteen classes.

Conclusion: The analog-db system demonstrates that a shareable, process-neutral design representation with versioning and schema governance can enable full design sharing, automatic retargeting, and AI-assisted design repair, overcoming the traditional barriers of foundry NDA restrictions and missing testbenches in analog IC design.
