Abstract: Sharing analog integrated circuit designs remains difficult: foundry non-disclosure agreements restrict the process details a design depends on, and the testbenches behind published results are rarely released. We present analog-db, an open-source, versioned database built on a shareable design representation. A domain-specific language captures each design as a process-neutral topology, reusable testbenches, and a machine-readable datasheet under one schema, so a design is shared in full and re-simulates on the process kits it is bound to. A parameterization scheme exposes functional sub-blocks and device sizes as named parameters that carry their matching constraints, making circuits composable and retargetable; a schema-governed contract and queryable catalog let AI design agents discover and reuse them directly. Across the regulator corpus, all 23 circuit-kit bindings on three open kits meet their own recorded specification bands (typical corner, matched devices, no layout) and 10 of 23 meet a common class band. Seventeen of the 23 imported sizings failed their testbenches and closed under a gm/ID sizing loop driven by the annotated sub-block roles, typically within one to three iterations. In a supervised case study, a coding agent working from the released artifacts sized the op-amp cores of a chopper instrumentation amplifier on an open 130nm kit, locating four hand-entry defects and a missing common-mode feedback loop that the sizing-only baseline did not repair. The database holds 68 circuits across sixteen classes, verifiable at schematic level under a tiered harness and tracked on a power/performance scoreboard, released at https://github.com/MacAnalog/spicexplorer-release.

</details>


### [111] [A Composable Evaluation System for Reproducible Omni-Modal Foundation Model Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01315)
*Hodong Lee,Sanghee Park,Dohoon Ryu,Jungwhan Kim,Junyeob Kim,Soyoon Kim,Geewook Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OmniEvaluator is a unified omni-modal evaluation framework that connects existing inference engines (four backends) and curated evaluation libraries (four frameworks) covering 1,000+ benchmarks across text, image, video, and audio through a single interface. It records every run as a fully configurable artifact for exact reproduction, aggregates results into a shared dashboard for cross-model comparison, supports federated GPU sharing across concurrent evaluations, and includes a CPU-runnable verifier that stabilizes rule-based scoring under configuration mismatch—matching commercial LLM judges without recurring API costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluation toolkits for text, image, video, and audio are mutually incompatible in their inference engines, prompt conventions, and metric implementations, forcing practitioners to maintain separate environments per toolchain and struggle with cross-modal result comparison. OmniEvaluator addresses this by unifying existing evaluation infrastructure rather than reimplementing it.

Method: OmniEvaluator operates as a higher-level orchestration layer that connects existing inference engines and curated evaluation libraries. It exposes four inference backends and four evaluation frameworks supporting over 1,000 benchmarks through a single interface. Each run is logged as an artifact capturing the full configuration for exact reproduction. A federated mode shares GPU inference servers across concurrent evaluations. A lightweight verifier runs on CPU to keep scores stable across engines and prompt variants where rule-based scoring fluctuates under configuration mismatch.

Result: The built-in verifier demonstrates score stability across engines and prompts, matching the performance of cost-efficient commercial LLM judges while avoiding recurring API costs. The system, demo video, and dashboard are publicly available.

Conclusion: OmniEvaluator provides a practical, unified solution for omni-modal model evaluation by integrating existing toolchains, ensuring reproducibility through artifact logging, enabling cross-model comparison via a shared dashboard, and reducing cost and infrastructure overhead via federated GPU sharing and a CPU-based verifier.

Abstract: Building an omni-modal foundation model means evaluating it across text, image, video, and audio. Excellent evaluation toolkits exist for each modality, but their inference engines, prompt conventions, and metric implementations are mutually incompatible, so practitioners end up maintaining separate environments for every toolchain and still struggle to compare results across them. OmniEvaluator grew out of this need in our own model development: rather than reimplementing benchmarks, it connects existing inference engines and curated evaluation libraries at a higher level, exposing four inference backends, four evaluation frameworks, and over a thousand benchmarks through a single interface. Every run is recorded as an artifact capturing the full configuration for exact reproduction, and results flow into a shared dashboard for cross-model comparison. A federated mode shares GPU inference servers across concurrent evaluations, and a built-in verifier, small enough to run on CPU, keeps its score stable across engines and prompts where rule-based scoring fluctuates under configuration mismatch, matching cost-efficient commercial LLM judges without their recurring API cost. The system, demo video, and dashboard are publicly available. (https://github.com/naver-ai/omni-evaluator)

</details>


### [112] [Automated Event Log Generation from Unstructured Text Using Finetuned LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01320)
*Maximilian Seeth,Gabriel Marques Tavares,Daniel Schuster*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a scalable framework that fine-tunes Large Language Models (LLMs) as automated data translators to convert unstructured textual resources (e.g., incident tickets, manuals, reports) into structured event logs for process mining, demonstrating that fine-tuning significantly outperforms few-shot or zero-shot prompting on a newly created text-to-log dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Process mining relies on structured event logs, but creating these logs requires costly and laborious manual effort by domain and process mining experts. As a result, large amounts of organizational knowledge stored in unstructured textual formats (incident tickets, manuals, textual reports) remain underutilized, creating a bottleneck for applying PM to real-world organizational workflows.

Method: The authors propose a framework that uses LLMs as data translators to bridge unstructured text and structured event data. They create a new text-to-log dataset and fine-tune LLMs on it, comparing the fine-tuned models against few-shot and zero-shot prompting baselines.

Result: Fine-tuned LLMs extract high-fidelity event logs from unstructured resources and outperform few-shot or zero-shot prompting by a large margin, indicating that fine-tuning is a necessary precondition for generating reliable event data.

Conclusion: The method provides a promising pipeline for making previously unused unstructured data available to process mining ecosystems, thereby expanding the applicability of process mining to investigate organizational workflows more broadly.

Abstract: Process mining (PM) provides a powerful framework for discovering and optimizing operational processes from event data. However, the efficacy of PM techniques is strictly predicated on the availability of structured event logs. Thus far, event logs have often been laboriously created by domain and process mining experts. This costly effort causes large portions of organizational knowledge, including incident tickets, manuals, and textual reports, to remain underutilized. We address this bottleneck by investigating the efficacy of Large Language Models (LLMs) as automated data translators. We propose a scalable framework that leverages LLMs as data translators to bridge the gap between unstructured textual resources and structured event data. We finetune LLMs on a newly created text-to-log dataset, demonstrating that the resulting models can extract high-fidelity event logs from unstructured resources. Our results show that this finetuning approach outperforms few-shot or zero-shot prompting by a large amount, highlighting finetuning as a necessary pre-condition for generating reliable event data. We conclude that our method provides a promising pipeline for making previously unused data available to process mining ecosystems, effectively expanding the possibilities of using PM to further investigate organizational workflows.

</details>


### [113] [LEAP: Likelihood Elicitation and Aggregation for LLM-based Probabilistic Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01337)
*Yufei Chen,Yiran Zhao,Xiaogang Xu,Qipeng Xie,Jiafei Wu,Zhe Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LEAP (Likelihood Elicitation and Aggregation for Probabilistic forecasting), a method that restructures how LLM-based forecasting systems use evidence in prediction. Instead of having an LLM read all evidence together (Monolithic Prediction), LEAP elicits likelihood parameters from each evidence item separately and aggregates them via an explicit prior and deterministic probabilistic model into a posterior distribution. LEAP supports continuous, single-choice, and multi-choice forecasts and improves prediction and calibration metrics across models and agent frameworks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based forecasting systems use a "Monolithic Prediction" design where the model reads all collected evidence together and produces a final forecast. This obscures how individual evidence items affect the result and collapses uncertainty across competing outcomes, degrading interpretability and forecast quality.

Method: LEAP reorganizes the prediction stage by: (1) examining each evidence item separately and eliciting likelihood parameters that describe its implications for the target; (2) combining these likelihoods with an explicit prior using a deterministic probabilistic model to produce a posterior distribution. This supports continuous, single-choice, and multi-choice forecasts while maintaining reproducible evidence contributions.

Result: Evaluated on a benchmark covering forecasting, information-seeking, and browsing tasks using LEAP's own agent loop and several agent CLI frameworks. Given the same evidence, LEAP improves most prediction and calibration metrics across models and remains stronger under controlled comparisons of prior access, inference budget, and aggregation.

Conclusion: LEAP demonstrates that per-evidence likelihood elicitation with explicit probabilistic aggregation outperforms monolithic evidence processing in LLM forecasting, yielding better predictions and calibration while providing more transparent, reproducible evidence contributions.

Abstract: LLM-based forecasting systems have improved on real-world tasks such as financial markets and sports outcomes, largely through stronger search and tool use. Many systems still ask an LLM to read all collected evidence together and produce the final forecast. We call this design Monolithic Prediction. It can obscure how individual evidence items affect the result and collapse uncertainty across competing outcomes. We propose LEAP (Likelihood Elicitation and Aggregation for Probabilistic forecasting), which reorganizes how collected evidence is used in the prediction stage. LEAP examines each evidence item separately and elicits likelihood parameters that describe its implications for the target. An explicit prior and a deterministic probabilistic model then combine these likelihoods into a posterior distribution. This procedure supports continuous, single-choice, and multi-choice forecasts while preserving reproducible evidence contributions. We build a benchmark covering forecasting, information-seeking, and browsing tasks, and evaluate LEAP on our own agent loop and several agent CLI frameworks. Given the same evidence, LEAP improves most prediction and calibration metrics across models and remains stronger under controlled comparisons of prior access, inference budget, and aggregation.

</details>


### [114] [Cheap Verifiers, Large Blind Spots: Measuring the Reliability Cost of Cost-Saving Cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01345)
*Dushyant Rajput*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper studies self-improving inference cascades where a cheap student model is fine-tuned on rejections from a frontier verifier model. It finds that the verifier's blind spot (accepting wrong student answers) is large and grows adversarially with student capability, corrective fine-tuning degrades rather than improves the student, and crucially, all in-loop metrics computed through the verifier remain flat (3% error) while true error swings up to 32%, making the system blind to its own degradation. The authors provide a two-population conservation law (ε∞ ≲ q₀β₀) explaining this blindness.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inference cascades reduce cost by using a cheap model for most queries and escalating hard cases to a frontier verifier. A natural extension is to close the loop by fine-tuning the cheap student on the verifier's rejections to progressively reduce escalation rate and cost. The question is whether this self-improving loop actually works reliably in practice on real LLMs.

Method: The authors empirically measure the self-improving cascade loop on real LLMs with student models scaling from 0.5B to 32B parameters and verifiers of varying capability. They measure the verifier's blind spot β (fraction of student's wrong answers accepted), test naive corrective fine-tuning on verifier-rejected tails with both cross-family and same-family teachers, and develop a synthetic study plus a theoretical two-population conservation law to explain observed behavior.

Result: Four key findings: (1) The verifier's blind spot β grows with student capability (0.12 to 0.55 as student scales 0.5B to 32B) and shrinks with verifier capability, making it worst in the cheap-student, cheap-verifier regime cascades exist for. (2) Using a frontier verifier reduces β to ~0.05 but escalates on 46% of hard-MATH queries against a 39% true error rate. (3) Naive corrective fine-tuning on verifier-rejected data degrades and ultimately collapses the small student across all teachers tested. (4) In-loop metrics computed through the verifier read a flat 3% error while true delivered error swings up to 32%, showing the system is structurally blind to its own degradation.

Conclusion: The self-improving cascade loop is self-defeating at this scale: the reliability of such a system cannot be read from any metric computed through its own verifier. The verifier's blind spots and the feedback dynamics combine to produce optimistic self-assessment while true quality degrades, captured by the conservation law ε∞ ≲ q₀β₀. This implies fundamental limits on closed-loop self-improvement without external ground truth signals.

Abstract: Inference cascades cut cost by answering most queries with a cheap model and escalating a hard tail to a frontier model that acts as verifier. A natural extension closes the loop: fine-tune the cheap student on the verifier's rejections so the escalation rate, and cost, fall each round. We measure this loop on real LLMs and report four findings. First, the verifier's blind spot, the fraction of the student's wrong answers it accepts, is large and moves adversarially: it grows with student capability ($β$ from 0.12 to 0.55 as the student scales 0.5B to 32B) and shrinks with verifier capability, so it is worst in the cheap-student, cheap-verifier regime cascades exist to create. Second, buying it away returns the saving: a frontier verifier drives $β$ to about 0.05 but then escalates on 46% of hard-MATH queries against a 39% true error rate, paying the frontier price on nearly half of all traffic. Third, naive corrective fine-tuning on the verifier-rejected tail does not improve the small student but degrades and ultimately collapses it, across every teacher we tried (cross-family and same-family), so at this scale the self-improving loop is self-defeating. Fourth, through all of this the cascade's own dashboard, every metric computed through the verifier, reads a flat 3% error while true delivered error swings up to 32%: the system is blind to its own degradation by construction. We then give the theory that explains the blindness, a two-population conservation law, $ε_\infty \lesssim q_0 β_0$, under which every in-loop metric improves while true quality does not, and a synthetic study that validates the mechanism. The practical conclusion: the reliability of a self-improving cascade cannot be read from any metric computed through its own verifier.

</details>


### [115] [SymFold: Synergizing Evolutionary and Structural Priors for Accurate Protein Inverse Folding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01353)
*Handong Wang,Jiaxin Qi,Baisheng Lai,Jianqiang Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces a symmetric dual-path architecture for protein inverse folding that jointly leverages pretrained sequence knowledge from protein language models (PLMs) and pretrained structural knowledge from multimodal protein language models (MPLMs) to iteratively guide sequence generation. The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard inverse folding benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current protein inverse folding methods follow a serial pipeline—a structure encoder predicts a coarse sequence, then PLMs refine it. This post-hoc refinement is bounded by the quality of upstream predictions. Although MPLMs can directly encode structure with pretrained structural knowledge, they are not effective for inverse folding on their own.

Method: The authors propose a symmetric dual-path architecture that integrates both PLMs (for pretrained sequence evolution knowledge) and MPLMs (for pretrained structural knowledge) in an iterative, mutually guiding manner for protein sequence generation.

Result: The method achieves state-of-the-art performance on standard protein inverse folding benchmarks, surpassing prior approaches. Ablation studies confirm the rationale of the symmetric dual-path design.

Conclusion: The symmetric dual-path architecture that fuses both sequence and structural pretrained knowledge offers a promising new direction for protein inverse folding, overcoming the limitations of both serial refinement pipelines and direct MPLM-based encoding approaches.

Abstract: Protein inverse folding aims to recover amino acid sequences for a given 3D protein structure, underpinning broad applications such as enzyme engineering and drug discovery.Current methods often follow a serial pipeline, in which a structure encoder predicts a coarse sequence, which is then refined by protein language models (PLMs). However, because PLMs only perform post-hoc sequence edits, the refinement is bounded by the quality of upstream predictions.Thanks to recent multimodal protein language models (MPLMs), we could directly encode structure to generate sequences with pretrained structural knowledge, but we observe that they are not effective for inverse folding. Therefore, we introduce a symmetric dual-path architecture that both leverages PLMs for pretrained sequence evolution knowledge and MPLMs for pretrained structural knowledge to iteratively guide protein sequence generation.Through extensive experiments across standard protein inverse folding benchmarks, our method achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing prior approaches, and ablation studies validate the rationale of our symmetric design, revealing a promising direction for the community.

</details>


### [116] [EDGE: Error Dependency Graph-Guided Multi-Error Attribution in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01360)
*Jun Hou,Priya Pitre,Yi Fang,Xuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EDGE is an Error Dependency Graph-guided framework for multi-error attribution in LLM agents, which constructs an error dependency graph from observed failure events, validates causal dependencies via counterfactual rollout, and guides a two-stage LLM-as-judge detector for error attribution and repair analysis.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing attribution methods only identify a single responsible agent, step, or root cause, but LLM agent failures often contain multiple related errors with dependencies between them. Current approaches fail to model these dependencies explicitly.

Method: EDGE constructs an error dependency graph from observed error events, validates a reliable causal subset through counterfactual rollout, and uses the inference graph to guide a two-stage LLM-as-judge detector for error attribution. The intervention-validated subgraph provides a basis for explanation and repair analysis.

Result: Experiments on TRAIL and MAST show EDGE improves category-level multi-error attribution across most evaluated models and settings. The graph helps across prompting strategies in adapted Who&When-style prompts.

Conclusion: Dependency structure serves as a useful diagnostic prior for agent failures, going beyond isolated root-cause prediction to enable more reliable multi-error attribution and repair.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agent failures often contain multiple related errors rather than a single mistake. Existing attribution methods usually identify a responsible agent, step, or root cause, but do not explicitly model dependency between errors. We introduce EDGE, an Error Dependency Graph-guided multi-Error attribution framework. EDGE constructs an error dependency graph from observed error events and validates a reliable causal subset through counterfactual rollout. The inference graph guides a two-stage LLM-as-judge detector for error attribution, and the intervention-validated subgraph provides a more reliable basis for explanation and repair analysis. Experiments on TRAIL and MAST show that EDGE improves category-level multi-error attribution across most evaluated models and settings. Experiments with adapted Who&When-style prompts show that the graph helps across prompting strategies. These results suggest that dependency structure is a useful diagnostic prior for agent failures beyond isolated root-cause prediction.

</details>


### [117] [Neuro-Symbolic Geometric Abstraction (NeuSOGA): From Observations to Symbolic Mathematical Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01408)
*Qingde Li,Qingqi Hong,Jie Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper presents NeuSOGA (Neuro-Symbolic Geometric Abstraction), a framework that progressively transforms geometric observations into topological abstractions, geometric abstractions, and ultimately symbolic mathematical representations using a hybrid architecture that combines Euclidean Distance Transforms, Segment Anything, adaptive multi-scale geometric abstraction, and Implicit Area Splines. The resulting representation is an analytical implicit model that is interpretable, editable, and mathematically explicit, unlike opaque neural latent encodings. Experiments on ModelNet40 point clouds and diverse sensing modalities demonstrate compact symbolic representation while preserving geometric and topological structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: A fundamental challenge in AI is transforming observations into explicit symbolic representations suitable for abstraction, interpretation, and reasoning. Modern AI systems achieve perceptual capabilities through large-scale statistical learning, but the resulting knowledge is encoded in latent parameters that are difficult to inspect or manipulate analytically. Inspired by Neuro-Symbolic AI and theories of human abstraction, the authors aim to bridge this gap by investigating the formation of symbolic mathematical representations from geometric observations.

Method: NeuSOGA is a progressive framework that transforms observations through stages: (1) topology-guided structural discovery using Euclidean Distance Transforms, (2) foundation-model perception using Segment Anything, (3) adaptive multi-scale geometric abstraction, and (4) symbolic synthesis through Implicit Area Splines. The architecture generates an analytical implicit representation supporting arbitrary-order smoothness, additive composition, and closed-form evaluation.

Result: Experiments on ModelNet40 point clouds, arbitrary-view projections, and segmented optical observations demonstrate that NeuSOGA successfully transforms diverse observations into compact symbolic representations while preserving essential geometric and topological structure across different sensing modalities and viewing directions.

Conclusion: NeuSOGA provides an interpretable and explainable pathway from observation to symbol, establishing a bridge between neural perception and symbolic reasoning. The generated representations remain interpretable, editable, and mathematically explicit, unlike neural latent encodings, offering a new direction for neuro-symbolic AI in geometric abstraction and mathematical reasoning.

Abstract: A fundamental challenge in artificial intelligence is the transformation of observations into explicit symbolic representations suitable for abstraction, interpretation, and reasoning. While modern AI systems achieve remarkable perceptual capabilities through large-scale statistical learning, the resulting knowledge is typically encoded within latent parameters that are difficult to inspect or manipulate analytically. Inspired by Neuro-Symbolic AI and theories of human abstraction, this paper investigates the formation of symbolic mathematical representations from geometric observations.
  We propose NeuSOGA (Neuro-Symbolic Geometric Abstraction), a framework that progressively transforms observations into topological abstractions, geometric abstractions, and ultimately symbolic mathematical representations. The architecture combines topology-guided structural discovery using Euclidean Distance Transforms, foundation-model perception using Segment Anything, adaptive multi-scale geometric abstraction, and symbolic synthesis through Implicit Area Splines.
  The resulting representation is an analytical implicit model supporting arbitrary-order smoothness, additive composition, and closed-form evaluation. Unlike neural latent encodings, the generated representation remains interpretable, editable, and mathematically explicit. Experiments on ModelNet40 point clouds, arbitrary-view projections, and segmented optical observations demonstrate that NeuSOGA transforms diverse observations into compact symbolic representations while preserving essential geometric and topological structure across sensing modalities and viewing directions.
  NeuSOGA provides an interpretable and explainable pathway from observation to symbol and establishes

</details>


### [118] [EdiTikZ: Scientific Figure Editing from Revision Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01409)
*Christian Greisinger,Zhixue Zhao,Steffen Eger*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces DaEdiTikZ, a large-scale dataset of 391K revision-derived TikZ figure edit pairs mined from arXiv, GitHub, and TeX SE, along with 781K inferred directed edit instructions. The authors train compact Qwen3.5-based EdiTikZ models (4B/9B) via joint reconstruction and editing learning plus RL, achieving performance at or above costly proprietary agentic systems including GPT-5.6-Sol and Gemini-3.1-Pro.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Scientific figure editing is important yet underexplored because publication-ready figures require iterative refinement. Existing approaches depend on costly proprietary agentic systems, focus mainly on evaluation, or rely on synthetic edit supervision. The authors instead seek a scalable, naturally occurring source of supervision from real-world scientific revision trajectories.

Method: The authors (1) mine 391K plausible TikZ edit pairs from arXiv, GitHub, and TeX SE as naturally occurring revision data; (2) use a VLM conditioned on rendered figures and TikZ code to infer 781K directed edit instructions; (3) construct DaEdiTikZ-Bench, a human-refined benchmark with 790 instances; and (4) train compact Qwen3.5-based EdiTikZ models (4B and 9B) using joint learning of reconstruction and editing, followed by reinforcement learning with complementary rewards for rendered fidelity and edit application.

Result: The 9B EdiTikZ model outperforms all tested baselines in automatic evaluation. In human evaluation (9 annotators, 4,320 ratings), it surpasses GPT-5.6-Sol and is on par with Gemini-3.1-Pro. Under severe out-of-distribution shifts, it remains competitive with GPT-5.6-Sol near its 2K training sequence-length regime.

Conclusion: Naturally occurring scientific revision trajectories provide a scalable and effective alternative to synthetic data or proprietary agentic pipelines for training scientific figure editing models. Compact models trained on such data can rival or exceed much larger proprietary systems, and the released models and datasets will support further research in this space.

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have shown strong performance in generating scientific figures from text or images. However, producing publication-ready figures requires iterative refinement, making scientific figure editing an important yet largely unexplored task. Existing approaches rely on costly proprietary agentic systems, focus primarily on evaluation, or construct training supervision from synthetically generated edits. Instead, we leverage naturally occurring scientific revision and development trajectories as a scalable source of supervision. To this end, we introduce DaEdiTikZ, the first large-scale dataset of revision-derived scientific figure edits, constructed by mining 391K plausible TikZ edit pairs from arXiv, GitHub, and TeX SE and inferring 781K directed edit instructions with a VLM conditioned on rendered figures and TikZ code. We further introduce DaEdiTikZ-Bench, a human-refined benchmark with 790 instances, and train two compact Qwen3.5-based EdiTikZ models (4B and 9B) by jointly learning reconstruction and editing, followed by reinforcement learning (RL) with complementary rewards for rendered fidelity and edit application. Automatic evaluation places our 9B model above all tested baselines, while human evaluation with 9 annotators and 4,320 ratings places it above GPT-5.6-Sol and on par with Gemini-3.1-Pro. Under severe out-of-distribution shifts, it remains competitive with GPT-5.6-Sol near its 2K training sequence-length regime. Models and datasets will be released.

</details>


### [119] [Parsing the Stream: A Live Trace Model for Long-Horizon Agents and Their Observers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01466)
*Egor Pakhomov,Erik Nijkamp*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper proposes a live trace model—an append-only event ledger incrementally folded into typed run state and compiled into per-consumer views—to address the problem of long-horizon agent traces outgrowing both human observers and the agent's own bounded context. Evaluated against deterministic ground truth, the compiled view reduces input tokens ~14-15x and cost 5-7x for LLM-based monitoring readers while improving accuracy (0.85-0.87 vs 0.48). For the agent itself, incremental fold-based state succeeds on 120-link sequential dependency tasks (30/30 vs 8/30 for full-context prompting), with deterministic auditability as the remaining advantage over cheaper alternatives.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agent traces grow unboundedly, exceeding both the context window of the agent itself (which must fold state back into its bounded context) and the attention/cost budget of human or LLM observers monitoring the run. Existing approaches rely on budget-capped raw trace reading which degrades accuracy and costs more.

Method: The authors present a live trace model consisting of an append-only event ledger that is incrementally folded into typed run state, which is then compiled into per-consumer view schemas. They evaluate both consumer sides: (1) an LLM reader proxy for human observers answering monitoring questions from compiled views versus raw traces; (2) the agent itself on 120-link sequential-dependency tasks, comparing full-context prompting, prompt-level scratchpads, and the fold mechanism. They also decompose the fold via two-arm attribution and derive eleven candidate requirements from observed failures.

Result: For observers, compiled views require ~14x and 15x fewer input tokens (by reader) and 5-7x lower cost than budget-capped full-trace reading, with higher accuracy (0.85-0.87 vs 0.48). For the agent, fold-based incremental state succeeds 30/30 on 120-link sequential tasks versus 8/30 for full-context prompting; scratchpads match fold accuracy at lower cost, but the fold retains deterministic aggregate accuracy and cost advantage from compactness, with deterministic auditability as its unique remaining value.

Conclusion: The fold's distinctive value over alternatives lies in deterministic auditability and serving the observer and agent from the same compact state. The benefits are conditional on schema coverage (questions were co-designed with the view schema), and the fold ceases to help on an order-sensitive task family, delimiting its applicability. Eleven candidate requirements for trace folding are derived from observed failures.

Abstract: A long-horizon agent's trace outgrows both of its consumers: the human observer monitoring the run, and the agent itself, whose bounded context the trace must be folded back into. We present a live trace model, an append-only event ledger folded incrementally into typed run state and compiled into per-consumer views, and evaluate it for both consumers against deterministic ground truth. For the observer side, evaluated with an LLM reader as proxy, the compiled view answers monitoring questions using approximately 14x and 15x fewer input tokens (by reader) and at 5-7x lower cost than a budget-capped single-call reading of the raw trace, with higher accuracy (0.85-0.87 versus 0.48). Because the questions were co-designed with the view schema, we treat the token and cost reduction, conditional on schema coverage, as the transferable result. For the agent, on 120-link sequential-dependency tasks, mechanisms that maintain the task's running statistic in per-step state succeed where full-context prompting fails (30/30 versus 8/30 under a clean protocol, n=30, labeled descriptive owing to benchmark-system co-development); a prompt-level scratchpad matches the fold's accuracy at lower cost, and a two-arm decomposition attributes the fold's accuracy to its deterministic aggregate and its cost advantage to its compactness. The fold's remaining value over cheaper alternatives is deterministic auditability and serving the observer from the same state. We derive eleven candidate requirements for trace folding from observed failures and delimit them with an order-sensitive task family on which the fold ceases to help. Code, benchmarks, a regenerable synthetic corpus, and all workbench traces are released.

</details>


### [120] [Harness-of-Harness: Multi-Day Autonomous Software Development with Continual Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01481)
*Haoyang Yan,Min-le Su,Hangfan Zhang,Zhanhao Li,Chen Zhang,Shao Zhang,Yang Chen,Lei Bai,Shuyue Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces Harness-of-Harness (HoH), a framework that enables LLM-based coding agents to autonomously and continuously improve software during development. HoH orchestrates existing coding-agent harnesses into iterative planning-coding-testing loops, balancing repair with capability growth through small, verifiable increments and progressive exposure of tools and skills. On three benchmarks (GameCraft-Bench, FrontierSWE, ProgramBench) across three harness-model pairs, HoH achieves an average relative gain of 52.25% and up to 82.86% after three iterations, and in a multi-day deployment autonomously builds a complete first-person-shooter game.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous software development with LLM coding agents is challenging because agents must transform high-level requirements into complete, functional software without human intervention, requiring sustained self-improvement rather than one-shot generation.

Method: HoH is a meta-framework that operates on top of existing coding-agent harnesses, organizing their executions into iterative planning-coding-testing loops. It sustains improvement by balancing repair with capability growth, scoping development into small verifiable increments, separating implementation-time testing from independent evaluation, and constraining verifiable outputs rather than prescribing workflows. It progressively exposes deliverables, role-specific tools, and skills, encourages reuse, and maintains versioned project histories.

Result: Across GameCraft-Bench, FrontierSWE, and ProgramBench, three harness-model pairs (Codex/GPT-5.5, OpenCode/DeepSeek-V4-Pro, Pi/MiniMax-M3) consistently outperform standalone harnesses, with an average relative gain of 52.25% and a maximum gain of 82.86% after three iterations. A multi-day deployment with over 70 iterations autonomously produced a first-person-shooter game with a coherent storyline, core mechanics, human-playable experience, polished visuals, and integrated audio.

Conclusion: HoH demonstrates that wrapping existing coding-agent harnesses with an iterative, structured meta-controller significantly improves autonomous software quality and capability, enabling agents to deliver complete, polished, human-playable software systems over extended development cycles.

Abstract: This paper studies autonomous software development, in which LLM-based coding agents transform high-level requirements into complete, functional, and usable software systems without human intervention. We introduce Harness-of-Harness (HoH), a framework that enables coding agents to continually improve software during autonomous development. HoH operates on existing coding-agent harnesses, and organizes their executions into iterative planning-coding-testing loops. To sustain improvement across loops, HoH balances repair with capability growth, scopes development into small and verifiable increments, separates implementation-time testing from independent evaluation, and constrains verifiable outputs rather than prescribing agent workflows. It progressively exposes deliverables, role-specific tools, and skills, encourages reuse rather than recreation, and maintains versioned project histories. On GameCraft-Bench, FrontierSWE, and ProgramBench, three harness-model pairs (Codex with GPT-5.5, OpenCode with DeepSeek-V4-Pro, and Pi with MiniMax-M3), HoH consistently outperforms the corresponding standalone harnesses, achieving an average relative gain of 52.25 percent and a maximum gain of 82.86 percent after three iterations. In a multi-day deployment with more than 70 iterations, HoH autonomously develops a first-person-shooter game, featuring a coherent storyline, fully implemented core mechanics, human-playable experience, polished visuals and integrated audio. Github: https://github.com/Flesymeb/HarnessOfHarness Project Page: https://flesymeb.github.io/HarnessOfHarness/

</details>


### [121] [When Guardrails Look Effective: Construct Validity Failures in LLM Agent Commerce Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01519)
*Peiying Zhu,Sidi Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper audits whether interactive simulations with language-model agents produce economically meaningful results or merely "look economic." In a multi-turn hotel transaction testbed, the authors show that a reported welfare gain from marketplace guardrails (+87.4, +35.0, +28.8) is confounded by changes in offer schemas and buyer choosers; holding these fixed changes contrasts to +7.2, -13.9, +23.8. They further demonstrate stochastic instability across generations and non-monotone incentive effects. They propose a construct-validity contract (incentive validity, protocol isolation, stochastic stability, welfare accounting) that returns INVALID or INCONCLUSIVE before policy claims are made, and find the original estimate INVALID under protocol isolation while the controlled study remains INCONCLUSIVE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-model agents are increasingly used to simulate markets and evaluate policies, producing quantities like prices, profits, and welfare. The authors are concerned that these outputs may look economic without actually instantiating the underlying economic behavior claimed, posing a construct-validity risk for policy conclusions derived from such simulations.

Method: The authors build a multi-turn buyer-seller testbed with configurable hotel transactions. They compare an initial implementation that reported welfare gains from guardrails against controlled versions holding offer schemas and buyer chooser fixed. They assess single-generation vs. multi-generation stochastic effects via residual analysis and bootstrap intervals, test seller-incentive monotonicity, and run scripted positive controls with a profit-maximizing seller. They formalize a construct-validity contract with four checks: incentive validity, protocol isolation, stochastic stability, and welfare accounting.

Result: Holding schema and chooser fixed changes gains from +87.4/+35.0/+28.8 to +7.2/-13.9/+23.8. Single-generation 14B effects averaged +229 but dropped to +37.6 (95% CI [-34.2, 109.3]) after three generations, with generation residuals explaining 49.9% of variation. Increasing seller profit pressure produced less profit than the default prompt (non-monotone). A profit-maximizing seller already attains first-best welfare, so guardrails mostly redistribute/reduce welfare, only creating welfare when sellers are forced into inefficient bundles.

Conclusion: The original welfare estimate is INVALID under protocol isolation, and the controlled study remains INCONCLUSIVE under incentive validity and stochastic stability. The case does not prove guardrails are ineffective; rather, it shows their apparent value is unidentified until simulated agents and protocols pass the proposed validity checks. The paper contributes a framework to prevent premature policy claims from LLM-agent simulations.

Abstract: Interactive simulations increasingly evaluate policies in markets populated by language-model agents. Their outputs can look economic---prices, profits, consumer surplus, and welfare---without instantiating the behavior named in the claim. We audit this risk in a multi-turn buyer--seller testbed for configurable hotel transactions. An initial implementation reported welfare gains from two marketplace guardrails of +87.4, +35.0, and +28.8 across a Qwen2.5 1.5B--14B ladder. It also gave guarded and unguarded agents different offer schemas and choice procedures. Holding the schema and buyer chooser fixed changes the paired contrasts to +7.2, -13.9, and +23.8. The four largest 14B single-generation effects averaged +229; after three generations per profile-condition, they averaged +37.6 (95% bootstrap interval [-34.2, 109.3]), while generation residuals account for 49.9% of variation in this post-hoc probe. A seller-incentive check is non-monotone: increasing profit pressure produces less profit than the default seller prompt. Scripted positive controls show why this matters. A profit-maximizing seller already attains first-best welfare, so guardrails mostly redistribute and reduce welfare; they create welfare only when the seller is explicitly programmed to force inefficient bundles. We contribute a construct-validity contract separating incentive validity, protocol isolation, stochastic stability, and welfare accounting, and returning INVALID or INCONCLUSIVE before substantive policy claims. In our case, the original estimate is INVALID under protocol isolation, while the controlled study remains INCONCLUSIVE under incentive validity and stochastic stability. The case does not show that guardrails are ineffective; it shows their apparent value is unidentified until the simulated agents and protocol pass these checks.

</details>


### [122] [EvoSCM: Scientific Belief Revision Through Causal Model Evolution and Experimentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01526)
*Qing Zhao,Haowei Li,Weijian Deng,Pengxu Wei,Liang Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoSCM equips LLM scientific agents with an explicit population of structural causal models (SCMs) that evolve through a closed discovery loop—abducing latent mechanisms, designing interventions, committing to falsifiable predictions, and revising hypotheses via inductive correction rules—demonstrated on DiscoverPhysics to outperform baselines in accuracy and experimental efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM scientific agents express hypotheses in free-form text, leaving beliefs implicit and difficult to test or revise; there is a need for explicit, evolvable causal representations that ground agent reasoning in testable structure.

Method: EvoSCM maintains a population of competing SCM hypotheses that evolve over rounds. Each round involves: (1) abducing latent mechanisms from accumulated evidence, (2) designing discriminative interventions, (3) committing to falsifiable predictions, testing them experimentally, (4) inducing correction rules from prediction-observation discrepancies to revise causal structures/mechanisms, and (5) deductively validating the revised population against evidence and structural consistency.

Result: On the DiscoverPhysics benchmark, EvoSCM consistently improves scientific discovery over baselines, yielding more accurate explanations and predictions while making more effective use of experimental interactions.

Conclusion: Explicit, evolving structural causal models provide a principled substrate for scientific agents, enabling testable, revisable beliefs that improve both explanatory accuracy and experimental efficiency in scientific discovery.

Abstract: Scientific agents must learn not only how to reason, but also what to believe. However, existing LLM agents typically express scientific hypotheses in free-form text, leaving their beliefs implicit and difficult to test or revise. We introduce EvoSCM, which equips scientific agents with explicit structural causal models that evolve as new experimental evidence is collected. EvoSCM maintains a population of competing SCM hypotheses, each encoding a candidate causal explanation of the environment, and evolves them through a closed discovery loop. In each round, the agent abduces latent mechanisms from accumulated evidence, designs discriminative interventions, and commits to falsifiable predictions that it tests through experimentation. Discrepancies between prediction and observation are inductively distilled into correction rules that revise the causal structures and mechanisms of each hypothesis, and the agent then deductively validates the revised population against accumulated evidence and structural consistency to guide the next round. We evaluate EvoSCM on DiscoverPhysics, a benchmark requiring agents to uncover the hidden dynamics of noncanonical physical worlds through experimentation. EvoSCM consistently improves scientific discovery over baselines, yielding more accurate explanations and predictions while making more effective use of experimental interactions.

</details>


### [123] [Can LLMs Discover Scientific Laws in Real and Parallel Worlds?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01552)
*Yiming Huang,Ziche Liu,Zhuohang Wu,Yiqian Wang,Junxia Cui,Xinkai Zou,Linjun Mao,Nan Huang,Naicheng Yu,Kaijie Zhu,Yue Ma,Kun Zhou,Letian Peng,Jingbo Shang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces SCILAWS-BENCH, a benchmark for evaluating whether LLMs can discover scientific laws from real published research and data, comprising 118 problems from 381 papers with 8M data points across six disciplines, tested in two complementary settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLM scientific law discovery often use synthetic settings or reuse published targets already familiar to the models, failing to assess genuine discovery ability.

Method: Builds 118 problems from 381 published papers covering 291 candidate laws and ~8M real data points across six disciplines. Each problem has two settings: SCILAWS-REAL (propose laws from fixed real observations, evaluating held-out predictive fit and scientific validity) and SCILAWS-PARALLEL (active querying of residual-calibrated worlds to recover synthesized hidden laws derived from published forms).

Result: Finds that predictive fit can diverge from scientific validity, memorization shapes whether models reproduce or move beyond published formulas, and best-of-N studies reveal a selection bottleneck.

Conclusion: Provides a paper-grounded benchmark with new empirical perspectives for evaluating AI for scientific discovery.

Abstract: Scientific equation discovery has long been central to scientific progress, proceeding through iterative cycles of hypothesis generation, observational testing, and refinement under scientific constraints. As LLM capabilities advance and their role in AI for Science expands, it remains an open problem whether they can genuinely discover scientific laws and how this ability should be evaluated. Existing evaluations, however, often either simplify discovery through synthetic settings or reuse published targets that may already be familiar to LLMs. We therefore introduce SCILAWS-BENCH, a benchmark for scientific law discovery built from published research and real scientific data. It comprises 118 problems drawn from 381 scientific papers, covering 291 candidate laws and roughly 8M real data points across six scientific disciplines. Each problem is instantiated in two complementary settings: (1) SCILAWS-REAL asks models to propose laws from fixed real observations and evaluates held-out predictive fit and scientific validity derived from the source literature, and (2) SCILAWS-PARALLEL asks models to actively query residual-calibrated worlds and recover synthesized hidden laws derived from published forms. This two-setting task design preserves each problem's scientific context while separately evaluating fixed-record law discovery and active recovery of a newly synthesized hidden law. We find that predictive fit can diverge from scientific validity, memorization shapes whether models reproduce or move beyond published formulas, and our best-of-N study reveals a selection bottleneck. Our work provides a paper-grounded benchmark and new empirical perspectives for evaluating AI for scientific discovery. Project page: https://yiyihum.github.io/SciLaws-Bench

</details>


### [124] [Selective Agent Guidance via Entropy: Learning Autonomous Policies from Imperfect VLM Teachers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01567)
*Matteo Merler,Giovanni Bonetta,Davide Zago,Rossella Cancelliere,Bernardo Magnini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SAGE is a framework that trains a lightweight RL policy by selectively querying a VLM teacher only when the learner is uncertain, distilling advantage-weighted guidance into the policy, and reducing VLM usage to a fraction of training steps with zero calls at deployment, improving over unguided RL in sparse-reward visual reasoning and navigation tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Using VLMs directly as policies is expensive and brittle: they require querying at every step, do not improve from interaction, and repeat systematic errors. The goal is to leverage VLM priors for decision-making while overcoming these limitations by distilling them into a cheap, learnable policy.

Method: SAGE operates in three phases: (1) selectively queries the VLM teacher only when the learner policy is uncertain (measured via entropy); (2) executes the teacher's suggested action during training to gather environment experience; (3) distills teacher guidance into a lightweight RL policy, weighting distillation by environment-derived advantages (rather than treating all VLM advice as equally useful) to handle unreliable teacher suggestions.

Result: Across sparse-reward visual reasoning and navigation tasks, SAGE learns policies that require no VLM guidance at evaluation time and improves over unguided RL in several environments, including cases where the distilled policy exceeds the VLM teacher itself. Selective guidance is most beneficial when VLM help enables discovery of high-reward trajectories, less useful when unguided exploration already succeeds or teacher actions don't yield informative experience. SAGE also significantly reduces VLM query count to only a fraction of training steps.

Conclusion: VLMs need not be used as fixed policies to be useful; instead they can serve as temporary, imperfect sources of guidance whose value is tested and internalized through environment interaction. Selective, advantage-weighted distillation provides an efficient pathway from expensive VLM priors to cheap autonomous policies.

Abstract: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) provide useful priors for interactive decision-making, but using them directly as policies is expensive and brittle: they must be queried at every step, do not improve from environment interaction, and can repeat systematic errors. We study how to learn a cheap autonomous policy from an online, expensive, and imperfect but informative VLM teacher. We propose SAGE (Selective Agent Guidance via Entropy), a framework that queries a VLM only when the learner is uncertain, executes the suggested action during training, and distills guidance into a lightweight Reinforcement Learning (RL) policy. Because VLM advice is not always reliable, SAGE can weight teacher-action distillation using environment-derived advantages rather than treating all suggestions as equally useful. Across sparse-reward visual reasoning and navigation tasks, SAGE learns policies that act without VLM guidance at evaluation time and improves over unguided RL in several environments, including settings where the learned policy exceeds its VLM teacher. The results show that selective guidance is most beneficial when the VLM can help the agent discover high-reward trajectories, and less useful when unguided exploration already succeeds or teacher actions do not lead to informative experience. SAGE also reduces VLM usage by prompting the teacher only on a fraction of training steps and requiring no VLM calls at deployment. Overall, our results suggest that VLMs don't need to be used as fixed policies to be useful; they can instead act as temporary, imperfect sources of guidance whose value is tested and internalized through interaction.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [125] [Inverse kinematic solution for generic 3R positional robots using Conformal Geometric Algebra](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00311)
*Abhilash Nayak,Durgesh Haribhau Salunkhe*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a novel approach to solving the inverse kinematics of generic 3R robots using Conformal Geometric Algebra (CGA). The method reformulates the problem as the intersection of two circles, directly yielding a univariate polynomial in theta_2 without eliminating theta_1 and theta_3, providing new geometric insights beyond previous joint-space-only interpretations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Previous geometric interpretations of 3R robot inverse kinematics were limited to the joint-space domain and required solving complex algebraic equation sets. The authors aim to leverage the advantages of Conformal Geometric Algebra to provide deeper geometric insights and simplify the solution process.

Method: The approach reframes the inverse kinematic problem of 3R robots as the intersection of two circles using Conformal Geometric Algebra (CGA). This formulation directly produces a univariate polynomial in terms of theta_2 without the need to eliminate theta_1 and theta_3 through traditional algebraic manipulation.

Result: The method successfully yields a univariate polynomial in theta_2 directly, and provides a geometric interpretation based on the intersection of two circles within the CGA framework, offering further insights into the kinematic properties of 3R robots.

Conclusion: Using CGA, the inverse kinematic model of generic 3R robots can be solved more elegantly by modeling the problem as the intersection of two circles, bypassing the traditional elimination steps and offering enhanced geometric understanding of the robot's kinematics.

Abstract: The inverse kinematics of generic 3R robots has been investigated through multiple approaches, mainly algebraic methods involving the solution of certain equation sets. Previous geometric interpretations of the solution, characterized as the intersection of a pair of conics have been confined to the joint-space domain. In this article, we study the Inverse Kinematic Model (IKM) of 3R robots, using the advantages of Conformal Geometric Algebra (CGA) to provide further insights on its kinematic properties. Our approach directly yields a univariate polynomial in terms of theta_2 without the need to eliminate theta_1 and theta_3 by reframing the problem as the intersection of two circles, which are fundamental elements within this algebraic framework.

</details>


### [126] [Geometric analysis of generic 3R robots, and necessary and sufficient conditions for a class of orthogonal robots to have four IKS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00316)
*Durgesh Haribhau Salunkhe,Abhilash Nayak*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a kinematic analysis of a generic 3R robot by combining algebraic and geometric approaches, using conformal geometric algebra (CGA) to derive an inverse kinematic model. It proposes a generic framework to study conditions for four inverse kinematic solutions (IKS) and their distribution throughout the workspace, and derives necessary and sufficient conditions for a class of orthogonal robots.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior algebraic approaches to 3R robot kinematics yield concrete results but are limited to special robot classes or architectural simplifications. Geometric approaches handle generic robots intuitively but lack the rigor of algebraic results. The authors aim to unify both paradigms to analyze generic 3R robots without constraints on the robot architecture.

Method: The approach builds the inverse kinematic model from conformal geometric algebra (CGA), combining algebraic rigor with geometric insight. Using this inherited IKS model, a generic framework is constructed to examine the conditions under which a 3R robot possesses four inverse kinematic solutions, and to study how the number of IKS varies across the workspace. The necessary and sufficient conditions are then derived for orthogonal-type robots using this framework.

Result: The proposed framework enables a generic and intuitive analysis of the IKS count and its spatial distribution for generic 3R robots. Concrete necessary and sufficient conditions for four IKS are established for a class of orthogonal robots, demonstrating the practical applicability of the methodology.

Conclusion: By fusing conformal geometric algebra with geometric reasoning, the paper delivers a generic framework for 3R robot kinematic analysis that overcomes the limitations of prior algebraic-only or geometric-only approaches, producing rigorous conditions for IKS existence and distribution while remaining applicable to generic robot architectures.

Abstract: The kinematic analysis of a generic 3R robot has been investigated with multiple approaches in the past. The algebraic approaches have established concrete results but are unfortunately limited to special classes or architectural simplifications. Geometric approaches on the other hand have extended the analysis to generic robots while also providing an intuitive understanding of their kinematic properties. We use the best of both approaches to present the kinematic analysis of a generic 3R robot, using the inverse kinematic model inherited from a method based on conformal geometric algebra. The paper discusses a generic framework to study the conditions for a 3R robot to have four inverse kinematic solutions (IKS) and allows to study the distribution of IKS as seen in workspace. The necessary and sufficient conditions for a class of orthogonal robots are presented using the proposed approach.

</details>


### [127] [Scene Graph-based Driving Scenario Extraction for Automotive Egocentric Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00333)
*Stefan Ramdhan,Kyanna Dagenais,Vera Pantelic,Victor Bandur,Mark Lawford*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a method for extracting driving scenarios from unlabelled egocentric sensor data streams using scene graphs and Linear Temporal Logic (LTL). The approach processes sensor data and HD maps into scene graph sequences, formally specifies scenarios of interest in LTL, and uses an off-the-shelf model checker to extract scenario instances. The method is evaluated on the Argoverse 2 dataset (850 real-world driving logs) and dashcam footage.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extracting critical scenarios from large unlabelled real-world sensor datasets is challenging but essential for scenario-based coverage analysis in automated driving systems development. Automatically locating spatially and temporally critical scenarios in large datasets can enable more effective ADS dataset analysis.

Method: The method has three main steps: (1) process egocentric sensor data and HD maps to generate a sequence of scene graphs representing driving scenarios; (2) use Linear Temporal Logic (LTL) to formally specify driving scenarios of interest; (3) extract all scenario instances from the dataset using an off-the-shelf model checker that evaluates the LTL formula against the scene graph sequence. The approach works on both simulated and real-world datasets.

Result: The method was evaluated on training and validation datasets from Argoverse 2 (850 15-second real-world driving logs) and several dashcam videos. Effectiveness for extracting and querying scenarios was demonstrated by comparing against a rule-based benchmark based on track annotations and HD maps.

Conclusion: The proposed scene-graph and LTL-based approach effectively extracts driving scenarios from egocentric datasets, demonstrating applicability to both simulated and real-world data with validation against rule-based benchmarks.

Abstract: Extracting scenarios from unlabelled real-world sensor data streams is a critical but challenging task in the development process of automated driving systems (ADS). Automatically sifting through large datasets to spatially and temporally locate critical scenarios can enable scenario-based coverage analysis of ADS datasets. In this paper, we present a method for extracting scenarios from egocentric datasets using scene graphs and Linear Temporal Logic (LTL). We first process egocentric sensor data and HD maps to generate a sequence of scene graphs representing a driving scenario. Next, we use LTL to formally specify driving scenarios of interest, then extract all instances of the scenarios from the dataset using an off-the-shelf model checker, which evaluates the LTL formula against the sequence of scene graphs. Our approach can be used on both simulated and real world datasets. We evaluate the method on the training and validation datasets from Argoverse 2 consisting of 850 15-second real-world driving logs, and several videos of dashcam footage. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our approach for extracting and querying scenarios by evaluating against a rule-based benchmark based on track annotations and HD maps.

</details>


### [128] [Risk-Aware Decision-Making for Autonomous Overtaking: A World Model-Based Mixture-of-Experts Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00385)
*Yongzhi Liu,Sunan Zhang,Jinchang Xu,Jiawei Wang,Yushu Qiu,Chen Lv,Weichao Zhuang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes WM-RMoE, a World Model-based Risk-aware Mixture-of-Experts framework for autonomous highway overtaking that uses learned latent dynamics for multi-step rollouts and trajectory-level risk assessment, coupled with a hierarchical gating mechanism coordinating long-horizon, short-horizon, and rule-based safety experts. A Gaussian Mixture Model preserves multimodal maneuvering branches to avoid mode averaging. Results show superior safety compliance, decision stability, and generalization, with the unique ability to produce foresighted overtaking maneuvers across diverse traffic densities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard safe reinforcement learning methods rely on implicit value-based risk estimation rather than explicit dynamics modeling, failing to capture complex risk propagation over multi-step horizons. This leads to locally safe but latently risky behaviors in long-term highway overtaking scenarios, motivating the need for trajectory-level and explicit risk-aware decision-making.

Method: The framework consists of three key components: (1) a learned latent dynamics model enabling parallel multi-step rollouts and cumulative risk evaluation that elevates safety assessment from the action level to the trajectory level; (2) a hierarchical gating mechanism that dynamically coordinates experts across long-horizon, short-horizon, and rule-based safety modules to handle varying interaction intensities; and (3) a Gaussian Mixture Model integrated to preserve multimodal maneuvering branches, mitigating behavioral mode averaging.

Result: WM-RMoE significantly outperforms representative baselines in safety compliance, decision stability, and generalization capability. The risk-aware formulation uniquely enables the framework to generate foresighted and semantically distinct overtaking maneuvers across diverse traffic densities.

Conclusion: Explicit trajectory-level risk modeling via world models, combined with adaptive expert coordination and multimodal preservation, is an effective approach for addressing long-horizon safety challenges in autonomous overtaking, offering both improved quantitative performance and qualitatively distinct, foresighted behaviors.

Abstract: Autonomous highway overtaking demands foresighted decision-making to handle complex interactions, stochastic traffic evolution, and temporal risk accumulation. However, standard safe reinforcement learning approaches typically rely on implicit value-based risk estimations rather than explicit dynamics modeling, thereby struggling to accurately capture complex risk propagation over multi-step horizons. This limitation frequently results in behaviors that are locally safe but induce substantial latent risks in the long term. To address this, a World Model-based Risk-aware Mixture-of-Experts (WM-RMoE) framework is proposed. First, a learned latent dynamics model facilitates parallel multi-step rollouts, elevating safety assessment from the action level to the trajectory level via cumulative risk evaluation. Second, to enhance robustness under varying interaction intensities, a hierarchical gating mechanism dynamically coordinates experts across long-horizon, short-horizon, and rule-based safety modules. Furthermore, a Gaussian Mixture Model is integrated to preserve multimodal maneuvering branches, thereby mitigating the issue of behavioral mode averaging. Experimental results demonstrate that WM-RMoE significantly outperforms representative baselines in terms of safety compliance, decision stability, and generalization capability. Furthermore, benefiting from the risk-aware formulation, the proposed framework uniquely exhibits the ability to generate foresighted and semantically distinct overtaking maneuvers across diverse traffic densities.

</details>


### [129] [Beyond Object Selection:Markerless Gaze-based Robot Placement at Arbitrary Position](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00478)
*Yuzhi Lai,William Marx,Shenghai Yuan,Peizheng Li,Zhuoyu Ran,Andreas Zell*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a task-oriented evaluation perspective for cross-device alignment in gaze-based assistive manipulation, introducing a markerless interaction framework, a cross-device dataset, Graph-based Reference Selection, and a novel Gaze-Surface Intersection Error (GSIE) metric that directly measures spatial target error rather than relying solely on conventional pose accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: For gaze-based assistive manipulation, conventional pose accuracy does not necessarily translate to task accuracy because translational and rotational errors jointly affect the transformed gaze ray and can compensate for each other. Standard pose-based evaluation overlooks how these errors impact actual gaze-specified task performance.

Method: 1) A markerless interaction framework with a dedicated cross-device dataset. 2) Graph-based Reference Selection to address sparse robot references. 3) Multiple task-specific alignment pipelines benchmarked under a unified protocol. 4) Introduction of Gaze-Surface Intersection Error (GSIE) to directly measure the spatial error of the gaze-specified target.

Result: Experiments show that alignment methods ranked highly by conventional pose metrics are not always optimal in terms of GSIE, demonstrating that task-level evaluation is essential for gaze-based manipulation and that pose-based ranking can be misleading.

Conclusion: Evaluating gaze-based manipulation at the task level, via metrics like GSIE, is critical for correctly assessing cross-device alignment quality—conventional pose metrics alone can yield suboptimal method selection for practical gaze-assisted manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Gaze-based assistive manipulation typically supports object selection, while arbitrary-position placement requires accurate spatial alignment between the headset and robot. However, for gaze-based manipulation, pose accuracy does not necessarily translate into task accuracy: translational and rotational errors jointly affect the transformed gaze ray and may compensate for each other. To study cross-device alignment from this task-oriented perspective, we present a markerless interaction framework and a dedicated cross-device dataset. We propose Graph-based Reference Selection to address sparse robot references. We further develop and benchmark multiple task-specific alignment pipelines under a unified protocol. Specifically, we introduce Gaze--Surface Intersection Error (GSIE), which directly measures the spatial error of the gaze-specified target. Experiments show that alignment methods ranked highly by conventional pose metrics are not always optimal in GSIE, demonstrating the importance of evaluating gaze-based manipulation at the task level.

</details>


### [130] [Exploring Nonlinear Body Oscillations for Natural Quadruped Gaits](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00539)
*Annika Schmidt,Davide Calzolari,Florian Loeffl,Arne Sachtler,Daniel Seidel,Milan Herrmann,Robert Burger,Thomas Gumpert,Antonin Raffin,Tristan Ehlert,Maximilian Pries,David Wandinger,Florian Schmidt,Manuel Keppler,Jinoh Lee,Alin Albu-Schäffer*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a proof-of-concept framework for designing compliant quadruped robots (eBert) whose nonlinear resonance modes (NNMs) are predictable during design, enabling multi-gait locomotion to emerge from mechanical resonance without dedicated controllers. Six NNMs were identified and validated, and black-box optimization showed each mode naturally develops into distinct gaits at different speeds, transferring largely to hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional quadruped robots do not exploit mechanical resonance for locomotion because nonlinear dynamics make such designs difficult to predict. This requires costly dedicated locomotion controllers. The authors aim to leverage embodied intelligence—where posture, muscle stiffness, and mechanical resonance naturally produce effective gaits—by making nonlinear dynamics predictable in the design phase.

Method: The paper introduces new theoretical tools for analyzing nonlinear normal modes (NNMs) of compliant robots. These tools are applied to the highly compliant quadruped robot eBert to identify six NNMs, validated in both simulation and hardware. Black-box optimization determines step length, allowing each NNM to develop into distinct gait patterns at varying speeds.

Result: Six nonlinear normal modes were identified and validated in simulation and on the physical eBert robot. Simulations demonstrated that each NNM naturally evolves into a distinct gait with different speeds, and these results largely transferred to hardware, showing the robot can exploit its own mechanics for task-specific movement.

Conclusion: Exploiting nonlinear resonance shaped by gravity, inertia, and elasticity can enable emergent multi-gait locomotion in compliant robots with minimal active control. This embodied intelligence approach may serve as a foundation for designing a new generation of agile and efficient quadruped robots.

Abstract: Animals' body morphology shapes the gait patterns they can perform, where mechanical resonance reduces the need for active control. By tuning posture and muscle stiffness, they leverage their embodied intelligence to achieve effective gaits for different speeds. In contrast, most quadruped robots are not specifically designed to exploit mechanical resonance due to the complexity of nonlinear dynamics and require dedicated locomotion controllers. To provide an alternative, we present a proof of concept framework making the nonlinear dynamics of a robot predictable in the design process and show how this knowledge can be leveraged such that multi-gait locomotion can emerge from nonlinear resonances, shaped by gravity, inertia, and elasticity. We present the highly compliant quadruped robot eBert, on which we identify six nonlinear normal modes (NNMs) using our new theoretical tools and validate their existence in simulation and hardware. With black-box optimization to determine step length, simulations show how each NNM naturally develops into a distinct gait, manifesting different speeds, which also largely transfers to the robotic hardware. Our experiments show that eBert can exploit its mechanics to generate task-specific movements which may serve as foundation for designing a new generation of agile and efficient robots leveraging embodied intelligence.

</details>


### [131] [Potential-Guided Particle Steering for Negation-Constrained Dexterous Grasping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00555)
*Geonho Kim,SooGon Kim,Jongmin Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces an inference-time framework for negation-constrained dexterous grasping, using Sequential Monte Carlo combined with classifier-free guidance to respect "avoid" instructions without requiring negation-specific training data. It also constructs NegGrasp, a benchmark with paired positive/negative instructions, reducing violation rates from 57.9% to 17.2%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language-driven dexterous grasp models like DextER fail when instructions specify where not to grasp (negation constraints), because existing training corpora contain virtually no avoidance instructions and collecting examples for every constraint is impractical. Additionally, models may misinterpret forbidden parts as grasp targets since every mentioned part in training denotes a contact target.

Method: The method is an inference-time framework combining Sequential Monte Carlo (SMC) with classifier-free guidance. It guides sampling toward the instructed part while pruning candidates headed toward the forbidden region, requiring no negation-specific training examples. A frozen 3D part-grounding model localizes the forbidden region from language. A benchmark, NegGrasp, is constructed with paired positive/negative instructions and constraint-aware metrics that credit a grasp only if it accomplishes the task and respects the constraint.

Result: On the NegGrasp benchmark, the method reduces the violation rate of the strongest baseline from 57.9% to 17.2%, while improving both constraint-aware and physical success metrics.

Conclusion: An inference-time SMC-with-CFG framework can effectively handle negation-constrained dexterous grasping without negation-specific training data, substantially reducing constraint violations while maintaining or improving grasp success.

Abstract: Language-driven dexterous grasp models, such as DextER, perform well when instructions specify where to grasp, but we find they fail systematically when an instruction also specifies where not to grasp (e.g., "grasp the handle but avoid the body"). Existing training corpora, DexGYSNet among them, contain virtually no avoidance instructions, and collecting examples for every possible constraint is impractical. Moreover, because every part mentioned during training denotes a contact target, models may interpret a forbidden part as another region to grasp rather than one to avoid. We therefore introduce an inference-time framework for negation-constrained dexterous grasping that requires no negation-specific training examples. Combining Sequential Monte Carlo with classifier-free guidance, our method guides sampling toward the instructed part while pruning candidates headed for the forbidden region, without any negation examples during training. A frozen 3D part-grounding model localizes the forbidden region from the language instruction. To evaluate this setting, we construct NegGrasp, a benchmark of paired positive/negative instructions with constraint-aware metrics that credit a grasp only if it both accomplishes the task and respects the stated constraint. On NegGrasp, our method reduces the violation rate of the strongest baseline from 57.9% to 17.2% while improving both constraint-aware and physical success.

</details>


### [132] [A Wearable Pneumatic Device for Continuous, Closed-Loop, Bidirectional Tactile Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00612)
*Cosima du Pasquier,Aliyah Smith,Serin Huber,Joshua Phelps,Ilana A. Cohen,Ava Chen,Monroe Kennedy,Allison M. Okamura*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a system of two wearable pneumatic haptic devices enabling continuous, closed-loop, bidirectional tactile interaction. Each device supports up to twelve pressure sensing channels with textile-based pneumatic pouches that serve as sensors, actuators, or both. The system achieves force bandwidth exceeding 30 Hz, rapid step responses, and extended pressure retention. Human studies demonstrate improved force/stiffness discrimination, 23.1% reduction in applied pressures and 27.4% reduction in task duration during teleoperation, and an 18.8% lower subjective mental workload under visually constrained conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable continuous, closed-loop, bidirectional tactile interaction at perceptually relevant force and temporal scales for teleoperation and other applications, unifying tactile sensing and haptic feedback within a single pneumatic modality.
