Method: Two wearable pneumatic haptic devices are developed, each with up to twelve pressure sensing channels connected to textile-based pneumatic pouches. Each channel can act as a sensor, actuator, or both, with local closed-loop control for stable skin indentation. A distributed architecture supports sustained pressure output, rapid dynamic response, and wireless pairing of identical devices for real-time tactile signal transmission and reproduction. Device-level characterization and human studies (force/stiffness discrimination, teleoperated manipulation) were conducted.

Result: Device characterization showed force bandwidth exceeding 30 Hz, rapid and well-damped step responses, and extended pressure retention. Human studies showed pressure-based fingertip feedback enables discrimination of force and stiffness, reduces applied pressures by up to 23.1%, reduces task duration by up to 27.4%, and lowers subjective mental workload by 18.8% under visually constrained conditions during teleoperated manipulation.

Conclusion: By unifying tactile sensing and haptic feedback within a single pneumatic modality, the device provides a practical foundation for bidirectional touch interaction in teleoperation.

Abstract: We present a system of two wearable pneumatic haptic devices that supports continuous, closed-loop, bidirectional tactile interaction at perceptually relevant force and temporal scales. A single device can contain up to twelve pressure sensing channels connected to textile-based pneumatic pouches. Each channel in a device can be used as a sensor, an actuator, or both. As an actuator with integrated sensing, the channel generates stable skin indentation through local closed-loop control. As a sensor, a channel can be mounted (or worn) on any surface, including on a robot gripper or on the human body, and used to measure touch interactions with the environment or a human user. A distributed architecture supports sustained pressure output, rapid dynamic response, and wireless pairing of identical devices in a system to transmit and reproduce tactile pressure signals in real time.
  Device-level characterization demonstrates force bandwidth exceeding 30 Hz, rapid and well-damped step responses, and extended pressure retention compared to prior compact pneumatic platforms. Human studies show that pressure-based fingertip feedback enables discrimination of force and stiffness, improves teleoperated manipulation by reducing applied pressures by up to 23.1% and task duration by up to 27.4%, and lowers subjective mental workload by 18.8%, particularly under visually constrained conditions. By unifying tactile sensing and haptic feedback within a single pneumatic modality, the device provides a practical foundation for bidirectional touch interaction in teleoperation.

</details>


### [133] [DSG: Dynamic 3D Scene Graph Construction for Embodied Agents in Changing Indoor Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00619)
*Ming Liao,Chao Ye,Jianing Fei,Weiyang Lin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DSG is a dynamic 3D scene graph construction framework that detects object changes via dual-view rendering on semantic-aware 3D Gaussians and performs spatial relationship reasoning using multi-granularity visual context with LLMs, validated on the new DynTHOR benchmark and real-world scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Object positions in indoor environments change frequently due to human activities or agent interactions, causing previously constructed scene graphs to become outdated and inconsistent with the current scene state.

Method: DSG builds a semantic-aware 3D Gaussian scene representation, uses dual-view rendering for object change detection to update scene graph nodes, and proposes a spatial relationship reasoning method that injects multi-granularity visual context into a large language model to identify richer inter-object spatial relations. A new benchmark, DynTHOR, is introduced on AI2-THOR for evaluation.

Result: Extensive experiments on DynTHOR, 3RScan, and real-world scenes show DSG consistently outperforms existing methods in both object node construction and spatial relationship reasoning, significantly improving dynamic scene graph construction accuracy.

Conclusion: The proposed DSG framework effectively handles dynamic environments by detecting object changes and reasoning spatial relationships, achieving state-of-the-art performance across simulation and real-world benchmarks.

Abstract: In indoor environments, object positions frequently change due to human activities or embodied-agent interactions, causing previously constructed scene graphs to become inconsistent with the current scene. To address this issue, we propose DSG, a dynamic 3D scene graph construction framework that detects object changes and performs spatial relationship reasoning. First, we construct a semantic-aware 3D Gaussian scene representation and develop a dual-view rendering-based object change detection method to enable reliable scene graph node updates. Second, we propose a spatial relationship reasoning method that incorporates multi-granularity visual context, enabling a large language model to identify a richer set of interobject spatial relationships. Furthermore, we introduce DynTHOR, a dynamic indoor scene graph benchmark built on the AI2-THOR simulation platform for evaluating scene graph construction in dynamic environments. Extensive experiments on Dyn-THOR, 3RScan, and real-world scenes demonstrate that DSG consistently outperforms existing methods in both object node construction and spatial relationship reasoning, significantly improving the accuracy of dynamic scene graph construction.

</details>


### [134] [AM-Bench: A Modular Simulation Suite and Benchmark for Aerial Manipulation Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00641)
*Yutong Wang,Dongjae Lee,Xiaofeng Guo,Yuanzhu Zhan,Yufei Jiang,Bavin Saravanan,Muqing Cao,Jia Xie,Chenyang Mao,Sebastian Scherer,Junyi Geng,Guanya Shi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AM-Bench is a modular simulation suite and benchmark for multirotor-based aerial manipulation (AM) policy learning, spanning underactuated, fully actuated, and overactuated embodiments across 12 tasks, while enabling system-level evaluation of how embodiment, control, disturbances, and policy choices interact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standardized manipulation benchmarks largely focus on ground-supported systems, ignoring the distinct system-level challenges of aerial manipulation such as environmental disturbances, coupled manipulator-floating base dynamics, and constrained degrees of freedom. Existing benchmarks predominantly measure end-to-end policy performance, failing to capture how embodiment, low-level control, and policy design jointly determine task success in AM.

Method: AM-Bench provides a modular simulation suite featuring representative embodiments (underactuated, fully actuated, overactuated), 12 tasks spanning contact, transport, and constrained interaction, configurable aerodynamic disturbances and actuator saturation, standard low-level controllers, and baseline policy-learning algorithms. It enables diagnostic system-level evaluation rather than just end-to-end policy comparison.

Result: Three simulation studies demonstrate the benchmark's diagnostic value, examining high-level policies, policy-control interfaces, and embodiments. Real-world validation of modeled effects and a hardware test of the full learning pipeline confirm the simulation findings.

Conclusion: AM-Bench addresses a gap in manipulation benchmarks by providing a platform that makes visible the complex interactions between embodiment, control, disturbances, and policy learning in aerial manipulation, with diagnostic studies and hardware validation supporting its utility.

Abstract: Standardized benchmarks have played a central role in advancing robot manipulation learning, yet most focus on ground-supported manipulation systems, which limits their applicability to dynamics-critical domains such as aerial manipulation (AM). AM presents distinct system-level challenges, including environmental disturbances, coupled dynamics between the manipulator and floating base, and constrained degrees of freedom. Consequently, task performance depends jointly on robot embodiment, low-level control, and high-level policy design. We introduce AM-Bench, a modular simulation suite and benchmark for multirotor-based AM policy learning. AM-Bench includes representative embodiments spanning underactuated, fully actuated, and overactuated systems, 12 tasks across contact, transport, and constrained interaction, configurable aerodynamic disturbances and actuator saturation, standard low-level controllers, and baseline policy-learning algorithms. Unlike prior manipulation benchmarks that primarily emphasize end-to-end policy performance, AM-Bench enables system-level evaluation of how embodiment, control, disturbances, and policy choices interact. We demonstrate its diagnostic value through three simulation studies spanning high-level policies, policy--control interfaces, and embodiments, together with real-world validation of modeled effects and a hardware test of the learning pipeline.

</details>


### [135] [Human-robot conversation with multiple participants in noisy public spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00648)
*Divesh Lala,Yogeeswaran Muthukumaran,Vincent Fernandes,Kazushi Kato,Shota Fujiki,Zihao Chi,Masaya Iwasaki,Taiken Shintani,Megumi Kawata,Kazuki Sakai,Koji Inoue,Yuicihiro Yoshikawa,Tatsuya Kawahara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents an audio system with a single multi-channel microphone array that enhances multi-speaker speech in noisy environments for two real-world scenarios demonstrated at the 2025 World Expo in Osaka: an attentive listening system with the android ERICA and a conversation support system with mobile Teleco robots (one acting as an avatar for a remote operator). The system also provides spatial audio for more immersive avatar-based interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spoken dialogue systems for autonomous robots and avatars in noisy real-world environments (e.g., open public spaces) require enhanced speech signals for both speech recognition and clean speech transmission to remote operators.

Method: The proposed audio system uses a single multi-channel microphone array to enable speech enhancement of multiple speakers in noisy environments and to provide spatial audio for immersive avatar-based conversational interactions. It was validated across two scenarios: attentive listening with the android ERICA and conversation support via mobile Teleco robots.

Result: The system was demonstrated as a proof-of-concept at the 2025 World Expo in Osaka, supporting multi-party conversation in both scenarios and delivering both enhanced speech and spatial audio capabilities.

Conclusion: A unified audio system based on multi-channel microphone array processing can serve both autonomous robot speech recognition and remote operator avatar communication needs, adding spatial audio immersion to enhance conversational interactions in noisy public environments.

Abstract: For noisy real-world environments such as those in open public spaces, spoken dialogue systems for both autonomous robots and avatars should be carefully designed to provide enhanced speech signals. These signals can be used either for speech recognition or, in the case of an avatar system, transmitted as clean speech to a remote operator. This work proposes an audio system that can be used for both these scenarios and was demonstrated as a proof-of-concept at the 2025 World Expo in Osaka. The first scenario is an attentive listening system with the android ERICA, and the second is a conversation support system with mobile Teleco robots, with one of them acting as an avatar for a remote operator. Both systems feature multi-party conversation and use a single multi-channel microphone array. We describe how our audio system not only enhances the speech of multiple speakers in a noisy environment, but provides a form of spatial audio which allows for more immersiveness in avatar-based conversational interactions.

</details>


### [136] [Fleets Need a Context Plane: Rethinking Cooperative Perception for Autonomous Drones](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00659)
*Liangkai Liu,Xiaoxiao Wu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces the "context plane," a bounded, structured runtime interface for cooperative perception in drone fleets that enables lightweight, dynamic feature-sharing policies without retraining perception models. On UAV3D, mission-aware sharing matches full-sharing accuracy using only 5-10% of bytes, and the interface costs approximately 0.01% of data-plane bandwidth with 0.10 ms per policy decision on a Jetson AGX Orin.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cooperative perception systems for drone fleets fix feature-sharing policies at design time or adapt to only a single context signal, which poorly suits aerial missions where bandwidth, formation geometry, scene coverage, and objectives may change during flight. Context-blind sharing incurs significant accuracy loss (up to 7.7 AP from wrong peer selection, 5.9 AP discrepancy within mission regions under constrained budgets), motivating the need for an explicit, flexible runtime context interface.

Method: The authors propose the "context plane": a standardized interface where each drone publishes a compact descriptor (at most 1 KB at 10 Hz) capturing fleet context. Lightweight, replaceable policies consume this fleet context to decide computation, sharing, and fusion strategies at runtime. Experiments use a released DiscoNet checkpoint on UAV3D to control feature exchange at evaluation time without retraining, testing mission-aware and budget-constrained peer-selection policies. A ROS 2 prototype on a Jetson AGX Orin measures overhead.

Result: Mission-aware sharing matches full-sharing accuracy with only 5-10% of bytes transmitted. The optimal peer-selection policy varies with byte budget; wrong choices lose up to 7.7 AP. Under constrained budgets, two policies with identical full-scene accuracy differ by 5.9 AP within the mission region. The context plane consumes ~0.01% of data-plane bandwidth and each policy decision takes 0.10 ms, demonstrating negligible overhead.

Conclusion: An explicit context interface can support low-overhead runtime adaptation for cooperative perception without modifying or retraining the perception model. Existing sharing schemes can be re-expressed as fixed policies within the context plane, and jointly considering multiple context axes (peer selection, byte budget, mission region) is essential for effective aerial cooperative perception.

Abstract: Cooperative perception allows a drone fleet to combine observations from multiple viewpoints. However, existing systems typically fix their feature-sharing policies at design time or adapt to only one context signal. This is a poor fit for aerial fleets, whose missions, bandwidth, formation geometry, and scene coverage can change during flight. We quantify the cost of context-blind sharing on UAV3D by controlling feature exchange at evaluation time using a released DiscoNet checkpoint, without retraining. Mission-aware sharing matches full-sharing accuracy while using only 5-10% of the bytes. The best tested peer selection policy changes with the byte budget, and choosing the wrong policy loses up to 7.7 AP. Moreover, under a constrained budget, two policies with the same full-scene accuracy differ by 5.9 AP within the mission region, showing that multiple context axes must be considered jointly. We therefore propose the context plane, a bounded, structured interface for runtime context. Each drone publishes a descriptor of at most 1 KB at 10 Hz, and lightweight, replaceable policies use the fleet context to decide what each drone computes, shares, and fuses. Existing sharing schemes become fixed policies within this interface. In our ROS 2 prototype on a Jetson AGX Orin, the context plane uses approximately 0.01% of the data-plane bandwidth, and each policy decision takes 0.10 ms. These results show that an explicit context interface can support low-overhead runtime adaptation without modifying or retraining the perception model.

</details>


### [137] [Behavior--Realization Separation for Constrained Physical Human--Robot Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00669)
*Yongyan Cao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a two-layer architecture for physical human-robot interaction that separates behavior specification (a behavior layer providing desired contact-point acceleration) from constrained realization (a realization layer converting these demands into feasible robot commands via a receding-horizon quadratic program). The realization layer reports desired-versus-realized acceleration error rather than hiding it in saturation, and uses an unconstrained counterfactual to separate regularization effects from constraint intervention. Experiments on a planar robotic study and a simulated 7-DOF Franka FR3 demonstrate that the same realization layer accepts impedance/admittance behavior reassignments without reconstruction, and horizon-wide torque enforcement yields far smaller workspace overshoot and actuator-feasibility violations than instantaneous clipping or first-step-only enforcement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional physical human-robot interaction (pHRI) control software tightly couples desired-behavior specification with constrained realization, making it difficult to distinguish whether performance degradations stem from the behavior model itself or from the effects of hard constraints (e.g., actuator limits, workspace boundaries). This coupling obscures evaluation and hinders flexibility in swapping behaviors without reconstructing the control stack.

Method: The authors decompose pHRI control into two layers: (1) a behavior layer that specifies a desired contact-port acceleration as a function of state and human force (memoryless affine behaviors), and (2) a realization layer that solves a receding-horizon quadratic program (QP) to convert the desired acceleration into feasible robot commands. Behavior changes only alter objective coefficients through (Cθ, Gθ), while the optimization variables and feasible set remain fixed. The realization layer reports total desired-versus-realized acceleration error, compares against an unconstrained counterfactual to isolate constraint effects, and uses plant data to expose model error. Torque feasibility is enforced across the full horizon per solve.

Result: In a planar study, the realization layer accepts impedance-admittance-impedance behavior reassignment without reconstruction under existing rate limits. On a torque-controlled 7-DOF Franka FR3 simulated in MuJoCo under a sustained 20 N push, slack-relaxed workspace boundary is held to within ~0.1-0.2 mm, versus 4.4 cm (impedance) and 4.7 cm (admittance) overshoot from instantaneous clipping. With a derated actuator budget, horizon-wide torque enforcement maintains a frozen-model plan feasible to 2.1×10⁻⁴ N·m, while a first-step-only ablation exceeds the budget by up to 11.329 N·m; on the executed nonlinear plant the gap is smaller but still favors horizon-wide enforcement (0.161 vs. 0.380 N·m).

Conclusion: The results provide a focused proof-of-concept that separating behavior specification from constrained realization yields clear diagnostic benefits (constraint effects distinguished from model/behavior errors), enables flexible swapping between impedance and admittance behaviors without reconfiguration, and delivers substantially improved constraint satisfaction (in both workspace and actuator domains) compared to instantaneous clipping or myopic single-step enforcement.

Abstract: Physical human--robot interaction software often couples desired-behavior specification with constrained realization; we treat these as separate layers. A \emph{behavior layer} supplies a desired contact-port acceleration $a_k^{\mathrm{id}}=f_θ(e_k,\dot e_k,F_{h,k})$. A \emph{realization layer} converts it into constrained robot commands and reports total desired-versus-realized acceleration error instead of hiding it in saturation. A same-objective unconstrained counterfactual separates regularization from constraint intervention, while plant data expose model error. This paper implements a receding-horizon quadratic program realizing memoryless affine behaviors. Changing the behavior modifies objective coefficients through $(C_θ,G_θ)$ while the robot-command variable and feasible set remain unchanged. A planar study instantiates impedance and admittance; the same running layer accepts an impedance--admittance--impedance reassignment without reconstruction, under its existing rate limit. On a torque-controlled 7-DOF Franka FR3 in MuJoCo, the runtime freezes task-space dynamics per solve and enforces torque feasibility across its horizon. Under a sustained 20~N push, it holds a slack-relaxed workspace boundary to within approximately 0.1--0.2~mm, versus 4.4~cm (impedance) and 4.7~cm (admittance) overshoot from instantaneous clipping. A derated actuator budget then activates the torque constraint: horizon-wide enforcement keeps its frozen-model plan feasible to $2.1\times10^{-4}$~N$\cdot$m, whereas a first-step-only ablation plans up to 11.329~N$\cdot$m beyond budget; on the executed nonlinear plant, where both share the same local-model error, the gap is smaller but still favors horizon-wide enforcement (0.161 vs.\ 0.380~N$\cdot$m). These results are a focused proof of behavior--realization separation.

</details>


### [138] [ADAPT: Agile Diffusion Action Priors for Robust and Steerable Online Text-Driven Humanoid Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00677)
*Yan Wu,Chenhao Li,Kaifeng Zhao,Gen Li,Marco Hutter,Siyu Tang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ADAPT is an end-to-end framework for interactive, text-conditioned humanoid whole-body control, combining a diffusion-based action prior with residual reinforcement learning for robust language-grounded skill execution, smooth transitions, and downstream task adaptation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-motion pipelines generate kinematic motions that require a separate tracker, lacking true closed-loop control. There is a need for an end-to-end framework where a humanoid robot can continuously respond to natural language commands in real-time while maintaining balance, natural motion, and smooth transitions between skills.

Method: ADAPT learns a diffusion-based action prior from text-labeled humanoid state-action trajectories for direct language-to-action execution. A lightweight residual reinforcement learning policy is trained on top of the frozen diffusion controller to improve long-horizon robustness and enable smooth prompt switching. The diffusion policy is also reusable as a steerable text-conditioned motion prior for downstream task adaptation.

Result: Experiments demonstrate robust language-grounded skill execution, smooth interactive transitions during prompt changes, and style-preserving downstream control, validating the effectiveness of the hybrid diffusion + RL framework.

Conclusion: ADAPT establishes a viable end-to-end paradigm for interactive text-conditioned humanoid control, addressing key limitations of prior kinematic-only approaches through a closed-loop, action-level framework that is both directly executable and adaptable to downstream tasks.

Abstract: We present ADAPT, an end-to-end framework for interactive, text-conditioned humanoid whole-body control. Unlike dominant text-to-motion pipelines that generate kinematic motions for a separate tracker, ADAPT solves language control with an end-to-end closed-loop control framework, where the robot must continuously respond to changing commands while maintaining balance, natural motion, and smooth transitions. ADAPT learns a diffusion-based action prior from text-labeled humanoid state-action trajectories, enabling diverse motion skills to be directly executed from language commands. To improve long-horizon robustness and smooth prompt switching, we train a lightweight residual reinforcement learning policy on top of the frozen diffusion controller. We further show that the same diffusion policy can be reused as a steerable text-conditioned motion prior for downstream task adaptation. Experiments demonstrate robust language-grounded skill execution, smooth interactive transitions, and style-preserving downstream control.

</details>


### [139] [Context-Aware Intelligent Vehicles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00682)
*Liangkai Liu,Shuyao Shi,Mingke Wang,Noah T. Curran,Chuan Li,Fan Bai,Kang G. Shin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This position/survey paper argues that context-situational factors should be treated as a first-class principle in next-generation intelligent vehicle systems, operationalized as a unified shared state across the software stack. It systematically reviews state-of-the-art context-aware methods across environment understanding, planning/control, safety/security, and connected vehicles, and identifies four technical challenges: multi-modal context fusion, temporal context modeling, handling rare events, and collaborative context sharing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Intelligent vehicles run adaptive applications under tight accuracy, latency, cost, and reliability constraints while operating in complex, uncertain, rapidly changing environments on resource-constrained platforms. Existing systems do not treat context—which gives meaning to sensor signals and constrains decisions—as a first-class design principle, limiting robustness and efficiency.

Method: The paper provides a systematic review of state-of-the-art context-aware methods across four application domains: (i) environment understanding, (ii) planning and control, (iii) safety and security, and (iv) connected vehicles. It then performs a trend analysis to distill recurring design patterns and derive technical challenges for building a general contextual engine.

Result: The trend analysis of context-aware design reveals four key technical challenges for a general contextual engine in future intelligent vehicles: multi-modal context fusion, temporal context modeling, handling rare events, and collaborative context sharing.

Conclusion: The authors hope this survey will motivate the development of robust and efficient context-aware vehicle applications, positioning context as a foundational, shared state for learning, risk assessment, and closed-loop control across the software stack.

Abstract: Intelligent vehicles increasingly support adaptive applications beyond driving themselves, ranging from context-aware ADAS and automated driving to in-cabin monitoring and fleet management, all under tight requirements on accuracy, latency, cost, and reliability. Meeting these requirements is challenging because vehicles operate in complex, uncertain, and rapidly changing environments while running on resource-constrained computing platforms. This paper argues that context-situational factors that give meaning to sensor signals and constrain decisions-should be treated as a first-class principle for next-generation vehicle systems, and operationalized as a unified, shared state for learning, risk assessment, and closed-loop control across the software stack. We systematically review state-of-the- art (SOTA) context-aware methods spanning (i) environment understanding, (ii) planning and control, (iii) safety and security, and (iv) connected vehicles. Based on a trend analysis of context-aware design, we identify four key technical challenges in building a general contextual engine for future intelligent vehicles: multi-modal context fusion, temporal context modeling, handling rare events, and collaborative context sharing. We hope this survey will motivate the development of robust and efficient context-aware vehicle applications.

</details>


### [140] [One Print, Many Moves: Monolithic Origami-inspired Folding Actuator for Composable Soft Multi-DoF Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00751)
*Jaehyung Jang,Zhenish Zhakypov,Jasmin Elena Palmer,Melissa Klein,Jee-Hwan Ryu,Allison Mariko Okamura*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MONORIGAMI introduces a monolithic 3D-printable soft folding actuator that uses origami-inspired spatial stiffness programming (varying material thickness) to convert unconstrained soft deformation into precise, repeatable folding motions. Each actuator acts as a scalable motion primitive, and multiple actuators can be linked and oriented to program multi-DoF trajectories. The approach is validated through three distinct soft robotic systems: a 4-DoF wearable haptic device for VR, a 3-DoF joystick for teleoperation, and a modular underwater gripper.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional soft actuators excel in compliance but suffer from uncontrolled deformations that compromise accuracy and prevent scaling to multi-degree-of-freedom (multi-DoF) systems, limiting their practical utility in high-precision robotic applications.

Method: The design follows a spatially programmed stiffness anisotropy strategy: stiffness tiers are created by patterning material thickness in an origami-inspired geometry with distinct facets and creases, preserving compliance along desired folding directions while restricting deformation in unwanted directions. The actuator is fabricated via a single-material, single-print 3D printing process with no assembly required. Actuators serve as modular motion primitives that are mechanically programmed by linking and orienting them to achieve multi-DoF trajectories.

Result: Three fully 3D-printed soft multi-DoF robotic systems were demonstrated from the same fundamental module: (1) a compact 4-DoF wearable haptic device delivering high-fidelity cutaneous feedback in VR, (2) a 3-DoF joystick for kinesthetic feedback in teleoperation, and (3) a modular robotic gripper for underwater operation with geometry-encoded grasp trajectories. These validate compact multi-axis integration, controlled physical interaction, and geometry-programmed operation across diverse environments.

Conclusion: MONORIGAMI provides a general, composable, accessible, reliable, and scalable platform for high-precision soft multi-DoF robotics, addressing long-standing limitations in both soft actuator design and fabrication by eliminating uncontrolled deformation without sacrificing compliance.

Abstract: Conventional soft robot actuators excel in compliance, but their uncontrolled deformations compromise accuracy and hinder scaling to multi-degree-of-freedom (DoF) systems. We introduce a MONOlithic ORIGAMI-inspired soft folding actuator design (MONORIGAMI) that establishes a design strategy based on spatially programmed stiffness anisotropy to preserve material compliance along desired folding directions while selectively restricting deformation in unwanted directions. The actuator leverages stiffness tiers based on material thickness, patterned in an origami-inspired geometry with facets and creases, converting unconstrained soft deformation into accurate, repeatable, and composable folding motions without additional reinforcements. The design is fully 3D-printable through a single-material, single-print process that requires no assembly. Each actuator serves as a scalable motion primitive, and linking and orienting multiple actuators mechanically programs multi-DoF trajectories. Using the same fundamental module, we demonstrate three 3D-printed soft multi-DoF robotic systems spanning distinct application domains: (1) a compact 4-DoF wearable haptic device for high-fidelity cutaneous feedback in virtual reality (VR), (2) a 3-DoF joystick for kinesthetic feedback in teleoperation, and (3) a modular robotic gripper capable of underwater operation with geometry-encoded grasp trajectories. These systems demonstrate the module's capabilities for compact multi-axis integration, controlled physical interaction, and geometry-programmed operation across different environments. Together, these results show that MONORIGAMI provides a general, composable, accessible, reliable, and scalable platform for high-precision soft multi-DoF robotics, addressing long-standing limitations in both soft actuator design and fabrication.

</details>


### [141] [A Compact Robotic Finger with 2-DoF MCP Joint Embedding DoF-Selective Passive Continuously Variable Transmission for Wide Force-Speed Operating Range](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00769)
*JaeHyung Jang,Jee-Hwan Ryu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This letter presents a compact two-degree-of-freedom robotic finger incorporating a passive continuously variable transmission (CVT) specifically assigned to the flexion-extension DoF, achieving a wide force-speed operating range. The mechanism uses a force-responsive moving-pulley-inspired wire-routing structure to passively increase the flexion moment arm based on applied load, yielding a 4.19-fold maximum and 3.6-fold mean output-force amplification without actuators, sensors, or control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human robotic fingers with multiple degrees of freedom typically trade off force and speed capabilities. Inspired by the functional differentiation of the human metacarpophalangeal (MCP) joint, the authors seek a compact mechanism that achieves DoF-specific transmission differentiation—selectively providing variable transmission where needed (flexion-extension) while preserving direct transmission elsewhere (abduction-adduction)—to broaden the force-speed operating envelope in compact multi-DoF robotic hands.

Method: The proposed mechanism implements an output-side passive CVT using a moving-pulley-inspired wire-routing structure embedded in the flexion pathway. Wire tensions generate forces on the pulley that passively increase the flexion moment arm and transmission ratio according to applied load, eliminating the need for extra actuators, sensors, or controllers. Direct transmission is maintained for the abduction-adduction pathway to preserve that DoF's functionality.

Result: Experiments show a maximum output-force amplification of 4.19-fold and a mean amplification of 3.6-fold across tested flexion angles from 15 to 75 degrees via moment-arm adaptation. Ball-rolling experiments further confirm that passive transmission adaptation is achievable while preserving abduction-adduction functionality.

Conclusion: The results validate a scalable transmission design strategy for compact multi-DoF robotic hands, demonstrating that load-responsive passive transmission differentiation can substantially expand the force-speed operating range without additional active components or control complexity.

Abstract: This letter presents a compact two-degree-of-freedom (DoF) robotic finger with a flexion-selective passive continuously variable transmission (CVT) to achieve a wide force-speed operating range. Inspired by the functional differentiation of the human metacarpophalangeal (MCP) joint, the proposed mechanism realizes DoF-specific transmission differentiation by selectively assigning passive CVT to the flexion-extension DoF while preserving direct transmission for abduction-adduction. For a wide force-speed operating range, a force-responsive passive CVT is embedded in the flexion pathway, while direct transmission is preserved for the abduction-adduction pathway. To selectively realize transmission adaptation within a multi-DoF MCP mechanism, an output-side passive CVT employing a moving-pulley-inspired wire-routing structure is introduced. The resultant force generated by the wire tensions acting on the pulley that passively increases the flexion moment arm and transmission ratio according to the applied load without additional actuators, sensors, or control. Experimental results demonstrate a maximum output-force amplification of 4.19-fold and a mean amplification of 3.6-fold across the tested flexion angles ranging from 15 degrees to 75 degrees through moment-arm adaptation, thereby substantially expanding the achievable force-speed operating range. Furthermore, dexterous ball-rolling experiments verify that passive transmission adaptation can be achieved while preserving abduction-adduction functionality. These results demonstrate a scalable transmission design strategy for compact multi-DoF robotic hands.

</details>


### [142] [Non-Prehensile Throwing: A Reinforcement Learning Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00771)
*Abdullah Mustafa,Ryo Hanai,Ixchel G. Ramirez-Alpizar,Floris Erich,Ryoichi Nakajo,Yukiyasu Domae,Tetsuya Ogata*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a reinforcement learning approach for non-prehensile robotic throwing that leverages sliding and rolling contact modes without analytical contact models, achieving 99% simulation and 97% real-world success rates on a UR5e throwing diverse objects up to 350 cm.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing non-prehensile throwing approaches rely on model-based optimization with simplified contact models and low-dimensional trajectory parameterizations, which limit solution quality and reachable workspace. For large, heavy, and/or deformable objects that cannot be grasped, these limitations are particularly problematic.

Method: The method proposes a reinforcement learning (RL) framework that: (1) leverages sliding and rolling contact modes beyond dynamic grasping; (2) directly optimizes joint-space trajectories without analytical contact models or custom parameterizations; (3) formulates the MDP as a dynamical system evolving joint states conditioned on throwing target, object model, and initial configuration; (4) plans joint-jerk trajectories offline at low control rate, upsampled to high-rate velocity commands; (5) employs minimum-jerk system identification to minimize sim-to-real robot-dynamics gap; (6) trains uncertainty-aware policies to mitigate object-modeling errors, particularly dynamic friction sensitivity.

Result: In simulation, the policy achieves 99% success across thousands of configurations and generalizes to unseen objects. Sensitivity analysis shows robustness to mass uncertainty but high sensitivity to dynamic friction, consistent with the sliding-based release mechanism. Real-world deployment on a UR5e near physical limits (5 m/s end-effector velocity) throws diverse objects including heavy (790 g) and large (20x20x28 cm) items to targets up to 350 cm distance or 180 cm elevation with 97% success rate.

Conclusion: The RL-based approach using sliding and rolling contacts outperforms model-based optimization methods, enabling non-prehensile throwing of heavy and large objects over extended workspaces while maintaining high reliability both in simulation and zero-shot real-world deployment.

Abstract: Robotic throwing enables fast object transport and extends a robot's reachable workspace beyond traditional pick-and-place. While prehensile (grasp-based) throwing works well for graspable items, non-prehensile (grasp-free) throwing is better suited for large, heavy, and/or deformable objects. Existing approaches rely on model-based optimization with simplified contact models (e.g., dynamic grasping) and low-dimensional trajectory parameterizations, which limit solution quality and reachable workspace. We propose a reinforcement learning approach that additionally leverages sliding and rolling contact modes and directly optimizes joint-space trajectories without analytical contact models or custom parameterizations. The Markov Decision Process (MDP) is formulated as a dynamical system that evolves the robot's joint state conditioned on the throwing target, object model, and initial configuration. Joint-jerk trajectories are planned offline at a low control rate and upsampled into smooth, high-rate velocity commands for deployment. For sim-to-real transfer, we minimize the robot-dynamics gap through minimum-jerk system identification and train uncertainty-aware policies to mitigate object-modeling errors, particularly sensitivity to dynamic friction. In simulation, the policy achieves 99% success across thousands of configurations and generalizes to unseen objects. Sensitivity analysis shows robustness to mass uncertainty but high sensitivity to dynamic friction, consistent with the sliding-based release mechanism. Deployed zero-shot on a UR5e operating near its physical limits (5 m/s end-effector velocity), our method throws diverse objects including heavy (790 g) and large (20x20x28 cm) items to targets up to 350 cm distance or 180 cm elevation, achieving a 97% real-world success rate.

</details>


### [143] [Connectivity-Aware Graph Extension for Decentralized Multi-Robot Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00804)
*Béatrice Garcia Cegarra,Elena Vanneaux,Quentin Picard,David Filliat*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a novel frontier-connectivity-based exploration graph extension strategy for decentralized multi-UAV exploration under intermittent communication. The method maintains stable area partitioning among agents that is robust to disconnections and spatial layout changes, and is validated by applying it to two state-of-the-art area partitioning methods in simulation, showing improved exploration efficiency under low communication rates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-UAV exploration in unknown environments requires coordination under intermittent communication. Since centralized coordination is infeasible under such constraints, decentralized operation is a baseline assumption. Existing exploration graph extension approaches underperform in this setting, motivating the need for a strategy that keeps area partitioning among agents stable and robust to communication disconnections and spatial layout changes.

Method: The authors propose a novel exploration graph extension strategy based on frontier connectivity within a decentralized framework. The method extends exploration plans while maintaining stable area partitioning among agents, and is designed to be robust to disconnections and spatial layout changes. The proposed extension is applied on top of two state-of-the-art area partitioning methods and evaluated in simulation.

Result: Simulation experiments demonstrate that the proposed frontier-connectivity-based graph extension strategy outperforms existing graph extension approaches, achieving higher exploration efficiency under low communication rates.

Conclusion: The frontier-connectivity-based exploration graph extension method improves decentralized multi-UAV exploration performance under intermittent communication, particularly at low communication rates, and generalizes across different area partitioning methods.

Abstract: Exploring unknown environments with multiple UAVs requires coordination under intermittent communication, making decentralized operation a baseline assumption. We propose, within a decentralized framework, a novel exploration graph extension strategy based on frontier connectivity to extend exploration plans and maintain area partitioning among agents stable and robust to disconnections and changes in spatial layout. The proposed extension method is applied to two state-of-the-art area partitioning methods and evaluated in simulation. Experiments show improved performance over existing graph extension approaches with higher exploration efficiency under low communication rate.

</details>


### [144] [Peg-in-Bench: A Modular Benchmark for High-Precision Robotic Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00906)
*Yosel Delgado,José G. Buenaventura-Carreón,Floris Erich,Roman Mykhailyshyn,Tomohiro Motoda,Koshi Makihara,Yukiyasu Domae*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a reconfigurable, fully 3D-printable peg-in-hole benchmark for evaluating task generalization in high-precision robotic insertion. It features modular components (peg geometries, tolerance levels, configurable bases) that generate diverse insertion tasks, plus a scenario generation tool for standardized, reproducible task configurations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing peg-in-hole benchmarks rely on fixed task configurations, which limits their ability to assess robustness and generalization across different insertion scenarios in robotic manipulation.

Method: The authors design a benchmark with modular 3D-printable components—multiple peg geometries, tolerance levels, and configurable base structures—that combine to create a wide variety of insertion tasks. A scenario generation tool produces standardized configurations and machine-readable task descriptions, varying layouts, orientations, and task structures under controlled physical conditions.

Result: The benchmark enables systematic evaluation of adaptation to unseen insertion scenarios through reconfigurable task generation, with reproducibility supported by open-source STL files and the scenario generation tooling.

Conclusion: The proposed reconfigurable benchmark addresses the limitation of fixed-configuration peg-in-hole benchmarks by providing a flexible, reproducible evaluation platform for assessing generalization in high-precision robotic insertion tasks.

Abstract: High-precision insertion remains a fundamental challenge in robotic manipulation due to the strict alignment requirements and contact-rich interactions involved. Although peg-in-hole tasks are widely used for evaluation, existing bench- marks often rely on fixed task configurations, limiting their ability to assess robustness and generalization across different insertion scenarios. This paper introduces a reconfigurable peg-in-hole benchmark designed to evaluate task generalization in high-precision insertion. The benchmark consists of a set of fully 3D-printable modular components, including multiple peg geometries, tolerance levels, and configurable base structures that can be combined to generate a large variety of insertion and assembly tasks. By varying object layouts, orientations, and task structures while maintaining controlled physical conditions, the benchmark enables systematic evaluation of adaptation to unseen scenarios. To support reproducibility, we additionally provide a scenario generation tool capable of producing standardized task configurations and machine-readable task descriptions. The scenario generation tool and the STL files of the benchmark pieces are available through the project repository: https://github.com/aistairc/peg-in-bench.

</details>


### [145] [Knowing When to Stop: Adaptive Action Chunking via Internal Cross-Attention Dynamics in VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00908)
*Runze Xu,Xiaolong Shan,Shuang Dai,Yu Wang,Jincheng Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a training-free, adaptive action chunking method for Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models. By monitoring the entropy of action-to-observation cross-attention over the prediction horizon, the method detects when attention becomes dispersed and hits a high-entropy plateau—an indicator of reduced grounding for further open-loop execution. It then truncates the action chunk dynamically at inference time, improving task success on π0.5 and X-VLA across RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO, and real-world tasks with negligible overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed action chunking horizons in VLA frameworks create a fundamental efficiency-accuracy trade-off: short chunks incur frequent inference and oscillatory behavior, while long chunks risk misalignment with newly observed states. The paper seeks an adaptive mechanism to select execution horizon dynamically without retraining.

Method: The method leverages the observation that as the prediction horizon extends, action-to-observation cross-attention in the action expert becomes progressively dispersed, with entropy rising toward a plateau—correlating with higher action prediction error. A training-free truncation mechanism monitors for sustained high-entropy plateaus and dynamically halts open-loop execution at inference. It reuses attention weights already computed by the policy, adding negligible overhead.

Result: Evaluations on π0.5 and X-VLA over RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO, and three real-world manipulation tasks show improved average task success compared to fixed-horizon and adaptive chunking baselines, while maintaining efficient closed-loop control.

Conclusion: Cross-attention dynamics provide a practical, internal, training-free signal for adaptive action execution in VLA frameworks, resolving the fixed-horizon trade-off between efficiency and accuracy.

Abstract: Action chunking is a standard execution strategy in modern Vision-Language-Action (VLA) frameworks, but fixed execution horizons impose a trade-off between efficiency and accuracy. Short chunks require frequent inference and may cause oscillatory behavior, whereas long chunks can become misaligned with newly observed states. We address this limitation with an adaptive action chunking approach based on internal cross-attention dynamics in the action expert. We observe that, as the prediction horizon extends, action-to-observation cross-attention becomes increasingly dispersed and its entropy rises toward a plateau. This pattern is associated with higher action prediction error and provides an online signal that the current observation offers limited grounding for further open-loop execution. Based on this observation, we introduce a training-free truncation mechanism that detects sustained high-entropy plateaus and dynamically selects the execution horizon during inference. The method uses attention weights already computed by the policy and introduces negligible additional overhead. Evaluations on $π_{0.5}$ and X-VLA across RoboTwin 2.0, LIBERO, and three real-world manipulation tasks show improved average task success over fixed-horizon and adaptive chunking baselines, while preserving efficient closed-loop control. These results show that cross-attention dynamics can provide a practical internal signal for adaptive action execution in VLAs.

</details>


### [146] [VerNav: Verifier-First Low-Latency Vision-and-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00920)
*Zhixin Wang,Chengzheyi Yao,Leyuan Liu,Xiaosong Zhang,Yongzhao Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: VerNav is a verifier-first framework for Vision-and-Language Navigation that replaces per-step autoregressive generation with batched action verification to reduce decision-stage latency by over 10x while maintaining competitive navigation performance, using an entropy-based adaptive generator for uncertain decisions and a two-stage alignment scheme (VPO + step-level reinforcement fine-tuning).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicit reasoning in LLM-based VLN improves instruction understanding and semantic grounding, but autoregressive generation at every navigation step accumulates large decision-stage latency over multi-step navigation, making it impractical for real-time embodied agents.

Method: VerNav employs a verifier-first design: (i) a verifier performs batched action verification instead of per-step autoregressive generation; (ii) an entropy-based adaptive generator is activated only for uncertain decisions to produce compact state evidence; (iii) a two-stage alignment scheme is introduced—Verifier Preference Optimization (VPO) for local action-preference alignment in static training, and step-level reinforcement fine-tuning with dense progress rewards over multi-step navigation rollouts during dynamic task execution.

Result: On the Room-to-Room (R2R) benchmark, VerNav's verifier-only decision path achieves competitive navigation performance among representative LLM-based VLN agents while reducing average decision-stage LLM latency per step by more than 10x compared to autoregressive methods.

Conclusion: VerNav demonstrates that a verifier-first paradigm can significantly reduce latency in LLM-based VLN without sacrificing navigation accuracy, suggesting a promising direction for real-time embodied navigation systems.

Abstract: Vision-and-Language Navigation (VLN) requires an agent to navigate through unseen 3D environments according to natural-language instructions. Explicit reasoning can improve instruction understanding and semantic grounding, but autoregressive generation at every step accumulates large decision-stage latency over multi-step navigation. We propose VerNav, a verifier-first framework for low-latency LLM-based VLN. The verifier reduces decision-stage latency by replacing per-step autoregressive generation with batched action verification, while an entropy-based adaptive generator is invoked only for uncertain decisions to produce compact state evidence. To further improve navigation performance with the verifier, we introduce a two-stage alignment scheme: (i) VPO improves local action-preference alignment in static verifier training, and (ii) step-level reinforcement fine-tuning provides dense progress rewards over multi-step navigation rollouts during dynamic task execution. Experiments on the Room-to-Room (R2R) benchmark show that the verifier-only decision path of VerNav achieves competitive navigation performance among representative LLM-based VLN agents while reducing average decision-stage LLM latency per step by more than $10\times$ compared with autoregressive methods.

</details>


### [147] [ProxPI: Proximal Prior Injection for Sampling-Based MPC under Learned-Prior Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00941)
*Euncheol Im,Myotaeg Lim,Yisoo Lee*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes Proximal Prior Injection (ProxPI), a method for combining learned policies with model predictive path integral control that keeps sampling centered on the nominal (task-aware) distribution while incorporating the policy via a soft proximity cost, enabling escape from stale policy priors without sacrificing in-distribution performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing policy-guided MPPI approaches center the sampling distribution on the policy output (policy-centered warm-start). When the learned policy is out of distribution or mismatched with the current task, this restricts exploration around an unsuitable solution and prevents recovery toward the task optimum, degrading performance.

Method: ProxPI retains nominal-centered MPPI sampling (i.e., sampling around the task-optimal trajectory) and incorporates the policy prior through a soft proximity cost in the optimization objective. This avoids re-centering the sampling distribution on the policy output while still leveraging the policy as a prior.

Result: ProxPI matches the in-distribution performance of existing prior-injection schemes while enabling the optimizer to escape from an inaccurate policy and recover vanilla MPPI-level performance in out-of-distribution settings. Theoretical analysis shows that re-centering on the prior discards the optimizer's correction at every update, whereas nominal-centered sampling retains it and converges; larger rollout budgets do not fix the failure. Simulations and real-robot experiments demonstrate robust performance under both conditions.

Conclusion: Proximal Prior Injection provides a principled integration of learned policies into MPPI that is robust to policy distribution shift, preserving the benefits of learned priors while maintaining the optimizer's ability to adapt and recover to task-optimal solutions.

Abstract: Combining learned policies with model predictive control can leverage learned task priors while retaining online adaptation to new objectives and constraints, but performance degrades when the policy is out of distribution. In policy-guided model predictive path integral (MPPI) control, a policy-centered warm-start approach centers the sampling distribution on the policy output. When the prior is mismatched, centering the sampling distribution on the policy output restricts exploration around an unsuitable solution and prevents recovery toward the task optimum. We propose Proximal Prior Injection (ProxPI), which retains nominal-centered MPPI sampling and incorporates the policy through a soft proximity cost. This matches the in-distribution performance of existing prior-injection schemes while enabling the optimizer to escape an inaccurate policy and recover vanilla MPPI-level performance. We theoretically show that re-centering on the prior discards the optimizer's correction at every update, whereas nominal-centered sampling retains it and converges to a solution set by both the task cost and the prior, and that this failure is not removed by a larger rollout budget. Simulations and real-robot experiments demonstrate robust performance under both in-distribution and out-of-distribution tasks.

</details>


### [148] [HitMem: Hierarchical Temporal 3D Memory with Multi-Modal Context-Aware Retrieval for Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.00950)
*Ruijie Tang,Chenye Zou,Guoquan Wu,Jun Wei,Wei Chen,Jiaxin Zhu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: HitMem is a hierarchical temporal 3D memory framework with multi-modal context-aware retrieval for embodied agents, designed to handle dynamic environments where objects move due to human activity. It uses a lightweight topological graph with support relationships, temporal decay for memory regulation, and a specialized two-stage retrieval process triggered by detected object displacement, combining trajectory-based spatial constraints with semantic class affinities. Evaluated on the Dyna-THOR benchmark, it improves object relocation accuracy and reduces exploration costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing 3D memory frameworks assume static worlds, failing in dynamic environments where objects are displaced. This causes memory-observation conflicts requiring costly geometric recomputations or inefficient global re-exploration.

Method: HitMem comprises: (1) a lightweight topological graph unifying semantic and spatial information with support relationships from continuous perception; (2) a temporal decay mechanism regulating memory activeness to mitigate stale representations; (3) a multi-modal context-aware retrieval mechanism that filters candidates using semantic, spatial, and temporal features by default, and activates a two-stage retrieval process upon detecting displacement—combining external agent-trajectory spatial constraints with semantic common-sense from class affinities.

Result: On the constructed Dyna-THOR benchmark, HitMem significantly improves object relocation accuracy, reduces exploration costs, and enhances overall task execution performance in dynamic environments.

Conclusion: Hierarchical temporal memory with decay-aware regulation and context-aware displacement-triggered retrieval is an effective paradigm for embodied agents operating in dynamic scenes, overcoming static-assumption limitations of prior 3D memory systems.

Abstract: Executing long-term tasks in dynamic environments requires embodied agents to maintain robust and adaptive 3D scene representations. However, most existing 3D memory frameworks rely on static world assumptions. When objects are displaced by human activities or unobserved events, agents encounter memory-observation conflicts and often require costly geometric recomputations or inefficient global re-exploration. To address this, we propose HitMem, a hierarchical temporal 3D memory framework with a multi-modal context-aware retrieval mechanism. Through continuous perception, HitMem unifies semantic and spatial information into a lightweight topological graph that captures support relationships, while a temporal decay mechanism dynamically regulates memory activeness to mitigate the impact of stale representations. In addition, the multi-modal context-aware retrieval mechanism defaults to filtering candidates using integrated semantic, spatial, and temporal memory features, and activates a specialized two-stage retrieval process when object displacement is detected. This process combines spatial constraints inferred from external agent trajectories with semantic common sense grounded in class affinities, efficiently identifying high-probability candidate regions. Extensive evaluations on our constructed Dyna-THOR benchmark demonstrate that HitMem significantly improves object relocation accuracy, reduces exploration costs, and enhances task execution performance in dynamic environments.

</details>


### [149] [Accelerating Reinforcement Learning via MPC Solver-Gradient Guidance for Weights-varying MPC](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01061)
*Baha Zarrouki,Arslan Thobani,Jasper Hoffmann,Mattia Piccinini,Rudolf Reiter,Felix Jahncke,Sébastien Gros,Davide Scaramuzza,Johannes Betz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SG-RL is a solver-sensitivity augmentation for RL-based online MPC cost-weight adaptation that combines sample-based closed-loop return objectives with bounded solver-derived gradients to improve sample efficiency and stability in learning adaptive cost weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed MPC cost-function weights become suboptimal under changing conditions, motivating context-dependent online adaptation. Learning such policies is challenging due to the bias-variance tradeoff between sample-efficient-but-biased GB-PL (which uses low-variance solver gradients on predicted trajectories) and unbiased-but-sample-inefficient RL (which optimizes realized return from environment samples).

Method: SG-RL keeps sampled closed-loop return as the objective (retaining RL's unbiasedness) while injecting bounded solver-derived gradients as auxiliary guidance to improve stability and sample efficiency. It is instantiated in PPO with four modular algorithms that integrate solver-gradient guidance into actor-update scaling, policy loss, advantage estimation, and value-function learning.

Result: On two full-scale autonomous racing platforms with intentional model mismatch, SG-RL matches PPO's best closed-loop return with up to 70.6% fewer samples, outperforms GB-PL baselines by at least 54% in closed-loop return, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen environments.

Conclusion: SG-RL effectively bridges the bias-variance gap between RL and GB-PL for MPC cost-weight adaptation, achieving RL-level performance with substantially improved sample efficiency and better robustness than purely gradient-based approaches, including zero-shot generalization to novel scenarios.

Abstract: In Model Predictive Control (MPC), cost-function weights shape closed-loop behavior, yet changing conditions often make fixed parametrizations suboptimal and motivate context-dependent online adaptation. Learning such policies is difficult because behavior depends implicitly on numerical MPC solutions, producing nonlinear, potentially nonsmooth, long-horizon dependencies on policy parameters. This creates a bias-variance tradeoff: Reinforcement Learning (RL) optimizes realized closed-loop return from environment samples but is sample-inefficient, whereas Gradient-Based Policy Learning (GB-PL) uses low-variance solver gradients from differentiable MPC to optimize surrogate losses on predicted trajectories but can be biased under model mismatch. We propose Solver-Gradient Guided Reinforcement Learning (SG-RL), a solver-sensitivity augmentation for RL-based online MPC cost-weight adaptation. SG-RL keeps sampled closed-loop return as the objective and uses bounded solver-derived gradients as auxiliary guidance to improve stability and sample efficiency. We instantiate SG-RL in Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with four modular algorithms that inject solver-gradient guidance into actor-update scaling, policy loss, advantage estimation, and value-function learning. On two full-scale autonomous racing platforms with intentional model mismatch, SG-RL reaches PPO's best closed-loop return with up to 70.6% fewer samples, outperforms GB-PL baselines by at least 54% in closed-loop return, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen environments.

</details>


### [150] [Adaptive Depth-Map-Guided Bundle Adjustment for Correspondence-Free Multi-View Point Cloud Registration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01089)
*Yiran Zhou,Yingyu Wang,Shoudong Huang,Liang Zhao*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents an adaptive layered depth-map-guided bundle adjustment (ADM-BA) framework for correspondence-free multi-view point cloud registration of irregular steel scrap in robotic cutting workcells, using a global 2.5-D grid with adaptive multiple depth hypotheses and softmax-based layer assignment to jointly refine sensor poses and layered depth map.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense 3-D measurement is needed for robotic processing of irregular steel scrap to replace manual visual assessment in hazardous cutting workcells. Existing multi-view registration methods relying on feature extraction and data association often establish wrong correspondences on smooth metallic surfaces, repeated structures, occlusions, and partial overlaps, leading to inaccurate pose estimation and distorted reconstruction that propagate to downstream measurement and planning tasks.

Method: Proposes a correspondence-free framework by representing the scene as a global 2.5-D grid where each cell maintains adaptively multiple depth hypotheses. Raw depth observations are directly projected into the global map forming depth constraints without explicit feature correspondences. A softmax-based layer assignment links each observation to compatible depth hypotheses at conflicting grid cells. A nonlinear least-squares formulation jointly refines sensor poses and the layered depth map, with correspondences implicitly induced by the depth-map representation and projection model.

Result: Experiments on self-collected industrial datasets demonstrate the method achieves consistently competitive reconstruction accuracy while maintaining robustness and low computational cost in challenging industrial scenarios. Open-source code is released.

Conclusion: The adaptive layered depth-map-guided bundle adjustment framework provides a reliable, computationally efficient, correspondence-free alternative for multi-view point cloud registration in challenging metallic and reflectively difficult industrial environments, with publicly available implementation for further use and extension.

Abstract: Robotic processing of irregular steel scrap requires dense 3-D measurement to replace manual visual assessment in hazardous cutting workcells. The reconstructed map is used to estimate piece dimensions, boundary geometry, feasible preheating and cutting regions, and collision-aware torch paths. The reconstruction errors therefore propagate directly to downstream measurement and planning. Existing multi-view registration methods commonly rely on feature extraction and data association to establish correspondences between views. In workcells with smooth metallic surfaces, repeated structures, occlusions, and partial overlaps, however, wrong correspondences may be established, leading to inaccurate pose estimation and distorted reconstruction. This paper presents an adaptive layered depth-map-guided bundle adjustment framework for correspondence-free multi-view point cloud registration. The scene is represented by a global 2.5-D grid, where each cell can adaptively maintain multiple depth hypotheses. Raw depth observations are directly projected into the global map to form depth constraints without explicit feature correspondences. At grid cells where multiple surfaces produce conflicting depths, a softmax-based layer assignment links each observation to compatible depth hypotheses. The resulting nonlinear least-squares formulation jointly refines sensor poses and the layered depth map, with correspondences implicitly induced by the depth-map representation and projection model. Experiments on self-collected industrial datasets show that the proposed method achieves consistently competitive reconstruction accuracy while maintaining robustness and low computational cost in challenging industrial scenarios. We release the open-source code implementation at: https://github.com/YiranZhou-Robotics/ADM-BA.git

</details>


### [151] [DNC-IMM: Early Lane-Change Intention Recognition via Neural Calibration Based on Driving Context Information](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01120)
*Woong-Chan Byun,Seung-Hyun Kong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper proposes a Dual Neural-Calibrated Interacting Multiple Model (DNC-IMM) for early lane-change intention recognition in autonomous driving. It uses a neural network to calibrate both the transition-probability matrix and measurement likelihoods of a conventional IMM, preserving probabilistic interpretability while improving adaptability. Intentions are inferred from the calibrated IMM mode posterior. Experiments on the highD dataset show strong performance, especially at 2-3 s prediction horizons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Early recognition of lane-change intention is critical for proactive decision-making in autonomous driving and ADAS, but existing methods lack adaptability to driving context while maintaining interpretable probabilistic structure.

Method: The DNC-IMM encodes driving-context features (target-vehicle motion, gaps to surrounding vehicles, relative velocities) via a neural network that calibrates both the transition-probability matrix and measurement likelihoods. Intention is determined from the calibrated IMM mode posterior instead of a separate classifier.

Result: On the highD dataset, the method reliably recognizes lane-change intentions before lane crossing, with notably strong performance at the earlier 2-3 s prediction horizons.

Conclusion: DNC-IMM enhances conventional IMM adaptability through neural calibration of model parameters while preserving its probabilistic structure and interpretability, achieving effective and early lane-change intention recognition.

Abstract: Early recognition of lane-change intention is essential for proactive decision-making in autonomous driving and advanced driver assistance systems. This paper proposes a Dual Neural-Calibrated Interacting Multiple Model (DNC-IMM) that improves adaptability to driving context while preserving the probabilistic structure and interpretability of a conventional IMM. The proposed method encodes driving-context information, including target-vehicle motion, gaps to surrounding vehicles, and relative velocities, with a neural network that calibrates both the transition-probability matrix and measurement likelihoods. The final intention is determined from the calibrated IMM mode posterior rather than from a separate direct classifier. Experiments on the highD dataset demonstrate that the proposed method reliably recognizes lane-change intentions before lane crossing and provides particularly strong performance at the earlier 2-3 s prediction horizons.

</details>


### [152] [On Global Regulatability of Robot Manipulators by Classical PID](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01207)
*Cheng Zhao,Jingru Zhu,Lei Guo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a channel-wise extended PID (EPID) controller for uncertain MIMO nonlinear systems with heterogeneous vector relative degrees across channels, proving that independently designed EPID parameters guarantee semiglobal stability and setpoint regulation, thereby justifying the common channel-by-channel tuning practice in engineering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing PID-type control literature for MIMO nonlinear systems has largely overlooked cases where the relative degree components vary across channels. The authors aim to fill this gap by extending PID control theory to systems with non-uniform vector relative degrees, and to provide theoretical justification for the widely used but previously unsubstantiated engineering practice of channel-by-channel PID tuning.

Method: The authors design a channel-wise EPID controller where each input uses proportional, integral, and derivative terms of the corresponding tracking error, with the derivative order selected per-channel based on its relative degree. Under growth conditions on the unknown nonlinearities, they construct an admissible set of EPID parameters derived from prescribed initial-state bounds, uncertainties, and reference signals.

Result: It is proven that any parameter choice from the admissible set guarantees semiglobal stability of the closed-loop system and achieves the desired setpoint regulation for uncertain MIMO nonlinear systems with channel-varying relative degrees.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate that EPID parameters can be designed independently per channel in strongly coupled, uncertain MIMO systems, providing rigorous theoretical backing for channel-by-channel tuning in practice, including in flight control systems.

Abstract: This paper studies a class of uncertain multi-input multi-output (MIMO) nonlinear systems using extended PID (EPID) control. We focus on systems possessing a well-defined vector relative degree whose components may vary across channels, a setting that received limited attention in the existing literature on PID-type control. We develop a channel-wise EPID controller, where each control input is constructed from the proportional, integral, and derivative terms of the corresponding tracking error, and the highest derivative order is selected according to the relative degree of that channel. Under suitable growth conditions on the unknown nonlinearities, we construct an admissible set of EPID parameters specified by prescribed initial-state bounds, the uncertainties and the reference signal. We show that any parameter choice from this set guarantees semiglobal stability of the closed-loop system and achieves the desired setpoint regulation. These results indicate that the EPID parameters can be designed independently for each channel, justifying the common engineering practice of channel-by-channel tuning in strongly coupled and uncertain MIMO systems, including flight control systems.

</details>


### [153] [EmbodiedSkills: A Unified Framework for Orchestrating, Training, and Deploying VLA Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01281)
*Wei Wang,Wenqiao Zhang,Yutong Lin,Yuqian Yuan,Tianwei Lin,Jinhao Mao,Zhenxuan Fan,Mingjian Gao,Yang Dai,Wentong Li,Zheqi Lv,Zheng Dong,Yingjie Niu,Jiaqi Zhu,Jun Xiao,Chao Li,Yueting Zhuang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EmbodiedSkills is a unified framework that treats each skill decision as an execution proposal with runtime prerequisite checking, bounded low-level VLA execution, and post-action verification, connected through a shared executable-skill interface. It records structured trajectories for supervision and optional online adaptation. Instantiations with Qwen3-VL and OpenPI/pi0.5 achieve 86.20% average success across 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks and 97.40% across four LIBERO suites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLA models can map observations and instructions to actions, but long-horizon tasks require more than action prediction—agents must coordinate perception, planning, execution, progress verification, and recovery. A model-generated action or skill decision does not guarantee the operation is valid in the current state or that its outcome will be verified.

Method: EmbodiedSkills proposes a unified framework that treats each skill decision as an execution proposal. The runtime checks prerequisites before execution and verifies outcomes afterward. A fixed executable-skill interface connects high-level skill selection, bounded low-level VLA execution, and post-action verification in a single agent loop, allowing low-level VLA policies to be replaced without changing the loop. The interface records planning, execution, verification, and recovery events as structured trajectories, which provide supervision and support optional online adaptation. It is instantiated with Qwen3-VL and OpenPI/pi0.5 on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO benchmarks.

Result: Task-adapted low-level VLA policies achieve 86.20% average success across 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks and 97.40% across the four LIBERO suites. On four memory-dependent RMBench tasks, the same approach achieves 12.5% average success.

Conclusion: EmbodiedSkills provides a trainable and inspectable agent layer that turns standard VLA policies into closed-loop embodied systems with structured verification and recovery capabilities. The results establish strong execution performance of the task-adapted low-level VLA policies while highlighting challenges remain for memory-dependent tasks.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models map visual observations and language instructions directly to robot actions, but long-horizon tasks require more than action prediction. An agent must coordinate perception, planning, execution, progress verification, and recovery as the physical state evolves. An action prediction or a model-generated skill decision does not, by itself, guarantee that the proposed operation is valid in the current state or that its outcome will be verified. We propose EmbodiedSkills, a unified framework that treats each skill decision as an execution proposal: the runtime checks its prerequisites before execution and verifies the outcome afterward. A shared executable-skill interface connects high-level skill selection, bounded low-level VLA execution, and post-action verification within a single agent loop. Because this interface remains fixed, low-level VLA policies can be replaced or adapted without changing the agent loop. The interface also records planning, execution, verification, and recovery events as structured trajectories, which provide supervision for individual components and can support optional online adaptation when interactive feedback is available. We instantiate EmbodiedSkills with Qwen3-VL and OpenPI/pi0.5 on RoboTwin 2.0 and LIBERO. Task-adapted low-level VLA policies achieve an average success rate of 86.20% across 50 RoboTwin 2.0 tasks and 97.40% across the four LIBERO suites. These results establish the execution performance of the task-adapted low-level VLA policies used in EmbodiedSkills. On four memory-dependent RMBench tasks, the same task-adapted execution approach achieves 12.5% average success. The framework provides a trainable and inspectable agent layer for turning these policies into closed-loop embodied systems.

</details>


### [154] [Integrating Traffic Noise Emission Modelling into Variable Speed Limit Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01339)
*Jiawen Meng,John Pravin Arockiasamy,Alexey Vinel*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper proposes a noise-aware variable speed limit (VSL) framework that integrates traffic-state estimation with a CNOSSOS-EU-based emission indicator to dynamically adjust speed limits. Evaluated via microscopic simulation over 24 hours, it achieves a 2.9 dB(A) receiver-based noise reduction relative to unrestricted traffic while maintaining average speeds 11.3 km/h higher than a fixed low-speed regime, demonstrating the feasibility of environmental objectives in operational speed control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static speed management strategies fail to respond to short-term variations in traffic noise emissions.
