Although VSL systems are widely deployed for safety and congestion management, traffic noise is rarely treated as an explicit operational control objective, leaving a gap for noise-aware adaptive speed control.

Method: The framework integrates aggregated traffic-state estimation with a simplified CNOSSOS-EU-based emission indicator. A stage-based controller with time-varying reference thresholds dynamically adjusts discrete speed-limit levels in response to estimated emission conditions. Evaluation uses microscopic traffic simulation calibrated with empirical motorway data, replicated across multiple stochastic realisations and analysed over a 24-hour period.

Result: The adaptive strategy reduces receiver-based equivalent sound level by 2.9 dB(A) versus unrestricted traffic while maintaining average vehicle speeds approximately 11.3 km/h higher than a permanently imposed low-speed regime. Speed reductions are selectively activated near calibrated emission targets rather than enforcing a constant intermediate limit. Traffic stability shows moderate increases in speed variability versus unrestricted operation but substantially lower braking intensity than uniform low-speed enforcement.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate the feasibility of integrating environmental performance indicators into operational speed control, offering a practical complement to conventional infrastructure-based noise mitigation measures.

Abstract: Road traffic noise remains a major environmental challenge, yet most speed management strategies are static and do not respond to short-term variations in traffic noise emissions. Although variable speed limit (VSL) systems are widely deployed for safety and congestion mitigation, traffic noise is rarely treated as an explicit operational control objective.
  This paper proposes a noise-aware VSL framework that integrates aggregated traffic-state estimation with a simplified CNOSSOS-EU-based emission indicator. A stage-based controller with time-varying reference thresholds dynamically adjusts discrete speed-limit levels in response to estimated emission conditions. The framework is evaluated using microscopic traffic simulation calibrated with empirical motorway data and replicated across multiple stochastic realisations.
  Over a 24-hour evaluation period, the adaptive strategy reduces the receiver-based equivalent sound level by 2.9 dB(A) relative to unrestricted traffic conditions, while maintaining an average vehicle speed approximately 11.3 km/h higher than a permanently imposed low-speed regime. Period-wise analysis shows that speed reductions are activated selectively when emission levels approach calibrated targets, rather than enforcing a constant intermediate limit. Traffic stability indicators reveal moderate increases in speed variability compared with unrestricted operation, but substantially lower braking intensity than under uniform low-speed enforcement.
  These results demonstrate the feasibility of integrating environmental performance indicators into operational speed control, providing a practical complement to conventional infrastructure-based noise mitigation measures.

</details>


### [155] [Scalable Rao-Blackwellized Online Planning for High-Dimensional POMDPs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01351)
*Jiho Lee,Nisar Ahmed,Kyle Hollins Wray,Zachary Sunberg*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper extends the Rao-Blackwellized online POMDP (RB-POMDP) framework to improve scalability in high-dimensional partially observable robotic domains by using hybrid continuous-discrete belief representations. It analytically propagates uncertainty for marginalized state components during tree-based planning to reduce Monte Carlo variance. Validated on a search-and-rescue task integrated with FastSLAM 2.0, the approach achieves higher cumulative rewards with fewer particles and planning simulations under equal computational budgets compared to purely sampling-based POMDP solvers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sampling-based POMDP solvers degrade in performance as belief dimensionality increases due to high variance inherent in Monte Carlo-based value estimation, posing a fundamental challenge for online planning in high-dimensional partially observable robotic domains.

Method: The approach extends the Rao-Blackwellized online POMDP (RB-POMDP) framework by employing hybrid continuous-discrete belief representations. Uncertainty associated with marginalized state components is analytically propagated during tree-based planning to reduce sampling-induced variance in value estimation. The framework is integrated with FastSLAM 2.0 for a robotic search-and-rescue scenario.

Result: Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed RB-POMDP planner achieves higher cumulative rewards using significantly fewer particles and planning simulations than purely sampling-based methods, under equivalent computational budgets.

Conclusion: Structured high-dimensional robotic problems that admit tractable sufficient statistics can be effectively leveraged within the RB-POMDP framework to enable computationally feasible online decision-making under uncertainty.

Abstract: Online planning under uncertainty remains a fundamental challenge for robotic systems operating in partially observable environments with high-dimensional state spaces. While sampling-based POMDP solvers enable approximate decision-making in large or continuous domains, their performance degrades as belief dimensionality increases due to the high variance inherent in Monte Carlo-based estimation. In this work, we extend the Rao-Blackwellized online POMDP (RB-POMDP) framework to improve its generalizability in high-dimensional settings through hybrid continuous-discrete belief representations. By analytically propagating uncertainty associated with marginalized state components during tree-based planning, the proposed approach reduces sampling-induced variance in value estimation. We demonstrate the effectiveness of this framework in a robotic search-and-rescue task by integrating it with FastSLAM 2.0. Experimental results show that the proposed planner achieves higher cumulative rewards using significantly fewer particles and planning simulations than purely sampling-based methods under equivalent computational budgets. These results suggest that structured high-dimensional robotic problems admitting tractable sufficient statistics can be effectively leveraged within the RB-POMDP framework for computationally feasible online decision-making.

</details>


### [156] [Obstacle-Aware Autonomous Coverage and Navigation for Outdoor Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01384)
*Leonardo Gargani,Matteo Frosi,Matteo Matteucci*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: The paper presents a unified ROS 2 architecture for long-duration outdoor coverage with autonomous platforms, integrating coverage planning, dual-antenna RTK-GNSS/EKF-based robust localization, and Nav2-based execution. Three controller-aware refinements improve a mature coverage planner, and a Behavior-Tree mission manager handles multi-goal execution, recovery, energy-aware goal management, and autonomous docking. Validation through simulation and real-world trials across varied outdoor areas achieves 93.1%–96.1% coverage of planned areas.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-duration outdoor coverage with autonomous platforms faces unsolved challenges beyond classical planning, including localization drift in open spaces, obstacles in cluttered sites, controller feasibility in turn-heavy maneuvers, and persistent autonomy with energy management.

Method: The authors propose a unified ROS 2 architecture combining: (1) coverage planning with three controller-aware refinements added to a mature planner; (2) robust localization using dual-antenna RTK-GNSS fused in an EKF for accurate position and heading; (3) Nav2-based execution; and (4) a Behavior-Tree mission manager coordinating multi-goal execution, layered recovery, cost-aware goal management, and autonomous docking for return-to-charge.

Result: The stack was validated through simulation and real-world trials across multiple outdoor areas with varying geometries and obstacle densities, reliably completing missions while sweeping 93.1% to 96.1% of the planned coverage area.

Conclusion: The proposed integrated stack reliably completes outdoor coverage missions in varied environments, achieving high coverage percentages (93.1%–96.1%), demonstrating its effectiveness for persistent outdoor autonomy.

Abstract: Long-duration outdoor coverage with autonomous platforms remains challenging beyond classical planning: deployments face localization drift in open spaces, obstacles in cluttered sites, controller feasibility in turn-heavy maneuvers, and persistent autonomy with energy management. We propose a unified ROS 2 architecture for outdoor coverage that combines coverage planning, robust localization, and Nav2-based execution. A dual-antenna RTK-GNSS fused in an EKF keeps the robot pose, both position and heading, accurate across long missions; three controller-aware refinements are added to a mature coverage planner; a Behavior-Tree mission manager coordinates multi-goal execution, layered recovery, cost-aware goal management, and autonomous docking for return-to-charge. We validate the stack through simulation and real-world trials across multiple outdoor areas with varying geometries and obstacle densities. Overall, these results show that the proposed stack can reliably complete outdoor coverage missions across varied areas, sweeping 93.1% to 96.1% of the planned coverage area.

</details>


### [157] [Autonomous robotic bridging using distributed swarm control without inter-agent communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01394)
*Vishwaak C. Thamaraiselvan,Cody L. Lundberg,Michail Theofandis,Suhas Chelian,Nicholas R. Gans*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SCARAB is a swarm-capable autonomous robotic aquatic bridging system using distributed swarm control and multi-model sensing, enabling agents to localize themselves, form formations, and navigate to targets without GPS or RF communications, with potential military and civilian applications.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The current Improved Ribbon Bridge used by the Army has limitations in predictability, crew survivability, and logistics footprint. There is a need for autonomous aquatic bridging that can perform dispersed, unpredictable river crossings while minimizing crew risk and logistical overhead.

Method: The approach employs distributed swarm control and multi-model sensing of agents and docking targets. Agents localize themselves and join formations without relying on GPS or RF communications (though these can be used in non-contested environments). The system is validated through physics-engine-based simulations of multiple unmanned surface vehicles (USVs) operating under currents and wind conditions.

Result: The system was demonstrated via physics-engine-based simulation of several USV agents in the presence of environmental disturbances including currents and wind, showing feasibility of the swarm-based bridging approach in contested and non-contested scenarios.

Conclusion: SCARAB offers a viable autonomous alternative for aquatic bridging operations, promising enhanced crew survivability and reduced logistics footprint, with applicability to both military river crossings and civilian disaster relief such as flooding response.

Abstract: We describe SCARAB--Swarm-Capable Autonomous Robotic Aquatic Bridging. Using distributed swarm control and multi-model sensing of agents and docking targets, agents can localize themselves, join into formations and proceed to desired target locations. Our technologies would eventually allow the Army to perform unpredictable, dispersed river crossings, enhance crew survivability, and minimize the logistics footprint compared to the current Improved Ribbon Bridge. Our methods operate without GPS or RF communications, though these can be used in non-contested environments (e.g., civilian disaster relief for flooding, etc.). We demonstrate our system via physics-engine-based simulation of several agents using unmanned surface vehicles (USVs) in the presence of currents and wind.

</details>


### [158] [Evaluating Multimodal LLMs as Generalist Vision-Language-Action Agents for Drone Control: Commanding, Approaching, Tracking and Searching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01404)
*Jaewoo Park,Minyoung Lee,Sukmin Seo,Moonbin Yim,Hyunwook Yoon,Dohoon Ryu,Daehee Kim,Myungseo Song,Jihyuk Byun,Seunggyu Chang,Taeho Kil,Jiseob Kim,Bado Lee,Geewook Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper introduces DroneCATS-Agent, an architecture where a Multimodal Large Language Model (MLLM) serves as a swappable component in a drone's control loop, and DroneCATS, a benchmark treating the model as the independent variable. The agent handles yaw/search behaviors, deliberation under uncertainty, and self-declared arrival without fine-tuning or function-calling schemas. Evaluating models from 2B to frontier scale across four embodied capabilities reveals that spatial perception is not the bottleneck—instead, the critical gap lies in action protocol discipline: small models navigate reliably but fail to declare arrival correctly, while multi-drone commanding amplifies this divide through blind coordinate copying.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent systems that drop MLLMs into drone control increasingly narrow the model's decision-making, limiting its autonomy. The authors want to widen the model's role back—entrusting it with the full action space (yawing, searching, deliberating, self-declaring arrival) declared solely via prompt—to systematically measure how far MLLMs extend from perception into acting, especially at the edge of small onboard models.

Method: The authors build DroneCATS-Agent, an architecture where the MLLM is a swappable component, and DroneCATS, a benchmark treating the model as the independent variable. Evaluation covers four core capabilities: approaching a visible target, tracking a moving one, searching outside the initial field of view, and commanding a multi-drone fleet. The roster spans models down to 2B parameters in addition to frontier models, all without fine-tuning or function-calling schemas.

Result: Even the simplest embodied settings remain far from solved. A stark paradox emerges: small open models often navigate into the success radius more reliably than frontier models but lose episodes by declaring arrival prematurely or not at all. Multi-drone commanding amplifies this divide—small models fail by blindly copying a single coordinate across distinct views. Spatial perception holds up across models, but action protocol discipline (sustaining a declared protocol and emitting the correct terminating action) does not.

Conclusion: What separates a deployable edge model from a frontier model is not navigation capability but protocol discipline—the ability to plan persistently and emit the correct terminating action. The open problem is closing this gap at onboard compute costs, and DroneCATS is designed to measure precisely that distance between current models and deployability.

Abstract: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) are strong perceivers of images and video. We ask how far that reach extends into acting: dropping an MLLM directly into a drone's control loop, with its entire action space declared solely in the prompt. Recent systems approach this setting but increasingly narrow the model's decision-making. We widen it back. We introduce DroneCATS-Agent, an architecture where the MLLM is a swappable component, and DroneCATS, a benchmark treating the model as the independent variable. Beyond merely flying toward a pixel, our agent entrusts the model to yaw and search, deliberate when unsure, and self-declare arrival---all without fine-tuning or function-calling schemas. Evaluating frontier and open models across four core capabilities---approaching a visible target, tracking a moving one, searching outside the initial view, and commanding a multi-drone fleet---reveals that even the simplest embodied settings are far from solved. Crucially, to identify what breaks first at the edge, our roster scales down to 2B parameters. The findings expose a stark paradox: it is not the flying that fails. Small open models often navigate into the success radius more reliably than frontier models, yet lose the episode by declaring arrival prematurely or not at all. Multi-drone commanding amplifies this divide, with small models failing by blindly copying a single coordinate across distinct views. Viewed as vision-language-action agents, the models' spatial perception holds up, but their action protocol does not. What separates a deployable edge model from a frontier model is not navigation, but the discipline to sustain a declared protocol and emit the correct terminating action. The open problem is closing this gap at onboard compute costs---yielding a fast model that plans persistently and knows exactly when it is done---and DroneCATS is built to measure that distance.

</details>


### [159] [Vision-Based Leader-Follower Formation Control for Cooperative UAVs in GPS-Degraded Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01420)
*Deekshitha Angadi,Naveena Budda,Vikas Agarwal,Rojesh Arunkumar Mulasa,Ravi Killamsetty,Mohamed Samshad,Narsimlu Kemsaram*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a vision-based cooperative formation framework for a follower UAV that uses a front-facing RGB-D camera with a lightweight YOLO detector and pinhole camera model to estimate the leader's relative pose, enabling formation keeping and serving as a GPS backup when absolute positioning fails.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multi-UAV cooperation requires reliable relative perception for formation keeping, especially when absolute positioning sensors (e.g., GPS) degrade or fail, so a vision-based alternative for leader-follower localization is needed.

Method: A lightweight YOLO-based detector trained on a dedicated drone dataset runs onboard to predict leader bounding boxes. These detections are fused with depth from an RGB-D camera via a pinhole camera model to estimate the leader's relative pose, which feeds a leader-follower position controller. The framework is implemented as ROS nodes and evaluated in the XTDrone physics-based simulation with sensor noise and communication dropouts.

Result: The framework maintains stable leader-follower formations with reasonable computational cost under nominal conditions and simulated positioning sensor failures, demonstrating acceptable detection accuracy and formation-keeping error.

Conclusion: The proposed vision-based framework provides a practical basis for extending cooperative formation control to real-world multi-UAV systems, serving as a viable backup when GPS or external localization is unavailable.

Abstract: Cooperation in multi-UAV systems requires reliable relative perception so that follower vehicles can maintain formation and continue their mission safely even when absolute positioning sensors degrade or fail. This paper presents a vision-based cooperative formation framework running on a follower UAV that uses a front-facing RGB-D camera to detect, track, and localize a leader UAV in real-time. A lightweight YOLO-based detector is trained on a dedicated drone dataset and deployed onboard to predict leader bounding boxes, which are then fused with depth information via a pinhole camera model to estimate the leader's relative pose. These estimates provide a leader-follower position controller and can also be used as a backup when GPS or external localization is unavailable. This framework is implemented as a set of ROS nodes and evaluated in a physics-based multi-UAV simulation built on XTDrone, with sensor noise and communication dropouts. We evaluate detection accuracy, runtime, and formation-keeping error under nominal conditions and under simulated failures of the positioning sensors. The results show that the proposed framework maintains stable leader-follower formations with reasonable computational cost and provides a practical basis for extending vision-based cooperative formation control to real-world multi-UAV systems.

</details>


### [160] [Does Imitation Learning Preserve Temporal Robustness in Dexterous Manipulation? An Expert-Learner Comparison Across Task Execution Speeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01453)
*Clinton Enwerem,John S. Baras,Calin Belta*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper investigates temporal robustness (performance across execution speeds) of imitation-learned dexterous manipulation policies compared to their expert demonstrator. Using ParcelStow, a contact-rich task, the authors find that while both a scripted expert and an ACT policy achieve 100% success at nominal speed, their performance diverges dramatically at higher speeds — expert drops to 84% while ACT drops to 53%, with most ACT failures stemming from insertion misalignments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imitation-learned manipulation policies are typically evaluated for robustness to scene, object, or instruction variation, but rarely for robustness across task execution speeds. This leaves an open question about how much temporal robustness a learner retains relative to the expert it imitates, which is critical for real-world deployment where operation speed matters.

Method: The authors instantiate their evaluation in ParcelStow, a contact-rich task requiring the robot to acquire, reorient, and insert a parcel. They compare a scripted expert and an Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) policy under identical task conditions, initial-condition draws, and speedup factors. Demonstrations span the speedup range for manipulation phases after parcel acquisition. They analyze stage-level failures, relative-motion handoffs, and the effect of force closure during acquisition.

Result: Both expert and ACT achieve 100% task success at nominal speed, but success rates diverge at maximum demonstrated speed: expert achieves 84% and ACT only 53%. Two ACT policies with different initializations degrade by 34 and 48 percentage points from nominal to maximum speed, versus 16 points for the expert. Stage-level analysis shows 35 of 47 ACT failures at maximum speed are insertion misalignments. Under relative-motion handoff, all ACT acquisitions preserve the parcel through reorientation and free-space transfer, but only 64% complete the task versus 95% after expert acquisition. None of the 414 acquisitions without force closure succeeded across all policies and speeds.

Conclusion: Equal nominal task success does not imply preservation of expert performance across execution speeds. Imitation-learned policies lose temporal robustness more sharply than their expert demonstrators, with failures concentrating at insertion stages. Force closure during acquisition is a critical prerequisite for downstream task completion. The authors release code, data, and evaluation scripts for reproducibility.

Abstract: Dexterous manipulation policies learned by imitation are typically evaluated for robustness to variation in scenes, objects, or instructions, but their performance across task execution speeds is less often examined. This leaves open how much temporal robustness a learner retains relative to the expert it imitates. We compare an expert and learner under the same task conditions, initial-condition draws, and speedup factors. We instantiate the evaluation in ParcelStow, a contact-rich task in which the robot acquires, reorients, and inserts a parcel. The demonstrations span the speedup range for the manipulation phases after parcel acquisition. A scripted expert and an Action Chunking with Transformers (ACT) policy trained from the expert's demonstrations both achieve 100 percent task success at nominal speed. Their success rates diverge within the demonstrated range: at its maximum, expert success is 84 percent and ACT success is 53 percent. Two ACT policies with different parameter initializations show similar degradation, decreasing by 34 and 48 percentage points from nominal speed to the maximum demonstrated speed, compared with 16 points for the expert. Stage-level analysis shows that 35 of ACT's 47 failures at the maximum demonstrated speed are insertion misalignments. Under the relative-motion handoff, every ACT acquisition retains the parcel through reorientation and transfer in free space, but only 64 percent complete the overall task, compared with 95 percent after expert acquisition. Across all evaluated policies and speeds, none of the 414 acquisitions without force closure completes the task. Equal nominal task success therefore does not imply preservation of expert performance across execution speeds. Code, data, and evaluation scripts are available at https://github.com/coenwerem/parcelstow.

</details>


### [161] [A System for Fast, Resilient, and Adaptable Loco-Manipulation Behaviors on Humanoid Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01518)
*Duncan Calvert,Luigi Penco,Dexton Anderson,Tomasz Bialek,Arghya Chatterjee,Beomyeong Park,Robert Griffin*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: This paper presents a robot-local, runtime-editable behavior authoring and runtime system for humanoid robots that coordinates locomotion, whole-body motion, perception, contact, and operator supervision through an architecture combining object-centric Affordance Templates, tree-structured organization, a behavior scene, and primitive scene actions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Humanoid robots are valuable for physically demanding, hazardous, and repetitive work in human-built environments, but require coordination of multiple complex systems including locomotion, whole-body motion, perception, contact, and operator supervision. The authors argue that behavior architecture itself can be a primary enabler of capability, speed, and reliability, and that runtime editability enables fast behavior creation and adaptation.

Method: The system combines object-centric Affordance Templates, a tree structure providing organization and logic, and runtime-editable perception via a behavior scene and primitive scene actions. Action primitives are executed through a whole-body controller supporting concurrent body motions and walking. An operator interface remains continuously synchronized to the robot for runtime authoring, monitoring, and repair.

Result: Demonstrations cover six task variants on Unitree H1-2 and Alex. The system executes a push door traversal in 34 seconds and sorts six balls by color in 45 seconds under human disturbance. Timed authoring sessions show scratch creation of new loco-manipulation behaviors and adaptation of existing ones in hours. Comparison against the literature finds the approach competitive with recent learned systems.

Conclusion: The behavior-authoring and runtime system demonstrates that a well-designed behavior architecture with runtime editability can serve as a primary enabler for humanoid robot capability, enabling fast creation and adaptation of loco-manipulation behaviors while remaining competitive with learned systems.

Abstract: There is tremendous value in humanoid robots taking on physically demanding, hazardous, and repetitive work in spaces built for humans. However, a useful robot for these spaces must coordinate locomotion, whole-body motion, perception, contact, and operator supervision. We present a robot-local, runtime-editable behavior authoring and runtime system that addresses these challenges. We argue that behavior architecture can be a primary enabler of capability, speed, and reliability, and that runtime editability enables fast behavior creation, adaptation, extension, and combination.
  Our behavior architecture combines object-centric Affordance Templates, a tree structure that provides organization and logic, and runtime-editable perception through a behavior scene and primitive scene actions. Our operator interface remains continuously synchronized to the robot for runtime authoring, monitoring, and repair. Action primitives execute through a whole-body controller that supports concurrent body motions and walking.
  Demonstrations of our system cover six task variants on Unitree H1-2 and Alex. We execute a push door traversal in 34 seconds and sort six balls by color in 45 seconds under human disturbance. Timed authoring sessions show scratch creation of new loco-manipulation behaviors and adaptation of existing ones in hours. Comparison against the literature finds our approach to be competitive with recent learned systems.

</details>


### [162] [SG-AMP: Scene-Graph-Guided Active Perception and Semantics-Aware Motion Planning for Pepper Plants](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01579)
*Rohit Menon,Shiva Rudra Lolla,Niklas Mueller-Goldingen,Gokul Chenchani,Ribana Roscher,Maren Bennewitz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SG-AMP integrates depth completion with input-conditioned uncertainty, persistent panoptic mapping, plant scene-graph reasoning, and semantics-aware active view-motion planning for robotic pepper harvesting. It uses a scene graph to hypothesize unobserved pepper-peduncle attachments and directs close-range sensing accordingly, with candidate views selected by expected information gain and class-dependent motion costs. It reports strong perception metrics on pepper data and improved uncertainty estimation on NYUv2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable robust robotic perception and active exploration in agricultural environments, particularly for harvesting peppers, where partially observed structures (e.g., occluded pepper-peduncle attachments) require intelligent hypothesis generation and targeted sensing rather than merely scanning uncertain areas.

Method: The system (SG-AMP) combines four components: (1) robust depth completion with input-conditioned uncertainty, (2) persistent panoptic mapping, (3) plant scene-graph reasoning that explicitly hypothesizes unobserved pepper-peduncle attachments, and (4) semantics-aware active view-motion planning. View selection is driven by expected information gain, and motion costs are class-dependent to distinguish protected plant parts (peppers, peduncles, stems) from conditionally traversable foliage.

Result: On pepper data, the perception network achieves 55.27% semantic mIoU, 38.67% PQ, and 40.62 mm depth RMSE. Input-conditioned uncertainty improves NYUv2 NLL from -1.6518 to -1.6925 and AUSE from 0.0102 to 0.0087.

Conclusion: SG-AMP demonstrates that integrating scene-graph-driven hypothesis reasoning with uncertainty-aware perception and semantics-aware planning enhances robotic inspection in agricultural settings, improving both perception accuracy and uncertainty calibration while enabling targeted exploration of unobserved structurally important regions.

Abstract: We present SG-AMP, integrating robust depth completion with input-conditioned uncertainty, persistent panoptic mapping, plant scene-graph reasoning, and semantics-aware active view-motion planning. Beyond inspecting uncertain observed regions, the scene graph explicitly hypothesizes unobserved pepper--peduncle attachments and directs close-range sensing toward them. Candidate views are selected according to expected information gain, while class-dependent motion costs distinguish protected peppers, peduncles, and stems from conditionally traversable foliage. On pepper data, the perception network achieves $55.27\%$ semantic mIoU, $38.67\%$ PQ, and $40.62\,\mathrm{mm}$ depth RMSE, while input-conditioned uncertainty improves NYUv2 NLL from $-1.6518$ to $-1.6925$ and AUSE from $0.0102$ to $0.0087$.

</details>


### [163] [Facet-0: A Robotic Foundation Model for Contact-Rich Precise Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01596)
*Haoyuan Deng,Haichao Liu,Wenkai Guo,Yuan Ling,Zaijia Yang,Yuanjiang Xue,Haosheng Sun,Liangzi Wang,Ziwei Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Facet-0 is a robotic foundation model for sub-millimeter precision assembly that predicts and values the contact consequences of actions by unifying multimodal representation learning with reinforcement learning around a joint action-wrench proposal, achieving 82% mean success across five precision tasks with 0.5 mm placement accuracy and 50 ms command latency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world robotic assembly at sub-millimeter tolerances requires spatial precision, compliant interaction, and robustness to contact failures—capabilities that existing robotic foundation models lack due to their inability to explicitly reason about contact outcomes during manipulation.

Method: Facet-0 unifies multimodal representation learning with RL post-training around a joint action-wrench proposal: causal wrench history is aligned with vision-language semantics and kinematic state, flow matching generates action chunks together with the expected future wrist-wrench profile, and a distributional Action-Wrench Critic distinguishes motions with similar task progress but different contact outcomes. Phase-aware rewards and contact-selective credit concentrate policy improvement on decisive interactions, while a lightweight bounded actor reuses frozen representations for on-robot adaptation; RL operates over executable Cartesian actions with an auxiliary wrench head preserving predictive action-contact coupling. The model is trained on ManuFacet-1K, a 1,000-hour force-synchronized corpus spanning three embodiments and multiple manufacturing cells.

Result: The bounded task-adapted system reaches 82% mean success on five sub-millimeter computer-assembly tasks, compared to 15% for the strongest baseline, with 0.5 mm placement accuracy and 50 ms command latency.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling and valuing contact consequences—through joint action-wrench generation and a distributional critic—enables robotic foundation models to achieve dramatically higher success on high-precision assembly tasks, demonstrating that contact-aware RL post-training is essential for agile manipulation at industrial tolerances.

Abstract: Real-world robotic assembly at sub-millimeter tolerances demands spatial precision, compliant interaction, and robustness to contact failures. We present Facet-0, a robotic foundation model that predicts and values the contact consequences of its actions. Facet-0 unifies multimodal representation learning and reinforcement learning (RL) post-training around a joint action-wrench proposal: a causal wrench history is aligned with vision-language semantics and kinematic state, and flow matching generates each action chunk together with the future wrist-wrench profile it is expected to induce. Deployment rollouts train a distributional Action-Wrench Critic to distinguish motions with similar task progress but different contact outcomes, while phase-aware rewards and contact-selective credit concentrate policy improvement on decisive interactions. To accommodate part-specific dynamics, a lightweight bounded actor reuses the frozen representation for on-robot adaptation; RL remains defined over executable Cartesian actions, while an auxiliary wrench head preserves predictive, non-commanded action-contact coupling. Trained on ManuFacet-1K, a 1,000-hour force-synchronized corpus spanning three embodiments and multiple manufacturing cells, the bounded task-adapted system reaches 82% mean success on five sub-millimeter computer-assembly tasks, compared with 15% for the strongest baseline, with 0.5 mm placement accuracy and 50 ms command latency.

</details>
